<meta>This page serves as the dedication and memorial preface for the work, establishing the connection between the deceased author Hugo Schulz and the famous surgeon Ferdinand Sauerbruch.</meta>

# In Memory of Hugo Schulz

The final work of Hugo Schulz—the translation of the medical prescriptions of Saint Hildegard of Bingen <note>Hildegard of Bingen (1098–1179) was a Benedictine abbess and polymath whose medical writings, *Physica* and *Causae et Curae*, blended natural science with spiritual theology.</note>—helped him endure days of heavy suffering with inner strength. It characterizes him as one of the few who, while devoted to detailed individual research, expect the ultimate solution only from great, universal connections. For this reason, he loved the history of medicine, which he mastered like almost no other. It provided his fundamental scientific works of earlier years with a longed-for and necessary philosophical <note>original: "weltanschauliche," referring to a comprehensive world-view or life-philosophy.</note> completion. For Hugo Schulz, the past and the present were always merely changing symbols of the eternal ideas of our art <note>The "art" refers to the "Ars Medica" or the art of healing.</note>. He possessed the wisdom to behold greatness and the grace to recognize it in the small.

His final thanks were given to his friend Ehrismann, into whose hands the dying man placed the proof sheets.

May this book be given as a remembrance to all those who revered and loved this truly noble man and great, kind physician.

->Sauerbruch.<- <note>Ferdinand Sauerbruch (1875–1951) was one of the most influential surgeons of the 20th century; his endorsement here lends significant weight to Schulz's legacy.</note>

<summary>A memorial dedication by the surgeon Ferdinand Sauerbruch honoring Hugo Schulz, noting that Schulz spent his final days translating the medical works of Hildegard of Bingen.</summary>
<keywords>Hugo Schulz, Hildegard of Bingen, medical history, Ferdinand Sauerbruch, Ehrismann, philosophy of medicine, dedication</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page serves as the introduction to the translation, establishing the historical context of Hildegard von Bingen’s medical writings and previous attempts to translate them.</meta>

In the year 1903, Paul Kaiser published the text of a manuscript kept in the Royal Library at Copenhagen under the title: "*Hildegard’s Causes and Cures*" <note>original Latin: "Hildegardis causae et curae"</note>. Paul Kaiser himself translated several sections of the handwritten text into German and published them in the "Therapeutic Monthly Journals" by O. Liebreich in 1902 under the heading: "The Writing of Abbess Hildegard on the Causes and Treatment of Diseases." Likewise, Johannes Bühler included a larger German-translated excerpt from the *Causes and Cures* in his book: "Writings of Saint Hildegard of Bingen, Leipzig 1922." However, as far as I have been able to ascertain, a complete translation of the text has not yet reached the public.

To facilitate orientation while reading the present translation, a few preliminary remarks are permitted.

The author of the *Causes and Cures* was born—as I gather from her biography by Johannes May: "Saint Hildegard of Bingen of the Order of Saint Benedict, 1098 to 1179"—in the summer of 1098 as the youngest of ten children of Hildebert, the Viscount of Bökelheim Castle on the Nahe, and his wife Mathilde. Sickly and weak from the beginning, she learned to walk only with difficulty and repeatedly suffered from nervous ailments throughout the further course of her life. At the same time, even as a child, she possessed a visionary disposition that was striking to those around her, and this disposition did not leave her in the time that followed. As early as eight years old, Hildegard was handed over to monastic discipline and schooling. She remained faithful to the monastic state and the Rule of Saint Benedict and, from the year 1147 until her death in 1179, she led the Benedictine convent founded at her instigation on the Rupertsberg near Bingen as its Abbess.

The majority of Hildegard’s many writings are theological in content. Among these, her book: "*Scivias*" (Know the Ways) <note>original Latin: "Scivias", a contraction of "Scito vias Domini"</note>, which made its way from the Rupertsberg in 1150, aroused the greatest interest on all sides and was read with zeal in German, Dutch, and French monasteries. In addition to this, however, Hildegard was also blessed at birth with the gift of observing the things and events of the outside world with open eyes and forming her own [thoughts] about them...

<summary>This preface introduces the first complete translation of Hildegard of Bingen's medical treatise, "Causes and Cures," and provides a biographical sketch of her early life and monastic career.</summary>
<keywords>Hildegard von Bingen, Causes and Cures, Scivias, Paul Kaiser, Benedictine Order, Rupertsberg, medical history, biography</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Hildegard of Bingen's medical writings, specifically focusing on the origins and influences of her work "Causae et Curae."</meta>

to be able to form thoughts. A significant inspiration and advancement in this field was granted to her by Bishop *Siward of Uppsala*, who was deeply interested in the natural sciences and medicine <note>original: "Arzneikunde"</note>. He visited the monastery on the *Disibodenberg*—where *Hildegard* lived before her move to *Bingen*—on the occasion of an official duty. In any case, two works are to be regarded as the result of her natural disposition and its further development through the study of writings available in her own monastic library, as well as those likely acquired through inter-library exchange. According to *Hildegard’s* own account, these were composed between 1150 and 1157: the "Physica, or Book of Simple Medicine" <note>original: "liber simplicis medicinae"</note> and the less extensive "Causes and Cures, or Book of Composite Medicine" <note>original: "liber compositae medicinae"</note>, the translation of which is presented here.

If one wishes to read an author from a long-past era as they intended to be read, and to understand them as they wished to be understood, it is a fundamental requirement—though admittedly not always easy to fulfill—that one strives to the best of one's ability to think oneself into the spirit and views of that age. As we can see from the records of *Hildegard’s* birth and death years, her life fell during the reigns of the last emperors of the Salian dynasty and the first of the Hohenstaufens. The Crusades had begun and had not remained without influence on the formation and development of the cultural and moral views of the Occident <note>The "Occident" refers to the Western world, specifically Medieval Europe.</note>. Cultural history teaches us on every page that these views differed in many respects from those of today. Standing upon that ground, living and thinking under the influence of the knowledge and ethical concepts of that time, *Hildegard* wrote her *Causes and Cures* <note>original: "causae et curae"</note>. Much that may initially seem strange to us or deviant from our habits when reading her book finds its simple explanation in the circumstances just described.

We may safely assume that personal experiences and things she witnessed herself play a role in *Hildegard’s* works. She was active as a physician <note>original: "Aerztin"</note> within the domain of her monastery and its wider surroundings, and she certainly tended and maintained her monastic pharmacy and the little herb garden <note>original: "Würzgärtlein"</note>. However, the actual foundation for her work could only be shaped by reading what had already been set down in writing by others before her. Unfortunately, *Hildegard* does not comment on this question at all. It could therefore be seen as an idle pursuit to indulge in further conjectures regarding the source material used by *Hildegard*. I believe, however, I may point out one thing: according to available information, there seems to have been—at least in earlier times—a connection between the University of *Salerno* <note>Salerno was the site of the most important medieval medical school in Europe.</note> and the *Benedictine Order*,

<summary>This page discusses the intellectual origins of Hildegard of Bingen's medical texts, attributing her knowledge to both personal experience as a monastic physician and the influence of contemporary scholars like Bishop Siward of Uppsala.</summary>
<keywords>Hildegard of Bingen, Causae et Curae, Physica, medieval medicine, Disibodenberg, Salian dynasty, Hohenstaufen, University of Salerno, Benedictine Order</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the intellectual and historical context of Hildegard of Bingen’s medical works, specifically addressing the sources of her knowledge and the intended audience for "Causes and Cures."</meta>

...[the Benedictine Order], which had its mother house on *Monte Cassino*, there seems to have been some relationship. Should this assumption be correct, it is conceivable that from this mother house, as well as from *Salerno* itself, the monasteries belonging to the same order were provided with scientific material. However, even here one does not move beyond mere conjecture. During *Hildegard’s* lifetime, however, the then already quite highly developed Arabic school of medicine had not yet succeeded in drawing the University of *Salerno* into its sphere of influence; thus we can at least conclude one thing: that *Hildegard* was not influenced in her chains of thought by the newly emergent <term>Arabism</term> <note>The term "Arabism" here refers to the influx of Arabic medical and scientific knowledge—such as the works of Avicenna and Rhazes—into Western Europe via translations, which revolutionized medicine in the late 12th century.</note>, but rather stood entirely upon the foundation of the teachings of *Galen* <note>Claudius Galenus (129–c. 216 AD), the Greek physician whose theories of the four humors dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> and his successors.

The circle of readers upon which *Hildegard* could count was, by and large, a fixed one. The circumstance that she made use of the Latin language required, from the outset, a knowledge of the same on the part of the reader. This prerequisite was met by relatively few individuals from the laity, whereas the majority of the clergy were, by the nature of their office, already proficient in Latin. There exists here a clear contrast between the writings of the Abbess *Hildegard* and the work which—admittedly nearly 200 years later—the Regensburg canon *Konrad von Megenberg* published in the German language under the title *The Book of Nature* <note>original: "Buch der Natur"</note>, and whose translation into Modern High German I published in the year 1897. When one compares the *Causes and Cures* <note>original: *causae et curae*</note> with the *Book of Nature*, one has the impression with both that their authors intended to offer their readers a kind of <term>household book</term> <note>original: "Hausbuch"; a practical manual intended for use in a home or community setting rather than for specialized academic study.</note> of medicine and natural history respectively. This applies to the *Causes and Cures* all the more as it also mentions animal diseases and their treatment, as well as details relating to agriculture. Both authors—the Abbess and the Canon—could not really go beyond the scope of a household or folk-book, since both, as laypeople in the fields of natural science and medicine, stood from the beginning in only a loose connection with the subjects they treated.

In the translation of the Latin text of *Hildegard’s* book, I have adhered as closely as possible to its wording. I am entirely aware that both the smoothness of the style and the convenience perhaps desired by the reader in becoming acquainted with the *Causes and Cures* may have suffered somewhat as a result. At first, I had made an attempt to form short, easily manageable sentences in place of the often abundantly rambling periodic structure of the original text. However, I then turned away from this—

<summary>The author argues that Hildegard was influenced by traditional Galenic medicine rather than the newer Arabic schools and explains that her Latin text served as a practical medical manual for the educated clergy.</summary>
<keywords>Hildegard von Bingen, Monte Cassino, Salerno, Galen, Arabism, Konrad von Megenberg, Book of Nature, Causes and Cures, medical history, translation methodology</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page concludes Dr. Hugo Schulz’s preface to Hildegard of Bingen’s medical writings. It follows a discussion contrasting Hildegard’s Latin works with the later German vernacular writings of Konrad von Megenberg, explaining why the editor chose to preserve Hildegard’s original style rather than modernizing it too heavily.</meta>

...come. In following this approach, I was increasingly overcome by the feeling that the author herself was being diminished. Her image—as she stood there in her strict <term>monastic habit</term> <note>The traditional clothing of a nun; Hildegard belonged to the Benedictine Order.</note>, intent only on offering the best of her knowledge to her contemporaries—began to fade, and foreign features took its place. Her unique sentence structure, and her way of using comparisons and evidence—which often seem quite naive to our modern sensibilities—simply belong together and must be accepted as a whole.

Greifswald, March 1932. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; Dr. Hugo Schulz.

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, the editor Dr. Hugo Schulz explains his decision to preserve Hildegard of Bingen's original, often "naive" style of argumentation and syntax to maintain her historical authenticity.</summary>
<keywords>Hugo Schulz, Hildegard of Bingen, monastic habit, editorial philosophy, Greifswald, medical history</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the primary text following Dr. Hugo Schulz’s preface. It moves from his editorial introduction into the cosmological narrative, likely based on Hildegard of Bingen’s medieval views on the origin of the universe and the celestial hierarchy.</meta>

# I.

**On the Creation of the World.** God was and is without beginning before the creation of the world. He was and is the light and the lamp of the world and was the life. Thus, when God wished to create the world, He made it out of nothing, yet the matter of the world lay within His will.

**On Matter.** For when God’s will revealed itself to carry out the work, the matter of the world came forth without delay from this will of His—just as it had been God’s intent—in the form of a dark, shapeless mass <note>original: "ungestalten Klumpen"; this refers to the primordial chaos or "materia prima" before it was organized into the elements.</note>.

**On the Creation of the Angels.** And the word of the Father rang out: Let there be light! <note>original Latin: "Fiat lux."</note> And the light and the radiant angels were created. For when He had spoken: "Let there be light!", there arose, besides the lights of the world, also the light that proceeds from the angels. But when He said: "Let there be the lights of the world!", this is to be understood as the light of the air <note>The physical light of the sun, moon, and stars that illuminates our atmosphere.</note> that we see.

**On the Fall of Lucifer.** Lucifer, however, caught sight of an empty space toward the north <note>original: "Mitternacht" (midnight). In medieval cosmology, the North was often associated with darkness, cold, and the dwelling place of the devil, based on passages like Isaiah 14:13.</note> where nothing was happening, and he intended to take up his residence there with the aim of putting into effect more and greater things than God; for he knew nothing of God’s intention to create the rest of creation. For he had not seen the face of the Father, nor did he know His mighty will, nor had he tested His goodness; because, even before he could have experienced this, he attempted to rebel against God. God had not yet revealed this, but kept it hidden, just as a mighty and brave man acts who hides his power for a time from his unsuspecting fellow men until he can recognize what they think of him and what they might begin or do. Now, as Lucifer in his perverse will soared toward nothingness—because that which he wanted to do was nothing—he fell down into the same and could find no firm footing, because he had no ground beneath him. For he had neither the highest height above him, nor the deepest depth beneath him, that could have held him and preserved him from the fall.

<vocab>Creation, World, Matter, Angel, Lucifer, Light, Will of God</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the origins of the universe, where God creates matter from His will and brings forth both the angels and physical light. It concludes with the rebellion of Lucifer, who, misinterpreting God's hidden plans, falls into a void of "nothingness."</summary>
<keywords>Cosmology, Creation, Matter, Angels, Lucifer's Fall, Divine Will, Light vs. Darkness</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological narrative, moving from the fall of Lucifer to the unique position of humanity as a "microcosm" and the relationship between the physical body and the soul.</meta>

For when he <note>Lucifer</note> soared toward nothingness, the undertaking of this ascent brought forth evil; and soon, through the zealous wrath of God, evil took fire within itself—without clarity and without light—revolving and turning like a wheel, revealing the glowing darkness within its interior. Thus evil separated from good, and there was no contact between the good and the evil, nor between the evil and the good. But God, the Father of Good, remained untouched by it like a wheel, because His Fatherhood is full of His goodness; and so this Fatherhood is the most just, most loving, strongest, most powerful and—viewed by this standard—comparable to a wheel. Now, however, this wheel is somewhere and is itself full of certain things. For if a wheel possessed nothing more than its outer circumference, it would be empty. And if by chance someone else came along and wanted to lend a hand there, this would not be possible. For two smiths cannot perform their work on one wheel at the same time <note>This metaphor illustrates the absolute sovereignty of God as the sole Creator; there is no room for a second "smith" or creator like Lucifer.</note>. O man, look upon man! For man has heaven and earth and everything that is created united within himself in one form, and everything lies hidden within him <note>A reference to the "Microcosm" theory, common in medieval thought, which suggests that the human being contains the structures of the entire universe.</note>.

**On Fatherhood.** Thus, Fatherhood is comparable to the circumference of the wheel; Fatherhood encompasses the wheel as a whole. The divine essence is within it, and from it all things originate, and outside of it there is no creator. Lucifer, however, is not a whole, but is split into many parts, because he wanted to be something he should not be. For when God created the world, He had decided since primordial times that man should come into being.

**On the Creation of the Soul.** When God created the light, which was fleeting and could fly everywhere, He had simultaneously decided to bestow a physical mass upon the spiritual life—the breath of life—an upright form made of earthly clay, which could neither fly nor blow away; and because of this inability, it was incapable of raising itself up <note>Unlike the angels or the "fleeting light," the human body is bound by gravity and physical matter.</note>. It was meant to be bound in this way so that it would look up to God all the more closely. Therefore, the old serpent <note>A traditional term for the Devil.</note> hated this binding, because man, despite his physical weight, could nevertheless raise himself up to God with the help of his reason.

**On the Elements and the Firmament.** God also created the elements of the world; they are in man and man works with them. They are: fire, air, water, and earth, and these four elements are so closely linked and connected with one another that none can be separated from the other

<summary>This page describes the separation of evil from God, the singular creative power of the "Divine Wheel," and the creation of man as a microcosm whose physical limitations are designed to turn his reason toward God.</summary>
<keywords>Lucifer, Divine Fatherhood, Microcosm, Creation of Man, Four Elements, Reason, The Wheel of God, Fall of Evil</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological description likely authored by Hildegard of Bingen. While the previous page discussed the fall of Lucifer and the nature of humanity as a microcosm, this page transitions into the physical and spiritual mechanics of the heavens, specifically the firmament, the stars, and the meteorological phenomena of storms and thunder.</meta>

...can be, and they hold each other so firmly that they are called the firmament <note>The "firmament" (from the Latin *firmamentum*) was understood in medieval cosmology as the solid structure or "strengthening" of the sky that supported the heavenly bodies and separated the upper and lower waters.</note>.

### **Of the Sun and the Stars.**

The sun, which is nearly the highest among them, radiates its light and its fire through them. Surrounding it stand certain stars of such great size and radiance that they are, in a sense, spread out like mountains through the firmament toward the earth. It is for this reason that they appear brighter the closer they are to the earth. In the vicinity of this same sun, however, there are also other stars of lesser size and brightness. Compared to the magnitude of the stars mentioned previously, these form the "valleys," so to speak, and are consequently less visible.

### **Of Storms.**

When the celestial air is dominated by greater heat and a more intense glow of fire, this heat sometimes causes a sudden boiling and a dangerous overflowing of the waters, sending them down to the earth. This is the origin of storms and the tearing of the clouds. It is just as when a pot, placed over a roaring fire, suddenly boils over and casts out foam <note>This "boiling pot" metaphor is a classic medieval explanation for thermodynamics, using everyday domestic imagery to explain complex natural forces like evaporation and precipitation.</note>. These storms occur, according to God’s judgment, mostly because of misdeeds that already lie in the past, or because of the sinful works of humans just recently committed, or also to point toward impending disasters, such as wars, famine, and sudden death. For all our works interact with the elements and are stimulated by them, because our actions stand in a direct relationship with the elements <note>This reflects the "Microcosm/Macrocosm" theory: the belief that the human body and soul are a small-scale version of the universe, and therefore human morality can physically affect the weather and the stars.</note>. If, however, there is less heat and fiery glow in the celestial air, the boiling and overflowing of the waters is less intense. This is like the contents of a pot on a weaker fire, which therefore boils only a little and produces only a moderate amount of foam. But when fire and water in the air are in the correct proportion to one another, this produces mild weather and a pleasant warmth, similar to a pot sitting on a gentle flame. Yet when the sun ascends so that its fire burns fiercely in the heights of heaven, the air is sometimes rendered dry and parched by the sun’s heat, and at times the fire of that same sun already borders upon the fire of thunder.

### **Of Thunder.**

Within thunder exists the fire of judgment, coldness, and a foul odor <note>Hildegard often associated thunder with the "fire of judgment," viewing it as a physical manifestation of divine displeasure or the terrifying power of God's voice.</note>. If the fire of thunder is ever touched by the sun's fire, it is triggered by this to send out a few flashes of lightning of no great significance.

### **Of Lightning.**

It thunders a little and then ceases, just as when a person, provoked to anger over something, [stops] their anger...

<vocab>Sun, Stars, Firmament, Storm, God's Judgment, Misdeeds, Elements, Thunder, Lightning, Weather</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the medieval view of the cosmos, explaining the physical hierarchy of the sun and stars and using a domestic "boiling pot" metaphor to illustrate how storms are created. It emphasizes the moral connection between human sin and natural disasters, portraying weather as a tool of divine judgment and a reflection of human action.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, cosmology, firmament, meteorological metaphors, divine judgment, elements, microcosm, Hildegard of Bingen, theology of nature</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological and meteorological descriptions of Hildegard of Bingen, following the discussion of storms as divine judgments. This section transitions from the mechanics of thunder and lightning into the formation of hail, snow, and rain, concluding with the symbolic and physical role of the four cardinal winds.</meta>

...does not let it break out, but suppresses and thus masters it. But sometimes the thunder-fire <note>original: *Donnerfeuer*. In Hildegard’s cosmology, thunder-fire is the active, energetic force within the storm, distinct from the physical lightning bolt itself.</note> is greatly excited by the excessive heat of the sun and put into great agitation, so that it sends out strong and dangerous lightning and raises its voice mightily, comparable to a man who, being in a violent rage, allows this to manifest in a dangerous deed. Then it also sometimes happens that the uppermost fire of the thunder, touched by the sun's fire, causes the cold that resides in the thunder to gather at one point—just as water drives ice together in one place—and this cold then drives the hail toward the clouds; the clouds receive it, distribute it, and send it to the earth.

### **Of Hail** 
Hail is, so to speak, the eye of thunder <note>A striking metaphor suggesting that hail is the "vision" or focus of the storm's power.</note>. But when the sun is in its descent in winter, it does not send its fire to the height of heaven; it burns more below the earth than above it, and thus does not glow in the height of heaven.

### **Of Snow** 
From this it happens that the waters which are located in the higher layers of air are, as it were, sprinkled with dust by the cold and yield snow <note>Hildegard views snow not just as frozen water, but as "dusted" water, perhaps observing the granular nature of snowflakes.</note>.

### **Of Rain** 
But if the waters are afterwards mild in the warmth, they send out rain; and when the sun then brings neither too much heat nor too much cold, it occasionally sends out a warm rain, just as a human, when they are in a more pleasant mood, sheds many tears out of pure joy.

### **Of the Winds** 
Four principal winds <note>original: *Hauptwinde*. These are the four cardinal winds—East, West, South, and North—which were viewed in the Middle Ages as the pillars holding the atmosphere in place.</note> belong to the firmament below and above the sun; they hold it together and surround the entire circle of the earth—that is, from the lower to the upper part of the firmament—as if with a cloak. The **East Wind** encloses the air and sends the mildest dew onto the dry land. The **West Wind** mixes with the flowing clouds so that it may hold back the waters so they do not burst forth. The **South Wind**, however, holds the fire back within its discipline and prevents it from burning everything. The **North Wind** holds the outermost darknesses in check so that they do not exceed their measure. These four winds are the wings of divine omnipotence. If they were all unleashed at once, they would entangle all elements, separate from one another, stir up the sea, and dry up all the waters.

### **Of the Day of Judgment** 
But now the four winds are enclosed by the key of God's majesty, as long as...

<meta>This page continues Hildegard of Bingen’s cosmological observations. Following the previous discussion on the formation of hail and snow, she now explores the stability of the elements, the nature of "nothingness," and the anatomical structure of the universe, specifically how the winds act as the "bones" and "soul" of the firmament.</meta>

the elements remain within their boundaries and will not rule over any human in a threatening way, except at the end of time, because then everything will be purified, and then they will raise a song in harmony. There is no creature that can exist through only a single quality; it must possess several.

### **Of Nothingness.**
Nothingness possesses no quality upon which it could be founded, and is therefore nothing. In the same way, other creatures that, out of their own will, unite themselves with nothingness, lose all their qualities and become nothing. <note>Hildegard often equates "nothingness" with the absence of God or the result of sin, which strips a being of its divinely-ordered purpose.</note>

### **Of the Firmament and of the Winds.**
The firmament encompasses the fire, the sun, the moon, the stars, and the winds. It exists through all of these and is strengthened by their inherent powers so that it cannot break apart. For just as the soul holds the entire human body together, so too do the winds hold the entire firmament together, so that it does not fall into disorder. They are invisible, just as the soul—which comes from God's secret counsel—is invisible. Just as a house has no stability without its stone corner-pillars, neither the firmament, nor the earth, nor the abyss, nor the entire world with all its components could exist without these winds, because all of this is connected to them and held by them. Indeed, the entire earth would burst and break apart if these winds were not there, just as a human would fall apart if he possessed no bones. For the Principal East Wind holds all eastern lands together, the Principal West Wind the entire western region, the Principal South Wind the entire south, and the Principal North Wind the entire north.

### **Of the Collateral Winds.**
Each of these principal winds has two other, weaker winds at its side, like two arms, into which it sometimes breathes a portion of its power. These weaker winds <note>original: *Seitenwinde*. Often translated as collateral or secondary winds in medieval meteorology.</note> possess the same natural constitution as their principal winds, so that each of these weaker winds imitates its principal wind as its superior, except that they possess significantly less power; and because they travel one and the same path with their principal wind, just as the two ears have one and the same path for hearing in the head. When they are driven by the avenging command of God, they receive their breath and their power from their principal winds and are then in such great unrest, stirring up such a roar and such dangerous collisions, just as bad humors <note>original: *schlechten Säfte*. In medieval medicine, "humors" or juices were the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which caused illness when imbalanced.</note> provoke dangerous disturbances in humans when they plunge them into sickness. However, those principal winds have never since...

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues Hildegard of Bingen’s discussion of the winds as the structural supports of the universe. Having previously compared the winds to the bones and soul of the cosmos, she now details the specific functions and "limbs" of the four principal winds—East, West, South, and North—and their roles in both the daily maintenance of the world and its eventual destruction at the end of time.</meta>

...since the creation of the world, [the winds] have been moved in their full power, nor will they be moved so until the **Last Day**. <note>original: "Jüngsten Tage." This refers to the Day of Judgment or the apocalypse at the end of time.</note> But then, when they reveal their total might and let their blowing go entirely, the clouds will be torn apart by their force and their clashing, and the uppermost layers of the **firmament** <note>In Hildegard's view, the firmament is the structural shell of the universe that holds the stars and planets.</note> will be massed together and shattered—just as the human body decays and all its limbs collapse when its soul is freed from the body upon leaving it.

The **East Wind** has two wings with which it draws the entire circle of the earth to itself. One wing regulates the path of the sun from the heights to the depths; the other wing rushes toward the sun so that it acts as an obstacle to it, preventing it from following its course further than it should. This same wind also gives all moisture its dampness and causes all seeds to sprout.

The **West Wind**, on the other hand, possesses, as it were, a mouth with which it can blow apart and scatter all waters, thus distributing and spreading all waters onto the correct paths so that one water does not rise above another, but instead keeps to its proper course. For it has power over the air that carries the water. This wind causes all greenery to wither, as well as everything else in its vicinity.

The **South Wind**, by contrast, carries a sort of iron rod that has three branches at the top and is pointed at the bottom. For its strength, with which it holds the firmament and the **abyss** <note>original: "Abgrund." This usually refers to the deep waters or the lower foundations of the earth.</note> together, is comparable to that of steel. For just as steel masters and tames all metals, and just as the heart gives the human his strength, so too the power of this wind holds the firmament and the abyss together in its domain so that they do not fall apart. At its upper end, it carries three powers like three branches: one moderates the heat of the sun at its rising, one at noon diminishes its scorching heat, and one in the evening cools its warmth, so that in these regions it does not exceed its measure. At the bottom, however, it is pointed because its power is also fixed in the abyss, so that moisture and cold do not rise upward from the depths beyond their boundaries. This same wind brings everything to ripeness: the leaves on the trees and the grass, the grain, the fruits, the wine, and all other products of the earth—it causes them to thrive unto maturity.

The **North Wind**, however, has four pillars with which it holds the entire firmament and the entire depth. Once it has pulled these upward, then the firmament will be massed together with the abyss. These four pillars hold the **four elements**, which are closely bound together there in the northern region and find their end just as they are supported upon pillars so that they do not fall down. But when, on the Last Day, this wind has shaken the four pillars with its force, then the firmament will also be folded together...

...as one is accustomed to folding together writing tablets. This wind is cold, brings chill, and binds and holds everything together through its coldness, so that it does not fall apart.

**Of the Sun.** The sun is, as was already stated above, located at the summit and, in a sense, at the center of the **firmament** <note>In the medieval worldview, the firmament was the solid structure of the sky that held the stars.</note>. It consists of fire and air and, through its fire, holds together the firmament with all its supports and its entire foundation—the air, the celestial bodies, the stars, and the clouds—so that they do not fall down or be scattered everywhere, just as the earth supports all the creatures that are upon it. The air of the heavens is also held together by the sun. When the sun stands at the highest point of the firmament, the fire runs to meet it and serves it. The sun strengthens the entire firmament and spreads its radiance over the whole earth, through which the earth then brings forth greenery and flowers. In that time, the days are long because the sun runs its course in the heights of the firmament, and it is summer. 

But when the sun inclines down toward the earth, the cold of the earth, which stems from the water, comes to meet it and causes all the greenery to wither. Because the sun has now inclined toward the earth, the days are short, and it is winter. Furthermore, in winter, the heat of the sun is greater beneath the earth than above it. For if the cold beneath the earth were as strong as it is above it, or if in summer the heat beneath the earth were as great as it is above it, the entire earth would burst as a result of this **excess** <note>original: "Uebermaßes." Hildegard emphasizes "discretio" or balance; any extreme or lack of moderation leads to destruction.</note>. When winter is imminent, storms rise up from the water and darken the light of the sun; therefore, the days also become dark. If, on the other hand, summer is approaching, the tempests fall down beneath the earth, and for that reason, the days are often beautiful and bright because the warmth of the sun is present. The sun is constant in its course, always full, and does not decrease. It sends its light to the moon when it approaches it, just as a man pours his seed into a woman.

**Of the Moon.** The moon consists of fire and a thin air; it stands in the air, has its dwelling place within it, and the air itself is strengthened by the moon. When it no longer has light, it goes beneath the sun. From the sun, a sphere then spreads out which draws the moon toward the sun, just as **agate** <note>1) original: "Achat." While we know agate as a gemstone, medieval science often attributed magnetic or attractive properties to various stones; here it is used to describe the "pull" between celestial bodies.</note> draws iron to itself. The sun ignites the moon, but the other planets and stars, as well as the air and the other celestial bodies in the vicinity of the moon, strive with all their might toward the moon and run together to help it in its ignition. After the moon is ignited, it gradually increases in size until it has become full, much like a woodpile or a house, once set on fire, begins to burn slowly at first...

...until finally the whole thing is in flames. Meanwhile, and as long as the moon is waxing until it becomes full, the sun strengthens the uppermost **firmament** <note>The fixed, solid structure of the sky in medieval cosmology.</note> and never departs from it. The sun carries the day with it and keeps it close because the uppermost firmament is bright, while the moon carries the night because the earth is dark. But when the moon is completely full, so that it appears like a pregnant woman, it releases its light from itself and passes it to the stars, and thus the stars become brighter.

### **O f  t h e  D e w .**

With the help of this warmth, the stars then warm and strengthen the air, and the warmed air sends down its **sweat** <note>The author uses the physiological term "Schweiß" (sweat) to describe the natural process of condensation.</note>—that is to say, its dew—onto the earth and makes it fertile. Therefore, the earth, moistened everywhere, allows the fruits to arise. For while the moon is waning—because it yields its light to the stars—and then, ignited by the sun, grows again to its full size, the stars gradually release the light and warmth they received from the moon into the air to warm and strengthen it. The air pours its sweat from above down to the earth to fertilize it. Thus, when the moon has become full again, the stars have meanwhile become empty of light so that they may take in fresh light and warmth from the moon once more; and when the moon has waned, the stars are full again to strengthen the air and earth; but when the stars have waned, the moon is full again.

### **O f  t h e  P u r i f i c a t i o n  o f  t h e  A i r .**

When the stars shine in the nights such that glowing spheres, like glowing spears, sometimes seem to fly through the air, this happens because the stars send their fire into the air to strengthen it, so that it in turn can fertilize the earth through its warmth. Therefore, one often sees and finds that the air purifies itself from the fire and heat of the stars, causing various impurities like mud to fall down from it.

### **O f  t h e  A i r - T h r e a d s .**

In a similar way, when summer and winter part—because summer is fading and winter is approaching, or when winter yields and summer wishes to arrive—a kind of **coagulum** <note>original: "Gerinnsel"; a clotted or thickened substance.</note>, like white threads, flies about in the air where the air is purifying itself. These **air-threads** <term>gossamer: fine spider silk often seen in fields during seasonal transitions, which medieval observers interpreted as atmospheric "purification."</term> sink to the earth at the meeting of those two seasons, winter and summer, when they collide with one another.

But if a lunar eclipse should become visible, the reason for this is that the elements and the storms occasionally collide with one another, as

...as if they had to settle a dispute between them. Then, however, the moon is neither completely extinguished nor does it wane, but the storms only darken it for a short time. The power of the moon is so great that it masters those storms and sends out its light again, because the moon's power is greater than those storms.

### Of the Five Planets. 

There are also five more planets; they receive their light from the fire and from the **heavenly air** <note>German: *Himmelsluft*. In this context, it refers to the ether or the pure atmosphere of the higher celestial spheres.</note> and constitute the power and strength of the firmament. They have their orbits in the heights as well as in the depths of the firmament so that they may shine where the sun does not radiate and where it barely lets its glow be seen. Wherever they are located and traverse their paths, they serve the cycle of the sun, holding back its speed as well as consuming its fire, so that under their influence the sun cannot send out such fiery heat as it would if they did not hinder it. Just as the five senses of the human body hold its frame together and form its adornment, so also those five planets hold the sun together and serve as its ornament.

### Of the Resistance of the Firmament. 

The firmament rotates with great speed, and the sun, together with the other planets, runs slowly against it in the opposite direction and **inhibits** <term>inhibits [hemmt]</term> <note>The author conceptualizes the sun and planets as a "brake" that prevents the outer heavens from spinning too fast and shattering.</note> its speed. For if the sun did not restrain the firmament by its resistance, or if it and the other planets ran with the same speed and in the same direction as the firmament's rotation, everything would be thrown into confusion and the entire firmament would break apart. 

If the firmament were immovable and did not revolve, the sun would stand over the earth for almost the entire summer without it ever becoming night, and stay beneath the earth for almost the entire winter without it ever being day. But as it is, it revolves in such a way that, while the firmament moves toward the sun and the sun toward the firmament, the firmament is more quickly condensed and made more resistant by the sun's heat—that is to say, when the sun traverses the firmament and completely penetrates and saturates it with its fire. 

Before the **Fall of Adam**, the firmament was immovable and did not rotate. After his fall, however, it began to move and revolve. From the **Last Day** <note>The Day of Judgment or the end of the world.</note> onward, it will stand immovable once again, just as it was on the first day of creation before Adam’s fall. But now it rotates so that it may receive its power and strength from the sun, the moon, and the stars; for if it stood still, it would be liquefied and softened in a short time and would flow apart. For this same reason, because—

...as it rotates at a fixed measure, it also purifies the elements. This purification sometimes manifests in the form of water-bearing, black clouds, such as we see. This is like when water, placed in a pot over a fire, throws up foam under the influence of the **boiling heat** <term>boiling heat [Siedehitze]</term> and is thus purified.

### Of the Harmonies of the Firmament.

In its rotation, the firmament produces wonderful tones <note>This refers to the "Music of the Spheres" (musica mundana), an ancient philosophical concept suggesting that the movement of celestial bodies creates a divine harmony inaudible to the human ear.</note>, which we cannot hear because of its immense height and breadth, just as a mill or a wagon makes sounds as it turns. However, the firmament possesses such height and extension around the earth so that humans and animals on earth do not perish, precisely because it is so far removed from them. For humans and animals would be destroyed by the fire, winds, water, and clouds if it were in their vicinity. Just as body and soul are one and support each other, so it is with the firmament and the planets, which also warm and strengthen each other. 

But as the soul gives life and strength to the body, so also the sun, the moon, and the other planets warm and strengthen the firmament with their fire and thus grant it its power. For the firmament is to be compared to the **head** of a human, the sun, moon, and stars to the **eyes**, the air to **hearing**, the winds to **smell**, the dew to **taste**, and the flanks of the earth to the **arms** and to the **sense of touch**. All other creatures in the world are compared to the **belly**, but the earth to the **heart**; for just as the heart holds the body together above and below, so the earth provides a solid foundation for the waters flowing on its surface and, on the other hand, acts as a barrier for the subterranean waters, so that they do not burst forth in an unruly manner.

### Of Lucifer’s Fall and the Creation of the Firmament.

The abyss is like the **feet** and the **gait** of a human. When the **Devil** <note>original: "Teufel"</note> fell from heaven, where he wished to sit and rule and yet could not create or bring forth a single creature, God immediately established the firmament so that the Devil might see and understand what and how much God could do and create. At that time, God also gave the sun, the moon, and the stars their place in the firmament, so that the Devil might see in them and recognize how much **splendor and glory** <note>original: "Pracht und Herrlichkeit"</note> he had lost.

### Of the Stars.

The stars are equal to one another neither in size nor in brightness; rather, some are larger, others smaller, some of stronger, others of weaker radiance. The firmament, however, is [influenced] from above by...

### Of the Twelve Zodiac Signs and the Planets.

Just as the firmament is guided in its orbit by the seven planets, so these also provide it with service and care, as it were, through the twelve **zodiac signs** <term>zodiac signs [Sternbildern]</term>. For when the sun has reached the sign of **Capricorn** <note>original: "Steinbock." Hildegard uses the literal meaning of the mountain goat to explain the sun's upward "climb" after the winter solstice.</note>, the two planets that had accompanied it as far as the sign of **Sagittarius** <note>original: "Schützen"</note> give it a sign, so to speak, to go upwards again and return to its former path, and they urge it to rise again as before. This sign is called **Capricorn** because it has a tendency to climb upward. 

The names of the twelve zodiac signs signify nothing else than that they—like the other five planets—according to their purpose, either cause the sun to rise high in the summer or receive it in the winter during its descent, thus performing their service to it in this way. But once the sun has reached the sign of Capricorn, the remaining three planets run beneath it and slowly push it upwards to the sign of **Aquarius** <note>original: "Wassermann"</note>. When it begins to rise here, it warms the earth within its foundations and likewise the waters that are beneath the earth; for this reason, the waters beneath the earth are warmer in winter than in summer. 

In the sign of Aquarius, the planet that constantly receives its heat from the sun—namely, the one that stands below the sun in the sign of **Cancer** <note>original: "Krebs"</note>—as well as the other approaching planets, now move backward. They accompany the sun as far as the sign of **Pisces** <note>original: "Fische"</note>. When it has reached this sign, it stands, as it were, in the middle of the waters. The fish, however, which had previously kept themselves hidden from the cold, now feel the sun's warmth and begin to spawn. 

The other planet, which stood to the right of the sun in the sign of Cancer, runs toward it here and pulls it upward to the sign of **Aries** <note>original: "Widder"</note>. Once the sun has reached the sign of Aries, the two lower planets also run toward it here, catch it, rise slowly upward with it, and push it forward like a ram with its horns. But as the sun rises higher, around the sign of **Taurus** <note>original: "Stier"</note>, these two planets remain behind, and two others come toward it—planets that are only rarely seen and only rarely show themselves unless they bring some kind of wonders <note>This likely refers to rare planetary conjunctions or comets interpreted as omens.</note> with them. These drive the sun forward with great force, like a bull that strongly...

<meta>This page continues the explanation of the sun's journey through the zodiac, detailing how specific planets guide the sun's movement and influence the seasonal changes on Earth from late spring through early winter.</meta>

...gores with its horns <note>This refers to the planets associated with the sign of **Taurus** (the Bull) mentioned at the end of the previous page, pushing the sun upward.</note>, and they lead the sun to its highest point, so that when it has reached the sign of **Gemini** <term>Gemini [Zwillinge]</term>, one of them moves to one side and the other to the other side of the sun. In this way, they separate until they have reached their highest position. Then, in the sign of **Cancer** <term>Cancer [Krebs]</term>, when the sun must already turn toward its descent, the planet which goes to its right hurries slightly ahead of it. There, it finds the other planet that stands below the sun, and immediately this planet, noticing the other, moves backward a little, and the other follows it. Then this planet returns again, and the first gives way, and the former follows. Thus, both go for a time, stepping forward and backward like crabs, until they move the sun toward its descent. The planet that stood to the left of the sun remains there, and now both planets accompany the sun and hold it during its descent, so that it does not run downward too quickly. 

In this way, they lead it to the sign of **Leo** <term>Leo [Löwe]</term>. Here, the two planets that stood in the sign of **Aries** <term>Aries [Widder]</term> meet the sun with a low humming sound. The sun, however, as if it were angered by the difficulty of its turning point, emits great heat, so that lightning and thunder resound because it turns only with difficulty toward its descent. But when it has reached the sign of **Virgo** <term>Virgo [Jungfrau]</term>, the two planets that had already stepped into its path in the sign of **Taurus** run to meet it there. From this point on, it travels its path more slowly and gently, because its heat and severity decrease, since the earth at this time no longer brings forth new fruit, but stands almost entirely in the full enjoyment of ripeness. 

Then both planets travel with the sun until the sign of **Libra** <term>Libra [Waage]</term>, where growth and withering lie, as it were, on a scale—where growth ceases and drought approaches. Now, one of the two planets again moves to one side and the other to the other side of the sun, and both separate just as they did in the sign of Gemini. Thus, they steer the sun to the sign of **Scorpio** <term>Scorpio [Skorpion]</term>, and here one of them remains behind. But then another planet meets the sun here, namely the one which ran below Cancer. Also, the planet which ran toward the sun there and went backward and forward now remains with the sun, and so both go together with it. Then, in the sign of Scorpio, all creeping creatures also seek out their hiding places in which they can conceal themselves over winter. 

The two previously mentioned planets then travel further with the sun to the sign of **Sagittarius** <term>Sagittarius [Schützen]</term> and remain there. Now, in the sign of Sagittarius, no planet travels together with the sun in the way they had until then; instead, they allow it to run its course at its own discretion, comfortably and slowly, because it is now already in its deeper descent.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the sun's celestial journey, picking up after its passage through Virgo. It describes the sun's descent through the autumnal and winter signs and the vital role of planets in maintaining the Earth's temperature during this period. The text then transitions into a macrocosm-microcosm analogy comparing the structure of the heavens to the anatomy of the human body.</meta>

It is just like when a ship is occasionally allowed to drift slowly downstream by itself without using oars, and these remain inactive for a time. But because the sun moves downward <note>This refers to the sun's lower path in the sky during autumn and winter.</note>, its heat becomes especially noticeable under the earth, as well as in the waters distant from the earth. Those two planets which accompanied the sun as far as the constellation of **Sagittarius** <term>Sagittarius [Schütze]</term> now rise upward toward the clouds and, through their own inherent heat, warm the air more than usual. Otherwise, everything on the earth would perish. In this way, they serve the sun until the constellation of **Capricorn** <term>Capricorn [Steinbock]</term>, where these same planets again urge the sun toward its former ascent and assist it, as has already been explained. 

The sun, however, is to be regarded in a way as the "hat" of the firmament <note>The author uses a domestic metaphor here: just as a hat crowns and protects a person, the sun acts as the crowning, heat-giving lid of the celestial sphere.</note>, which lets its light shine upon the whole firmament, the earth, and the waters, and bestows its heat, though not to all in equal measure. For this heat is strongest in the center of the earth, and there the earth is also most fortified by the sun; everything—fruits and animals—is more vigorously developed there than in other places. Because the sun sends its rays to different parts of the earth at different distances, in those places where the lands are oriented downward <note>Likely referring to the regions further from the equator or the "center" of the known world.</note>, both the earth and its fruits, as well as the animals living there, possess less strength than those in the center of the earth. The vine requires great heat to thrive and grows through warmth; in a land where there is much solar heat, the wine is strong. Likewise, grain requires both heat and cold, and where solar heat and cold are both present, the grain grows abundantly.

## On the Diversity of the Fruits of the Earth.

There are certain regions that are warm, others that are cold, and still others that are lukewarm. Accordingly, humans, animals, and the fruits of the earth behave differently. Nevertheless, they are all of one kind, even though they possess greater strength in one place and lesser in another due to the sun’s heat.

## On the Stability of the Firmament.

The firmament is held together by the stars so that it cannot fall apart, just as a human being is maintained by their vessels <note>In medieval science, "vessels" refers to the veins, arteries, and other channels that provide structure and carry vital fluids.</note> so that they do not flow apart or fall into pieces. Just as the vessels run through the entire human body from the feet to the head, so too do the stars run through the firmament. And just as the blood moves within the vessels, and as the blood sets the vessels in motion, making them leap and produce pulses, so too does the fire move within the stars, causing them to move and throw off sparks like leaps and beating pulses. These are the ordinary...

<summary>This page explains how the sun and specific planets regulate Earth's temperature during the winter months and introduces a direct anatomical comparison between the stars of the firmament and the blood vessels of the human body.</summary>

Stars, which within themselves cause a kind of storm to arise, depending on what the works of men are like at that moment. The planets, however, in contrast to this, are never moved unless they are influenced by the sun and the moon and those larger constellations determine it. From the place where each star is positioned, it traverses the entire firmament in an upward direction, like a blood vessel in a human being that rises upward from the foot to the head. The stars provide light and heat to the entire firmament, just as the vessels that permeate the human liver <note>In medieval physiology, the liver was considered the source of heat and the site where blood was produced, making this a direct comparison between the heavens and human biology.</note> provide that same liver with blood and heat. The stars are placed across the entire firmament—both over the firmament that we see during the day, as well as over that which we behold at night. However, through the mighty brightness of the sun, which defines the day, the stars are hidden so that they cannot be seen during the day because the sunlight is stronger than the light of the stars. It is as if the common people remain silent when the princes are named, and only when the princes withdraw do the common people step into the foreground. Otherwise, the stars would be perceived by day just as well as by night.

**What the stars indicate.** The stars occasionally make many signs known within themselves, depending on how humans currently carry themselves in their works. However, they indicate neither the future nor the thoughts of humans, but only that which man has already demonstrated as his own will, or what he pursues in words or in deeds, because the air absorbs this. The air communicates it to the stars, and immediately they reveal the works of humans. But God created the stars for the service of man, so that they might shine for him and serve him. Therefore, they also make his works known, just as a servant announces the will and the work of his master. For just as the soul in a human first lights up and only then proceeds to the deed, so too the stars light up in the firmament and indicate the works of man while he is already engaged in them.

**What the planets indicate.** The sun, the moon, and the remaining planets do not always indicate human activity and conduct, but only rarely. If they do indicate something, however, it is a great matter and concerns public affairs <note>Hildegard suggests that while fixed stars reflect individual or common daily actions, the planets—the "wandering stars"—are reserved for significant historical or political events.</note>. The uppermost planet, which is called "the Eye," <term>The Eye [das Auge]</term> and the one which is the next one above the moon and is called "the Poor One," <term>The Poor One [der Arme]</term> are fastened like two pegs at the lowest point of the firmament and are not visible to humans. Only occasionally, when the clouds are darkened, a "weather-ray" <note>likely referring to lightning or a specific atmospheric phenomenon</note> appears in the clouds, coming from them as a sign that something is imminent. If, however, any signs should appear in the sun...

It does not look like a star, but rather shoots out arrows, as it were. Sometimes it appears in a pale glow, and then it announces something. **Of the Dawn.** The reason that the sun looks red in the morning at its rising lies in the cold and moisture of the air, because the moisture and cold prevailing at this time cause human eyes to become red. It is similar late in the day when the sun looks red in the evening: it comes from the coldness of the air, because it now bows down toward the ocean. But the evening star, which is also called "the Companion" <note>Likely Venus, which stays close to the sun</note>, is like an intimate, secret friend of the sun. It determines the yield of grain and wine; at one time there is more, at another time less. Afterward, the planet appears which is called "the Poor One" <note>Likely Mars, associated here with scarcity or the "withering" of crops</note> and lets its signs be seen, as has already been said above. It also diminishes the yield of the fruits on the earth.

**What the Moon Signifies.** If special signs appear on the moon, these are again caused by those two planets which, fixed like two pegs in the deepest depths of the firmament, approach the sun to make their signs known and thereby influence the moon, either by igniting it or darkening it. The moon, however, absorbs into itself the foul, useless vapors from the air as well as the heat of the pure air, the beneficial moderate movement of the air, the dangers of storms, the vigorous air which governs all growth, the air that allows fruits to develop, and that which brings about drought and want—and this is winter. All of this it gathers into itself like a man who fills wine into a wineskin, and in this...

...lifts and then drinks it back up. In the same way, the moon collects all this within itself while it is waxing and drinks it out again while it is waning. The same applies to the seasons, because these are regulated by the moon, and also to the condition of the air—such as downpours or the drought of winter or midsummer—because these too act under the influence of the moon. Everything is regulated according to the moon, for she is the mother of all seasons; and just as the sons of a mother are counted according to the mother, so all times are reckoned according to the moon.

Also, the air and the stars sometimes take into themselves the works of men and, in accordance with them, sometimes expand according to divine decree, unite, and send forth their breath. And when they rise up to do this, the moon is subsequently set in motion, and accordingly the days will be either bright and clear or stormy. Thus, the moon is beset by many dangers and storms, just as a mother endures many dangers and much suffering at the birth of her children. Therefore, the times of the moon are also healthy and unhealthy, ripe and unripe. If man acted as he truly should, all seasons and the air within those seasons would remain constant—so that in one spring it would be just as in the previous spring, in this summer just as in the last summer, and so with the others. But because man, in his disobedience, disregards the fear and love of God, all elements and times also overstep their rights. This can be compared to the internal organs <note>original: "Eingeweide"; referring to the viscera or "innards" as a microcosm of the universe.</note> in man. For when man exceeds the measure that is proper to him, his organs follow suit. With evil works, he disregards justice and burdens and darkens the sun and moon: and these then, following the example given to them, cause storms, downpours, and drought to occur. For the stomach and the bladder of man take in everything by which his body is nourished. If these two receive food and drink in excess, they bring to the whole body the storms of...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between cosmic cycles and the human body. ...corrupted humors <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säfte"; in medieval medicine, these are the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that determine health and temperament.</note>, just as the elements behave according to the example of humanity. For a man sows his seed when the proper time of warmth and cold has arrived, and this then rises into fruit. But who would be so foolish as to sow his seed during the greatest heat of summer or the harshest cold of winter? This seed would surely perish and not sprout.

**On the Time of Procreation.** So it goes for those people who heed neither the maturity of their age nor the phases of the moon, but wish to conceive at any time according to their own whim: their children waste away physically amidst much pain. Yet God still takes these young buds to Himself, even if they are physically frail. Therefore, a man must pay attention to the maturity of his body and also seek the correct lunar phases with as much care as one who directs pure prayers to God; that is to say: he should conceive his offspring at a time when his children do not have to perish from weakness. He should not act like a man who gulps down his food like a glutton, caring nothing for the proper mealtime, but he should be like one who observes the orderly time, so that he is not like a glutton. Thus, a man must behave and investigate the correct time for his procreation. A man should not approach a woman while she is still a young girl, but only when she is a maiden <note>original: "Jungfrau"; here referring to a woman who has reached physical/sexual maturity</note>, because she is then ripe. Likewise, he should not touch a woman before he has a beard, but only when he has a beard, because he is then ripe for the fertility of offspring. A person who lives for gluttony and drunkenness often becomes leprous and crooked in his limbs. But whoever maintains moderation in eating and drinking will have good blood and a healthy body. So too does the man who constantly gives in to his desires in his passion and in the excess of his physical strength; when the urge for procreation rises within him and he gives forth his seed wastefully, he often enough perishes by his own seed. But he who sheds his seed at the right time brings forth well-ordered children.

**On the Elements.** The elements, however, drink in everything that belongs to human nature when man takes the elements into himself, because man is with them and they are with man, and the blood of man flows accordingly. Therefore it is also written: "Heaven and earth lament over man!", because the restless strife in the works of men often sets the elements in motion, as when a man holds a net in his hand and moves it.

Likewise, man also sets the elements in motion, so that they send out their radiations according to his actions.

### **On the Influence of the Moon.**

The lunar cycle does not rule over human nature as if the moon were its god, nor as if man received some natural power from it, or as if the moon granted or took away anything from human nature or determined anything for it. Rather, the moon encounters man through the "flavor of the air" <note>Hildegard uses the concept of "air-flavor" to describe how the atmosphere carries qualities that affect the senses and the soul.</note> in every one of his life's works. Thus, the blood and the humors <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säfte"; in medieval medicine, these are the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that determine a person's health and temperament.</note> within a person are moved according to the timing of the moon's motion. This means: depending on how the moon moves the air during fair or foul weather, and how the blood and humors then flow within the person, the moisture within a person shapes their nature and outward behavior. 

But even when a person’s vessels swell—under the influence of anger, rage, lust, excessive drinking, grief, or physical illness, as well as the constant shifting in the chaos of human habits—the moisture within the person absorbs the "flavor" of all these things into its nature, just as every cooked dish retains its own flavor according to its kind. Nevertheless, as has been said, the Holy Spirit permeates the entire human being—as seen, for example, in the prophets, the wise, the good, and the righteous—by drawing them to Himself with careful selection. Like the sun piercing through storm clouds, He penetrates and illuminates them, so that this permeation by the fire of the Holy Spirit overcomes the wavering nature of man, as it is written: 

> All that is born of God conquers the world! <note>A reference to 1 John 5:4.</note>

And so man does not sin. Just as simple foods are transformed into something better tasting through the flavor of spices and thus lose their ordinary taste, so too is the inferior nature of man transformed into something better than it was at his conception through the fire of the Holy Spirit. 

Thus, man becomes different in his nature, because that which is heavenly conquers and overcomes that which is earthly. Therefore, everything rejoices in God, and the ancient serpent <note>A traditional reference to the Devil or the tempter in Eden.</note> has become an object of mockery. Now, however, as stated above, the remaining small stars make visible the various daily and lesser deeds of humanity. For if there is fair weather among the clouds and no movement of winds, storms, or downpours in the air—meaning the stars shine in their full brilliance—and then some cloud covers all the stars so that they cannot be seen, even though there is no movement in the air, and if this remains so throughout the night, or even in the same way through the second and third nights, then this is not without significance as a sign...

### **Why the Planets Announce Nothing of Their Own Power.**

All these portents <note>Signs or omens of future events.</note> do not arise from an inherent power of the planets, stars, or clouds; rather, God has permitted them, and they occur according to His will and counsel. This is because God wished to place the works of humans before their own eyes, just as a coin shows the image of its ruler. <note>Hildegard suggests the cosmos acts as a mirror reflecting human moral behavior back to humanity.</note>

### **On the Power of Fire.**

When God created the world, He founded it, as previously stated, upon the four elements: fire, air, water, and earth. Fire, which stands at the highest point of the firmament <term>firmament</term> <note>In medieval cosmology, the sphere of the stars and the uppermost part of the sky.</note> as well as among the elements, possesses five powers: heat, cold, moisture, air, and motion, just as man consists of five senses. Fire burns, but cold resists it, so that its heat cannot spread beyond its proper measure. Water comes to its aid with its moisture, so that its vapor can rise. Through the air, however, the fire catches flame, and through motion it is fanned, so that its flame shines bright.

### **On the Powers of the Air.**

The air, however, possesses four powers: it sends forth the dew, causes all plants to turn green, sets the breeze in motion through which it coaxes out the blossoms, and distributes warmth everywhere, through which it lets everything ripen, just as it is itself spread across the four parts of the world. The air is the breath that pours moisture into the germinating plants through the dew so that everything can turn green, brings forth flowers through its blowing, and gives full maturity to everything through its warmth. 

The air, however, which is closest to the position of the moon and the stars, moistens the celestial bodies just as the earthly air gives life and movement to the earth and to both the unreasoning animals and those endowed with senses, according to their kind, without itself diminishing in the process. But when these animals die, the air returns to its former place; it does not increase in quantity, but remains as it was before. The earthly air, which drenches the earth with moisture, lets trees and herbs turn green, grow, and move. Likewise, as long as it remains within them, it does not decrease in quantity, nor does it increase when it leaves the felled or uprooted plants. It remains in the same state as before.

### **On the Powers of the Soul.**

The soul of the human being, which comes down into man from heaven from God, [animates] him... <note>The text cuts off here, beginning the transition to the theological and biological nature of the soul.</note>

## On the Powers of Water.

Water possesses fifteen powers: heat, air, moisture, flooding, speed, and mobility. To the trees it gives their sap, to the fruits their taste, and to the herbs their greenness. With its moisture, it saturates everything with wetness, carries the birds, nourishes the fish, allows the animals to live in its warmth, holds back the worms <note>Likely referring to all manner of small creeping or crawling creatures, including larvae.</note> in its foam, and sustains everything, just as the Ten Commandments and the five books of Moses <note>The Pentateuch, the first five books of the Bible.</note> in the Old Testament do, all of which God has designated for spiritual insight. For from a living source spring the waters that wash away all impurity. In all mobile creatures, water is also easily moved, and for immobile creatures, it is the spark and cause of all growth. It flows through the warmth of the moist air; if it did not have this warmth, it would be hard <note>Frozen into ice.</note> because of the cold. Thus, through the warmth of the air it descends, and through its moisture it flows. If it did not have this air, it would not be able to flow. Through these three powers—heat, moisture, and air—it possesses its easy mobility, so that nothing can resist it once it has gained the upper hand. It provides sap to the trees, makes them flexible through its air, and through its warm moisture, it imparts taste to the fruits of the trees, according to their kind. Through its flowing moisture, herbs contain their greenness within them, and even stones sweat from its dampness. Thus, the power of water holds everything together so that it does not come to harm, because its moisture perspires within everything. Water also carries the waterfowl with its warmth and nourishes the fish, because they originated within it and live by its breath <note>Hildegard often characterizes the elements as having a "breath" or vital spirit that sustains specific types of life.</note>. However, the wild animals that can endure in the water do so through its warmth, and the creeping creatures receive their zest for life through the breath of the water, so that they may live in this way. Thus, water holds and carries everything through its powers.

## On Matter and the Animation of Creatures.

When in the beginning God's Word rang out, the mass of creatures was without fire and cold. The Spirit of God, which is fire and life, hovered over the waters. At that time, this same Spirit breathed life into every created creature, each according to its kind, and with this breathing, ignited a fire within it, so that everything created, according to its kind, would carry fire and life within itself. The working of the divine Word reveals itself in the greening <term>viriditas</term> <note>Greening power: Hildegard’s central concept of the divine vitality and "freshness" found in nature and the soul.</note>. There would be no greenness if it were not...

Every creature would have to stand abandoned without any comfort, fall apart and perish, if it were not strengthened by the foundation of the fiery spirit of life <note>A concept in medieval natural philosophy referring to the animating force that gives life to physical bodies.</note>.

### On the Mobility of Water.

Just as the Spirit of the Lord signifies fire and life and bestows upon every creature its being and existence, water is also easily mobile within itself, because it gathers, holds, and strengthens the other creatures. Furthermore, it is slippery and unstable, bringing life and death to many. It also holds within itself certain living beings which, due to their special nature, cannot rise above its surface because they would fall, and these it sometimes kills. Nevertheless, in the course of the water, wherever it flows, wind and fire are present.

### On the Sun and Its Waters.

The waters that are situated nearly in the middle of the sun—when the sun stands in the middle of the firmament <note>The vast, solid dome of the sky according to ancient and medieval cosmology.</note> as if in its heart—are very powerful and have an extensive course. From the heat of the sun, they are thick, and from the air, they are strong. They are not hindered by the power of flowing, because they do not flow. However, they are indeed moved by the wind. Also, the sun occasionally draws the fire that is within the waters to itself, and the storm wind sometimes gathers those same waters into massive waves, like a flame that sets some object on fire. Then the water rises up like a fire that flares up, and the water follows the fire, and so hills and mountains, as it were, arise. But when these subsequently recede and are subdued, they let fall their seed—the salt produced from fire and water—just as herbs cast out their seeds when they reach maturity.

### On Salt.

Salt derives its dryness from fire, but its taste stems from the moisture of the water.

### On the Diversity of Waters.

The streams that flow from these great waters—that is, from the sea—and the springs that arise from them are salty and contain a stronger fire and greater powers within them than other waters. In the same way, the heart has more strength than the rest of the body. For the great currents from which they originate possess greater powers than other rivers, because they flow over the first, healthy sand laid down from the beginning, and not over the sand they have formed or exposed during their course. Water forms, in a sense, the water-
