the body of the earth, and the earth is, as it were, the heart of the water, because the water surrounds and permeates the earth, just as the body encompasses and covers the heart; and the earth, in turn, holds the water, just as the heart holds the body. The water of the great sea that surrounds the world forms, so to speak, one side of those waters which are located above the <term>firmament</term> <note>In medieval cosmology, the firmament was envisioned as a solid dome or series of spheres separating the Earth from the celestial waters above.</note>, because the waters existing at the greatest height above the firmament and those at the greatest depth below the firmament are mutually connected. Opposite these waters are layered various strata of the firmament, which are folded together like the leaves of <term>writing tablets</term> <note>Referring to wax tablets or codices, suggesting the firmament has a structured, layered complexity.</note>, so that they may hold together the various currents and floods of the waters. But just as the soul encompasses reason, understanding, knowledge, and feeling within itself, so also the firmament holds the <term>four elements</term> <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water—the fundamental components of the universe in ancient and medieval science.</note> according to their distinct natures, and does not let them perish. These waters remain uninterrupted just as they were once placed, and they water their shield—that is, the earth—by pouring and spilling their water upon it, so that these waters sometimes spread out over the earth and sometimes rise and ascend. At other times they are poured out in rain, and thus the water strengthens its shield through the winds and the air, so that it does not flow apart and fall away. The sandy expanse by the sea, which since creation has been placed toward the <term>Orient</term> <note>The East.</note>, is frequently touched by the everlasting breaths of the earth. From this come the spices and the other medicinal herbs in this sand. If a human could have these, they would no longer have to suffer from any disease. Furthermore, if certain stones could flow out from this sand and thus come into the possession of man, they would drive away diseases, plagues, and decay from him. However, the water there is so vast and so deep that one cannot obtain them.

### On the Outflow of the Sea.

Because in the Orient the sand and the shore are so deep, the sea does not flow away there, even when it rises and expands. In the West, however, and in the South and the North, such a depth of sand and shore does not exist. Therefore, the sea often overflows its banks there and causes great, extensive flooding when, as was already mentioned above, it is driven to madness by the fire of the storms. Consequently, it also collects much that is useless and much filth within itself there, attracting all the decayed material from humans, livestock, birds, and crawling things. Because of this, the springs and rivers which flow out at these parts of the sea are not as healthy or as good as those which pour forth from the Eastern Sea.

<summary>This page describes the Earth as the 'heart' of the waters and explains the cosmic structure of the firmament, while noting that the deep sands of the East produce unreachable but perfect medicines.</summary>

## On the Various Properties of Waters.

The salt-containing springs and streams that, originating from the water of that eastern region, rise in various lands, are pure and, like the air, are colored somewhat green because of their purity. In sandy soil, they rise in drops wherever they flow; they are medicinal and useful, can be drunk, and are also good for cooking food. A sick person who diligently drinks these waters regains their health, because they take away from them the harmful vapors, stench, and the <term>putrefaction</term> <note>The process of decay or rotting; in medieval medicine, this often referred to the "spoiling" of internal fluids.</note> of the <term>bad humors</term> <note>Referring to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval doctors believed must be balanced for good health.</note> like a good ointment. If, however, a person is physically healthy, these waters harm them to some extent if they drink them, bringing internal sores, because the water finds nothing in the healthy person to cleanse.

The waters of the rivers and living, bubbling springs, however, which rise in that eastern region without a salty taste and come from the East, are pure and possess a pleasant warmth combined with a mild coldness, so that they are moderately warm and cold and useful for people for food and drink, for bathing and for washing. Upon the hands, however, they are somewhat sharp and hard.

But the salty waters that come from the West are quite murky, like a whirlpool. One can indeed cook food with them, because they are partially purified by the fire during the boiling process. Taken unboiled for drinking, however, they are harmful, because they draw to themselves all kinds of filth and putrefaction in the <term>Western Sea</term> <note>Likely referring to the Atlantic Ocean, which in medieval cosmology was often associated with the edge of the world and the setting sun.</note> and human corpses also float there. If, in an emergency and for lack of other water, it cannot be avoided that they must be drunk, they must first be boiled and then, when they have cooled down again, they may be drunk.

The salt-free rivers and the salt-free living springs that rise in the West and flow further from the West, where the sun sets and "becomes smaller," have colorless and thick water and reach <term>perfection</term> <note>Meaning a balanced or "tempered" state.</note> neither through warmth nor through cold, because there both warmth and cold are deficient. Therefore, these waters are also quite useless for human use—that is, for drinking, washing, and bathing—because they are not "cooked" by the sun. If necessity forces some essential task to be performed with them, they must be thoroughly boiled over a fire and then given some time to cool until they have reached the correct temperature. Then, however, they are somewhat fit for nourishment, because they are thoroughly boiled down with the food.

The waters, however, which are salty and rise from the sea in the southern region, whether they be springs or rivers, are quite bright in color but are nonetheless not very pure and are fit neither for nourishment nor for drink, because they are extremely poisonous. For small, very poisonous worms, as well as...

The waters of the rivers and the lively, bubbling springs that rise in the South, free from any salty taste, and are hot due to the intense heat prevailing there, would hardly be dried up by the glowing heat of their fire—even if they flowed separately from other waters—because they are in contact with and originate from inextinguishable fires. For the preparation of food, as well as for other uses, they would be well-suited at their <term>boiling heat</term> <note>original: *Siedhitze*</note>, because they have been selected and boiled down by the fire.

They are good to use for food and drink, for baths and for washing, because they have been freed from all impurity as well as from their bitterness, like water that is purified in a pot by the heat of the fire. For the power of the salt there is not healthy, because in those regions heat and cold are reversed. They are therefore suitable neither for the preparation of food nor for drinking, nor can they hardly be used for any other purpose. 

The other, salt-free waters, however, which rise from that same North near the East, are cold and useful, because they are touched slightly by the air that blows from the East. This air is healthy because it is tempered, that is, neither very warm nor very cold. This air blows between the mountains that were established there from the beginning and is, in a sense, a <term>wing</term> <note>original: *Fittich*; used here metaphorically to describe a protective or life-sustaining influence of the atmosphere.</note> for those living on the earth. Therefore, it is also healthier and more curative than other air. 

From the waters that take their origin here, small brooks and springs arise that are, so to speak, pure, yet quite murky. These waters also have a particular taste, like wine or something else. These waters sometimes suppress <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: *Wassersucht*; an archaic medical term for edema, the accumulation of fluid in the body's tissues or cavities.</note> and keep <term>gout</term> <note>original: *Gicht*; a painful form of arthritis; in this period, the term often covered a variety of joint and limb pains.</note> in check, if those plagued by these diseases drink them. The character of these waters resists the nature of other water <note>2) Likely referring to standard or common water.</note> and also that of water itself. They hem in the other waters in whose neighborhood they flow and become their master through the power of their nature. For the preparation of food, for drinking, bathing, and washing, however, they are not to be used, because they [affect] the others...

Also, when drunk, they cleanse the person internally of bad <term>humors</term> <note>original: *Säfte* (juices or humors). In medieval medicine, health was determined by the balance of four bodily fluids.</note>.

The salt-free rivers and the lively, bubbling, unsalted springs, which likewise rise almost in the middle of the northern region and flow onward from there, are pure and possess the color of rock crystal mixed with the color of iron. They are very cold and very useful because these waters, being uncontaminated, are neither foul-smelling nor poisonous, as the changing position of the sun does not affect them. These waters also possess the proper taste, are useful for humans and other animals, and are good to use for cooking, drinking, bathing, and washing. They are also suitable for certain medicines.

The salty waters, however—of both the springs and the rivers—that come from the northern corner in the western region are somewhat blackish, that is: colored black-brown; they are not particularly pure and are useful neither for food nor for drink, because they bring death. For there dwell in the northern region, where it borders the west, large and very evil <term>worms</term> <note>original: *Würmer*. In this context, the term often refers to serpents, dragons, or monstrous reptiles rather than simple earthworms.</note>, which can be neither looked upon nor touched by humans without the danger of death. These vermin take those waters into themselves and then spit them out again, which constitutes their nourishment. Therefore, these waters are so dangerous because they possess both heat and cold simultaneously there. The worms dwell there because they are of a cold nature and can endure the cold, but not the heat.

The salt-free waters, also coming from the northwest, are pure, whitish, or rather: gray-colored, and are to be used neither for livestock nor for humans—neither for food nor for drinking or other purposes—because they can be digested only with difficulty and people easily swell up because of them, as they are poisonous and wound the human intestines with <term>ulcers</term> <note>original: *Geschwüre*</note>. Weakly people are very much brought down by their consumption, while healthy people can hardly digest them.

In this same northern region, the nature of the waters is more varied and diverse than the nature of the waters in the east or in the south or in the west, because the waters in those regions are penetrated by the sun's heat and kept at a moderate temperature. In the northern region, this is not the case, because the sun does not reach those areas.

Swamp waters, however—regardless of which parts of the earth they are located in—behave consistently like a poison, because they carry with them unclean and harmful dampness from the earth and the poisonous foam of worms. These waters are the very worst...

Bread, however, and food and beer cooked with such water can be consumed in some quantity because they are purified by the fire <note>The author reflects a common pre-modern understanding that the heat of fire "cleanses" the impurities or "pestilence" found in stagnant water.</note>.

Wells and gushing springs, however, that flow from swamps have somewhat better water than the water from the swamps themselves, because they are freed from certain impure things. They can be tolerated reasonably well; nevertheless, they are neither particularly good nor of much use. But all waters that are harmful at their place of origin become all the healthier the further they flow from their source, because after their swift and long course, they lose what is harmful and poisonous within them and are sifted and purified of it during the long, continuous flow.

The water from wells, however, that are dug deep into the earth so that it stands still and has no outflow, is better and more pleasant for food and drink and for other human use than the water from gushing springs that flow away. Compared to bubbling and flowing spring water, it is like a mild ointment since, because it does not flow, it is repeatedly warmed through by the mild temperature of the air. 

Spring water is indeed <term>hard</term> <note>original: *hart*. In historical culinary terms, "hard" water was noted for making legumes and meats difficult to soften during boiling.</note>, and because of its hardness, it resists the food so that the food can only be softened with difficulty by the fire and cannot easily be cooked through. Because well water is purified and clean, it has only a little foam, but in exchange, it also cleanses the food correspondingly less; and the food is also less cleaned by it than food is accustomed to being cleaned by other water, which is freed from filth by boiling with such water. 

Spring water is indeed thinner and purer than river water, because where it arises and flows onward through the earth, the sand, or the rock, it is purified and becomes clean. It is good for humans as a drink because it is free of dirt, yet hard and in its powers somewhat close to wine. It is not suitable, however, for preparing food for a meal, nor for washing the eyes, because of its <term>sharpness</term> <note>original: *Schärfe*. This likely refers to a high mineral content or perceived "acidity" that would irritate the sensitive membranes of the eyes.</note>. 

The water of streams flowing upon the earth, however, is "thick" because it is permeated by the sun and the air; it is also quite foamy and not healthy for drinking, as the unsteady behavior of the air and the elements is mixed into it. It is also occasionally contaminated by "smoke"—that is: the vapors and the mists that come down from some unhealthy mountains—as well as sometimes by the air, which here and there comes into contact with certain harmful substances. Therefore, such water is not fit for drinking and harms people unless it is boiled first, so that...

Rainwater, however, is sharp and frees sick people from foul vapors, bad <term>humors</term> <note>original: *Säfte*. In historical medicine, health was determined by the balance of "humors" or bodily fluids; rainwater was believed to "scour" the system of excess or corrupt fluids.</note>, and putrefaction. In contrast, it harms healthy people somewhat because it finds nothing in them to purify. If it has stood for a time in cisterns, it becomes milder and is wholesome for both healthy and sick people. Nevertheless, spring water is considerably healthier at its source and in its further course. 

When the sun draws its heat to itself, the waters have the greatest cold and send out their foam—that is, the snow—which covers the earth, makes its growth-promoting power fertile, holds it back, and does not harm the fruits of the earth. Its water, however, is of no use for human consumption; it is thin and impure, and if someone drinks from it, ulcers and rashes very easily arise in them, and their intestines become filled with <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: *Schleim*. Associated with coldness and dampness in the body, which medieval physicians believed led to respiratory and digestive issues.</note>. After its consumption, rainwater removes phlegm from the stomach through its sharpness, but creates ulcers in the intestines. Drunk snow-water, however, extinguishes the...

For if someone were to drink water from cloudbursts or from melted hail, they would fall into a long-lasting, wasting disease <note>The German "Siechtum" implies a chronic, debilitating illness.</note> and their flesh would split and be destroyed, so that many would die for this reason.

## On the Powers of the Earth.

The Earth is cold by its nature and possesses seven powers, which are: it is half-cold in summer and warm in winter; it harbors both growth and drought within itself; it brings forth sprouts; it supports living creatures; and indeed, it carries everything. In this way, God also worked for six days and rested on the seventh, when He made everything He had created subject to humanity for their use. The Earth is, in fact, cold in its depths during the summer because the sun procreates through the power of its rays at this time; in winter, however, it is warm in its depths, for otherwise it would shatter as a result of the cold rigidity <note>This reflects the medieval "antiperistasis" theory, which suggested that a quality (like heat) is intensified when surrounded by its opposite (like the cold of winter).</note>. And so, in its warmth, it allows the greening to appear, and in its coldness, it allows the drought to be seen. For in winter, the sun standing above the earth is barren and fixes its warmth deep beneath the ground so that the earth can preserve various seeds, and thus, with warmth and cold, it brings all sprouts to the light of day. It also preserves all that walks and runs—the animals—from sinking into the earth, because the earth is hardened under the influence of warmth and cold and thus carries everything mightily. For God created the earth such that it allows everything to grow at the proper time and likewise ceases the growing at the proper time, just as the moon waxes and wanes.

## On the Growth of Trees, Grain, and the Vine.

The trees that grow in the Eastern regions and are watered by the waters of the East grow well and bring forth a good yield of various orchard fruits which have a good taste. However, they cannot be preserved for long. But the grain there is meager and does not grow well because the earth there is quite damp, and the soil that brings forth abundant grain must be sufficiently dry. For great cold harms grain more than heat does, because grain is naturally quite dry. The vineyards there in the East, however, bring a rich yield and provide a very good wine. But the garden plants and the other herbs which are watered there in that same eastern region by the waters and beside them...

The grain <note>In this context, "Korn" refers to cereal crops in general, such as wheat or rye.</note> from the western lands is strong but not high-yielding; the wine is strong but not palatable, yet very durable, because the earth there possesses both heat and cold.

Therefore, the people who consume these plants are sometimes cheerful, sometimes sad, and occasionally rash. The sap of these plants, as well as the plants themselves, grow well and thrive because the increasing heat and decreasing cold are not soon lacking there. For sorcery <term>sorcery: "Zauberei"</term> and other fantasies, however, they are well-suited, but they bring little health to the human body, because the day there draws to a close and the night approaches <note>A symbolic reference to the West as the place of the setting sun and encroaching darkness, often associated with spiritual decline.</note>. For whenever the King stood in His glory, evil became loud and wanted to cloud the supreme Ruler with darkness. But the evil froze along with its sharpness. 

The trees, however, which are fed in the South by the waters flowing there, thrive well and bear very many fruits, which are also durable because they are permeated by the heat. Grain thrives there in abundance; it is high-yielding and tasty. Likewise, there is also plenty of wine; it is tasty and has much strength. It does not spoil easily but is long-lasting, because it is tempered by the great heat in those regions. For wine thrives better in warmth than in cold weather, and cold harms wine more than heat. The garden and field crops that are watered in these same regions by these same waters coming from the South are, by contrast, weak, not very durable, pale in color, and quickly perish, because the proper atmospheric moisture <term>atmospheric moisture: "Luftfeuchtigkeit"</term> has been withdrawn from them. Also, they are not of much use for food and...

Grain is also obtained there only with great effort and is therefore meager, overgrown with darnel <note>original: "Lolch"; a weed often found in grain fields, historically associated with dizziness if consumed</note> and brome grass <note>original: "Trespen"; a common wild grass</note>, though it is quite hardy. Wine also grows but little there. It is sharp, rather harsh, and not very sweet, because the sun does not temper <note>In medieval natural philosophy, "tempering" refers to the balancing of the primary qualities: hot, cold, moist, and dry.</note> it there. Likewise, the field and garden herbs fed by northern waters do not yield much, nor are they worth much as medicine. They do not harm the healthy, but they are not suitable for the sick, because they grow neither under the proper warmth nor with sufficient moisture, but rather under the influence of the cold. Therefore, they do not thrive properly and bear only little fruit.

**On Rain.** 
The rain that falls suddenly and in a wide outpouring <term>outpouring: "Erguß"</term> upon the earth is harmful and damages the soil and the fruits of the earth, because it carries certain impurities with it. However, if it falls in a moderate amount, it is useful; it waters the earth and causes it to bring forth fruit. For it is of a mild nature, pure, and free from uncleanness for the sake of utility and fertility.

<meta>This page transitions from the physical properties of the natural world (water and rain) to the physiological and spiritual consequences of the Fall of Man, specifically focusing on human reproduction and physical attributes as described in Hildegard of Bingen's medical-theological framework.</meta>

## II.

**On Adam’s Fall.** God created man in such a way that all living creatures are subject to him. However, with the transgression of the divine commandment, man underwent a physical and spiritual transformation. The purity of his blood was changed into another state, so that instead of his former purity, he now pours forth the foam of his seed <note>The text refers to the medieval physiological belief that semen was a "foam" or distillation produced by the heating of the blood during passion.</note>. Had man remained in Paradise, he would have continued to live in an unchanging and perfect state. But all of this changed into a different and more bitter nature after the transgression.

**On the Seed.** Human blood, boiling in the burning heat of passion, secretes a foam from within itself which we call seed <term>seed: "Samen"; used here to refer to human semen</term>, just as a pot standing on a fire casts out foam from the water under the heat of the flame.

**On Conception.** If a human being arises from the seed of a weak person, or from seed that is thin and "uncooked" <note>In medieval medicine, "cooking" or "concoction" refers to the body's ability to process fluids using internal heat.</note> and is permeated with some kind of morbid matter or putrid substance, then in the vast majority of cases, that person will also be weak throughout their life and, as it were, full of decay—like a piece of wood that, eaten away by worms, lets the rot <note>original: "Mulm"; the fine, powdery dust produced by decaying wood</note> fall out. Therefore, such a person is often full of sores and putrefaction, and more easily draws corruption and decay from food into the decay they already have within themselves. However, a person who is free from this is healthier. But if there is an excess in the seed, the person conceived from it will become incontinent, immoderate, unrestrained, and debauched.

**Why Man is Not Hairy.** The reason that man is not covered in hair lies in his reason <term>reason: "Vernunft"; the intellectual soul</term>. For reason takes the place of hair and feathers. With its help, man covers himself and flies <note>Hildegard suggests that human ingenuity and tools allow man to "fly" or move through the world in ways animals cannot, serving as a functional equivalent to wings.</note> wherever he wishes. That the man has a beard and more hair on his body than the woman stems from the fact that man was formed from the earth and possesses more strength and warmth; he also moves about everywhere more than the woman. Thus, the earth, [nourished] by rain and the sun's warmth, brings forth...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical world following the Fall of Man, transitioning from the corrupted state of human biology to the nature of various animals—specifically crawling creatures (invertebrates and reptiles) and flying creatures—explaining their spiritual and physical relationship to the earth and humanity.</meta>

...poured out, herbs and grass come forth, and it nourishes hairy and feathered creatures upon its surface. The woman, however, is beardless because she was created from the flesh of man, is subject to man, and is of a calmer nature. In the same way, the crawling animals that arise from the earth are not hairy; instead, they lie in the earth and feel the rain and sunshine less than the other animals that are upon the earth.

## On the crawling animals.

Just as the other animals are created to serve humanity, so the crawling animals help and serve us by boring holes through the earth so that water and rain can moisten it. Therefore, they always lie in moist places in the earth, warming it through their exhalations and moistening it with their foam and sweat, so that the earth gains some cohesion and strength through such secretions and exhalations. The reason that worms are poisonous <note>original: "giftig"; in Hildegard’s time, this term applied to any creature that carried venom or was associated with decay.</note> lies in the foul odor and the putrefaction of the earth. Rain and dew wash the earth on its surface, and the sun warms it; for this reason, the surface of the earth is pure and brings forth pure fruit. Filth and decay, however, flow down into its interior. From this, the poisonous worms grow within it, just as worms also arise within a person from the purulent discharge <term>purulent discharge: "Wundjauche"; the thin, foul fluid from an infected wound</term> which then harms them. Thus do the worms develop in the earth, nourishing themselves from it. 

Furthermore, worms possess almost no bones; their venom takes the place of bones and blood and grants them strength. Some of them are hairless because they arise from the moisture of the earth, live within the earth, and flee the upper regions; they are thus penetrated neither by the air, nor the dew of heaven, nor the warmth of the sun, to which the other animals owe their hair. Because they have a nature opposite to that of man and the higher creatures, they are hostile to these, killing them with their venom and harming both humans and the animals that stand higher than themselves. However, although they carry venom within them, some are useful as medicine for both humans and animals—if not always as a whole, then with some specific body part—since they contain the good "sap" of the earth, and it is the good sap of the earth that also produces healing herbs. Thus the stag rejuvenates itself when it swallows a snake <note>Hildegard here references a popular medieval bestiary myth: the stag (deer) was believed to draw snakes out of their holes with its breath and eat them to shed its old age and renew its youth.</note>.

## On the flying animals.

All flying creatures and animals that can be and already are useful to man take their life from the air by divine decree, and they therefore remain above the surface of the earth. Worms, on the other hand, and crawling animals take their life from the sap of the earth and therefore prefer to dwell on and under the earth.

<note>Vocabulary from the original text: Woman, crawling animals, worms, venom/poison, moisture of the earth, sap of the earth, stag, snake, flying animals.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from a discussion of terrestrial and subterranean creatures (worms and crawling animals) to the nature of aquatic life. ### On Fish.

Fish, however, receive their life from the water-air <note>original: "Wasserluft"; in the medieval view of the four elements, this refers to the specific mixture of water and air that allows aquatic creatures to breathe and live.</note> of the rivers; accordingly, they remain in the waters and cannot endure dryness. When they die, the life in all of them vanishes along with their flesh, just as snow melts in the heat. What remains passes either into the air, or into the juice of the earth <note>original: "Erdsaft"; the vital moisture within the soil that nourishes plants.</note>, or into the water-air of the rivers from whence it came. From that point on, however, that which has perished does not bring any other creature that is already withered back to life. Just as the sap and the ability to grow green in trees and herbs that have been cut down dries up and becomes parched, and no longer makes other plants grow green because it is dried out, so also the life force of irrational animals <note>original: "unvernünftigen Tiere"; animals lacking a rational, immortal soul. In this worldview, their "soul" or life force is purely material and ceases to exist upon death.</note> can no longer make any other animal alive once it has withered and dried out within them, precisely because it no longer exists but has vanished completely.

### On the Various Types of Conception.

When a man, with the discharge of a strong seed <term>seed: "Samen"; in medieval medicine, "seed" or "sperm" was believed to be a highly refined product of the blood that carried the blueprint and "heat" for a new life.</term> in true love and affection, approaches a woman, and at the same hour the woman also feels true love for the man, a male child is conceived, because this is so ordained by God. It is not possible for it to be otherwise than that a male child is conceived, because Adam was also created from clay, which is a stronger substance than flesh. This boy will become wise and rich in virtue because he was conceived with strong seed and with proper mutual love and affection. 

But if the woman lacks love for the man, so that at this time only the man has the true loving desire for the woman and the woman does not for the man, a male child is nevertheless conceived if the man's seed is strong, because the man's feeling of love prevails. This boy, however, will be weak and possess no capable qualities, because here the woman's love for the man was lacking. 

If the man's seed is weak, yet he nonetheless has love and affection for the woman and she has the same love for him, a virtuous female child is fathered. If only the man feels desire for the woman and she does not for him, or if only the woman feels true love for the man and he does not for her, and if at the same hour the male seed is thin, then—because the seed lacks strength—a girl likewise arises from it. 

But if the man's seed is full of strength, yet neither the man toward the woman nor the woman toward the man has mutual loving affection, a boy is fathered because the seed nonetheless had its full strength. He will, however, become an unpleasant person because of the mutual dislike of the parents. But if the man's seed is thin and at the same hour neither of them feels love or affection for the other, then a

...girls are begotten with an unpleasant nature. The heat of such women, who are naturally corpulent, overwhelms the seed of the man; therefore, the child’s face frequently becomes similar to such women. However, those women who are naturally thin mostly bring children into the world who resemble the father in their countenance.

**On Diseases.** The reason that some people suffer from all kinds of illnesses lies in the phlegm <term>phlegm: "Phlegma"; in the medieval theory of the four humors, phlegm was a cold, moist substance. An excess was thought to cause sluggishness or specific diseases.</term> that they have within them in excess. For if man had remained in Paradise, he would not have had these phlegms—from which many evils arise—within his body; rather, his flesh would be entirely healthy and free from mucus. But because he turned toward evil and abandoned what was good, he became like the earth, which brings forth good and useful herbs alongside bad and useless ones, and contains both good and bad moisture and sap. For after the eating of the apple, the blood of the sons of Adam was transformed into the poison of the seed from which the descendants of men arise. Therefore, their flesh is also full of sores and porous. These sores and pores produce, so to speak, a storm and a damp smoke within man, from which the phlegms then arise and congeal, bringing various diseases to human bodies. This is the consequence of that first sin which man first took upon himself; because, if Adam had remained in Paradise, he would have possessed the most delightful health alongside the finest dwelling place, just as the most potent balsam gives off the most beautiful scent. Instead, and in contrast to this, man now has poison and phlegm within him, and all kinds of diseases.

**On Abstinence.** There are people who are abstinent when they wish to be. But if they do not wish to be abstinent, they are strong in their will. They are greedy and enjoy eating fatty foods. Consequently, a dangerous, poisonous, thick, and dry phlegm gathers within them, which is not moist but rather bitter. It causes thick, black, and weak flesh to grow upon the person, and if these individuals do not wish to abstain from the enjoyment of fatty foods, they easily contract leprosy <note>original: "Aussatz"; in the medieval period, this term often referred to a broad category of skin diseases believed to be caused by internal humoral corruption.</note>. The bitter nature of this phlegm produces a smoke in the vicinity of the liver and lungs of such people, much like the smoke of black bile <term>black bile: "Schwarzgalle" or melancholy; the humor associated with earth, coldness, and dryness. Its "smoke" or vapors were thought to affect the mind and temperament.</term>. Therefore, they are quick-tempered and hard-hearted. The moisture of their sweat is not clean, but foul. At the same time, they are not exactly weak, but are capable people—brave, and in their anger, tyrannical and grasping, precisely because of this predisposition. The phlegm arising from such a predisposition ruins and kills some of them in a short time because its power is great. To others, however, it grants a longer life.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of bodily humors and temperaments, moving from the general origins of disease in the Fall of Man to the specific physiological and psychological profiles of those with a phlegmatic disposition and those prone to a lack of restraint.</meta>

## On Lack of Restraint.

But there are also other people who possess an excessive nature and are more intemperate than their fellow men, so that they can hardly hold themselves back and, as a result, frequently become sick. They suffer from an abundance of moist phlegm <term>phlegm: "Phlegma"; in medieval medicine, one of the four humors. It was considered cold and moist, and an excess was thought to lead to sluggishness or respiratory issues.</term>, because a foul moisture develops within them. This harmful phlegm congeals inside them and sends a wicked smoke toward their chest and their brain. The moisture of this smoke-producing phlegm in the chest creates a cold moisture in the stomach, and the moisture of this same phlegm in the brain diminishes the sense of hearing in the ears. Thus, this moisture stands in the stomach and in the ears like a useless mist that damages good herbs and fruits <note>The author uses a "microcosm" metaphor here, comparing the internal environment of the human body to a landscape or garden affected by weather.</note>. This phlegm does not harm the lungs, because the lungs are moist themselves; however, it does harm the spleen because the spleen is fatty and repels moisture. If the spleen were to contain moisture, it would immediately fall apart and liquefy. The heart is also attacked because it is in permanent possession of its strength and always rejects excess moisture. 

People with this constitution are of a gentler and more cheerful disposition, but they are sluggish. Some of them live reasonably long because this phlegm does not kill them, though it does not provide them with particularly good health either. There are also other people who, while prone to sudden anger <term>sudden anger: "Jähzorn"</term>, nevertheless let their anger pass quickly. They are good-natured and of a cheerful temperament, but cold, of a wavering mind, and feel full after eating very little. From the three types of phlegm—the dry, the moist, and the lukewarm—they contract a kind of watery foam. This foam, arising from these three phlegm types, sends dangerous "arrows" into the veins, the marrow, and the flesh, much like boiling water that casts off a frothing foam.

## On the Phlegmatics.

When the various phlegm-bearing humors are stirred up in these people—because the phlegms are thrown into disorder by immoderation in eating and drinking, by unsuitable pleasure, grief, anger, and unrestrained desire—they then boil up like water in a thermal bath when a fire is lit beneath it. They spray, as it were, glowing hot drops and drive these like arrows into the flesh, the blood, and the vessels. They bore into a person as fiercely as when an acrid smoke hits the eyes. Those who are predisposed in this way flare up in anger more often, but they quickly consign it to oblivion because they love kindness <note>original: "Gutherzigkeit"</note>; just as when a storm approaches, the sun shines afterward. The power of such a phlegmatic disposition is shown by the fact that those who possess it are easily inclined toward both anger and joy. However, they do not reach a full old age.

<meta>This page continues the analysis of human temperaments and their origins in the Fall of Man. # Of the Melancholics <gloss>1)</gloss>

But there are other people who are of a sorrowful spirit, fearful and indecisive, so that there is no proper order or stability within them. They are like a violent wind that harms all plants and fruits <note>This continues the author's frequent use of "microcosm" metaphors, where the human body's internal state mirrors the weather and ecology of the natural world.</note>. From this, it follows that a phlegm forms within them that is neither moist nor thick, but something in between. It is like a tough slime that stretches out long like gum and gives rise to **black bile** <term>black bile: "Schwarzgalle" or Melancholy. In medieval medicine, this humor was associated with the element of earth and characterized as cold and dry. An excess was thought to cause depression and anxiety.</term>, which was created from Adam's seed in the beginning through the breath of the serpent, because Adam carried out the serpent's intention by eating [the forbidden fruit].

## On Black Bile as a Disease

**Black bile** is dark-colored and bitter; it breathes out all evil and sometimes even produces disease of the brain. It causes the vessels of the heart to seethe, as it were, and leads to sadness and doubt of every word of comfort, so that the person can no longer find joy in anything that belongs to the higher life or to comfort in this temporal existence. This black bile is inherent to every human being, specifically since the first temptation by the devil, because man violated God's commandment by eating the apple. From that eating onward, black bile has developed in Adam and his entire lineage and is a cause of every serious human illness. 

Because, however, the previously mentioned phlegm possesses only a moderate strength, it does not overcome the power of the black bile like the two other aforementioned phlegms do. Of those, one is so strong through its moisture and the other through its consistency and bitterness that they can resist the black bile just as a cooking pot hanging over a fire holds down the flames so that they cannot flare up. People who possess such a constitution become easily angered and are anxious in many important divine and human matters. Some of them also reach a great age, because the influence of the aforementioned phlegm is such that it neither brings the person completely to death nor allows them to become generally more vigorous—much like a man in a dungeon who is neither executed nor set free. Thus, as has been said, man lives by four **humors** <term>humors: "Säfte"; the vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that medieval physicians believed governed health and personality.</term>, just as the world consists of four elements.

## On the Mixture of the Elements

God has joined the world together from four elements so that none of them can be separated from the others. For the world would perish if one element could exist apart from the others. They are insolubly linked to one another. **Fire** rules, tames, and ignites the **air** and is more powerful than it. The air, however, which is closest to the fire, lets it flare up, just as a bellows does, and keeps it in check. Fire forms, in the same way—

<meta>This page continues a discussion on natural philosophy and cosmology, moving from the internal humors of the human body (discussed on the previous page) to the external relationship between the four elements: Air, Fire, Water, and Earth. The text explains how these elements regulate and sustain one another in a delicate balance established at Creation.</meta>

...[together] <note>The text likely completes the word *zusammen* (together) or *Balsam* (balm) from the previous page.</note> the body of the air, and the air is, as it were, the viscera and the wings and feathers of **fire**. Just as the body is not without viscera, so too fire is not without air, because air is the movement within fire. Fire would not burn, nor could it be ignited, if it did not have air. Fire also causes the sparkle and the warmth of the **water** and makes it flow; for water would not be liquid and would not flow, but would be stronger and more indissoluble than iron and steel if it did not have the heat of fire hidden within it, as can also be observed in ice <note>The author argues that ice is the "natural" state of water, and only the hidden element of fire allows it to remain liquid.</note>.

**Water**, however, forms the cold component of fire and is stronger than it, because it extinguishes fire. At the **creation** of creatures, the water was namely cold and did not flow when the **earth** was still "waste and void" <note>original: "wüst und leer", a reference to Genesis 1:2.</note>. But the **Spirit of God** hovered over the waters and warmed them, so that they might have fire within them and could flow in a liquefied state. This same coldness of the water naturally drives the fire out of it, and therefore it boils. For water contains fire within itself, and fire by nature contains the coldness of water, because water could not flow if it had no fire within it, and fire would never go out, but would burn on forever, if the coldness of water were not present in it.

Fire is also the **heat regulator** <term>heat regulator: "Wärmeregler". In this context, it refers to the sun or the elemental heat that governs the cycles of growth and ripening.</term> for the earth, in that it strengthens, dries, and brings its fruits to maturity. Earth, however, provides the resistance for fire that prevents it from exceeding its measure and goal. On the other hand, **air** is also the wind and a support for the water, just as it also provides a help and a brake for the fire, so that it keeps its flow in the correct proportion. If it did not keep the water within its proper boundaries and on the right path, it would flow along untamed and flood everything it reached.

Water, however, makes the air easily movable and enables it to fly swiftly along. It also grants it a fruit-bearing quality by making it sweat, thereby giving **fertility** to the earth, as it pours out **dew** from itself over it. Air is also, as it were, a cloak for the earth, because it keeps heat and cold away from it, maintaining it at an appropriate temperature and sending dew to the earth through its moistening. Earth, however, behaves like a sponge and like a substance that attracts and absorbs fertility from the air. If there were no earth, the air would be lacking its duty to fertilize the earth.

On the other hand, water is a binding agent for the earth, holding it together and taming it so that it does not flow apart. The earth, however, carries and holds the water together, gives it its proper paths, and ensures that it has its correct course on its surface and does not rise unduly in the depths. In its depths, it holds the water in the dark; on its surface, it regulates its course. The dew, however,

<meta>This page continues the discourse on natural philosophy, specifically how the interaction of the four elements—Fire, Air, Water, and Earth—produces meteorological phenomena like dew, frost, and mist. It builds on the previous page's description of fire as a catalyst for movement in the other elements.</meta>

...which, as previously noted, fertilizes the earth, stems from the correct mixture of fire and air.

**Of Dew.** When fire and air mutually fulfill their task during the summer season in an appropriate balance with one another, then, in mild and clear air and without the movement of strong gusts of wind, they sweat <note>The author uses the metaphor of "sweating" (German: *schwitzen*) to describe the condensation of dew, viewing it as a physiological process of the elements themselves.</note> **dew** because of the heat inherent in each of them. This dew spreads its fertilizing and nourishing power like a seed, bringing blessing and benefit to the fruits of the earth.

**Of Frost.** In winter, when the air inclines down toward the coldness of the earth, their union and meeting bring forth **frost**, which damages herbs and budding blossoms and makes the earth parched through its constricting power. Now, as has been said before, the elements of which the world consists are so interconnected and chained to one another that they can never be separated: fire does not exist without air, air not without water, and water not without earth—even though fire possesses greater power than air, water greater power than fire, and earth is more fertile and productive than these three. The harshness of one element serves to promote the milder effect of another, and the milder-acting quality of one softens the roughness of another. Through such agreement and moderation, they naturally harmonize with one another, so that none brings the other into confusion—unless the elements, called to serve as a divine judgment <note>A *Strafgericht* refers to a punitive act or divine retribution, suggesting that natural disasters are signs of God's displeasure.</note>, cause conflagrations, storms, floods, or infertility.

**Of Mist.** In certain mountains as well as in certain valleys and other places, a kind of **mist** sometimes rises by divine decree. At first it appears black; then, as it spreads further, it billows back and forth, possessing a somewhat foul and dangerous odor. When it spreads further across the land, it brings disease, pestilence, and death to both people and livestock. Sometimes, a kind of mist also rises from the moisture of the waters, slightly touching what is on the earth, and is thus scattered out into the world. It generates disease and pestilence in humans and animals here and there, but does not kill them. It destroys the first opening blossoms of the fruit trees and damages the fruits in such a way that trees and herbs draw their leaves together, causing them to wither as if they had been doused with hot water. Yet another kind of mist arises as a result of excessive heat combined with an abundant presence—

It emphasizes that nature remains in balance unless God directs the elements to act as a judgment through disasters or disease.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the elements, concluding the section on meteorological phenomena like mist and moving into a theological and physiological explanation of the four elements and the creation of the first human, Adam.</meta>

...originates from air and clouds and from their moisture. It is not harmful. A mist also rises from the cold and dampness of the earth and a mist from the various waters, but both bring no danger to man and livestock nor to the products of the earth, because it is in their nature to appear only at certain times of the day. If the sun looks red when it rises in the morning, the reason lies in the cold and dampness of the air, because then the prevailing moisture and cold redden human eyes <note>The author likely means that the atmospheric conditions that cause the sun to appear red—refraction through moisture—are the same conditions that irritate or affect human sight.</note>. It is similar in the evening. If the sun looks red then, it comes from the coldness of the air, because the sun is then inclining toward its setting.

**Why there are only four elements.** There cannot be more or fewer than four elements. They are of a twofold nature, belonging either to the heights or the depths. The former are of a heavenly kind, the latter of an earthly kind. That which belongs to the heights cannot be grasped with the hands; it consists of fire and air. But what dwells in the depths are tangible bodies endowed with form, and these consist of water and earth.

**Concerning the soul and the spirits.** The spirits are fiery and airy, but man <note>In this context, the author refers specifically to the physical composition of the human body.</note> consists of water and earth.

**Concerning the creation of Adam.** When God created man, the clay from which man was formed was clumped together by water, and God sent into this form the breath of life <term>Lebenshauch: the "vital breath" or spark of life, often associated with the soul in medieval theology</term> made of fire and air. Because the human form consisted of clay and water, the clay became flesh through the fire of the breath of life, and through its air, blood was produced from the water that had helped clump the clay together.

When God created Adam, the radiance of divinity shone around the mass of clay from which he was formed. Because of this, once the clump of clay had been given a shape, it appeared externally in the outlines of its limbs, but internally it was hollow. Thereupon, God created the heart, liver, lungs, stomach, intestines, and brain within the form from the same mass of clay, as well as the eyes and the tongue along with all the other internal organs. When God then let the breath of life enter the mass of clay, its components—which represent the bones, marrow, and vessels—were made firm by that same breath of life. The breath of life then distributed itself within the mass of clay just as a snail fits itself into the interior of its shell or the power to turn green <note>The text uses the concept of "greening power" (similar to the Latin *viriditas*), a life-force that allows plants to grow and humans to thrive.</note> lies within a tree. The individual parts were thus strengthened within themselves, just as silver takes on a different form when the smith throws it into the fire. Thus, the breath of life has its seat in the heart. Finally, flesh and blood were also created in the same mass from the soulful fire.

<meta>This page continues the physiological and theological exploration of the human body begun on the previous page, shifting from the creation of Adam's physical form to the specific functions of the organs and the way the four elements—fire, air, water, and earth—operate within the human constitution.</meta>

### Concerning the Hair.
The driving force of the soul sent foam and moisture to the head, which is to say: into the brain. For this reason, the brain is moist, and through this moisture, the head sends forth the hair.

### Concerning the Human Internal Organs.
The soul consists of fire, wind, and moisture, and it possesses the entire heart of the human being. The liver warms the heart, the lungs cover it, and the stomach is an internal space in the human body for the reception of food. The heart possesses knowledge as its own, the liver possesses feeling, and the lungs have the quality of a bellows<note>In medieval physiology, the lungs were often compared to bellows that fanned the "vital fires" of the heart.</note> and a pathway for reason. The mouth is the speaking-trumpet for what the person has to say, the receiver of the means of nourishment for the body, and it brings forth the voice. However, it does not receive the voice. The ear, on the other hand, receives the voice but brings none forth.

### Concerning the Ears.
The two ears are like two wings that carry all voices and tones in and out, just as wings carry birds through the air.

### Concerning the Eyes and the Nose.
The eyes are the pathways of the human being; the nose is his understanding. In the same way, the human being is also constructed from the remaining limbs.

### Why the Elements are within the Human.
Now, however, as has been said before, the elements—namely: fire, air, earth, and water—are found within the human being; they work within him through their own inherent powers and, during his actions, run swiftly in a circle like a wheel at its circumference. Fire, with its five previously mentioned inherent powers, has its place in the brain and the marrow of the human being, because at the creation of the first man from clay, glowing red fire from the divine power was kindled in his blood, just as blood is indeed red. Fire makes itself evident in the lively expression of the eye, in the coldness <note>The author here describes a complex sensory system where "fire" governs the active movement or "vitality" behind each sense, even when the sensation itself (like a cold wind or moist taste) seems opposite to heat.</note> of smell, the moisture of taste, the movement of air in hearing, and in the movement of the person triggered by feeling. Air, on the other hand, is represented with its four powers—as already mentioned—in the breath and in the intellectual activity of the human being. It serves the living breath, the soul, within the human, because it carries him and is the wing for his flight when the person draws the breath into himself and expels it so that he can live. The soul is the fire that permeates the entire body and makes the human being alive. The air also ignites the fire, and through the air, the fire burns in all parts of the body. Furthermore, the air makes itself known in the sending of dew, in the stimulation of growth, in the blowing of its movement, and as warmth in the physical growth of the human being. The water

...but is represented by its fifteen previously mentioned powers within the fluids and the blood of the human being.

**Concerning the Blood.** Water exists within the human being because blood is not absent from him. This provides moisture within the person so that the vital force <term>vital force: "Lebenskraft," the animating spirit or energy that sustains life</term> remains fresh and the solidifying of the bones within him endures. Through the coldness of water, the vessels in the human being are strengthened so that blood may flow through them, consisting of droplets and moving the entire body. Water also saturates the flesh with blood so that it may persist, just as water binds the earth together. However, the coldness inherent to water overcomes fire in such a way that it flows. Water, together with fire and its own coldness, permeates the earth in such a way that the earth receives its firmness through them. The severe cold that makes waters hard through ice resides within the stones. For this reason, stones cannot be softened, just as the bones in human flesh are hard. Thus, water manifests its warmth in the human blood, its air content in the breath, its moisture in the entire physical structure, its capacity to cause "flooding" <note>likely a reference to the discharge of humors or bodily fluids during purging or menstruation</note> during elimination, its easy mobility during growth, its thickness in the strengthening of individual body parts, its taste in the bearing of fruit, its virility <note>original: "Manneskraft," referring to male reproductive strength or potency</note> during erection, its wetness in active vigor, and its moisturizing power in all human joints. The earth is represented by its seven previously stated inherent powers within the flesh and the bones of the human being. Through these, the flesh is moist and grows.

**Concerning the Flesh.** Just as the earth is solidified by fire and water, so too is the flesh of the human being joined together by the vessels and by moisture. The coagulation of the bones comes about through its coldness. Meanwhile, fire overcomes all of this so that there is strength within the person. For human flesh comes from the earth and possesses a cold moisture, but the blood warms it. If it were not warmed by the blood, the flesh would turn back into clay, as it was before <note>A reference to the creation of man from the "dust of the ground" or clay in Genesis</note>. Thus, just as the earth is sustained by the sun’s heat, the flesh maintains its existence through the warmth of the blood. Due to its soft composition, it is bloody and contains the inherent powers of the earth within it. Under the influence of cold moisture it sweats; from heat, it becomes hot; and without cold, it would—just like the earth—have no stability.

**Concerning Procreation.** The human being owes his fertility to cold and warmth; he shares the joy of life with all other creatures and begets offspring from within himself. For warmth is his vital force and cold...

...its dryness, and through all of these, he fathers children. But when old age has approached a person, all of their external warmth retreats to their interior. Otherwise, they could not live. Thus, their flesh becomes cold on the outside, while they themselves become warm within. This is why an old person tires quickly at the start of any undertaking. Animals are attached to humans because humans feed on them and humans provide for their nourishment. Thus, man carries everything within himself, for every creature is contained in him. The earth manifests its coldness in the flesh of man through his warmth, and its warmth through his coldness; through growth, it shows its life-stimulating power; through decline, its dryness; in procreation, its ability to bring forth life; in the increase of the human race, its sustaining power; and in the supportive gathering of all limbs, its close connection with humanity. From fire, man derives his emotional and desire-driven life; from the air, his thoughts and their instability; from the water, his knowledge and his capacity for movement.

### Concerning the Animation of Adam.

While Adam still consisted only of earth, fire caused him to rise, air awakened him, and water permeated him so thoroughly that he was set fully into motion. Then God sent a sleep down upon him, and he was then "cooked" <note>This refers to the medieval concept of "coction," where internal heat matures or refines the biological components of the body</note> by these forces, so that his flesh was warmed by the fire, he breathed through the air, and the water—as if in a watermill—passed through him. When he later awoke, he was a prophet of heavenly things, knowledgeable of all the powers of the creatures and of all the arts.

### Adam’s Prophetic Gift.

God handed over all creatures to him so that he might make them his own through his virility <note>original: "Manneskraft," referring to male reproductive strength or general human potency</note>, because he knew of them and had knowledge concerning them. For man as such represents all creatures, and the breath of life, which has no end of life, is within him.

### Concerning the Infusion of the Soul.

That which enters the body as the soul is a breath and is sent by God. According to a person's bodily works, whether they be good or bad, the soul receives its reward. These works are, as it were, the binding agent of merits. Just as a small child at first knows nothing of what they will only understand later—because with increasing maturity they receive the insight that understands all things—and just as the child then makes these things their own by rejoicing in their works while observing them and acting beautifully with them, and just as they tire in old age, so too the soul moves forward, developing along with the works. By good works, the soul is enveloped as if in a royal mantle. By bad works, however, it is darkened, just as the earth is flooded by waters. As if to a certai= <note>original: "bestimm="; likely leading to "bestimmte" (certain/specific) on the following page</note>

...just as water flows through certain places, the soul flows through the body and raises it up. Even when the physical eyes are closed, the soul—with its <term>prophetic gift<note>Original: "weissagenden Begabung." In medieval thought, this is the soul's innate ability to perceive spiritual truths and future events because it is not bound by the physical limits of the body.</note></term>—often sees things to come, for it remembers that it can live even without the body.

## On Adam’s Sleep.

After Adam’s first sleep, his prophetic gift was based on truth because he had not yet sinned; later, however, it became interspersed with falsehood. Then Adam, created from the earth and set in motion by the elements, was transformed. Eve, on the other hand, created from Adam’s rib, was not transformed in this same way.

## On Eve’s Frailty.

<note>Original: "Schlechtigkeit." While this literally translates to "wickedness" or "badness," in this theological context, it refers to the moral and physical susceptibility that the author attributes to the female nature following the Fall.</note>

Through the creative power of the earth, Adam had become a man and owed his great strength to the elements. Eve, by contrast, was of a softer nature in her very marrow; she possessed a playful, cunning mind and a "delicious" life, because the heavy burden of the earth did not weigh her down as it did Adam. Just as she herself descended from man, the entire human race has proceeded from her. A human being is divided into two parts: that of waking and that of sleeping. Likewise, the human body is nourished in two ways: first by being filled with food, and second by being refreshed through sleep. But when the soul has eventually left the body, it will live with it in a different way; the soul finds this state difficult to endure because it is good by its very nature. Thus, the soul cries out to God and says:

> When will I put on my flesh again, with which I lived during my earthly days?

For when God created all creatures, the day was still one with the pure light, which the night had not yet divided.

## On Adam’s Exile.

After Adam’s sin, the night began its existence. All the elements were shrouded in deep darkness; it was during this darkness that Adam was sent into exile. When he then beheld the light of this world, he rejoiced because he himself now belonged to the darkness, and he spoke through tears:

> I must now live differently than God had granted me to live before!

So he began to work in the sweat of his brow. Previously, before Adam and Eve had transgressed the divine commandment, they shone with a radiance like the sun, and this radiance formed their clothing, so to speak. After the transgression of the divine commandment, however, they no longer shone as they had before; they had become dark and remained in this darkness. When they saw that they no longer radiated light as they had previously, they realized they were naked and covered themselves, as is written, with the leaves of trees. Adam, who before the Fall was free from any labor, shone like the sun,

For in the radiance that the saints will then possess, their holy works will shine and be set like precious stones in gold.

---

## Why Eve Fell First.

If Adam had fallen before Eve, his fall would have been so powerful and so entirely incurable that humanity would have fallen into such a great and incorrigible hardening of heart that they would neither have wanted to be redeemed nor been able to be. But because Eve, who was weaker than the man, transgressed the divine command first, the debt could be more easily erased. Adam’s flesh and skin were stronger and harder than they are now in humans, because Adam was formed from the earth and Eve was formed from him. But once they had fathered sons, their flesh became more and more frail and fragile, and so it will remain until the <term>Last Day<note>The Day of Judgment at the end of the world.</note></term>.

---

## Of the Great Flood.

When Adam was driven out of Paradise, the water—before the Flood—did not yet have the strong current in its course, nor the fluidity it had afterward. It had a kind of skin on its surface that restricted its flow somewhat, so that it flowed only slowly. <note>The author envisions a world with different physical properties before the cataclysm of the Flood, where the elements were more static or "heavy."</note> Also, at that time, the earth was not yet so muddy, but dry and easily crumbled, because it was not yet saturated with water. In contrast, it produced an excessive amount of fruit, in accordance with its purpose. 

At that time, people had forgotten God and acted more in the manner of animals than according to the will of God. Therefore, it happened that many loved animals more than humans, so that both women and men mixed and consorted with animals to such an extent that the <term>Image of God<note>Original: "Ebenbild Gottes." In medieval theology, this is the divine spark or likeness within humans that separates them from animals.</note></term> within them was almost completely destroyed. The entire human race was transformed and reshaped into monsters, so that even some people shaped their lifestyle and voice in the manner of wild animals in their running about, howling, and living. 

The wild animals, as well as the livestock, were not yet as wild before the Flood as they became afterward. Neither did humans flee from them, nor they from humans, and they were not frightened of one another. The wild animals and the livestock liked to stay with humans and humans with them, because they had come into existence almost at the same time during their first appearance. But the wild animals as well as the livestock caressed the humans and these in turn the animals. Therefore, they loved each other more and more in an unnatural way and mated with one another—

...each other. In the meantime, Adam had nonetheless fathered some sons who were so filled with the divine spirit that they would not involve themselves in any disgrace, but instead remained in holiness. Therefore, they were also called: Sons of God.

**Why Sons of God?** These looked about and investigated where such humans might be who had not involved themselves with the cattle and had not degraded themselves through intercourse with them, even though they were the sons of transgressors of the law, as was said above. <note>This refers to a medieval theological interpretation of Genesis 6, suggesting that the "Sons of God" were the righteous descendants of Seth, while the "Sons of Men" were the descendants of Cain who had fallen into animalistic behavior.</note> They were also called sons of men because they had not become degraded, neither in their outward appearance nor through association with animals. From their daughters, they took wives and fathered children with them, as it is written: *The children of God saw the daughters of men, how beautiful they were.*⁷) <note>original: "Die Kinder Gottes sahen die Töchter der Menschen, wie sie schön waren"; a reference to Genesis 6:2.</note> Even today, there are still wild animals as well as domestic animals that, in the aforementioned manner, have taken on a great deal of human nature from humans. Then the outcry of this injustice rose up before the eyes of God, because the image of God <term>Ebenbild Gottes<note>The "Imago Dei" or the theological concept that humans are created in God's likeness, which the author argues was physically and spiritually corrupted by these unnatural unions.</note></term> had been mutilated and destroyed, and His original intention had been turned into unchastity. Then the Spirit of God, who hovered over the waters at the creation of the world, sent the waters; and the "skin" of the waters—by which they had been somewhat held back until then, so that they could not flow as fast as they flow today—tore. The water became swift in its course and drowned the people. At that time, the water permeated the earth to such an extent that it became almost as hard as iron, gave all fruits a new and stronger sap than before, and allowed the grapevine to grow, which until then had not been visible. <note>According to biblical tradition, Noah was the first to plant a vineyard after the flood (Genesis 9:20).</note> Also the stones, which were created together with the earth and were covered by it, were laid bare and made visible by the water, and some of them, which were previously whole and intact, were shattered by the water.

## Of the Creation of the Stones.

The stones, however, with the exception of those which become visible in the rivers as light-colored blocks, grew neither before nor after, but were only created simultaneously with the earth and merely became visible through the general flood.

## Of the Rainbow.

At that time, God set his bow in the firmament of heaven to strengthen the firmament and as a counterweight against the waters. This bow is of a fiery nature and has the colors of the waters. These colors act as strongly against the water as the clouds do. Thus, the rainbow, with its fire and its colors, holds back the water, just as a net holds fish so that they do not flow out. After the Deluge, wisdom and virtues among men increased more...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the physical transformation of the Earth following the Great Flood, transitioning from the history of early humanity to a medieval cosmological description of the Earth’s geography and the elemental composition of the human body.</meta>

...increased more than they were previously present or visible. Before the Great Flood <term>Sintflut<note>The biblical Deluge of Noah. The author argues that the world's geography was fundamentally altered by this event.</note></term>, however, the whole earth was full of humans and animals. They were separated from one another neither by water nor by forests, because large rivers and extensive forests did not yet exist at that time. There were only springs and small brooks that could be easily waded through, as well as only a few forests, which people traversed without effort. 

After the Flood, however, individual springs and brooks expanded into large, dangerous currents, and massive forests grew up, through which humans and wild animals were separated from one another, so that afterward humans grew wary of animals and animals grew wary of humans. Furthermore, it did not rain before the Flood; only dew fell upon the earth. But after the earth had been saturated with water during the Flood and had become firm, it naturally demanded rain from the water.

**On the Position of the Earth.** The earth is of medium size and stands nearly on the floor of the firmament <term>Firmament<note>In the medieval worldview, the firmament was the physical vault or "shell" of the heavens that arched over the Earth.</note></term>. If it lay in the middle of the firmament, it would have to be larger and would also easily fall and be shattered if it had such a mass of air beneath it as it has above it. Toward the south, it behaves much like the slope of a mountain. Therefore, it also receives greater heat from the sun there, because the sun and the firmament are closer to it in that direction. Toward the north, however, the earth is high, acting as a protection against punishments⁸) <note>In medieval theology, the "North" was often associated with cold, darkness, and the source of evil or divine punishment.</note>. Greater cold also prevails there because neither the firmament nor the sun is close to the earth there. Instead, the expansion of the firmament is greater in that region.

**Man Consists of the Elements.** As we have seen before, the elements—just as they hold the whole world together—likewise provide the cohesion for the human body. They distribute their expansion and their duties within man in such a way that he is constantly kept in motion by them, just as the elements are spread and active throughout the rest of the world. Fire, air, water, and earth are within man, and he consists of these. From fire he has his warmth, from air his breath, from water his blood, and from earth his flesh. Likewise, man also owes his sight to fire, his hearing to air, his mobility to water, and his gait to the earth. 

The world is also in a prosperous state when the elements perform their service well and orderly, so that heat, dew, and rain divide themselves individually and moderately into their proper seasons and descend for the sake of maintaining the correct quality of the soil and the fruits, bringing good harvests and health. If they were all to [come] at once, suddenly and not...

fall upon the earth in their time, the earth would be torn asunder and its fruits and health would be destroyed. Likewise, the elements, when they work in an orderly fashion within a human, preserve him and make him healthy. If, on the other hand, they hold no concord within him, they make him sick and kill him. For when the unions of the <term>humors<note>original: "Säfte." In medieval medicine, health was believed to depend on the balance of four bodily fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note></term>, which arise in man from heat, moisture, blood, and flesh and are present within him, work in him in peace and in the correct mutual proportion, they bring health with them. If, however, they all strike him at once without distinction and fall upon him in excess, then they make him weak and kill him. Heat and moisture, as well as blood and flesh, were namely transformed into the contrary types of phlegm within man through <term>Adam’s Fall<note>The biblical concept of Original Sin. The author suggests that before the Fall, these bodily elements were perfectly balanced and not prone to disease.</note></term>.

**On the Different Qualities of Phlegm.** From the heat of fire a dry phlegm is drawn and secreted; from the moisture of the air, a moist phlegm; from the watery blood, a foamy phlegm; and from the earthy flesh, a lukewarm phlegm. If one of these develops excessively in a person and is not kept in check and moderated by the others, it destroys and weakens the person. If, however, each phlegm correctly preserves its measure, so that it is tempered by the others in such a way that it is forced to maintain its right proportion, then it makes and keeps the person healthy. For when one holds the upper hand in the "regiment" <note>meaning the ruling power or governance of the body</note>, the other is subject to it in servitude, and the other two then follow hesitantly and slowly <note>9) This refers to a specific manuscript citation or commentary in the original source.</note>, and thus the person is physically at rest.

**On the Humors.** There are four humors. The two most important of them are called phlegm; the other two are called <term>mucus<note>original: "Schleim." The text distinguishes between the primary "phlegms" and the secondary "mucuses" or slimes.</note></term>. Each particularly prominent humor is superior to the next following by a quarter and by half of a third part, and the weaker of the two acts to mitigate two parts and the rest of the third part, so that it does not exceed its boundary. For whichever is the uppermost humor, it rules the second in this manner, and these two bear the name: Phlegm. The second humor rules the third, and the third rules the fourth. These two—that is, the third and the fourth humor—are called: Mucus. The humors that are stronger in effect overtake the weaker ones with their quantitative proportion, and the weaker ones, through their lower content, act to mitigate their excess. If this proportion holds true for a person, he is in a state of rest. But as soon as any humor goes beyond its boundaries, the person is endangered. However, if one of the aforementioned mucuses has lawlessly exceeded its measure, it lacks the sufficient...
