powers to master the <term>humors<note>original: "Säfte." The bodily fluids believed in medieval medicine to determine health and personality.</note></term> that excel him, unless he is either driven by the subsequent phlegm, if he precedes it, or, if he is the following one, receives help from the preceding one. When such a phlegm has spread in a person in excess beyond its measure, the other humors within him cannot remain in peace—except for those people upon whom the grace of God has been poured out, either as strength as with **Samson**, or as wisdom as with **Solomon**, or as prophecy as with **Jeremiah**, or upon some heathens, as **Plato** was, and those like him. Where the other previously mentioned people err, these will be the strongest in righteousness through the grace of God, because the grace of God allows them to be subject to a certain changeability for a time; such that they are sick for a time and healthy for a time, fearful once and brave-hearted another time, sad once and then joyful again. God restores this in them so that when they are sick, He makes them healthy; if they have fear, He gives them courage; and when they are sad, He makes them joyful. 

When the dry phlegm in a person is stronger than the moist, and the moist is stronger than the foamy and the lukewarm, then the dry phlegm stands there like a mistress, the moist like a maid, and the foam with the lukewarm like the lesser, low servants staying in the background. The latter two then form, according to their capacity, the phlegm of the first two. A person in whom this relationship is found is wise by nature, yet prone to sudden anger and intense in his works. However, he is not persistent in them, because the dryness consumes this persistence and then it rises easily in them again like a flame that quickly falls and rapidly rises again. He is healthy and lives long, but does not reach the highest age because, after his flesh has been dried out by the fire, he does not have sufficient help from the moist phlegm.

### On Frenzy. <note>original: "Von der Hirnwut." This refers to a state of mania, delirium, or madness believed to be caused by an overheating or imbalance of the brain's humors.</note>
If by chance the foamy or the lukewarm phlegm—which then constitute the subordinate <gloss>10)</gloss> phlegm of the first-mentioned types (the dry and the moist) and should actually remain quiet—have exceeded their boundaries like a wave that is excessively stirred up in the water, then they are turned into poison. From them arises such a storm that no phlegm harmonizes with the other any longer, and they do not fulfill their duty as is proper. In this process, those previously mentioned come into such opposition to the two superior types of phlegm that now all four fight against one another. A person who has to endure this turmoil and this opposition in his body will become frenzied <note>The word "hirnwütig" implies a literal "brain-rage" or madness.</note>, because when a person's internal humors [fight] each other...

...struggles, rages, and destroys himself unless he is bound with ropes. He does this until the two mentioned—the foamy and the lukewarm—subside and return to their proper order. **On those afflicted with gout.** <note>original: "Gichtbrüchigen." In this period, this term covered a range of conditions including gout, arthritis, and various forms of paralysis or "palsy."</note> When the foamy and the lukewarm—which then form the phlegm of the moist and the dry—have developed excessively, such that the foam rises up and forms a hot, watery vapor, and the lukewarm pours out in drops, then they cause a storm through such conflict. This bows the person's neck, curves their back, and makes them completely afflicted with gout until they are released from this suffering. However, they can live a long time in this state.

**On the foolish.** <note>original: "Blödsinnigen." Historically used to describe those with cognitive impairments or mental illnesses; Hildegard describes here a state of fluctuating mania and cheerfulness.</note> In a person where the dry exceeds the foam, and the foam exceeds the moist and the lukewarm, that person is sometimes furious in their foolishness and sometimes cheerful in their foolishness. They are not weak, but of considerable physical strength, and can live long if it be God's will.

**On paralysis.** When the moist and the lukewarm—which both then form the phlegm of the dry and the foamy—are sprayed beyond their measure like a perilous storm wind, they are transformed, in a sense, into a general movement of the winds and produce a dangerous-sounding noise, like the sound of thunder. This sound echoes into the vessels, the marrow, and the temples of such a person. Whoever suffers from this becomes paralyzed and loses all strength throughout the entire body, until the two mentioned phlegms have retreated and returned to their proper path. However, if God permits it, they may live long.

**On good character.** But when the foam exceeds the dry, and the dry exceeds the moist and the lukewarm, the person has a good character, is benevolent and physically delicate, but does not live long.

**On senselessness.** <note>original: "Sinnlosigkeit." This refers to a loss of reason, delirium, or perhaps a tendency to faint or lose consciousness.</note> When the moist and the lukewarm—which then form the phlegm of the foam and the dry—exceed their measure, then in a short time the moist is whirled around like a wheel and sometimes casts the person down...

<summary>This page details how imbalances between the foamy, lukewarm, dry, and moist "phlegms" lead to specific conditions like gout, mental instability, and paralysis. It presents a medieval physiological model where bodily fluids behave like weather patterns (storms, vapors, and winds) to influence character and health.</summary>
<keywords>Hildegard von Bingen, humoral theory, phlegm, gout, paralysis, medieval medicine, mental health, physiology</keywords>

--- Page 62 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous section regarding the "foolish" (Blödsinnigen), specifically focusing on how the imbalance of internal humors leads to madness, despair, fear, and muteness.</meta>

...into water and sometimes into fire, and the lukewarm humor drives him to madness. This person is of such a nature that insight vanishes within him, which also makes him senseless. He is neither entirely healthy nor completely ill.

**On Madness.** If, however, the dry in someone exceeds the lukewarm, and the lukewarm exceeds the moist and the foam which immediately follow, then this person is sick in spirit and body. In his behavior, he is an abomination to himself and to other people—useless and reckless in all his affairs. He is only moderately healthy and yet can live a long time.

**On Despair.** <note>original: "Verzweiflung." In Hildegard’s medical-theological context, this describes a profound spiritual and psychological crisis, similar to what we might today call clinical depression or "melancholia," where one loses the ability to feel divine or human connection.</note> If, however, the moist or the foam—which then both represent the <term>phlegm<note>original: "Schleim." Hildegard uses this term to describe the secondary humors or residues produced by the primary qualities of dry, moist, foamy, and lukewarm.</note></term> of the dry and the lukewarm—have exceeded their measure in such a way that the moist sends a bitter smoke into the person, and the foam makes him discolored and slippery like a toad, they then cause a sound to arise in the person like the sound of the blowing North Wind. This rushes upon the heart and the senses of this person, so that he can have no hope or trust in God, nor in man, nor in any creature at all. This condition lasts as long as the two aforementioned phlegms have not ceased their terrifying activity. Therefore, it would be better for such a person to die than to live. Nonetheless, he may live for a long time.

**On the Fearful.** If, however, the lukewarm acts more strongly in a person than the dry, and the dry acts more strongly than the moist and the foam, he experiences much hardship, anger, and sadness in his inner life, interspersed with joy; but nothing comes of these emotions because, amidst all this, he is anxious like a wave in the water and is inwardly afraid of everything. Some of these people live a long time, but the greater number of them die soon.

**On the Mute.** If the moist or the foam—which in such a case constitute the residual phlegm of the above-mentioned humors, namely the lukewarm and the dry, and which should actually be in a state of rest—have exceeded their measure, then the moist produces a kind of boiling-hot, watery smoke. The foam then fetters and strangles the reason within the person, so that he cannot speak but becomes mute. Because such a person cannot speak outwardly, he is all the more wise within his spirit; and because his reason is restricted outwardly, his knowledge within him is fluid, and he feels it. A person of this kind is not inferior; rather, he is physically healthy and lives a long life.

<summary>This page details how specific imbalances of the four "phlegms" (dry, moist, foamy, and lukewarm) result in various mental and physical states, ranging from madness and suicidal despair to anxiety and muteness. Hildegard notably suggests that those who are mute may possess a deeper, "fluid" internal wisdom despite their lack of external speech.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medieval medical-theological analysis of how the four internal qualities—the moist, the dry, the foam, and the lukewarm—interact to determine a person's character, physical health, and lifespan.</meta>

## On Integrity
If, however, the moist in a person gains the upper hand over the foam, and the foam over the subsequent dry and lukewarm qualities, then these latter two—according to their capacity—form the <term>phlegm<note>original: "Schleim." In this system, phlegm is seen as a secondary byproduct or residue of the primary humors.</note></term> of the first two (the moist and the foam). Such a person possesses a very good character by nature and is of a cheerful disposition; their flesh grows well, they are not easily moved to anger, and they are not unpleasant in social company. However, they are physically delicate and will not live long, because the quality of dryness is only moderately present within them. <note>In medieval medicine, a certain amount of "dryness" was often associated with the firmness and longevity of the body’s structures.</note>

## On Cancer Patients
If, however, the dry or the lukewarm qualities—which in such a case form the phlegm of the moist and the foam—exceed their natural boundaries, they produce noisy burping and hiccups in the person. They cause <term>cancer<note>original: "Krebs." In the Middle Ages, "cancer" referred to a wide range of malignant growths, persistent ulcers, or "eating" sores that appeared to consume the flesh.</note></term> to arise within them, and cause worms to consume them, and the flesh of their body to swell into misshapen ulcers. This results in the growing swelling making one of their arms or legs larger than the other. They do this until they have ceased from this pestilence. Therefore, such a person cannot live long.

## On Gout
When the foam exceeds the moist, and the moist exceeds the dry and the lukewarm, then such a person likely possesses good knowledge; however, they are hasty and too impetuous in their thirst for learning, so that their knowledge is scattered in many directions like <note>original: "Spreu, die vom Winde zerstreut wird" - a biblical allusion to Psalm 1:4.</note> chaff scattered by the wind. Such a person strives to rule over others. Physically, they are healthy, except that they easily become weak in the legs and easily contract <term>podagra<note>A specific form of gout, usually affecting the joints of the foot, particularly the big toe.</note></term>. Yet, if it should so please God, they can live a long time.

## On Suicides
If, however, the dry or the lukewarm qualities—which then form the phlegm of the foam and the moist—have veered from their proper course, then the dry exceeds the foam and the moist, throwing them into disorder. The lukewarm quality causes a boiling, watery smoke to arise in the person, so that such a person plunges themselves into death, unless they are prevented from doing so by God or their fellow humans. Such a person is neither entirely healthy nor entirely ill, but somewhere between the two. They can live a long time if they are watched.

## On Gouty Conditions
But in one for whom the moist exceeds the dry, and the lukewarm exceeds the dry and the foam which remain here, that person is able to endure hardship both in themselves and in other people. They are of a sorrowful temperament and do not possess great anger, but are useful in their...

It emphasizes the physical and psychological consequences of these humors exceeding their natural limits.</summary>

## On Instability
However, if the dry or the foam—which then represent the <term>phlegm<note>original: "Schleim." This refers to the secondary waste products or "residues" created when the primary humors are out of balance.</note></term> of the moist and the lukewarm—exceed their boundaries, then in such a person the dryness strives upward like that plant called <term>bindweed<note>original: "Winde." Likely referring to Convolvulus, a climbing plant known for its rapid, winding growth, used here as a metaphor for erratic behavior.</note></term>, and the foam causes bitter burping to occur in him. Thus, both together create a disordered conduct in the person, so that he wants to run everywhere and would completely ruin himself if he were permitted. This state lasts in him until both types of phlegm have disappeared within him. He can, however, live a long life if God and his fellow men have protected him.

## On the Quick-Tempered
If the lukewarm exceeds the moist, and the moist exceeds the dry and the foam, then the person is cunning, shuns peace, and loves discord and strife. In body, he is thin, but he is a glutton when eating; he is also not healthy, yet despite this, he does not lie in bed but goes about. He can live a long life but does not reach a true old age, because he dies before attaining a high age.

## On Fainting Spells
But if the dry or the foam—which are then the phlegm of the warm and moist—exceed their limits, then the dry flares up so that the person becomes immoderate in drink, and the foam makes him greedy for food. Therefore, he is sometimes spun around like a wheel, so that he occasionally falls down like a dead man. This illness, however, preserves the life-spirit of the person, so that he does not have to die before the spell has ceased. Such a person is of weak health and does not reach a true old age.

## On Instability
If, however, in someone the foam exceeds the lukewarm, and the lukewarm exceeds the dry and the moist—which then follow him in this way like a lingering phlegm, as described for the others previously—then this person has at one time an inappropriate cheerfulness and a baseless sadness, and at another time a misplaced anger. He is, however, benevolent. Internally he suffers from a foul-smelling and dirty phlegm as well as from revolting sensations of smell and taste; he is also not clever and rarely reaches old age.

Where the dry or the moist—which then [form] the lingering phlegm of the aforementioned humors...

**On the Morose Disposition.** But if the lukewarm becomes stronger than the foam, and the foam stronger than the other two <term>phlegms<note>original: "Phlegmen." **Also on Brain-Fury.** If, however, by chance the dry or the moist—which then both form the subsequent phlegm of the aforementioned humors, the lukewarm and the foam, and which should actually be in a state of rest—have exceeded their boundaries, then the dry phlegm rises beyond its measure, winds its way into the person, and drives out their spirit almost in an instant. The moist phlegm then produces erroneous thoughts in him, so that he tears at himself and utters wicked and nonsensical words. At the same time, he is hot-tempered, wicked, and suffers from <term>brain-fury<note>original: "Hirnwut." An old term for acute mania or frenzy, believed to be caused by physical imbalances affecting the brain.</note></term>, full of unrest, and rarely reaches an advanced age.

**On Health.** When the aforementioned <term>juices<note>original: "Säfte." Also known as humors; the vital fluids that medieval medicine believed governed health and personality.</note></term> within the person maintain the correct order and the right measure, as has been explained previously, then he is at rest and in physical health. But if they have set themselves in opposition to one another, then, as already stated, they make him frail and sick.

**Why There are Only Four Juices.** A person cannot consist of only one juice, or of two or three, but of four, so that they may regulate each other, just as the world consists of four elements that maintain harmony among themselves.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between human behavior and the physical world. The previous page ended mid-sentence describing the dangers humans face when the elements—responding to human moral instability—become hostile forces of nature.</meta>

...men face many dangers, because fire is called forth like a lance, air like a sword, water like a shield, and earth like a javelin to execute <term>judgment<note>original: "Strafgericht." The elements are indeed subject to humanity, and just as they themselves are affected by the deeds of men, they carry out their respective duties. For when people mix among one another in battles, terrors, in hatred, envy, and sinful adversities, the elements transform into a different and contrary kind of heat or cold, or into violent outbursts and floods. This stems from God's original decree, because it was so arranged by God for the elements that they should behave according to the works of men; they are struck by human deeds when man works with and within them. However, if people stay on the right path and carry out both good and evil with <term>measure<note>original: "Maßen." A central concept in medieval philosophy (discretio), suggesting that even in a fallen state, human actions must be balanced and regulated to maintain cosmic order.</note></term>, then the elements fulfill their duties through the grace of God in the way that is necessary for humanity.

## On Penance.

But if the elements should ever unleash their terrors upon humanity in the manner described above, then let the people weep and cry out with sighs and tears, so that the elements may catch this and carry it to the blood of the unblemished Lamb <note>A reference to Jesus Christ, whose sacrifice is seen here as a cosmic force that can pacify the angered elements.</note>, and the grace of God will come to their aid.

## On the Fall of Lucifer.

Lucifer is so firmly locked away from heaven that it is not possible for him to move from the place of <term>Tartarus<note>original: "Tartarus." In this context, it refers to the deepest abyss of hell or the prison of the fallen angels.</note></term>. For if he were able to move, he would turn all the elements to evil through his power, such that he would make the <term>firmament<note>The sphere of the stars and planets.</note></term> run backward, darken the sun, moon, and stars, halt the waters in their course, and cause great disaster among all creatures. The entire host of demons clings to him, some of whom possess greater power and others lesser. There are also some among them who interact more frequently with humans, who do not flee holy places as easily, and who shrink less from the cross of the Lord and holy rituals. All of them work united with Lucifer against the world. 

Compared to the violence of Lucifer, however, the Devil possesses almost the same power, violence, and malice, as he represents, so to speak, Lucifer's lust for power and his will. Because Lucifer himself is not permitted to move, the Devil is sent into the world as the <term>Python<note>13</note><note>original: "Python." In medieval theology, this term often refers to a spirit of divination or a specific deceptive manifestation of the demonic, drawing on the serpent of Delphi.</note></term> of the demons. He possesses the power of deceiving through disguise and many other evil qualities. In this way, he seduced Adam in Paradise and declared him the lord of the earth. He will carry the breath of Lucifer, through Lucifer's power, to the <term>Antichrist<note>The ultimate adversary expected to appear at the end of time.</note></term>, so that the Antichrist may receive it into himself. When God forbade the blessed Evangelist John from writing down the voices that spoke out of the thunders <note>A reference to Revelation 10:4, where John is instructed to "seal up" the words of the seven thunders.</note>, it was because they revealed all the terrors and anxieties that humanity has endured before and since, and will yet have to endure. ## On Frenzy.

When one fluid <note>original: "Saft." Referring to the four humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) which medieval medicine believed governed health and temperament.</note> in a person exceeds the others in quantity and has not maintained its proper order and measure, such a person will be weak and sickly. But if two have risen up within the person at the same time, then he cannot survive, because he either <term>rages<note>original: "rast." This refers to "Raserei," a state of mania or violent delirium believed to be caused by an acute imbalance of the humors.</note></term> or his whole body perishes because the proper mixture is missing. However, if three have expanded beyond their measure at once, then the person falls into weakness and soon dies. But if all four have risen up without order, then the person is killed by them quickly, in an instant and a single moment, because he could never endure it but would completely collapse—just as on the Last Day everything will be thrown into confusion when the four elements fight amongst themselves.

The woman, however, was not transformed in the same way, because having been taken from the flesh, she remained flesh; for this reason, the skillful work of the hands <note>original: "kunstreiche Werk der Hände." ### On Conception

This is how it stands with the beginning of the emergence and the <term>coagulation<note>original: "Zusammengerinnung." In medieval embryology, the formation of a fetus was often compared to the curdling of cheese or the thickening of liquids.</note></term> of every human being: Within a person lie Will, Reflection, Power, and Consent. First, the Will appears, because every person has the will to do this or that. Then follows Reflection, which considers whether a thing is suitable or unsuitable, reasonable or shameless. Then follows Power, which carries within it the work of completion so that it may happen. Finally, Consent follows, because the work cannot be carried out if Consent does not approve it, such that it is agreed upon. These four powers are present at the creation of man. 

Then come the four elements, which evoke the four <term>humors<note>original: "Säfte." The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were believed to determine health and temperament.</note></term> in man, in excess and in storm: so that fire (the dry) excessively ignites the Will; air (the moist) excessively moves Reflection; water (the foam) causes Power to strike great waves; and the earth (the lukewarm) causes Consent for the deed to swell beyond measure. All of these together, as a result of their great excess, stir up a kind of storm and extract a slimy foam from the blood. This is the seed. 

If it falls into its proper place, the blood of the woman unites with it, and from this, it becomes bloody. The first beginning of man arises out of the feeling of lust which the serpent, with the apple, breathed into the first man, because the blood of the man is then stirred up through that feeling of lust. Therefore, this same blood introduces a cold foam into the woman, which congeals through the warmth of the mother’s flesh and is spread out into a bloody form. Because this foam remains continuously under the influence of that same warmth, it soon grows—through the dry secretions from the mother’s nourishment—to the consistency of a small human form, until the formative hand of the Creator, who shaped man, pours that same consistency entirely through the human figure, just as a blacksmith fashions his artwork. For when Adam transgressed the commandment of God, his manly strength in the generative organ was transformed into a slimy foam, and the blood of the woman into a perverse discharge. For from that strong and righteous nature...

--- Page 69 ---
<meta>This page continues the medieval physiological explanation of conception, detailing how the male and female contributions interact and how the four elements—Fire, Air, Water, and Earth—physically construct the human form before the soul is infused by God.</meta>

From the man's nature, blood contains his seed because his flesh was created from the earth. From the woman's true nature, however, her blood—being weak and tender—contains no seed; instead, it secretes only a thin foam in small amounts, because woman was not made from two sources like man (from earth and flesh), but was taken only from the flesh of the man. Therefore, she is weak and fragile, and she is a vessel for the man. 

Through the man’s impulse of love, the woman’s blood is stirred and gives a kind of foam to the man’s seed. This foam, colored more bloody than white, joins with the seed, warming it, giving it blood, and granting it strength. For when the seed has fallen into its place and lies there, it becomes cold. It remains, so to speak, only a slimy foam until <term>Fire<note>In medieval science, "Fire" represents the vital heat of the body.</note></term> (that is, warmth) heats it; <term>Air<note>Referring to the "pneuma" or vital breath.</note></term> (that is, breath) dries it; <term>Water<note>The fluid element responsible for the body's moisture and flow.</note></term> (that is, its liquid state) sends it pure moisture; and until <term>Earth<note>The solid element that provides structure and containment.</note></term> (that is, a thin membrane) holds it together. 

Then it will become "bloody"—meaning not entirely blood, but only somewhat infused with blood. The <term>four humors<note>original: "vier Säfte." These are the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed to determine health and temperament.</note></term>, which a person draws from the four elements, now remain in a moderate and balanced degree around this seed until it has, so to speak, congealed into flesh and attained firmness, so that a human shape can now be formed from it. 

Then the human figure is formed from it like a painting; the marrow and the vessels attach themselves like threads throughout the entire form, distributing themselves everywhere and shaping the joints like knots, while also surrounding the marrow as if with an eggshell-membrane that later becomes bone. Then the whole becomes as complete and clearly formed as a figure that a painter depicts recognizably in some form. Everywhere that limbs are later meant to be, divisions are split open right up to the thin membrane that initially holds the whole together—much like clay that cracks under the heat of the sun. Then the dry flesh is dried of <note>original: "Gift (frei=) getrocknet." This implies the purging of impurities or "melancholy" fluids during development.</note> impurities, and true flesh is filled with true blood. 

Through maternal warmth alone, the living being does not yet develop as a fat <term>coagulum<note>original: "Gerinnsel." A term for the early-stage embryo before it takes human shape.</note></term>, but rather through the fact that it remains within this warmth. This work is completed in one month—that is, while the moon waxes and wanes. Thus, the aforementioned coagulum also increases and becomes fatty; for if it were not kept alive in this fattiness, it would dry up completely, and through this dryness, the womb would become very difficult to move and painful.

Then, as God wills and has decreed it should happen, the breath of life approaches and touches that form without the mother's knowledge, just as—

...a strong, warm wind—like a wind that blows loudly against a wall—and it pours into and fixes itself into all the joints of the limbs of that <term>form<note>original: "Gebilde." Refers to the developing fetus or structure of the body.</note></term>. Because of this, all the individual limbs of this same form are gradually loosened from one another, just as flowers unfold in the heat of the sun. But there is still such weakness in the form that it cannot move; instead, it lies there as if sleeping and barely breathes. 

And the <term>spirit of life<note>original: "Lebensgeist." The vital force that animates the physical body.</note></term> permeates the whole form, filling it everywhere and strengthening it in marrow and vessels, so that it now grows more than it did before, until the bones are spread over the marrow and the vessels become so strong that they can hold the blood. Now the child moves, and the mother feels it, as if it received a sudden impulse, and from then on it remains constantly in motion. For the <term>living wind<note>original: "lebendige Wind."</note></term>, which is the soul, enters the form according to the will of Almighty God—as previously stated—giving it stability, making it viable, and wandering everywhere within it like a caterpillar spinning silk, by which it is covered and enclosed as if by a house. 

The <term>breath of life<note>original: "Lebenshauch."</note></term> senses within this form where the soul can divide, bend, and flex; it pays close attention to all the places where vessels lie, drying them out at first like the walls of a hollow reed and weaving them into the flesh, which it transforms into the red color of blood through the heat of its fire, because the soul is fire. Therefore, it permeates the entire form with its breath, just as a whole house is illuminated by a fire lit within it; and all the vessels keep their place, held fast just as the earth is held together with the waters. 

The soul, however, lets the blood flow like living air through all the parts mentioned here and maintains the flesh with a bloody moisture under constant dampness, just as food is cooked by the fire in a pot. To the bones it grants their strength and embeds them into the flesh, so that this same flesh is supported by them and cannot give way, just as a man builds his house out of beams so that it cannot collapse and be destroyed. 

This occurs during the second month, during which—from one phase of the moon to the next—the form receives its stability through the soul. The soul, which resides in the vessels, fills the flesh and bones with blood, which signifies the waxing of the moon. That the form cannot yet move, however, signifies the waning of the moon. Thus the soul remains in this form and examines all the parts of the body with which it will have to perform its works, because it feels that it must move the whole, just as the wheel makes the mill turn. It begins its course, following the path of the sun. Just as the sun rises in the morning and extends its path from the morning to <term>Terce<note>original: "Terz." <meta>This page continues the description of the soul’s integration into the human body, moving from the initial quickening described on the previous page to a detailed anatomical and symbolic journey of the soul through the various organs and limbs, eventually comparing the human lifecycle to the passage of a single day.</meta>

...so that she <note>The "she" refers to the soul, which in both German (die Seele) and Latin (anima) is a feminine noun.</note> might prepare them for their task by seeing the light as if through windows; in the chest, to enable the circulation of thoughts; and in the heart, when she wishes to show herself carried by the wings of knowledge. In the belly, she holds all its intestines together like a net and makes them durable in the form of empty cavities for the storage of food, by which the aforementioned <term>form<note>original: "Gebilde." This refers to the physical structure of the human body.</note></term> is nourished. This food is chewed by the teeth, which are planted like a root—without marrow and without water—through the moist fire of the soul, so that the food may be transported into the stomach, where it remains for its proper time. The soul also distributes the juice of the nourishment into the brain, the heart, the lungs, the liver, and into all the vessels, and with the strength of her heat, she fortifies the stomach and the intestines that contain the food, so that they do not fall apart but remain fixed in their place. Furthermore, she turns downward toward the thighs, strengthening them through her fiery heat and providing support to all the structures attached to the thighs, just as a house is secured and supported by columns. Then she also enters into the feet and the divisions of the feet, just as God designed and created them, because just as the pedestals of columns carry the columns themselves, so too do the feet of a human carry his legs. For the soul herself strengthens the form of the body, animates and illuminates it, because she is a flaming fire within the body, just as a fire shines throughout a whole house and into every corner, thus brightening the entire house.

Therefore, it happens that man is comparable to the sun and the moon; for, just as the first hour of the day announces the day, so too the soul, looking out through the windows of the eyes, perceives the light. And as the third hour of the day makes the day even clearer, so the soul multiplies thoughts within the chest. Just as the sun burns strongly in the sixth hour <note>Noon, the peak of the sun's power.</note> and has everything under its power, so the soul, with her rich knowledge, allows much to become manifest in her works within the heart. As the sun already declines toward a certain coldness at <term>Nones<note>original: "Non." The ninth canonical hour, roughly 3:00 PM.</note></term> because it has nearly completed its day's work, so it is with the soul when she works in the belly with the food by which the human is nourished—which, under the influence of the sun, takes root, grows, and reaches maturity. At <term>Vespers<note>original: "Vesperzeit." Evening, or the time of the evening prayer service.</note></term>, however, the sun hides beneath the earth and the night appears, because the soul remains in the legs, which carry the whole person, and at this hour the person—weary from work and empty of food—sleeps until the sun rises again. For the soul distributes the juice of the food correctly through the whole body and separates the superfluous waste, just as wine purifies itself from the dregs. At no point is the body without the soul, because she fills the entire body with her own inherent heat. For man is created from the four elements, of which two are spiritu...

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the soul’s relationship with the body, shifting focus to the four elements—fire, air, water, and earth—and how they constitute both the physical world and the human person. It explains the biological and spiritual necessity of balance between these opposing forces.</meta>

...and two are of a fleshly nature. Fire and air are spiritual, while water and earth are fleshly. These four join together within the human being to form a single whole; they also "cook" <note>The author uses the metaphor of "cooking" or "digesting" to describe how the elements transform raw matter into a living person.</note> the person so that, with all their appendages, they become flesh and blood. 

However, fire and water stand in opposition to one another and cannot dwell together. Therefore, it is necessary that each of the two be governed by a master. Water works against fire so that it does not burn further than is intended for it, and fire tames the water so that it does not flow further beyond the warmth of dryness than is right. These two properties of fire and water regulate the entire earth along with the air of the clouds, so that they may endure and not perish. 

It is the same in human blood, which takes its red color from the heat of fire and is made fluid by water. If the blood, despite its heat, were not rich in water, it could not flow at all, but would dry up and fall away like a scale. Likewise, if the earth were not rich in water, it would blow apart like chaff, and no creature could remain whole. Because of this, everything created depends on these two forces, and without them, no form would take shape; if these two were not joined into a whole in this way, the other forms could not sustain themselves. 

For God created man from earthly clay in such a way that, with the breath of the soul, he consists of watery, fiery, and airy earth. Thus the soul moves the human being by means of the four elements, because his form—shaped from earth—exists through God’s finger, is mixed by water, moved by air, and "cooked" by fire. 

The body possesses taste, and taste possesses pleasure; the soul, however, possesses desire, and desire possesses the will. The soul resembles fire, while the body resembles water, and both exist together. Thus, the human being is a work of God. Every achievement that the body requires, the soul creates within it; thus, the soul is the creative force, and the body is that which desires. The soul is more powerful than the body because she <note>The soul is referred to as "she" (die Seele) following the grammatical gender in the original German and Latin.</note> satisfies its desires. She would not possess the power that permeates and moves the human being—who is a work of God—without the human body, nor would she exist without the body; neither would the body of flesh and blood exist if it were not moved by the soul. 

However, the soul can live without the body, whereas the body cannot live in any way without the soul. After the Last Day <note>Judgment Day, or the end of time.</note>, the soul will demand its vessel back and will then rule over it according to her will. So too does the human being consist of two natures, body and soul, just as flesh does not exist without blood and blood does not exist without flesh—even though both are not alike in their composition—and just as the soul cannot exist at all without the body, and God is never without action <note>16) original: "Tat." This implies that God's essence is inseparable from His creative acts.</note>. For His work lay before eternity and endures within eternity...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the composition of the human being from the previous page, moving from the physical elements of earth, water, fire, and air to the spiritual reality of the soul. It draws a direct parallel between the soul's relationship to the body and God's relationship to humanity.</meta>

...hidden in him, just as the soul lies hidden and invisible within the body. However, the soul lives without the body and, after the Last Judgment <note>original: "Jüngsten Tage" — the final judgment at the end of time</note>, asks God for its covering back, so that it may clothe itself with it. In the same way, God—who was the life without beginning before eternity and in eternity—put on his garment, which was eternally hidden within him, at the appointed time. Thus, God and man are one, like soul and body, because God created man in his own image and as a likeness of himself. But just as every thing has a shadow, so too is man the shadow of God, and this shadow is the visible proof of his creation. Man is the visible representation of the almighty God in all his wonders and, because he has an origin, he is himself a shadow. But God is without beginning and end. Therefore, the whole heavenly harmony is a mirror of the Godhead, and man is a mirror of all God’s wonders.

**On Procreation.** According to the nature of his seed, the phlegms and the humors <term>humors: the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that medieval medicine believed controlled health and temperament</term> grow within him. Just as when summer wheat, winter wheat, or barley is sown, these naturally produce their grains accordingly. And just as the superfluous humors in a person are transformed into much mental confusion and various dangers until they peacefully dissipate and return to their proper balance, so too the child, from the first planting of the seed, is moved back and forth through many changing circumstances before the spirit of life becomes active within it, and before it works within the child in the right way.

**On the Afterbirth.** Now, after the male seed has fallen correctly into its place so that it must be formed into the shape of a human being, a thin membrane grows around this form from the woman's menstrual blood. This is comparable to a seed pod <note>original: "Samenkapsel"</note>, which holds the form together and surrounds it so that it cannot be moved back and forth and cannot fall. This happens because the curdled blood gathers there so that the form lies in its midst, like a person in the living chamber of his house. Within this, the form has warmth and is nourished for further support until birth by the dark blood of the woman’s liver.

**On Consciousness.** The child remains enclosed in its covering until its consciousness <note>original: "Bewußtsein" — the awakening of the soul's faculties or mental awareness</note> seeks to break forth fully developed. Now it can no longer remain enclosed and silent, because the child cannot cry out within the mother’s womb.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the formation and spiritual animation of the human being. It moves from the metaphysical union of soul and body to the specific physical and psychological transitions occurring at the moment of birth and early development, including the origin of human knowledge and the biological transformation of blood into breast milk.</meta>

...which took Eve from Adam's side <note>A reference to the biblical creation of Eve from Adam’s rib, used here to illustrate God's direct intervention in the physical structure of the body during childbirth</note>, is immediately present and displaces every corner of his dwelling within the female body. All connections in the female body accommodate this strong intervention, submit to it, and open up. While the child emerges, they remain in this state, and afterward they close again as they were before. The child’s soul, however, while the child emerges, senses the eternal power that sent it and rejoices in it.

## On Sensation.

Once the child has emerged, it soon begins to cry because it senses the darkness of the world. For when God sends the soul into the human body, its consciousness is as if it were asleep. However, after it has entered the body, the consciousness within it is awakened as it integrates into the flesh and the vessels <term>vessels: refers to the veins, arteries, and pathways of the humors, which were believed to be the channels for both physical fluids and vital spirits</term>.

## On Knowledge.

When the birth of a human is imminent, so that divine power opens the seal of the maternal womb, the child then notices the power of God there, and knowledge rises in its soul to learn and comprehend everything fully, if it is prompted to do so by its own wish and desire. For if a person, out of their own wish and desire, wants to know something of any action or any art <note>In this context, "art" refers to any skill, craft, or branch of learning</note>, the Holy Spirit pours the dew of his grace over the fresh impulse of that curiosity, whereupon they learn and grasp what they wish to learn. Just as a father and mother give an answer to a child when it asks them something, so too the Holy Spirit assists human knowledge in every art when a person wants to learn it by their own choice, desire, and through labor. 

But if a person turns toward something bad and toward an evil art and desires to learn it, then the Devil—who notices this—breathes upon the person's knowledge with his perversity and his cunning, so that they quickly learn the evil they wish to learn, because the human possesses the knowledge of good and evil. Once the child has emerged from the mother's womb, it becomes mobile and quick; it stays active, sweats, and possesses various phlegms and juices <term>juices: the humors or bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that determined a person's health and temperament</term>, according to the nature of its composition from its origin, and because these can increase or decrease in quantity through food and drink.

## On Milk.

When a woman receives the seed from a man and it begins to grow within her, then through that same natural power, the woman's blood is drawn upward to the breasts, and that which was intended to become blood from food and drink is transformed into milk, so that the child growing in her womb can be nourished. In the same proportion that the child grows in its mother's womb, the milk increases...

...also the milk in her breasts, so that the child might have its nourishment from it.

**On the Woman's Submission.** Just as the woman represents submission to the man until they become one, the woman also signifies the union of the male seed with the female blood, so that they become one flesh <note>A reference to the biblical concept in Genesis 2:24, where man and woman become "one flesh" through marriage and physical union</note>.

**Also on Conception.** When the male seed falls into its place, the woman's blood receives it with loving desire and draws it into itself, just as if a breath were drawing something in. Thus, the woman's blood mixes with the man's seed, and it becomes one blood, so that the woman's flesh is warmed by this mingled blood, and it grows and increases. In this way, the woman is then one flesh with the man through the man. The man’s flesh, however, is cooked <term>cooked: In medieval medicine, "coction" or cooking refers to the process where the body's internal heat transforms fluids, such as food into blood or blood into reproductive "seed"</term> both internally and externally by the warmth and sweat of the woman, and in turn draws some of the foam <note>original: "Schaum" – likely referring to the frothy or aerated quality of bodily humors during moments of high physical or emotional intensity</note> and sweat of the woman into itself. For, liquefied by the very strong power of the man’s will, his blood flows apart and is driven around as if in a mill, and takes some of the woman’s foam and sweat into itself. Thus his flesh is mixed with female matter, so that it becomes one flesh with and through her. 

Because man and woman are one flesh in such a way, the woman easily conceives a child from the man, provided she possesses the fertility for a child. That man and woman become and are one flesh in this way lay hidden in the man’s side when the woman, taken from the man’s side, became his flesh <note>This refers to the creation of Eve from Adam's rib, suggesting that their physical reunion in conception is a return to an original, divinely ordained unity</note>; and for this reason, man and woman flow together all the more easily into one through blood and sweat for conception. The eternal power that allows the child to emerge from its mother's womb thus makes the man and the woman into one flesh.

**On Adultery.** When a man or a woman, unmindful of their lawful union, have turned in their burning passion toward another alliance so that they consort in an unlawful union with another person, then the man has joined his blood—which is nonetheless the blood of his lawful wife—with a foreign woman. Likewise, the woman has brought her blood—which is the blood of her lawful husband—together with a foreign man. Therefore, the children who are later fathered by such lawful as well as unlawful husbands, or lawful as well as unlawful wives, will frequently be unhappy <note>In this context, "unhappy" or "miserable" (unglücklich) likely refers to a lack of physical or spiritual harmony, or a "tainted" nature resulting from the parents' moral transgression</note>, because they have inherited the basis for their conception from deviating customs and different blood, both from the father's and the mother's side,

It also warns that adultery disrupts this biological and spiritual harmony, potentially leading to the birth of "unhappy" or troubled children.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the discussion of how husband and wife become "one flesh" through the mixing of blood and seed. It shifts here to the spiritual consequences of violating the marital order and then moves into a proto-psychological analysis of how the "four elements" (fire, air, water, earth) within a father's seed determine the intellectual temperament of his child.</meta>

...have to thank. Such parents are therefore called transgressors before God of the righteous order that God established in Adam and Eve. Just as Adam and Eve, as transgressors of the divine commandment, surrendered themselves and all their descendants to death, so also do all those who desecrate the divine institution in such a way <note>A reference to adultery or illicit sexual unions</note> defile themselves and those who are thus begotten by them, and send them into misfortune, because the reason dwelling within them is sullied, and because they have made themselves common with the cattle in their moral conduct.

### **On Thin Seed.**

If a woman has conceived and shortly thereafter allows another man to approach her, at a time when the seed of the first man is still thin <note>The text suggests a window of time after conception where the womb was thought to still be "open" or the initial seed not yet "set"</note>, then often something of the sweat and the natural heat of the second man is added to the seed of the first, so that it is thereby contaminated, as if by a foul-smelling wind. It is as if one added some other liquid to milk to make it curdle, and it became contaminated thereby.

### **On the Diversity of Seed.**

A person in whom the dry element—that is, fire—predominates, has a hard head for learning the sciences <note>In the Middle Ages, "sciences" or *wissenschaften* referred to all branches of formal learning, such as the liberal arts</note>. But once they have learned them, they hold onto them strongly for a long time. In contrast, one in whom the moist—that is, air—prevails, possesses a quick understanding when learning the sciences, but cannot be lasting in their knowledge because, after having learned something, they easily let it fall into oblivion. 

But one in whom the foam—that is, water—prevails, has a rapid understanding in learning the sciences, but believes they have already grasped them before they have thoroughly gotten to know them, when in fact they know nothing. Therefore, they fail when they wish to show themselves with them, because they have not yet sufficiently made them their own. 

But one in whom the lukewarm—that is, earth—is present in abundance, has a hard and sluggish spirit for learning the sciences. If, however, they have grasped something from some of them with effort, they cannot retain it because of his hard spirit. Therefore, overcome by distaste because they can retain nothing, they frequently stop learning and also let go of what they had learned. Nevertheless, they sometimes possess understanding for individual things that have to do with the earth and worldly affairs.

### **On Carnal Lust.**

The blood vessels found in the liver and belly of the man meet in his genitals. Now, when the breath of lust emerges from the marrow of the man, it sinks down into his loins

<meta>This page continues from a discussion on how the four elements (fire, air, water, and earth) influence human temperament and intellect. and awakens in the blood the temptation toward desire. And because the region of the loins is quite restricted, narrow, and enclosed, that breath of wind <note>In medieval physiology, "wind" often refers to *pneuma* or vital spirits that were believed to move through the body to trigger physical reactions</note> cannot expand very far and heats up intensely there in a feeling of lust, so much so that the man forgets himself in this heat and cannot restrain himself from letting the foam of the seed <note>A reference to the Aristotelian view of semen as a frothy, "cooked" substance derived from the blood</note> exit. Because of the enclosed nature of the loins, the fire of lust burns more fiercely in the man, even if more rarely than in the woman. For just as a ship is endangered in the great waves that rise in rivers during strong winds and storms, so that at times it can only hold its own and resist with great effort, so too can the nature of the man only be tamed and restrained with difficulty during the onslaught of desire. In the waves, however, that rise under a lighter wind and in storms that come up during weak whirlwinds, a ship can hold its own, even if with effort; and the nature of the woman behaves in the same way regarding sexual desire, because it can be more easily controlled than the kind of sexual desire of the man. For the sexual desire of the man is like a fire that soon goes out and soon flares up, because a fire that burned constantly would also consume much. So also in the man, the feeling of desire sometimes rises and then sinks again, because if it burned within him without ceasing, the man would not be able to endure it.

### **Concerning Strong and Choleric Men**

There are, however, men of exceptional masculine strength, and these have a strong and coarse brain. Its outer, fine vessels, which hold its skin together, are colored somewhat red. Their complexion is also reddish, as can be seen in certain pictures painted with red color. They have coarse and strong vessels that carry hot blood the color of wax. They are stoutly built around the chest and have strong arms. However, they are not very fat: because the powerfully developed vessels, the hot blood, and the strong limbs do not allow their flesh to become thick through too much fat.

### **Concerning the Kidneys**

The wind in their loins is also of a more fiery than joyful nature. Subject to it are two small containers into which it blows like into a bellows. These two containers <note>This refers to the kidneys, which were historically associated with the loins and the seat of reproductive vigor</note> surround the trunk of all male power and assist it just like small bulwarks erected next to a tower to defend it. There are two of them so that they might all the more effectively surround, strengthen, and hold that aforementioned trunk, and with all the more emphasis and in the most suitable manner possible, receive that aforementioned breath of wind, draw it to themselves, and give it out again evenly, like two bellows blowing equally into the fire. When they then [strengthen] this trunk in its power...

### Once More on Adam’s Banishment.

When Adam became blind and deaf <note>In this context, blindness and deafness refer to the loss of spiritual perception following the original sin</note> during his transgression, the vital force within him went into exile into a foreign thing and fled unnoticed into the aforementioned regions of the male genitals and remained there. The men of whom we have spoken above are clever and are feared by others. They have an inclination toward women and tend to avoid and flee from other men because they love women more than men. They love the female form in the act of union so much that they cannot restrain themselves, because their blood ignites in great heat when they see or hear a woman, or remember her in their thoughts. For their eyes are directed toward the love of a woman like arrows when they see her; their hearing is like a very violent wind when they hear her, and their thoughts are like a storm wind that cannot be prevented from falling down to the earth.

These are truly masculine men, and they are called masters of fertility because they are always hot and fertile in the production of more and more offspring, like a tree that spreads itself far and wide with numerous branches. In the mighty fire of their embrace that is within them, they resemble arrows. If they have intercourse with women, they are healthy and cheerful; but if they must do without their company, they wither within themselves and go about like the dying, unless they pour out the foam of their seed <note>original: "Schaum ihres Samens"; a reference to the medieval belief that semen was a frothy substance produced by the "cooking" of blood through bodily heat</note> in luxurious dreams or thoughts, or in some unnatural way. At times they are under such a glow of passion that they approach some unfeeling creature that does not live and twist about with it so that, in order to defend themselves against the glow and relieve it, they pour out the foam of their seed, exhausted by their passion and the torment of the glow that is within them, because abstinence is too difficult for them.

If such men, driven by some necessity—be it out of shame or fear or love for God—wish to flee women, they must avoid them like poison and escape from them, because it is too difficult for them, and neither any sense of shame nor the will for abstinence can hold them back from embracing women if they catch sight of them. Their offspring, however, very often have a repulsive way of life, are incontinent in their desire, and show in their manners just as much of a contrast to other human habits as a deformity made from a raw and almost entirely fire-burnt block of wood compared to a beautiful figure made from good wood. For diabolical influence is present in such sexual unions to a high degree...

...degree, if they (the offspring) are conceived in excessive lust. However, if the drive for procreation in such men stays within becoming and right limits, then the children fathered by them will be very clever, capable, and mentally active, and their countenances will have a masculine expression and great beauty.

### Of the Blood-Rich.
<note>original: "Von den Blutreichen"; referring to the Sanguine temperament, characterized in medieval medicine by an abundance of blood, leading to a cheerful and balanced personality.</note> There are other men who have a warm brain and a lovely complexion, mixed of white and red, strongly developed vessels filled with blood, and thick blood of the correct red color. They possess within themselves a joyful moisture, which is suppressed neither by grief nor adversity, and which is fled and avoided by the repulsive nature of <term>melancholy</term> <note>original: "Schwarzgalligkeit," literally "black-galliness." In the four humors theory, black bile was thought to cause depression and bitterness.</note>. Because they possess a warm brain and blood of the right quality, and their <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säfte"; the vital bodily fluids believed to govern health and character.</note> can move freely, they have fatty flesh upon their bodies. Their natural disposition, which is located in their thighs, is more "windy" than "fiery"; they are therefore able to practice abstinence because the strong wind within their thighs tames and tempers the fire within them.

When such wind and such fire happen to fall into their two "vessels" <note>Referring to the testicles or the reproductive organs.</note>, they perform all their duties in honor and with sensible love, so that their lineage flourishes and grows green in honor. Therefore, one calls them a "golden edifice" during the proper embrace, because the intellect within them feels from whence it comes; thus, there is moderation in them and a human manner. For them, it is a necessity to mate in the proper manner of men, because the nature of woman is milder and more easily influenced than that of man. They can consort with women in honor and fruitfulness, but just as easily abstain from them, and they look upon women with good, modest eyes.

While the eyes of other men resemble arrows when looking at women, their eyes stand in honorable harmony with those of women; and while the listening of other men acts upon women like a storm wind, their words have, as it were, the tone of a zither. If the thoughts of other men are like a storm, these men are called sensible lovers in honor. They often endure many torments when they restrain themselves in their drive; however, there lies within them wise reflection—the "art of the woman"—which takes modest self-control from the female nature, and they also possess a perceptible insight.

Their offspring are self-controlled, happy, useful, and righteous in all their deeds, and remain free from envy, because the wind and fire in the thighs of their fathers have bestowed upon them the right moderation, since the fire did not overwhelm the wind as it flared up, but rather the wind tempered the fire. Those who emerge in this way will become useful people. But if the aforementioned men are without women, they remain without glory, like a day without the sun...

<summary>This page describes the 'Sanguine' temperament in men, characterizing them as balanced, honorable, and capable of self-control. It contrasts their moderate, musical nature with more violent temperaments and notes that their offspring inherit this sense of balance and utility.</summary>

In their interactions with women, however, they are as pleasant and brief as a day when the sun is bright. Because they are mild in their gaze, their words, and their thoughts, they discharge a watery, uncooked foam <note>In medieval physiology, semen was thought to be "cooked" or refined by body heat; a "watery" or "uncooked" discharge suggested a cooler, more temperate nature.</note> more frequently than other men; this happens to them both while awake and during sleep. More easily than many other men, they are freed from the heat of their desire by their own nature as well as by other circumstances.

### Of the Melancholic
<note>original: "Von den Schwarzgalligen"; referring to the Melancholic temperament, which in the theory of the four humors was governed by "black bile." While we associate melancholy with sadness, the author here describes it as a dark, heavy, and potentially violent force.</note>

There are, however, other men whose brains are fatty, and the membranes of the brain as well as its vessels are cloudy. They have a dark complexion, and their eyes are quite fiery and similar to those of vipers. They have hard and strong vessels <note>referring to veins and arteries</note> that contain a black and thick blood. They have strongly developed and hard flesh and coarse bones that contain only a little marrow. This marrow, however, burns so fiercely that such men are as unrestrained in their dealings with women as animals and snakes. The "wind" in their loins is of three kinds: fiery, windy, and permeated with the smoke of <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "Schwarzgalle"; the humor associated with the earth, autumn, and a cold/dry nature, believed here to produce a "smoky" or "sooty" temperament.</note>. For this reason, they have no true affection for any person; they are repulsive in social interaction, greedy and foolish, and yet debauched in their lust and immoderate in their dealings with women, like donkeys. If they ever cease from this desire, they easily fall into a disease of the head, so that they become "brain-frenzied" <note>original: "hirnwütig"; a term for mania or madness.</note>. When they follow their desire in interactions with women, they do not suffer from this head disease. Nevertheless, their embrace—which they ought to keep within moderate limits with women—is painful, repulsive, and deadly, like that of ravenous wolves. 

Some of them enjoy interacting with women in a human way because they possess strong vessels and fiercely burning marrow. Nevertheless, they hate women. Others, however, are able to avoid the female sex because they do not love women and do not want to have them, but are as cruel in their hearts as lions and behave like bears. In the works of their hands, however, they are skilled and capable, and they enjoy working. The wind of desire, which falls down into the two "vessels" <note>the testicles</note> of such men, arrives as unrestrainedly and with such sudden force as a wind that unexpectedly and violently shakes a whole house. It raises the "trunk" <note>a metaphor for the penis</note> so tyrannically that the same trunk—which ought to stand in full bloom—bends in the hatred of the viper’s way and, in such wickedness, shows its malice toward its own offspring like a death-and-destruction-bringing viper. The diabolical deception <note>original: "Blendwerk"; the idea that extreme lust is a deceptive illusion or "glamour" cast by the devil.</note> rages in the passion of such men to such an extent that, if they could, they would kill the woman in the embrace.

...would be. For there are no acts of love or sexual affection in them. Therefore, the sons or daughters they conceive in this way often possess a devilish madness in their vices and habits, because they were conceived without love. Their descendants will mostly be unhappy and confused in all their habits; they cannot be loved by others, nor do they themselves enjoy being among their fellow humans, because they are tormented by all kinds of imaginings. But if they do remain with other people, they show themselves to be hateful, envious, and of perverse morals toward them, finding no joy in their company. Some of those conceived by them nevertheless sometimes become clever and useful; yet, despite their usefulness, they display such an unpleasant and perverse nature that they can be neither loved nor honored. They are like common stones that lie around without luster, as if they were extinguished, and are not valued alongside noble stones because they do not possess that beautiful brilliance.

## Of the Phlegmatic
<note>original: "Von den Phlegmareichen"; refers to the Phlegmatic temperament, which in medieval medicine was associated with the element of water and characterized by a cool, moist, and often sluggish nature.</note>

Now there are also men who possess a fatty, white, and dry brain, and the vessels of their brain look more white than red. They have large and bulging eyes and a feminine coloring in the face. Their skin color is not fresh, but pale, as if extinguished. Their vessels <note>referring to the veins and arteries</note> are wide and feel soft to the touch, but nevertheless do not contain much blood. Their blood itself also does not have the correct composition but is quite foamy. They have enough flesh on their body, and this is soft like a woman’s flesh. Their limbs are indeed strongly developed, but they do not possess a courageous and resolute disposition. 

In their thoughts, however, and in conversation, they are brave and gallant, like a fire whose flame suddenly flares up and just as quickly sinks down again. They also show courage in their outward demeanor, but they do not prove this in their deeds; they show in their interactions with others that they care more about opinion than about action. The *wind* in their loins <note>In the medical theories of the time, "wind" or "breath" (pneuma) was thought to be the driving force behind physical arousal and movement</note> possesses only little fire, so that it warms only a little, much like lukewarm water. Their two vessels <note>likely referring to the testicles</note>, which should act like two bellows to fan the fire, are abandoned in their weakness and do not have the strength to raise the *stamm* <note>literally "trunk," a euphemism for the penis</note> because they do not have the full fire within them. 

Such men can be loved in the embrace <note>referring to sexual intimacy</note> because they can be present with men and women and are reliable. Likewise, they do not hold deadly hatred toward their fellow humans, but rather possess the moderate enjoyment of the first-created creature in their body—as Adam and Eve lived before the carnal embrace—while they are weaklings in this or that act of procreation. Because the seed in these men cannot be of the same nature as is usual for the male sex, they are also not able to

...to prove themselves as true men through a beard nor through other such masculine peculiarities. But since they begrudge no one anything, they love women from a good heart in their natural weakness—for women are also weak, and in her weakness, a woman is like a boy. <note>In the medieval worldview, women were often categorized as "incomplete" or "cooler" versions of the male form, hence the comparison to a boy.</note> From this it follows that such men do sometimes warm up to some extent and may then achieve a weak growth of beard, like soil that produces a little grass. But they lack the proper strength at the plow to furrow the earth, because they cannot lie with women as procreative men do, but are instead sterile. For this reason, they also do not have to endure much in their thoughts regarding sexual desire; only occasionally do they think of it and have a wish. Because they are afflicted with this bodily defect, they are also slow in their thinking; the vessels of their temples do not pulse with full vitality; they have brittle vessels, similar to reeds and other plants. It also cannot be said that their vessels are of a true masculine nature, because they are cold, and their seed <note>semen</note> is thin and uncooked, like foam. They are also unable to hold this back until the proper time.

### Of the Sexual Desire of the Woman
The fleshly desire of the woman is compared to the sun, which mildly and gently permeates the earth with its warmth continuously so that it can bring forth fruit. If the sun were to burn down upon the earth more intensely and without pause, it would damage the fruits more than it would nourish them. Thus, the fleshly desire in the woman is also coaxing and mild; instead, she possesses the enduring warmth necessary to conceive and give birth to offspring. If a woman remained constantly in the burning heat of sexual longing, she would be unsuitable for conception and procreation. When desire arises in the woman, it is less pronounced than in the man, because such a fire does not burn as fiercely in her as it does in him.

### Of the Sexual Desire of the Man
When the storm of passion arises in the man, it rolls around within him like a mill wheel, because his loins are comparable to a workshop into which the marrow <note>original: "Mark"; believed in medieval medicine to be the source of heat and vital fluids</note> sends its fire. Then this workshop moves the fire further toward the male genitals and lets it burn fiercely. If, on the other hand, the wind of desire goes forth from the marrow of the woman, it sinks into the <term>womb</term> <note>original: "Gebärmutter" (uterus)</note>, which hangs at the navel, and stirs the woman's blood to desire. And because the womb has a wide and open space in the vicinity of the woman's navel, that wind spreads into the woman's belly. Therefore, it burns more mildly there—even if more frequently because of its moisture—and for that reason is also able, whether out of fear or out of shame, more easily to [restrain itself]...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of female physiology and sexual desire, specifically contrasting the "foam of the seed" in women and men. It explores the relationship between the womb, the monthly cycle, and the influence of the moon on the human body’s humors.</meta>

...to abstain from sexual pleasure like a man. For this reason, the foam of the seed <note>The author uses "foam" to describe the reproductive fluids, which were thought to be a frothed, refined essence of blood.</note> is released less frequently by the woman than by the man, and in a smaller quantity relative to the male seed—as little as a single bite compared to a whole loaf of bread. Nevertheless, it often happens that the aforementioned foam is not released after pleasure because it mixes with the contents of the shining white and strong vessels of the womb, to be eventually expelled with the monthly flow <note>menstruation</note>. What remains is emptied by the womb; sometimes, if the mating instinct is aroused without the touch of a man, the foam is distributed and rubbed within her and completely nullified. 

In general, the fertile constitution of the woman is colder and more "bloody" in nature than that of the man. Her powers are lesser than the man’s, and therefore she burns with sexual desire less than the man. For the woman is a vessel to receive and give birth to offspring, and her vital breath <note>original: "Wind"; referring to the internal "winds" or spirits thought to move through the body in medieval medicine</note> is light, her vessels are open, and her limbs are more easily moved than those of the man. Fertile men who abstain from intercourse with women easily become ill, but not to the same degree as women, because they produce more seed than women. Infertile women, on the other hand, are healthy if they have no intercourse with men; but if they have husbands, they are sickly. 

Just as a flood of rain and storms sometimes arises and then there is silence again, and as the must <note>young, fermenting wine</note> during fermentation soon foams up and soon sinks down, so too do the bad humors <note>original: "Säfte"; refers to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that governed health</note> sometimes rise in a person and then decrease again. For if they remained constantly at a peak in the evil effect of their power, a person could not endure it but would perish in a short time. For the blood in every person increases and decreases with the waxing and waning of the moon.

### On the Changing of the Moon and the Humors

When the moon grows to its fullness, the blood in a person also increases, and when the moon wanes, the blood in a person is also diminished. This is a regular occurrence, in the woman as in the man. For once the blood in a person has reached the fullness of its growth, if it did not then decrease, the person could not withstand it but would burst apart entirely.

### On the Time for Procreation

When the blood in a person likewise increases during the waxing moon, the person—woman as well as man—is capable at this time of bringing forth fruit, that is: to procreate offspring. During the waxing moon, when the increase of blood in the person is taking place, the human seed is powerful and strong,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of human physiology and reproductive health, transitioning from the internal "winds" and vessels of the womb to the cosmic influence of the moon on human blood and the aging process for both sexes.</meta>

...and during the waning moon, when the blood in humans also decreases, the human seed is weak and lacking in strength, like yeast <note>The author compares the consistency and potency of the seed to yeast or dregs, implying a lack of vital "rise" or energy.</note>, and therefore of lesser quality for the production of offspring. If a woman has conceived during this time, whether it becomes a boy or a girl, such a person will be weak, sickly, and not particularly capable. Thus: during the waxing moon, the blood is increased in both the woman and the man, and during the waning moon, it decreases again in both the woman and the man until the fiftieth year of life.

### On the Monthly Flow.

While the blood in a man is diminished during the waning moon, in a woman the blood also decreases during the time of her monthly cleansing <note>A traditional term for menstruation.</note>. If a woman’s monthly cleansing occurs during a waxing moon, then she experiences more discomfort during this time than if it had happened during a waning moon, because during a waxing moon her blood was meant to increase in volume, which is then diminished during the monthly flow. However, after reaching the fiftieth year of life, human blood is no longer as strongly or easily increased or decreased under the influence of the moon as it has been before. But until the eightieth year, the blood allows a person’s flesh to increase somewhat more than it was accustomed to until then, because that same blood now stops increasing and decreasing in volume. After the eightieth year, both flesh and blood waste away in a man, the skin contracts, and wrinkles form, whereas in youth the skin was smooth and taut because it was completely filled with flesh and blood. Because flesh and blood decrease in a man after the eightieth year of life, he himself becomes weak and must therefore be strengthened by food and drink, like a child who always has need of this, so that what he lacks in flesh and blood is compensated for by food and drink.

In women, the monthly flow no longer occurs after the fiftieth year of life, except for those who possess such good health and strength that their monthly cleansing lasts until their seventieth year. Because the blood then no longer flows out as it did before, her flesh increases until the seventieth year of life, because it is then not reduced by the monthly flow. After the seventieth year, however, flesh and blood also waste away in women, their skin contracts, wrinkles appear, they become frail and, like a child, must be more frequently strengthened with food and drink, because they are emptied of flesh and blood and are weaker than men; while this misery of old age in men lingers until the eightieth year of life. Also in irrational animals <note>original: "unvernünftigen Tieren"; refers to animals that lack human reason</note>...
