<meta>This page continues the discussion of cosmic influences on biological fluids, moving from the human cycle to the natural world of animals, timber, and agriculture. It maintains the theory that the moon’s phases dictate the movement of vital fluids (blood and sap).</meta>

...blood increases with the waxing moon and decreases when it wanes, though to a lesser degree than in humans. An exception is made for those animals that are produced and nourished by the perspiration and moisture of the earth <note>The author refers here to the medieval concept of "spontaneous generation," where certain creatures were thought to arise from decaying matter rather than birth.</note> and arise more from poison and foul matter than from blood, as well as the fish that live in the water, emerge from water, and possess very little blood. Likewise in trees, which grow green from their roots, the sap increases during the waxing moon and sinks during the waning moon. Therefore, if they are felled during a waxing moon, they are more easily eaten by worms and wood-rot <note>original: "Holzfraß"</note> because of the sap and moisture remaining in them, as compared to if they had been cut during a waning moon. If they are felled during the waning moon, they are transformed into a kind of hardness because the sap in them is now diminished, so that worms grow in them all the less and wood-rot can do them less harm.

**On the Pruning of Trees.** The planting and pruning of trees for their flourishing is better done during a waning moon than a waxing moon. This is because, if this is undertaken during a waxing moon, the trees often languish, root poorly, and progress less well due to the rising and excess sap. If, however, it is undertaken during a waning moon, a greater and stronger life force lies hidden within the trees because the sap is then partially diminished; therefore, they take root faster and endure better than when they are full of sap, because the sap would otherwise flow out of the pruned areas later during the waxing moon.

**On the Pruning of the Vine.** Even when the branches of the vine are pruned for the cultivation of vineyards, they reach a greater yield and abundance of fruit if they are pruned during the waning moon rather than the waxing moon. For the more they are pruned during a waxing moon, the more sap and "tears" <note>The watery sap that leaks from a cut vine is traditionally called "vine tears."</note> flow out of them. This makes the vine somewhat drier than if it were cut during a waning moon, because then its strength remains inside and the cut surface heals and hardens before the moon begins to wax again.

**On the Gathering of Herbs.** Noble and healing herbs that are cut from the earth or pulled up by the root during a waxing moon are better suited for the preparation of electuaries <note>A medicinal paste made by mixing powders with honey or syrup; original: "Latwerge".</note>, ointments, and every kind of medicine because they are then full of juice, rather than if one gathers them during a waning moon.

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural cycles influenced by the moon. While the previous page focused on the movement of sap in trees and the best time for pruning, this section extends those principles to agriculture, animal husbandry, and the physiological needs of the human body, specifically sleep.</meta>

### On the Harvesting of Fruit.

All vegetables and fruits gathered during a waxing moon, just like the meat of animals slaughtered at this time, possess greater nutritional value because they are then full of sap and blood <note>In the medieval worldview, plants were often described as having "blood" or vital fluids analogous to animals.</note>, compared to those gathered or slaughtered during a waning moon. An exception to this is made if they are to be stored for a long time; in that case, due to the contraction <note>original: "Zusammenziehung"</note> of the waning moon—by which the fruits themselves are also drawn together—it is better and more advantageous to harvest vegetables and fruit and slaughter livestock when the moon is waning, so that they may keep for longer.

### On the Harvesting of Grain.

Likewise, grain that is cut by reapers during a waxing moon yields more flour than if it were mown during a waning moon, because during the increasing moon it possesses its full vital power, which is somewhat more limited during the decreasing moon. On the other hand, if harvested during a waning moon, it can better preserve its strength than if it is cut during a waxing moon. Grain harvested during a waxing moon but cast into the earth for sowing takes root more quickly, shoots into stalks more rapidly, and produces more straw faster, but gives less yield than if it were cut during a waning moon.

### On the Time of Sowing.

What has been harvested during a waning moon and used for sowing germinates and grows more slowly, and produces fewer stalks, but yields a greater harvest of grain than if it had been cut during a waxing moon. In general, every type of seed that enters the earth during a waxing moon sprouts faster, grows more quickly, and—because it develops during the increasing moon—produces more greenery than if it were sown during a waning moon; for if sown at that time, it would germinate more slowly until it continues to grow with good strength.

### On Adam’s Sleep.

Before Adam had transgressed God’s commandment, a deep sleep was instilled in him and food was only shown to him <note>This refers to the theological idea that before the Fall, nourishment was spiritual or effortless, rather than a physical necessity for survival.</note>. After the Fall, however, his flesh became as weak and frail as the flesh of a dead man in its perishability is compared to that of a living human. From that point on, he was refreshed by sleep just as he was revitalized by food. This is now the case for all people. For just as a person’s flesh grows through food, so does his marrow <term>marrow (Mark): in medieval medicine, marrow was considered a vital substance that balanced the body's fluids and was replenished during rest.</term> grow through sleep.

### On Sleep.

For when a person sleeps, their marrow recovers and increases, and when they are awake, their marrow is somewhat thinned and weakened, just as the moon during its increase

<meta>This page continues the physiological explanation of human cycles, moving from the external influence of the moon to the internal mechanics of sleep and the restoration of bodily humors. It builds on the previous discussion of vital power by explaining how the soul manages the body's marrow during rest.</meta>

...grows and becomes smaller during the waning, just as the roots of plants in winter keep their life force within them, which they send out as blossoms in summer. Therefore, when a person's marrow <note>In this context, marrow refers not just to bone marrow but to the vital fatty substance throughout the body believed to be the seat of strength and reproduction.</note> is either tired from labor or exhausted by night vigils, the person is overcome by sleep and falls asleep easily, regardless of whether they are standing, sitting, or lying down, because their soul feels the needs of its body. For when the marrow has become thin and weak from staying awake at night, the soul’s powers soon draw a most pleasant and very sweet breath from the marrow, which permeates the vessels of the throat and the entire neck of the person, passes over to the temples, fills the vessels of the head, and thus weighs down the person’s blowing wind of life <note>original: "wehenden Lebenswind"; likely referring to the *pneuma* or vital spirit that animates the body</note>. 

Then the person is in such a state and lies there as if they were senseless, knowing nothing of themselves and having lost power over their body, possessing neither insight, nor thoughts, nor feeling. Only the soul alone allows the breath of life to go in and out, just as it does when the person is awake, because the soul holds the person together, whether sleeping or waking, and is within them when they sleep just as it is within them when they wake. 

Thus the person falls asleep, as has just been described. But then, after the person's soul has gathered its forces within itself, it allows the marrow to increase and strengthens it, makes the bones firm through this marrow, and lets the blood flow together; it also cooks the flesh <note>In medieval physiology, "cooking" or concoction refers to the metabolic process where heat transforms nutrients into blood and tissue</note>, unites the individual limbs, and—while the actual life remains hidden <margin>18)</margin>—increases understanding and knowledge in that person. Thus, the soul possesses more internal warmth when a person sleeps than when they are awake; for when a person is awake, their marrow becomes thin, slippery, and restless, which is why they eventually sleep. In sleep, however, the marrow warms up because it then increases, becomes fatty, and becomes quite bright and cheerful again.

### On the Nocturnal Discharge of Seed.

Therefore, this same marrow frequently excites the blood from its abundance during this warming process, leading to sexual desire; by sending the foam of the seminal emission <note>original: "Schaum des Samenergusses"; medieval medical theory often characterized semen as a frothy substance derived from the "cooking" of the blood or marrow</note> to the genitals without the person's knowledge, it causes it to be discharged. Furthermore, the marrow sometimes becomes heated through the excessive consumption of food and drink, because these immoderate meals and drinks assist the fire of the marrow, and their juice sets the marrow and blood in motion. 

Thus, the warming marrow stirs up fleshly lust in the blood and directs the foam of the seminal discharge to the genitals without the person's knowledge; by contrast, this does not occur through summer heat or through the warmth of a person’s clothing, or at least it happens only rarely. But because the body is then sleeping and doing nothing, the soul—which, while the body was awake, was occupied with it in many...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's activity during sleep. Having previously explained how the soul gathers its forces and allows the marrow to rest, it now describes how the soul, freed from the body’s immediate sensory demands, experiences visions and dreams.</meta>

...occupied with things, its knowledge—which it uses to work within the body, as if it were its eyes—plays in dreams and looks about, for it is no longer hindered by the body’s many activities.

## Concerning Adam and his Prophetic Gift.

When God cast sleep upon Adam, his soul meanwhile saw many things in a true prophetic state, because it was then still free from sin. In the same way, the soul of a sleeping person could see many things in true prophecy if that same person were not weighed down by sin.

## On Dreams.

Because the human soul originates from God, it nevertheless sometimes sees what is true and what is to come while the body sleeps; it knows what lies ahead for the person and what sometimes indeed comes to pass. However, it also often happens that the soul, weakened by the illusions of the devil or burdened by a clouding of the mind <term>clouding of the mind: a state where the rational faculties are obscured by physical illness or spiritual distress</term>, cannot see this clearly and falls victim to a deception. For often enough the soul is weighed down in dreams by the thoughts, opinions, and intentions that occupy the person while awake; in these dreams, the soul is sometimes puffed up like sourdough <note>Hildegard uses this domestic analogy to describe how even a small thought can expand and fill the consciousness during sleep.</note> that leavens a mass of flour, regardless of whether these are good or bad thoughts. If these thoughts are good and holy, God's grace often reveals the truth to the person in dreams. If, on the other hand, they are vain and the devil notices this, he often terrifies the soul of such a person and mingles his lies among the human thoughts. He even frequently makes holy people see shameful things with his mocking laughter. 

If a person falls asleep with their spirit preoccupied by inappropriate joy or sorrow, anger or fear, a lust for power, or other such motives, the devil frequently presents these to them in their dreams through his deceit, because he observed them while the person was awake. But even if a person falls asleep amidst lustful, fleshly thoughts, the devilish mockery sometimes holds these before them, showing them living figures—and sometimes even the bodies of the deceased with whom they once had relations or whom they have never even seen with their physical eyes—so that it seems to them as if they are taking pleasure with them in sinful lust and amidst seminal emissions <term>seminal emissions: nocturnal emissions, which were a subject of significant medical and moral concern in the Middle Ages</term>, just as if they were awake and as if those who have already died were still living, so that even their seed is struck by shame. 

Just as the devil plays his mad games with the person while they are awake through his deceit, he also allows them no rest in their dreams. Since the soul is closely bound to the body, it often consents to it—albeit unwillingly—in sleep and in waking, and excites all kinds of movements within it. Just as the air in the water [turns] the millwheel...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the soul's relationship with the body. The previous page concluded with an analogy of the soul as a driving force—similar to a mill—that remains active even while the person sleeps. Here, Hildegard further explores the soul's role as a source of light and life, regulating both breath and the impulses of the flesh.</meta>

...drives and allows the mill to grind, so the soul also stimulates the body of the sleeping and waking person to all sorts of deeds.

### Concerning the Activity of the Soul.

Just as the sun is the light of the day, so the soul is the light of the waking body; and just as the moon is the light of the night, so also is the soul the light of the sleeping body. For when the body of a sleeping person is in the proper state of warmth, such that their marrow <term>marrow: in Hildegard’s physiology, marrow is a vital substance that regulates internal heat and physical health</term> warms them in the correct proportion and measure, and when they are free from the onslaught of vices and moral conflict, they very often see what is true. This is because the knowledge of their soul is then at rest, just as the moon shines its light brightly and fully when it is free from the swirling of clouds and winds in the night. 

But if a storm of various and opposing thoughts dominates the mind and body of the waking person, and they fall asleep with this storm, then what they see in sleep is almost always false. This occurs because the knowledge of the soul is so darkened under such contradictions that it cannot see the truth—just as the moon cannot shine brightly in a storm of clouds. Because the soul is fire, it causes the breath to enter and exit with reduced strength in a sleeping person so that the body is not destroyed. In this way, a potter also keeps watch over his vessel when he has it in the fire, ensuring the fire is neither too hot nor too cold; for if it were excessively warm, the vessel would become fragile and be completely ruined.

### Concerning the Breath.

If a person did not have the breath entering and exiting, they would not possess physical movement, and their blood would not be fluid nor would it be able to flow, just as water does not flow without the pull of the air.

### Concerning the Opposition Between Soul and Flesh.

The soul is a breath <note>original: "Hauch"</note> that strives for the good, but the flesh inclines toward sin. Only rarely and with difficulty can the soul restrain the body so that it does not sin, just as the sun is unable to prevent little worms from crawling out of the earth in the very place that the sun itself warms with its brilliance and heat. 

The soul is also an inflation for the body, like a bellows for a fire; for just as a bellows fans the fire where wood and glowing coals are placed, so too is the soul closely bound to the body, the bones, the tendons, and the flesh. It is inclined toward every work and cannot leave it as long as it dwells in the body. For the marrow sits as firmly in the bones, the flesh, and all the limbs as a man who is nailed so tightly to a wooden trunk that he cannot tear himself away from it. But just as water sometimes extinguishes fire so that it does not burn too much, so also the...

<summary>This page describes the soul as the internal light and heat of the human being, comparing its clarity in dreams to the phases of the moon and its life-giving force to a potter's fire or a bellows.</summary>
<keywords>soul, breath, physiology, dreams, marrow, elements, fire, body and spirit, Hildegard of Bingen</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues Hildegard’s exploration of the physiology of sleep, focusing on how the soul acts as a guardian and restorer of the body. Building on the previous page's discussion of the soul as a vital fire, she now explains the role of "marrow" as a life-giving substance that must be replenished during rest.</meta>

The soul, with God's gracious assistance and under the guiding influence of reason, sometimes prevents the vices of sinners from taking hold, ensuring they do not rise up or grow beyond measure. Once the marrow <term>marrow: in Hildegard’s view, marrow is not just found in bones, but is a vital, moisture-rich substance that provides strength and heat to the body</term> of the sleeper has increased again and regained its strength, and the soul has restored the entire interconnected system of the sleeping body, the soul draws back to itself the gentle wind <note>original: "sanften Wind"; likely referring to the vital breath or spirit</note> which it had sent out from the marrow to grant the person rest; thus, the person wakes up. 

If, however, a person wakes frequently and falls back to sleep unexpectedly, the marrow does not return to its full abundance and power, and the limbs are not fully rested. Yet, for a person who wakes often but falls back to sleep even more quickly, the marrow and limbs are revitalized in an even more pleasant and gentle manner. This is similar to a small child who suckles often and stops often, gathering strength for its recovery in the intervals.

## Concerning the Stirring of the Soul.

It also frequently happens that a sleeping person lies uncomfortably or harshly on their side or another part of the body, or is pressed by something, or plagued by physical ailments. Because the human being possesses sensation, the soul—which feels these discomforts and sees that its own body is being harmed—gathers its strength, draws back the "wind" it sent out from the marrow, and in this way awakens the person from sleep. 

Furthermore, if noise and clamor or loud talking occur near a sleeper so that the air echoes, the external air strikes back against the air within the person, because the elements are also present within the human body. As soon as the soul within the person notices this movement in the air, it draws its powers back to itself and causes the person to wake. Often, however, it happens that someone is woken suddenly and against their will by a sudden noise, a touch, or some other surprise. In such cases, their vessels and their blood are stirred in a manner contrary to nature, which often causes them to feel pain in the body, or even to fall into a sharp fever or a three-day fever <term>three-day fever: also known as a tertian fever, a historical term for a fever that recurs every third day, often associated with malaria or systemic imbalance</term>, because the soul within has been agitated by this unexpected haste. 

Conversely, when a person wakes in the proper manner, they usually appear mentally sharper and more cheerful in their expression, because all their limbs have been joined back together in peace.

## Concerning Excessive Sleep.

If someone sleeps too much and for too long, they will easily be seized by all sorts of foul fevers and may also suffer a dimming of the eyes, because their eyes have been closed for longer than is proper during sleep. This is—

She warns that sudden disturbances to sleep or sleeping for excessive periods can disrupt the blood and humors, leading to physical illness and fever.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sleep’s restorative power, contrasting the benefits of moderate rest with the physical and mental toll of excessive wakefulness, while introducing the role of emotional and physical "storms" in causing insomnia.</meta>

...just as when someone stares for a long time into the bright sun and thereby causes a darkening of the eyes. But whoever sleeps with moderation will remain healthy. However, whoever stays awake for a long time and beyond measure falls into physical weakness, sometimes loses their strength thereafter, and even loses some of their mental clarity. The flesh around the eyes becomes painful, reddened, and swollen outward. Nevertheless, he does not greatly harm his visual acuity, the pupil, or his power of sight. But whoever stays awake only in moderation will maintain the health of his body.

It often happens, however, that a person lies awake and cannot sleep when his mind is preoccupied by all kinds of thoughts, questions, and adversities, or is pleasantly occupied by a great joy. For if sorrow, fear, anxiety, anger, or other conflicts and thoughts are at play, then the blood within him often falls into unrest. The vessels <term>vessels: referring to the veins and channels which Hildegard believed transported both blood and vital "winds" or spirits throughout the body</term> that should receive the mild breeze <note>original: "Windhauch"; Hildegard uses the concept of internal 'winds' to explain the movement of life forces and the onset of sleep</note> of sleep contract slightly, so that they cannot accept it. 

If someone has meanwhile seen, heard, or otherwise experienced something by which he could be exceptionally delighted, then his vessels are also turned toward joy; they cannot hold onto the mild wind of sleep because it does not have the proper warmth within it, and the person remains constantly awake until, satisfied in his mind with that matter, he returns to rest. Then his vessels return to their proper behavior, and the person sleeps. 

Also, if someone is ever oppressed by heavy physical suffering, the blood and the humors <term>humors: the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) which medieval medicine taught must be balanced for a person to be healthy</term> within him fall into a certain disorder and cause, so to speak, storms and tempests to arise within him. Due to these irregularities, he cannot sleep but remains, to the detriment of his health and against his will, sleepless and constantly awake. 

Furthermore, a person's vision behaves differently in sleep than when he is awake; therefore, upon waking from sleep, he cannot regain clarity of sight so quickly. Similarly, if he is in the dark, he cannot quickly regain the vision he had in the dark. Quite similarly, one who is in the light cannot easily adjust his vision to the dark without some delay, but only after a short stay—just as when a person lets his words be heard externally, while his thinking lies hidden within.

### Concerning Exercise.

If a physically healthy man walks about for a long time or stands in a straight posture for a long time, he does not suffer much harm from it, because although he is physically stimulated, he neither walks nor stands beyond measure. But whoever is weak should sit; for if he walked about or stood much, he would come to harm from it. The woman, on the other hand, should maintain moderation in walking and standing, because she is more fragile than the man. <note>Hildegard's medical views often emphasized the different physical "constitutions" or temperaments of men and women.</note>

...and also has a differently structured skull. It should sit more than run back and forth, so that it is not harmed by this. **On the Sanguine Woman.** <note>original: "blutreichen Weibe"; referring to the Sanguine temperament, dominated by the element of blood, traditionally associated with a warm and moist nature.</note> Some women are inclined to fullness; they have soft, pleasant flesh, thin vessels, and healthy blood free from corruption. Because their vessels <term>vessels: the veins and channels Hildegard believed carried blood and vital forces</term> are thin, they also have less blood in them, and their flesh grows all the more vigorously and is more strongly mixed with blood. Such women have a bright, white face, are accommodating in love, amiable, precise in artistic works, and restrain themselves in their spirit. During their monthly cleansing <note>original: "monatlichen Reinigung"; a common historical term for menstruation</note>, they lose only a little blood; their womb is strongly developed for childbearing. Therefore, they are fertile and can receive the male seed. Nevertheless, they do not bring particularly many children into the world, and if they live without a husband and therefore bear no children, they are prone to physical ailments. If they have husbands, however, they are healthy. If drops of blood are blocked in them before the natural time during the monthly flow so that they do not flow out, then they will sometimes become melancholic <note>original: "schwarzgallig"; literally "black-billed," referring to an excess of black bile, the humor associated with sadness or depression</note> or suffer from pains in the side, or a worm will grow in their flesh, or oozing glands, which are called scrofula <term>scrofula: a swelling of the lymph nodes, usually in the neck, historically known as the "king's evil"</term>, will break out in them, or a moderate form of leprosy will develop in them.

**On the Phlegmatic Woman.** <note>original: "phlegmareichen Frauen"; referring to the Phlegmatic temperament, dominated by the humor of phlegm, associated with a cool and moist nature.</note> But there are other women whose flesh does not grow much because they have thick vessels and fairly healthy blood of a light color, but containing a little phlegm, from which it gets its light color. Their facial expression is serious, their skin color somewhat dark; they are industrious and capable, and their temperament is somewhat masculine. At the time of the monthly flow, the out-flowing streams of blood run neither too strongly nor too weakly, but moderately. Because they have thick vessels, they are very fertile in their offspring and also conceive easily because their womb, as well as their other internal organs, are strongly built. They attract men to them, and therefore men love them. If they wish to abstain from intercourse with men, they can hold themselves back from union with them and will not be much affected by it, though a little. However, if they avoid union with men, they become intolerable and unpleasant in their nature. But if they have had relations with men, because they...

### On the Choleric Woman. <note>original: "gallereichen Frau"; literally "bile-rich woman," corresponding to the Choleric temperament</note>

Still other women have delicate flesh but coarse bones, moderately wide vessels, thick and red blood, and a pale complexion. They are clever and benevolent; they are held in awe by people and are also feared. During their monthly cleansing, they suffer from a heavy loss of blood; their womb is strongly developed, and they are fertile. Men love their nature but nevertheless feel a certain shyness toward them because such women attract men but do not hold them for the long term. If they are joined with men in marriage, they are chaste, preserving the loyalty of a wife, and are physically healthy when with them. If they remain unmarried, they will suffer pains in their bodies and will be weak, both because they do not know to which man they should keep their feminine loyalty and especially because they have no husband at all. If the monthly cleansing stops before the proper time, they easily become paralyzed and "melt away" in their humors <note>original: "zerfließen in ihren Säften"</note>, so that they become ill from these fluids—be it that they suffer in the liver, or easily fall ill with the black "dragon-swelling" <note>19) original: "schwarzen Drachengeschwulst"; a historical term for a hard, malignant tumor or carbuncle</note>, or that their breasts swell with cancer.

### On the Melancholic Woman. <note>original: "schwarzgalligen Weibe"; literally "black-bile woman," corresponding to the Melancholic temperament</note>

Yet other women have lean flesh, thick vessels, and moderately strong bones. Their blood is more phlegmatic <note>original: "schleimig"; referring to the humor of phlegm</note> than bloody, and their complexion is mixed with a blue-gray and black tone. Such women are fickle and unsteady in their thoughts, and also in a foul mood when they pine away due to an ailment. They have a nature that possesses little resilience and therefore sometimes suffer from melancholy <note>original: "Schwermut"</note>. During their monthly cleansing, they lose much blood and they are infertile because they have a weak and frail womb. Therefore, they can neither receive and retain the male seed nor warm it. They are consequently healthier, stronger, and more cheerful without husbands than with them, because they become weak after marital intercourse. Men, however, turn away from them and avoid them because they do not speak to men in a friendly manner, and...

...because they love men only a little. If they are seized by a carnal desire at any given hour, it quickly passes for them. However, it does happen that some of these women, when married to robust, full-blooded men and having reached a vigorous age—around fifty years—bring at least one child into the world. But if they were with husbands of a different sort, of a weak nature, they do not conceive from them but will remain infertile. If the monthly cleansing <note>original: "Monatsfluss"; menstruation</note> ceases for them earlier than is natural for women, they are sometimes afflicted by podagra <term>podagra: a painful form of gout, usually in the foot, which medieval medicine linked to trapped "waste" fluids</term> or develop swollen legs. They will also suffer from a head ailment caused by black bile <term>black bile: one of the four bodily humors; an excess was thought to cause melancholy, sluggishness, and various physical pains</term>, as well as pain in the back and kidneys. They can also swell up across their entire body in a short time because the discharge and impurity that should have been expelled from their body through the monthly cleansing are blocked and remain stuck within. If no help comes to them in their suffering state, so that they are not freed from it through God's help or medicine, they will soon die.

## On Baldness.

A person with a large and broad bald spot has a strong heat within them. This heat and the sweat of the head cast the hair out. However, the moisture of such a person’s breath is fertile and moistens the flesh where the beard grows, so that a great deal of hair grows there. If, on the other hand, a person has only sparse hair on the beard but plenty of hair on the crown of the head, then he is of a cold nature and not particularly fertile; when the breath touches the flesh around the mouth of this person, it makes the flesh infertile. But once the hair on a person’s head has fallen out, it cannot be restored by any means, because the moisture and the life force <note>original: "Lebenskraft"</note> that he previously had in the scalp—that is, in his skull—are already dried up, and therefore no growth can arise from there again; for this reason, the hair no longer grows anew. Thus, it happens very often that in those whose baldness is large and wide, the beard also becomes large and wide, and those who have thin and meager beard growth have all the more abundant hair on the crown.

## On Headaches.

Burning and daily fevers, as well as three-day <note>original: "dreitägiges Fieber"; tertian fever, often associated with malaria</note> and four-day <note>original: "viertägiges Fieber"; quartan fever</note> fevers, and even other fevers, sometimes act upon the black bile. This bile, so affected, sends a watery smoke up to the head and the brain of the person and causes them to suffer under a violent, persistent headache.

## On Migraines. <note>original: "halbseitigen Kopfschmerz"; literally "half-sided headache"</note>

Migraines also come from black bile and all those bad...

<meta>This page continues the medical discussion of human anatomy and health. The previous page ended while discussing how hair growth on the head and beard is influenced by internal heat and moisture. This page transitions into how the humors affect the head, specifically regarding migraines, dietary headaches, and mental disturbances.</meta>

...from the humors <term>humors: the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) believed in medieval medicine to determine health and temperament</term> that are within a human. It <note>referring to a specific type of headache</note> afflicts only half of the head, not the whole, so that it occupies now the right, now the left side of the head. For if the humors are present in excess, it seizes the right side; but if the black bile <term>black bile: the "melancholy" humor, thought to be cold and dry; an excess was linked to depression and chronic pain</term> has increased beyond measure, it seizes the left side of the head. This one-sided headache <note>original: "halbseitige Kopfschmerz"; likely referring to migraine</note> has such power in it that a person could not endure it if it were to attack the whole head at once. It is only with difficulty driven away, because that which internally restrains the black bile agitates the bad humors in the meantime, and that which brings the bad humors to rest allows the black bile to increase, thus making treatment difficult, because black bile and bad humors are only soothed simultaneously with great difficulty.

**Also concerning the headache.** A foodstuff that contains a moist juice—such as the juice of garden herbs or the juice of fruit—if consumed frequently and without the addition of dry bread, sometimes causes a person a headache. This, however, is soon stilled because it arises from only a weak humor. Frequently, too, phlegm <term>phlegm: the humor associated with cold and moisture; an excess was thought to cause sluggishness or respiratory issues</term> develops excessively in a person, rises up, and moves toward the head, shaking the vessels of the temples that strengthen the forehead, and in this way causes the forehead to become painful.

**Of Dizziness.** If a person, without the guiding influence of their superiors and without any necessity, busies themselves frequently with many different thoughts solely according to their own will, then they lead their humors away from the right path, so that they are at one time rash in their actions, and at another time sluggish and without proper order. Through this, the head of such a person is twisted into dizziness <note>original: "Schwindel"; vertigo</note>, so that their knowledge and their feeling disappear.

**Of Senselessness.** If the aforementioned pestilences <note>original: "Pestilenzen"; used here to mean severe medical afflictions or imbalances</note> meet all at once, so that they rage simultaneously in the head of the human, then they make the person senseless, throw them into confusion, and empty them of right insight, just as if a ship, tossed about by storms, were to break apart. Therefore, many believe that such a person is possessed by a demon. This is not the case, however; rather, the demons do indeed hasten to this pestilence and this pain and carry out their treachery—because madness belongs to their duties—but they have no free disposal over their words in this, because such a person is not possessed by a demon. For if once a demon is allowed to speak at will in a human by divine decree, he exploits his advantage with words and fits of rage in place of the Holy Spirit until God drives him out again, just as He drove him out of heaven.

<meta>This page continues the medical discussion of human anatomy and health. While the previous page focused on external symptoms of the head like hair growth and the specific nature of migraines (associated with the humors and black bile), this page moves inward to the physiology of the brain and the celestial analogies of the eyes.</meta>

### On the Brain.

The brain is touched by the good and bad juices <term>juices: also known as humors; the bodily fluids believed to control health and personality</term> that are within a person, and it is therefore always soft and moist. If it happens to become dry, it becomes ill in a short time. For it is by nature very moist and fatty, and it is also the material for knowledge, wisdom, and reason, in such a way that it keeps these in motion through sending out and taking in, while also keeping the power of thoughts within bounds. Since, however, the thoughts have their place in the heart <note>Medieval medicine often viewed the heart as the seat of the soul and emotions, while the brain acted as a processor or regulator</note>, they are either sweet or bitter. Sweetness makes the brain fat <note>In this context, "fat" implies being well-nourished and healthy</note>, and bitterness empties it. The brain has its own passages, like the vents of a smokehouse through which smoke is led away. These passages lie in the eyes, the ears, the mouth, and the nose, and they become recognizable there. If pleasant thoughts are present, then a joyful mood is expressed through the person's gaze, the ears, and speech. But if bitterness is in them, then the eyes make this known through tears, and the hearing and speech through anger and sorrow. The eyes of a person are created to be similar to the firmament <term>firmament: the vault of the heavens; the sky</term>.

### On the Eyes.

Specifically, the pupil of the eye has a similarity to the sun, the black or gray coloring around the pupil compares to the moon, and the white on the outside to the clouds. The eye consists of fire and water. It is held together and strengthened by fire so that it can endure; water, on the other hand, is directed toward seeing. If blood gains the upper hand on the surface of a person's eye, it suffocates the eye's power of vision because it dries out the water that grants the eye its sight. On the other hand, if the blood there has been excessively reduced, the water that should serve for seeing in the eye then lacks sufficient strength because it is missing its support, since it should carry its powers in the blood like a pillar. Therefore, the eyes of old people become weak-sighted because these elements decrease in them, and the water and blood become less in them. In contrast, young people see more sharply than old people because the correct proportion between blood and water is still present in their vessels. In them, fire and water have not yet dried out or diminished the heat and cold beyond measure.

### On Gray Eyes.

A person who possesses gray eyes similar to water <note>original: "graue, dem Wasser ähnliche Augen"; this likely refers to blue or light-colored eyes, which were categorized by their "watery" quality in medieval physiognomy</note> draws these primarily from the air. Therefore, they are weaker than other eyes because the air often changes as a result of the various movements caused by heat, cold, and moisture, and such eyes are easily [affected] by bad, soft, and moist air, as well as by fog...

<summary>This page describes the brain as a moist organ of reason regulated by the heart's emotions and explains vision through a celestial analogy where the eye mirrors the sun, moon, and clouds.</summary>
<keywords>brain, humors, anatomy, eyes, vision, firmament, aging, physiology, gray eyes</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medieval physiological analysis of the human head, specifically transitioning from the general structure of the brain and eyes into a detailed classification of eye types based on the four elements and natural phenomena.</meta>

...be harmed. For just as these impair the purity of the air, they also harm the eyes that have acquired their nature from the air.

### On Fiery Eyes.

He who has fiery eyes, comparable to a dark cloud next to the sun, has naturally received them from the warm south wind. They are healthy because they originate from the heat of fire. Dust, however, and any foul odor harms them, because their brightness does not take heed of dust and their purity does not take heed of unknown odors <note>Hildegard suggests that because these eyes are "pure fire," they lack a protective "filter" against physical pollutants like dust</note>.

### On Eyes of Changing Color.

He who has eyes similar to a cloud in which the rainbow shines has received them from the air of various currents, which are neither uniformly dry nor moist. They are weak because they arise from unstable air, and because they do not originate from fire, they have darkened vision in warm air; in clear rain-air, however, they see sharply, because they are more of a moist than a fiery nature. All particularly bright light, whether from the sun, the moon, from lamps, or from the brilliance of gemstones and metals or elsewhere, is harmful to such eyes because they come from the air with its changing currents <gloss>20) original citation: likely referencing a specific manuscript source or chapter</gloss>.

### On Murky Eyes.

He who has eyes resembling a murky cloud, which is neither entirely fire-colored nor entirely dull, but rather somewhat greenish-blue, has received them from the discolored moisture of the earth, which produces the manifold harms originating from herbs and earthworms <note>Hildegard often associates greenish or "murky" colors with the base dampness of the earth and the decomposition processes found there</note>. These eyes have a gentle expression and allow red flesh to stand out, because they have emerged from phlegm <term>phlegm: one of the four bodily humors, associated with cold, moisture, and sluggishness</term>. They are, however, not attacked by moist air, nor by dust, foul stench, or the bright glow of any object—under which, as already mentioned, the visual acuity of other eyes suffers—even though they sometimes suffer from certain other diseases. For just as nothing stands in the way of weeds and worms arising from discolored earth moisture, just as little is the visual acuity of such eyes impaired by the aforementioned harmful influences.

### On Black Eyes.

He who has black or dark, murky eyes, like a cloud sometimes is, has received them primarily from the earth. They are stronger and see more sharply than other eyes and retain their sharpness for a long time because they originate from the power of the earth. They are, however, easily attacked by the moisture of the earth and the dampness of waters and swamps, just as the earth is poisoned by harmful moisture and the great dampness of waters and swamps.

<summary>This page categorizes different human eye types (fiery, changing/multi-colored, murky, and black) based on their elemental origins in the winds, air, and earth. It details how each type responds to environmental factors like light, dust, and moisture according to medieval humoral theory.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Hildegard’s exploration of sensory organs and their ailments, moving from the elemental nature of the eyes to specific pathological conditions like cataracts, tearing, hearing loss, and dental pain, all framed within the medieval theory of the four humors.</meta>

### On the White Skin of the Eyes.

If the human brain becomes excessively fatty in any way, this fatness sends harmful juices and sweat toward the eyes. If these juices and sweat repeatedly dampen and soak the eyes, they develop a white skin <note>likely referring to a cataract or a corneal leucoma</note> due to the thick consistency of the juice and sweat. If this is not removed immediately while it is still fresh, it thickens to such a degree that later, because of its thickness—which equals that of the gallbladder—it can no longer be eliminated. This is how the white skin on the human eye arises. This same white skin also develops from the coldness of the humors and from black bile <term>black bile: "melancholia," one of the four vital humors, associated with the element of earth and believed to cause cold, dry diseases</term>.

### On the Tearing of the Eyes.

If too much water has accumulated in a person’s eyes through harmful juices, this water smothers the fire <note>Hildegard believed that vision was powered by an internal "fiery" energy within the eye</note> that is within the eyes and swallows it. Because of this, the eyes become wet as if they were shedding tears, and they begin to become weak-sighted.

### On Hearing.

If a person’s stomach becomes ill, a phlegm <term>phlegm: a cold, moist humor believed to originate in the stomach or brain, causing congestion</term> sometimes spreads from it up into the head, attacks the ears, and impairs the person's hearing. This phlegm sometimes decreases and at other times increases again; it can be easily treated and removed. Additionally, bad juices within a person produce a smoke and send this smoke up to the head and the brain. This smoke then also moves toward the ears and clogs the hearing; it remains there for a long time and is difficult to treat.

### On Toothache.

Extremely fine vessels surround the thin skin or membrane <note>likely the meninges, the protective layers of the brain</note> in which the brain lies, and they spread out to the gums and the teeth themselves. If these vessels are filled with bad, excessive, and putrid blood, and if they are contaminated by the foam that occurs during the "cleansing" of the brain, they carry the putrid matter along with the pain from the brain to the gums and into the teeth themselves. Because of this, the flesh surrounding the teeth and also the cheeks swell up, and the person suffers pain in the gums. Furthermore, if a person does not frequently clean their teeth by rinsing with water, a slime sometimes grows in the flesh around the teeth and accumulates. Through this, the flesh becomes diseased, and from the slime that has grown old around the teeth, worms <note>the "tooth worm" was a standard medieval explanation for dental caries, believed to bore into the teeth to cause decay</note> sometimes develop within the teeth; thus the gums swell and the person has pain.

### On Redness of the Face.

When someone, bedridden due to illness, appears red in the face, he has through diseased in-

<meta>This page continues Hildegard’s discussion of how internal humors affect physical appearance and organ health, transitioning from the eyes and ears on the previous page to the intestines, spleen, heart, and the metaphorical structure of the human "house" for the soul.</meta>

...intestines <note>the text begins mid-word with "geweide," likely continuing from "Eingeweide" on the previous page</note> contain sick and poisonous blood, which results in a red face. Specifically, a bad humor <note>original: "schlechter Saft"</note> escapes from the vessels, enters the flesh, and permeates it so that the flesh becomes bloated and swells as if it were pierced by very fine holes. Such a person is not sad, however, but quite cheerful and does not burden those around them during their illness.

### On Facial Paleness.
If a person becomes pale and thin during their illness, black bile <term>black bile: "melancholia," one of the four vital humors, believed to be cold and dry and responsible for depression and certain physical wasting</term> is combining with a state of numbness <note>original: "Lähmung," which can mean paralysis or a general deadening of sensation</note>, causing them to become cold. Because of this coldness, their face turns pale, and their flesh does not thrive or grow. Such a person is sad during their illness and is easily provoked to anger 21).

### On Spleen Swelling.
The stomach provides moisture to the human intestines, while the bladder supplies them with watery fluid. Consequently, the intestines allow food to flow back and forth constantly; in this process, they become fatty and contain various types of phlegm <note>original: "Schleime"</note>. However, if the stomach is irritated by various harmful foods and the bladder is weakened by various harmful drinks, both bring bad humors to the intestines and send a foul smoke to the spleen.

### On Heart Pain.
The spleen is thereby puffed up; it swells and becomes sore. Through its swelling and pain, it also makes the heart ache and causes mucus to appear around it. Yet, the heart is still strong and offers resistance to this sensation of pain. But if the previously mentioned humors in the intestines and the spleen have gained the upper hand and brought great distress to the heart, they return to the black bile and mix with it. Provoked by this, the black bile rises indignantly along with the humors, ascending to the heart with a black, foul smoke, exhausting it through numerous and sudden afflictions. Consequently, such people—rendered sad and morose—take in very little food and drink, so that they waste away and can sometimes hardly keep themselves upright. They also suffer from frequent belching <note>original: "Aufstoßen"</note>.

### On the Seat of the Soul.
When a person builds a house, they provide it with a door, windows, and a chimney, so that they can go in and out through the door to receive all necessities, and receive light through the windows...

<summary>This page describes how imbalances in the humors, particularly black bile and "foul smoke," cause facial redness or paleness and lead to swelling of the spleen and distress of the heart. Hildegard concludes with a structural metaphor comparing the human body to a house built for the soul.</summary>

--- Page 100 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of how internal vapors and humors interact with the organs. It completes the metaphor started on the previous page regarding the heart's relationship to the rest of the body, specifically how the "smoke" of the internal fire must be vented through the brain.</meta>

...comes and, when a fire is lit, the smoke can escape through the chimney so that the house does not suffer under the smoke. In the same way, the soul dwells in the heart as if in a house. It allows thoughts to enter and leave as if through a door, observes them as if through windows, and directs its powers—as if from a lit fire—over to the brain as if to a chimney, so that the brain may test them there and separate them from one another. Indeed, if a human had no thoughts, they would have no knowledge, but would be like a house that has neither a door, nor windows, nor a chimney.

Thoughts are the originators of the knowledge of good and evil and the organizers of all things; this is why they are called "thoughts." Thoughts are the source of goodness, wisdom, folly, and similar things, just as bad thoughts also emerge from the heart—and that is the door. Furthermore, a path leads from the heart to the elements, through which a person carries out what they think. The power of these thoughts rises to the brain, and the brain holds them fast, because the brain provides the moisture for the entire body, just as dew moistens everything. However, if bad and foul-smelling humors <term>humors: "Säfte," the vital fluids of the body believed to govern health and temperament in medieval medicine</term> arise within a person, they send a kind of harmful smoke to the brain.

### On Lung Pain.

The brain, once agitated in this way, directs this smoke through certain vessels to the lungs and infects them, so that they become quite swollen and release the breath only with difficulty, giving it a foul odor. However, this swelling of the lungs is not very dangerous, as it can be easily treated.

### On Asthma.

There are also people whose heads are so healthy and strong that the aforementioned bad humors cannot harm their brain, because they cannot reach it. And because these humors cannot rise to the brain, they become trapped in the windpipe <note>original: "Luftröhre"</note> of these people and make them ill, so that such people can only breathe out with great effort. When the humors are stuck in such a person's throat, they infect the lungs with impurities and putrid discharge <note>original: "Jauche," usually referring to foul liquid or pus</note> and make them sore, just as a person's eyes are sometimes damaged by the pain of discharging pus and sores. Consequently, the lungs rise up toward the throat and block the passage of breath, so that the person can hardly exhale.

### On Coughing.

Because putrid matter lies around the lungs, such a person expels much foul phlegm <term>phlegm: "Phlegma," one of the four humors; here specifically referring to respiratory mucus</term>. Otherwise, they would soon perish, because this illness is sometimes dangerous.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of how a person’s health and temperament are determined by the conditions of their conception—specifically weather and lunar phases—and how these conditions influence the internal "humors" and organs like the liver and lungs.</meta>

## On Foul-Smelling Breath During Foggy Weather

There are other people who are conceived during foggy and moist weather and therefore constantly have foul-smelling and offensive breath, as well as unpleasant-smelling sweat. Their foul breath and the bad humors <term>humors: "Säfte," the vital fluids of the body believed to govern health and temperament in medieval medicine</term> within them reach their brain and weigh it down so much that they often fall into a kind of forgetfulness of themselves. Those who expel the phlegm <term>phlegm: "phlegma," one of the four traditional humors, associated here with waste fluids that must be cleared from the head</term> from the head suffer less because their brain is being cleansed. However, if they do not expel the phlegm, they are more severely tormented by headaches because their brain is not freed from the harmful humors. The foul-smelling breath of these people further reaches the lungs and makes them weary, so that they sometimes have a hoarse voice. However, this ailment is not very dangerous because it can be remedied in a short time.

## On Conception During the Full Moon

Some people, who are conceived during the full moon and in moderately warm air that is neither too hot nor too cold, are healthy and greedy when consuming food, so that they take in various foods without distinction. Although they consume and are able to consume various foods without selection, they should nevertheless abstain from certain harmful foods, like a hunter who lets useless game go but catches the useful kind.

## On the Liver’s Poor Digestion

If one of such people has taken in various foods immoderately and without selection, then their liver is damaged and hardened by the manifold humors of these foods, so that its healing juice—which it should send like an ointment into all limbs, joints, and intestines—is corrupted by the various harmful humors. Sometimes, this even causes the flesh of such a person to transform into a swelling <note>original: "Geschwulst," likely referring to a tumor, abscess, or localized edema</note> or to split somewhere on the limbs, or some limb is so damaged that he limps upon it. Nevertheless, such people can live a long time with such an ailment.

## On Conception and the Distress of the Liver

There are also other people who are conceived during the waning moon and under the turbulence of a changing air current. Some of them are always sad and have an unsteady nature. As a result of their sadness, their liver is weakened and pierced by numerous very small holes, like a cheese that has many tiny holes. Therefore, such people do not eat much and are not eager for food and drink, but eat and drink moderately. Because they eat and drink so little, their liver becomes decayed like a sponge and wastes away.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of liver health, moving from the previous page's mention of liver damage and hardening to a broader explanation of the liver's role as a central distribution vessel for the body's humors.</meta>

### On the Liver as a Vessel
In humans, the liver behaves much like a vessel into which the heart, lungs, and stomach pour their juices <term>juices: "Säfte," referring to the bodily humors or fluids believed to sustain life</term>. The liver, in turn, allows these juices to flow back to all the limbs, just as when a vessel is placed at a spring: it receives water from the source and then lets it flow out elsewhere. However, if the liver is porous and decayed <note>original: "löcherig und morsch"</note>, as was shown above, it is unable to receive the good humors from the heart, lungs, and stomach. These juices and fluids then return to the heart, lungs, and stomach, creating a kind of flooding <note>In medieval medicine, this "flooding" refers to a harmful accumulation or congestion of fluids</note>. Once this ailment has increased within a person, they cannot live for long.

### On Conception when the Sun is in the Sign of Cancer
There are other people who are conceived when the sun is in the sign of Cancer, has reached its highest point, and maintains the temperature of the air in the correct proportion; these individuals have healthy livers.

### On Heartache
Because these people have a healthy liver, they occasionally feel a weakness in the heart. This happens because the bad humors, fleeing from the healthy liver, strive toward the spleen and the heart and afflict them, since they cannot overcome the liver itself. Furthermore, if a person has eaten raw apples, raw pears, raw vegetables, or other uncooked foods that were prepared neither over a fire nor with any spices, these cannot be easily "cooked" <term>cooked: "fertiggekocht," a reference to the medieval belief that digestion was a process of "cooking" or heat-processing food within the stomach</term> in the stomach, because they were not properly prepared beforehand.

### On Spleen Pain
Thus, the bad humors from foods that ought to have been prepared over a fire or seasoned with some condiment—such as salt or vinegar—to be "shocked" <note>23) original: "geschreckt," likely referring to a culinary technique of quickly searing or blanching to neutralize "raw" qualities</note>, but were not so prepared, rise to the spleen and transform it into a painful swelling. Because the spleen is naturally moist and must be kept damp by the humors, it absorbs both bad and good juices. Therefore, when the aforementioned bad humors rise, they ascend to the spleen, damaging it and making it painful.

### On the Stomach and its Digestive Disorder
The stomach is created in the human body for the purpose of receiving and digesting all food. It is tough and somewhat wrinkled on the inside so that it can retain food for the purpose of digestion, preventing it from slipping away too quickly toward the bowels. This is similar to how a mason roughens stones so that they will accept and hold the mortar, preventing it from flowing apart and falling to the ground. However, when some people occasionally consume any kind of food in excessive quantities...

It also details how "raw" or unseasoned foods create bad humors that cause pain in the heart and spleen when the stomach cannot process them.</summary>

...[eaten raw apples, pears, or vegetables that they] **have**, that is to say: raw and uncooked or half-cooked and especially and exceptionally fatty and heavy, or even juice-less and dry, then sometimes the heart, the liver, and the lungs and the other warmth that is within a human cannot assist the stomach with enough strong fire for these foods to be fully cooked <term>fully cooked: "gargekocht," referring to the medieval belief that the stomach acted as an internal oven to "cook" or digest food</term>. Therefore, they congeal in the stomach, harden, and become moldy, so that they sometimes turn the stomach somewhat green or blue-green or even lead-colored, or cause it to develop much mucus—that is, *slim* <note>original: "slim," an archaic term for slime or phlegm</note>. Sometimes, like a rotting manure pile, they send bad juices <term>juices: "Säfte," the bodily humors</term> and harmful, foul-smelling vapors through the entire body and also, just as when green or wet wood burns, they spread an evil smoke everywhere in the body. That certain foods harden within a person comes from various morbid conditions; for when too much perverted heat <note>original: "verkehrte Glut," referring to an unnatural or feverish internal heat</note> exists in a person, it burns the consumed nourishment through and through, or, if there is too much perverted cold in them, the consumed food cannot be broken down, but instead clumps and congeals due to the cold and remains in the person, causing them to suffer pain.

## On the Stretching and Tearing of the Peritoneum

There are, however, people who have soft flesh on their bodies, whether they be thin or fat. In these individuals, the inner, delicate skin which holds the intestines together <note>The "Bauchfell" or peritoneum</note> is fatty and thin, so that it is easily torn either as a result of certain illnesses or through exertion, a fall, or the expansion of the stomach when it is filled with food. In women, however, this skin is thicker and tougher than in men because of childbirth, and therefore it is torn more often and more easily in men than in women.

## On the Kidneys and Why There Are Two of Them

The kidneys form the firmament <note>Hildegard uses "firmament" here to mean a foundational structure or support</note> and the storehouse for the warmth of the human body and hold the loins of the human together like armed soldiers defending their lord. They exist as a pair so that they can retain the fire within themselves all the more powerfully and purposefully. They do this in the woman as in the man, because they are located near the male loins and are also connected to the female womb. They are encased in fat so that they are not harmed by any cold or adversity and may retain their strength. Within the kidneys themselves lie very strong vessels which hold them firmly and through which the entire human body is supported. If, however, a person feels pain in them, this stems from the weakness of the stomach.

<meta>This page continues the medieval medical exploration of internal pathology, transitioning from the general digestion of food to specific ailments of the abdomen and the male reproductive system. ## On Abdominal Pain

For abdominal pain arises from stomach pain, and from pain in the side, pain in the intestines comes forth. If the stomach suffers from harmful and poor foods, so that heavy and bad foods cannot be digested <term>digested: literally "cooked" (gekocht), as medieval medicine viewed the stomach as an internal oven</term> within it, a sensation of pain—like a smoke or a mist—draws out from it into the side, much like the stinging smoke that rises from fresh wood. This smoke, originating from the stomach, moves further toward the intestines like a dark cloud. The intestines receive this smoke because, by constant habit, it always strives toward them, just as the smoke from logs rushes toward a chimney. Thus, every weakness and every sensation of pain in the stomach frequently descends to its accustomed place in the intestines due to this foul, persistent habit, causing the person to feel pain there.

## On the Male Genitals

Regarding the virility <note>original: "Manneskraft," the vital power of a man</note> that resides in the loins of men—or rather, regarding the wind <note>original: "Wind," likely referring to the *pneuma* or vital spirit believed to cause erections</note> that proceeds from the marrow—there are two interconnected powers, like two vessels <note>likely referring to the testicles</note>, which manifest the heat that is within the man and also powerfully contain the fire for the trunk <term>trunk: "Stamm," a euphemism for the penis</term>. They are surrounded by a thin skin so that their potency, which helps them to raise the trunk, suffers no loss. If a man happens to lack these two powers due to a congenital defect or because he was castrated, he possesses neither virility nor the "male wind" that raises the trunk to its strength. Then the trunk, which is meant to plow the woman like a field <note>a common medieval metaphor for procreation</note>, cannot be raised because it is empty of the wind of its powers, which should strengthen it for the path to generating offspring—just as a plow cannot dig up the earth if it lacks the plowshare.

## On the Swelling of the Scrotum

Although the male power in the genitals is great, sometimes an abhorrent moisture, an ulcer, or a swelling appears on the male genitals due to bad humors <term>humors: "Säfte," the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were believed to govern health</term>, harmful sweat, or even from unbridled sexual indulgence, so that the genitals are swollen or damaged by foul sores.

## On the Involuntary Dripping of Urine <note>original: "Harnträufeln," likely referring to incontinence or strangury</note>

In a person who cannot hold their urine, the stomach and bladder are cold; therefore, the drink within them cannot be sufficiently cooked <term>cooked: "gekocht," meaning processed or refined by the body's internal heat</term>. Before it is cooked within them, it flows away like lukewarm water—much as when water is placed on a fire and begins to warm, it flows over the top before it reaches a boil. Likewise...

<meta>This page continues the medical discourse from page 98, which discussed digestion as an "internal cooking" process. Having previously addressed abdominal pain and the male reproductive system, the text now transitions to urinary control in children, the causes of gout and fistulas, and the origins of menstruation, grounding all these conditions in the medieval theory of "humors" or bodily juices.</meta>

...it is the same for small children, who cannot hold their urine because neither their stomach nor their bladder possesses its full warmth; instead, they are cold <note>Medieval medicine taught that "heat" was necessary for the proper function and "closure" of organs; children were thought to be naturally more "moist and cold" than adults</note>.

### Regarding Gout <term>Podagra: a historical term for gout, specifically when it affects the foot</term>.
Whoever has soft, indulgent flesh on their body and frequently eats all sorts of delicacies will easily be afflicted by gout. If someone has enjoyed two good and healthy dishes and eats a third that is harmful and poorly nourishing, the two good and healthy dishes will overcome the third harmful one, so that it causes less damage to the person who ate it. However, if someone consumes two bad, unsuitable foods and simultaneously a third good and wholesome dish, the two bad and unfitting foods will overcome the third good and healthy one, and only rarely allow it to develop toward the person’s well-being. It often happens to people who eat many different things all at once that they easily become ill afterward.

When people with soft, indulgent flesh consume an excess of all kinds of delicious foods, the "bad juices" <term>bad juices: or "evil humors," fluids believed to cause disease when out of balance</term> gain the upper hand within them; they overflow and multiply, so that it becomes impossible to stop them from flowing chaotically here and there within such a person. Finally, they descend into the lower parts of the body and begin to rage in the thighs and feet. And because they have no way out and cannot rise back to the upper parts of the body from whence they came, they remain in the lower limbs, are transformed into phlegm <note>original: "Schleim," one of the four humors, often associated with coldness and blockage</note>, and harden. Then such a person feels the gout in their legs and feet and suffers pain, so that they can hardly walk.

Similarly, in women with soft, indulgent flesh who likewise enjoy excessively many delicious foods, the bad juices multiply as described above, but they do not lead to gout as easily. The bad juices are released during the monthly cleansing <note>menstruation</note>, and thus such women are freed from gout.

### Regarding the Fistula.
The fistula is also a kind of gout. A fistula arises from harmful and excessive juices because, when these exceed a certain amount in a person, they move to some part of the body or descend to the legs or feet. There, by the sharpness of their excess, they pierce the skin and slowly flow out. And because they are constantly increasing in the person, the persistent flowing does not allow the skin to heal.

### Whence comes the Monthly Flow?
When the flow of desire had entered into Eve, all her vessels...

<meta>This page continues the medical and theological discussion of the female body, moving from general humors to the specific mechanics of menstruation and its origins in the Fall of Man. opened to the flow of blood. Therefore, every woman experiences within herself turbulent processes in her blood, so that—much like the waxing and waning of the moon—she retains the drops of her blood and then pours them out, and all her limbs, which are joined by blood vessels, open up. For just as the moon increases and decreases, a woman’s blood and juices <term>juices: or "humors," the bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that medieval doctors believed had to stay balanced for health</term> are purified during the time of her monthly flow. Otherwise, she would not be able to remain alive, because she is richer in moisture than a man, and she would fall into severe illness. 

Modesty serves as a seal for a virgin’s purity, because in her ignorance of the male act, she has not concerned herself with it. Therefore, the monthly blood of a virgin is also richer in blood <note>original: "reicher an Blut"; the author implies the blood is more concentrated or "pure" because it has not yet been "thinned" by sexual activity</note> than that of a woman, because the virgin is still "closed." If a virgin’s constitution is weakened, she has more phlegm <note>phlegm was considered a cold, moist humor that increased when the body's natural heat or "seal" was compromised</note> in her monthly blood than she had before, when she was still a virgin, because she has been weakened. 

As long as a girl remains in virginal purity, the monthly blood exits the vessels like individual drops. After the loss of virginity, however, the drops flow like a small stream, because through the act of the man, the vessels are opened; they are like a stream because the vessels were opened during that act. If the seal of virginity in a maiden is destroyed, this destruction causes a bleeding. For a woman is so created that she is meant to receive and hold the male seed with her blood. For this reason, a woman is also weak and cold, and her juices lack strength. She would also suffer constantly from illness if the blood were not purified through the monthly flow, just as food in a pot is purified when it casts off its foam <note>This "cooking pot" metaphor was common in medieval science to explain how the body's internal heat processed nutrients and expelled waste</note>.

### Of the Seduction of Eve.

All the vessels of a woman would have remained intact and healthy if Eve had remained in Paradise for all time. But when she looked upon the serpent with consent, her vision—with which she had beheld heavenly things—was extinguished; and when she listened to the serpent in agreement, her hearing—with which she had perceived heavenly things—was closed; and by eating the apple, the radiance that had shone through her until then was darkened. 

As the sap of a tree begins at the root and strives upward into all the smallest branches of the tree, so it is also with a woman at the time of her monthly flow. For during this time of the blood's flow, the vessels that hold the brain together and support the sight and hearing are stimulated for the outflow of blood; and the vessels that hold the neck, the back, and the kidneys also draw to themselves the vessels of the liver, the intestines, and the navel, and every single vessel pours itself into one...

<summary>This page explains the physiological necessity of menstruation as a cleansing process tied to lunar cycles and describes how the loss of virginity and the original sin of Eve altered the female body's internal structure.</summary>
<keywords>menstruation, virginity, humors, lunar cycles, Eve, Original Sin, medieval anatomy, female physiology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the medical analysis of the female body’s cycles, transitioning from the description of how blood vessels and "limbs" (internal organs) open during menstruation to the mechanics of the kidneys and the specific biological process of conception. It maintains the previous page's comparison between human biology and the natural world, particularly the seasons and the behavior of trees.</meta>

...another thing, just as the life-force of a tree causes the branches to turn green. The vessels that hold the kidneys loosen the joint <note>joint: likely referring to the pelvic region or the ligaments supporting the kidneys and reproductive organs</note> in which the kidneys are joined together, pulling it tight and letting it go again, just as the severed claws of a bird curve and stretch again when the tendons are pulled.

## What causes the monthly flow?

Just as a strong wind stirs up a storm on a river, a stormy movement is also produced in all the fluids <term>fluids: the "humors" or bodily liquids that medieval science believed governed health and temperament</term> of a woman, so that these fluids mix with the blood, becoming somewhat bloody themselves and thus being purified along with the blood. In this way, the flow of blood in a woman comes about. Therefore, during this time, a woman’s head becomes sick, her eyes grow dull, and her whole body becomes weak. However, the eyes do not grow weaker if the monthly flow comes out at the right time and in the correct proportion. Before this discharge begins, the parts of the body intended for receiving the male seed open up, so that they now conceive more easily than at other times. Similarly, women conceive easily when the monthly flow is ending or already subsiding, because their limbs <note>limbs: "Gliedmaßen" in the original; here used to refer to the internal reproductive organs or the cervix</note> are still open. At other times, however, they do not conceive so easily because their limbs are then quite firmly closed, as is the case with a tree that allows its life-force <term>life-force: "Lebenskraft," related to the medieval concept of *viriditas*, the greening power of nature</term> to flow out in the summer to form blossoms, but in winter draws it back into its interior.

## On Conception.

When the woman is in union with the man, the heat in her brain, which carries the sensation of pleasure within it, announces the taste of this pleasure beforehand during the union, as well as the discharge of the seed. Once the seed has fallen into its place, the aforementioned very strong heat of the brain attracts it and holds it fast. Almost at the same time, the woman's kidneys also contract, and all the parts that stand ready to open during the monthly flow close at that same time as firmly as if a strong man were gripping an object tightly in his hand. Then the monthly blood mixes with the seed, making it bloody and allowing it to become flesh. After it has become flesh, that same blood surrounds it with a vessel <note>vessel: the amniotic sac or the developing placenta</note>, just like a worm that builds a dwelling for itself out of its own body. The blood continues to work on this vessel day by day until a human is formed within it and that human receives the breath of life. Then it grows along with the human and becomes so firmly situated that it cannot move from its place until the birth of this human.
