<meta>This page continues the discussion of female physiology and the cycles of life, moving from the mechanical and fluid-based causes of menstruation on the previous page to the spiritual and biological origins of womanhood, conception, and childbirth. It maintains the previous page's recurring analogy between the human body and the natural world, specifically trees and the elements.</meta>

### About Eve.

The first mother of the human race was created similar to the ether <term>ether: in medieval science, the pure, unchanging substance that fills the heavens</term>, because just as the ether holds the stars unchanged, so she also carried the human race within her in constant purity and virginity without burden when it was said to her: "Increase and multiply!" And this is now fulfilled in full pain <note>This refers to the "Fall of Man," contrasting Eve's original state with the post-Eden reality of painful childbirth</note>.

### About Conception.

For now, a woman resembles the earth furrowed by the plow; she receives the male seed, envelops it with her blood, and warms it with her own heat. Thus the seed continues to develop until the breath of life <term>breath of life: the "soul" or vital spirit believed to be granted by God during pregnancy</term> is sent into it and the time of ripeness has come when it is to emerge.

### About Birth.

But when the child is to emerge from the woman, such a terror and such a trembling comes over her that every woman shudders in this terror, her vessels shed excessive blood, all the connections of her limbs <note>limbs: likely referring to the pelvic joints and ligaments that must widen for birth</note> are injured and loosen amid tears and cries of woe, as it is said: "With pain you shall give birth!"—that is: under such pain with which the earth will be transformed at the end of time. All women have blood more infused with phlegm <note>original: "Schleim"; refers to one of the four humors believed to govern the body</note> than men, because they are open like the wood upon which the strings are stretched for playing the zither, and because they are windowed <note>windowed: a medieval concept that women were more porous and susceptible to the "winds" and environmental elements</note> and airy; therefore, the elements are also more active in them than in men, and their humors exceed those of men in quantity.

### About Fertility.

The monthly flow in a woman is the expression of the generative life-force and its bloom, which puts forth leaves in the offspring. Just as a tree blooms and turns green and brings forth fruit from its life-force, so also does the woman bring forth blossoms and leaves in the fruit of her womb from the life-force of the streams of the monthly blood. But just as a tree without life-force is called barren wood, so also is a woman described as infertile if the life-force for her bloom is lacking in her prime of life. Just as a young tree indeed possesses the power to grow, but at first brings forth no blossoms and fruits, and only when it has reached the strength of its branches does it produce blossoms and fruits in full greenness—but finally, in all the time since it was planted, its life-force is narrowed again so that only one part of its wood is still solid to the core, while the other is brittle and rotten—just so is it with the woman. The girl indeed possesses the power to be able to grow into a strong age, but lacks the blood of her bloom. In a strong age, however, when her limbs have become strong, the life-force peculiar to the blood brings forth the bloom for offspring. In full and complete—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the human life cycle, transitioning from the mechanics of birth to the aging process of men and the maturation and eventual menopause of women. In advanced age, however, his <note>Referring to the aging man discussed in the preceding section</note> blood decreases, such that the driving force for the bloom of his blood fades; his flesh contracts, becoming harder and tougher, but nonetheless weaker than it was before. Little girls, however, do not yet have the flowing brooks of the monthly flow and therefore do not receive offspring, because their limbs are not yet fully developed, just as there is no completion where only the foundation for a house has been laid and no wall has yet been raised. But when a girl has reached her twelfth year, her limbs are strengthened until her fifteenth year, just as a wall is finished when it stands at its full height upon the foundation. From the fifteenth year until the twentieth, the connection of her limbs also reaches its completion, like a house that stands finished with beams and a roof, into which all sorts of household goods are then placed. Thus the woman, having thrived to completion in her vessels and in the connections of her limbs, can now receive, hold, and warm the male seed. If, on the other hand, a woman has conceived a child before her twentieth year, this happens either through the excessive heat of her and her husband's nature or through too frequent embraces of the two. She nonetheless brings a weak and somehow frail child into the world.

### On the Cessation of the Monthly Flow.

From the fiftieth, and sometimes not until the sixtieth year of life, the woman is narrowed in the window-like places <note>original: "fensterähnlichen Orten"; likely referring to the pores or physiological openings through which medieval doctors believed menstrual blood exited the body</note> and increases there, so that the brook of the monthly flow returns again into its dwelling—that is: into the limbs—like a field which, after long cultivation, can no longer receive, germinate, or bring to ripeness any seed for fruit or grain, except for flowers and other good grasses. This state lasts in the woman until the eightieth year of life, from which point she is entirely brought down from her strengths. From the fiftieth or, in some women, sometimes from the sixtieth year of life, the monthly flows cease and the womb <term>womb: the uterus, which medieval writers often described as an independent, mobile organ</term> begins to fold and contract, so that they can no longer receive offspring—unless it should happen that a woman, as a result of some superfluity, perhaps conceives a child once in a while up until her eightieth year. In such a child, however, some defect will always come to light, just as it frequently occurs with those who, as tender maidens, conceive and give birth in their tenderness before their twentieth year. From the eightieth year onward, the woman is made to fade from her strengths, like the day that strives toward its end.

### On the Suppressed Monthly Flow.

In some young women, however, the drops of their blood-brooks are often restricted

...the flowing blood of the woman [ceases] out of grief, because the vessels that carry this blood to the flow of the stream are then constricted by sighs and dry out. But just as a tree blooms and turns green in the summer from the sun, the **monthly flows** of women are often opened by joy; and just as cold wind, frost, and winter cause the leaves and branches of trees to wither, so too the streams of blood that must flow from the woman often dry up through sadness. 

And since in some women the **humors** <term>humors: the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval medicine believed determined a person’s health and temperament</term> within them increase and overflow into all sorts of destructive evils due to an excess of illness, they constrict the vessels that carry the blood-streams. This causes their monthly flows to decrease because the "storms" within their humors produce an improper coldness and a distortion of heat, so that their blood is cold at one time and boiling at another. Therefore, it flows in a distorted way, now here and now there, and the vessels that should bleed at their proper time remain closed because of the dryness within them and do not flow. 

There are yet other women who have weak and thick flesh, which grows more from their weakness and a poor physical constitution than from a proper life force. This flesh overgrows the vessels and presses upon them so that they are so severely narrowed that their blood-streams are choked and cannot flow out at their proper time; the mouths of these vessels are so burdened and closed that they cannot let the streams of their blood escape. Consequently, the **womb** of such women also becomes so excessively fatty that the path to it is as firmly closed as a sealed vessel <note>The author compares the womb to a storage jar or container that has been plugged up</note>, preventing what is inside from flowing out. Thus, her confined blood would not be able to flow at its rightful time.

### On the Skull.

As for the fact that the **skull** in the head of women is divided <note>This likely refers to the cranial sutures, which the author believes physically react to the menstrual cycle</note>, this signifies that a path is provided, in a sense, through which the vessels open when they release the streams of the monthly time. At that same time, the skull itself opens and creates a path for the vessels so that the cleansing effect of the monthly flow can occur. But after this cleansing is finished, the skull closes again and holds the vessels firm, so that they let no more blood flow out, just as a pile of stones and wood stops a river so that it does not flow beyond its measure. 

Sometimes, however, some women suffer pains from all sorts of fevers, as well as stomach, side, and kidney pain, and these pains prevent the skull from closing at the right time—much like when storms and floods wash over a dam. Then the blood-streams in the woman flow at the improper time and not [at the correct interval...]

## **On Conception and Childbirth.**

Women, however, who have within them diseased humors <term>humors: the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval medicine believed determined a person’s health and temperament</term> with piercing pains—because the gout <note>original: "Gicht"; in medieval medical texts, this often referred to any sharp, migratory, or inflammatory pain, not strictly the modern clinical definition of gout.</note> is in them—give birth under great suffering. This is because, when the "closures" of the child are opened, the humors stir up a storm, so that the pathways for its exit are obstructed by swelling or by ulceration. This is like how surging and powerfully rushing floods suddenly obstruct the proper path of a stream, so that this stream cannot flow along its correct course, and it can no longer be recognized where its path lies. Through this hindrance, the path for the child is obstructed until the breath of life—the soul—by God’s grace, presses out with the body, whether the child be alive or dead. 

Also, if a woman is very obese, the path for the child swells due to the richness of the mother’s flesh and becomes blocked, so that the woman in labor suffers much until, through God’s grace, the breath of life that is in the child presses out with its body. If, however, the woman is in the right physical condition—so that she is neither too fat nor too thin, nor weak—then the path of the child is not hindered by improper disturbance. Nevertheless, mother and child still suffer the birth pain determined from the very beginning <note>A reference to the biblical "pains of childbirth" described in the Book of Genesis as a result of the Fall of Man.</note>, and these pains remain within their proper measure. Even in women who are not fat or weak, the path for the child is not hindered, even if they are very thin. Nevertheless, they will still undergo the birth pain established from the beginning. 

Very many children suffocate through the tumult of the humors and because of the fattiness of the womb, so that they die because, as stated, the proper path is obstructed for them. It is, however, a dangerous thing to give any medicine to a woman whose life is at risk while she suffers in labor, whether she suffers from superfluous humors, obesity, or any other present illness. Therefore, medicines that restrict harmful and superfluous humors must not be administered during the exit of the child because of the danger to the child being born. Just as a person would suffocate if they were buried under the earth, so too the child would suffocate through the strong effect and the "smoke" <note>original: "Rauch"; likely referring to the potent vapors or aromatic intensity of medicinal herbs which were thought to overwhelm the delicate senses of a newborn.</note> of the aromatic remedies and herbs if these are applied during its imminent exit.

<meta>This page continues the medieval physiological exploration of the female body and fetal development. ### Also Concerning Conception.
When a woman has received the male seed, the reception of this seed acts so strongly that it draws the woman’s entire menstrual flow to itself, like a suction tube or a cupping glass <note>original: "Schröpfkopf"; a glass vessel used in bloodletting or "cupping" to create a vacuum on the skin and draw fluids to the surface</note>, which the barber-surgeon <note>original: "Bader"; a traditional medical practitioner who managed public baths and performed minor surgical tasks like bloodletting</note> places on a person's body to draw out much blood and pus. The seed, however, is at first like milk in the woman, then it curdles and finally becomes flesh, just as milk first curdles and the formation of cheese follows the curdling. Thus the child lies in the menstrual blood and is nourished by it until birth.

### Also Concerning Childbirth.
As soon as labor has begun, the child emerges amidst a great outpouring of blood, like a flood of waters carrying stones or wood in its current. The mucus, however, and the impurity of the menstrual blood initially remain behind in the mother, because they cannot be expelled from her so quickly, but are instead discharged gradually afterward in the following time. The purification of a woman who is naturally dry and not overabundant in humors <term>humors: the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval medicine believed determined a person’s health and temperament</term> is completed in a short time after birth; conversely, the purification of a woman who is naturally moist and provided with plentiful humors takes longer than for one who is dry and does not have many humors within her.

### Concerning the Delicacy of the Child.
The reason that children do not walk soon after they are born is that a human is conceived from delicate seed and, as long as they are still a child, their flesh and bones are very fragile, and because it requires a great effort for a human to straighten themselves upward to walk. Other creatures do not do this, because they walk upon their feet soon after their birth. This is because they are inclined forward toward the earth, just as a child crawls on hands and feet before it is able to straighten itself upward to walk. However, although newly born animals can stand on their legs, they cannot sit as a child sits when it cannot yet raise itself upon its feet. Because animals possess their strength in their legs and feet, they run soon after birth. But because a human has their strength above the navel, and while still a child is weak in their feet and legs, they cannot walk at that time.

### Why Humans do not Swim.
Because a human is heavy in their body, works with their hands, walks with their feet, and is straightened upward, and because they are not of a very airy nor watery nature, it is not in their nature to be able to swim in the water, provid- <note>original: "voraus-"; this is a catchword, likely the beginning of "vorausgesetzt" (provided that/assuming)</note>

For this reason, some among them can swim by nature because, just as they walk upon the earth leaning forward, so too do they swim in the water leaning forward. Man does not do this, because he walks completely upright and swims only by bending forward and stretching himself out.

**Concerning the Breasts.** In the region of the female navel—that is, both above and below the navel—certain vessels intertwine with one another. Some of these spread upward toward the breasts, while others descend downward to the womb. All of these receive their contents and are nourished by the essence <note>original: "Saft"; literally "juice" or "sap," referring here to the nutritive fluids extracted from digestion</note> of food and drink; however, those leading to the breasts receive more than those extending to the womb. While a woman is still a girl, her breasts grow until the vessels leading to the womb begin to carry out the streams of the monthly periods <term>monthly periods: menstruation, which medieval writers often described as a necessary cleansing of excess fluids</term>, and then they cease to grow, unless they are later sometimes filled like a sponge.

**Also Concerning Conception and Milk.** In a woman, conception draws the monthly flow to itself, so that the streams which were previously directed outward from that same woman now pass over to the conceived child. When this child begins to be formed into flesh and bone—at which point it also receives movement from the spirit of life—the vessels leading to the breasts open to it under the influence of the life-movement of that same fruit of the womb, as well as through the power of the elements. These vessels then bring milk to the breasts from the essence of the food and drink by which the woman’s body is nourished. 

The milk is white in color because, when food and drink are consumed, they separate into two parts in a pregnant woman. One half of them goes to the aid of the womb, while the other half benefits the milk in the breasts. In the same way, blood is of two kinds: it is red when it is at rest, but appears white when it is set in motion during the commingling <note>original: "Vermischung"; a common medieval euphemism for sexual intercourse</note> of man and woman. The milk receives its white color specifically from the grain and other cooked foods consumed with the nourishment, because grain has white flour and a food throws up a white foam when boiling. Similarly, the food and drink in a pregnant woman bring a kind of white foam to her breasts through their essence. ...carry the monthly flow outward <note>original: "Monatsfluß"; referring to menstruation</note>, while the vessels ascending to the breasts are opened for the milk. The reason that enough milk is present in the breasts every time the child sucks is that the child, through the act of sucking, draws the milk through the aforementioned vessels to the breasts, and by sucking, keeps the access of those upper vessels to the breasts constantly open.

## Concerning the Monthly Flow.

Once the child has ceased to suck, the milk in the woman decreases and disappears; thus, the vessels leading to the womb open again and direct their streams outward according to the proper seasons. However, if a woman conceives again while she is still nursing her child, she may continue to breastfeed without danger to the child until that which has been conceived is formed into flesh and bone within her. At that point, she must stop nursing the child, because the strength and health in the milk descend through the monthly flow to the newly conceived life, so that from then on, the milk from this woman’s breasts provides more mucus <note>original: "Schleim"; often used in medieval medicine to describe phlegm or "cold" humors</note> than health.

## Concerning Digestion.

When a person eats, the small vessels that perceive taste distribute it throughout the body. The inner vessels—those of the liver, the heart, and the lungs—take up the finer essence <note>original: "Saft"; literally "juice" or "sap," the nutritive fluid extracted from food</note> from the food within the stomach and carry it through the entire body. In this way, the blood in a human is increased and the body is nourished, just as fire is fanned into a blaze by a bellows, or as grass turns green and grows through the wind and the dew. For just as the bellows fans the fire, and as wind and dew bring forth the grasses, so too does the essence from food and drink cause the blood and blood-water <term>blood-water: a medieval term for the clear components of blood, such as serum or lymph</term>, as well as the flesh of the human, to arise and increase. 

But just as the bellows is not the fire, and the wind and dew are not the grass, so too is the essence from food not itself blood, nor is the essence of drink itself blood-water. Rather, the essence from the food is colored like blood and exists within it, and the essence from the drink takes on the color of the blood-water and remains within it. Both of these build up the blood into a fluid and cause it to rise, just as leaven <note>original: "Sauerteig"; sourdough or yeast</note> causes the whole mass of flour—that is, the dough—to rise. They remain within them, unite with them in this way, disappear with and in them, and are thus consumed.

## Concerning the Stool.

Whatever has become foul-smelling from the consumed food and drink sinks down to the lower parts of the human being, and is there in...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of human physiology from page 108, moving from the specific processes of breastfeeding and early digestion into the wider mechanics of how the body processes food and drink into blood and vital energy. It concludes the analogy of the winepress for digestion and begins to explore the relationship between nutrition and the "rational" forces within the human body.</meta>

...is transformed into putrefaction <note>original: "Fäulnis"; in medieval medicine, this refers to the breakdown or fermentation of food in the stomach, which was seen as a necessary part of the "cooking" process of digestion</note> and, once it has undergone this breakdown, it is expelled from the human being, just as when grapes are brought to the winepress: the wine is caught in a vat, and what remains—that is, the skins—is thrown away.

## Concerning the Blood.

When a person drinks, the finer essence contained in the drink increases the blood-water <note>original: "Blutwasser"; likely referring to the serum or plasma, the watery part of the blood that allows it to flow</note>, and that which is spoiled descends with the drink and exits the person after it has been "cooked" <note>the body was thought to "cook" nutrients into blood</note>, just as the liquid part of wine floats at the top of a vat while the dregs lie at the bottom. For the blood is increased by the essence drawn from solid foods, and the blood-water is increased by drink. Furthermore, the blood could not exist without blood-water, just as a person cannot exist on solid food without drink; if the blood possessed no blood-water, it would be solid and would have no waves to allow it to flow. Likewise, a person who only ate and never drank would completely wither away and would be unable to live. Therefore, a person who consumes harmful or excessive food nourishes bad blood, and he who takes in bad or excessive drink nourishes bad blood-water within himself, because the harmful essences from food and drink unite with his blood and blood-water.

## Concerning Nutrition.

When a person eats and drinks, the pull of reason <note>original: "Zug der Vernunft"; refers to the rational soul's role in directing the body's vital functions</note>—which is natural to life and dwells within the human—carries the taste, the finer essence, and the scent of the food and drink upward toward the brain. It warms the brain by filling its vessels. The remaining components of the food and drink, which reach the stomach, warm the heart, the liver, and the lungs. These organs draw that same taste, essence, and scent into their own vessels so that they are filled, warmed, and nourished—much like when a completely dried-out piece of intestine is placed in water, and it then becomes soft, swells, and fills up. Thus, when a person eats and drinks, their vessels are filled and warmed by the essence of food and drink; that essence warms the blood and the blood-water within the vessels, and the blood found in the flesh draws its red color from the contents of those vessels.

## Concerning Hunger.

Once the ingested food has passed into putrefaction and dried out, the vessels are emptied of their liquid contents, and the blood in the flesh loses its red color and becomes watery. Then the vessels desire to be filled again, and the blood of the flesh craves its red color. This is the hunger that a person suffers.

<summary>This page describes the medieval understanding of digestion as a refining process similar to winemaking, where food creates blood and drink creates "blood-water." It explains that hunger is the physical sensation of the blood losing its color and the vessels becoming empty.</summary>
<keywords>digestion, blood-water, nutrition, hunger, physiology, humors, reason, brain, vital organs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 109 regarding the mechanics of digestion. Having previously explained how food is "cooked" and filtered like wine in a press, the text now moves into the practical behavioral consequences of these processes: the sensation of thirst, the timing of sleep after meals, and the proper way to hydrate upon waking.</meta>

### Concerning Thirst.

When a person eats, he works while eating like a mill at its grinding, and through the labor of eating, the person becomes warm internally, dries out, and thus begins to wither from within. This is thirst. Then he must drink something and eat once more, and when he dries out again from the heat of eating, he thirsts anew and must drink again. He must proceed in this way between eating because if a person did not drink while consuming solid food—that is, while he is eating—he would become burdened in both mind and body and would neither be able to prepare good blood-sap <note>original: "Blutsaft"; in medieval physiology, this refers to the nutrient-rich fluid extracted from food that the liver then transforms into blood</note> nor subsequently have proper digestion. 

However, if he drinks excessively while eating, he creates a harmful, stormy inundation within the humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the vital fluids of the body which must remain in balance for health</note> of his body, because the good humors within him become diluted. And while in the meantime the food passes into putrefaction <note>referring to the breakdown or fermentation of food in the stomach</note> and dries out, the vessels and the blood crave moisture due to that heat, and that is thirst. Then the person must drink something and moisten the dryness within himself; otherwise, he brings upon himself affliction and sluggishness of mind and body. Furthermore, a human being, who has the "living life" <note>likely referring to the *viriditas* or vital greening power often mentioned in Hildegardian medicine</note> within him and is nourished by various foods, has a greater need for drink during the meal than those animals have that feed only on hay and grass.

### Concerning Sleep.

A person should not sleep immediately after eating, before the taste, the juice, and the scent of the foods have reached their proper places. Rather, he should abstain from sleep for a short time after eating so that, if he sleeps immediately after the meal, this sleep does not guide the taste, the juice, and the scent of the foods to wrong and unsuitable parts of the body and scatter them to and fro like dust within the vessels. But if a person has abstained from sleep for a short time and then lies down to rest for about an hour, the flesh and blood increase thereby, and he becomes healthy from it.

### Concerning Thirst at Night.

It often happens that a person feels thirst both during the day and at night upon waking, due to the heat and dryness of the food. He must, however, take care not to drink too quickly while sleep is still within him, because he could thereby bring diseases upon himself and would excite his blood and his humors to an unnatural turbulence. Rather, even if he is very thirsty, he must refrain from drinking for a while after waking until sleep has completely departed from him.

### Concerning Drinking.

When he is thirsty after sleeping, whether he be healthy or sick, he should drink wine or beer and no

...water, because the water would then bring more harm than help to his blood and humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval medicine sought to keep in balance.</note>.

### Concerning the Affliction of Paralysis.

When a person is plagued by gouty paralysis <note>original: "gichtischer Lähmung"; in this period, "gout" was a broad term for various rheumatic or arthritic pains and stiffness.</note>, he has suppressed humors within him that are like restless water waves. Because of this, such a person cannot maintain the right measure in his temperament or his outward behavior. He should, therefore, drink wine on an empty stomach; or, if he cannot have wine, he should drink beer brewed from barley or wheat on an empty stomach. If he can have neither of those, he should boil bread with water, strain it through a cloth, and drink that water lukewarm. He should do this every day, and the waves of the gouty storms within him will be tamed. If such a person has lost weight in a short period, he should drink moderately on an empty stomach as previously instructed; but if his body is otherwise healthy, he may enjoy wine, beer, or the water boiled with bread in sufficient amounts on an empty stomach, and the gout within him will remain quiet.

### Concerning Daily Fever.

Anyone who suffers from daily fever attacks <note>The "quotidian" fever, which recurred every 24 hours.</note>, which are brought about by various foods, should not drink anything while fasting <note>original: "nüchtern"; specifically referring to the state of having an empty stomach before the first meal of the day.</note> because he is internally dry. If he were to drink on an empty stomach, the liquid would run through his body and make him sick rather than healthy. Instead, he should first eat so that his vessels are warmed by the prior intake of the nourishment from the food. Then he may take a drink of wine, and it will cause him no harm. If he has no wine, let him drink beer; and if that is not available to him, he may take mead <note>original: "Meth"; a fermented honey drink.</note>; or if he does not have that, he should boil water, let it cool, and drink it.

### Concerning Three- and Four-Day Fevers.

Whoever has the three- or four-day fever <note>Tertian and quartan fevers, which recurred every 48 or 72 hours, often associated with malaria in the Middle Ages.</note> should not drink on an empty stomach, except in a case of great necessity when he is seized by a very violent thirst. Then he may drink a small amount of cold water while fasting. At breakfast, however, he should drink wine, which is more wholesome for him than drinking water. If he has no wine, he can drink barley beer; or if he lacks that, take mead; or if he lacks that, boiled and cooled water. No one should drink on an empty stomach unless forced to do so by a state of weakness, and it is then healthier for him to drink wine instead of water. If someone drinks wine on an empty stomach without a compelling reason, it makes him greedy in his craving for food and drink, and senseless and foolish in his thoughts.

### Concerning Eating and Nourishment.

As long as a person is still fasting, he should first consume a dish...

...which is prepared from fruit and flour, because this is a dry food and grants a person healthy strength. He should also first consume a warm dish so that his stomach becomes warm, and not a cold dish, because if he enjoys a cold dish first, it makes his stomach so cold that it can hardly become warm again afterward through warm dishes. But as his first meal, he should take a warm dish until the stomach is properly warmed. If he eats a cold dish afterward, the warmth spread over the stomach overwhelms the subsequent cold in the food. He should avoid all fruit and all things containing juice and moisture, such as herbs, at his first meal, because these would bring him rot and phlegm <note>original: "Schleim"; in medieval medicine, phlegm was one of the four humors associated with cold and dampness, thought to cause sluggishness or respiratory issues when in excess.</note> as well as unrest in the humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which must remain balanced for health.</note>. Later, when he has already eaten something, he can enjoy them, and they will then bring him more health than weakness.

**Concerning Breakfast.**

For a physically healthy person, it is good and wholesome for proper digestion that he refrains from breakfast until shortly before noon or around noon. For a sick, frail, and physically diminished person, however, it is good and wholesome if he eats breakfast early in the morning, so that he takes the strength, which he does not have of himself, at least from the food. Also at night, a person can, if he wishes, take the same foods and drinks that he enjoyed during the day, and he should also eat early enough before the night so that he can take his walk before he goes to rest.

**Concerning the Diversity of Drinks.**

Noble and strong wine, when drunk, excites the vessels and the blood of a person in an improper way and draws the juices and all moisture within the person to itself, just as purgative drinks <note>Medicines used to "purge" or cleanse the bowels.</note> do, and thereby occasionally leads to the discharge of urine with danger before the proper time. This the wine from the Hunsrück <note>A mountain range in Germany known for its vineyards; the author suggests this regional wine is milder than "noble" imported varieties.</note> does not do, because it is not so strong that it could excessively excite a person’s humors. Therefore, the powers of a heavy wine should be tempered either by dipping bread into it or by adding water, because it serves neither a healthy nor a sick person for drinking unless it is tempered in this way. It is, however, not necessary to dilute Hunsrück wine so much, because it does not possess such strong powers. If a person nonetheless wishes to add water to it or dip bread into it and drink it so, it is all the more pleasant to drink, but not any healthier. However, wine naturally has something watery within it, because it is nourished by dew and rain. This is why a person who drinks wine, even if he drinks it constantly and no water, nevertheless has watery fluids in his blood.

**Also on the Blood.** The blood would namely be parched and dry, and would not flow—just as marrow does not flow—if it did not have the moisture <note>original: "Schleim"; here referring to the phlegmatic or watery element of the humors that provides fluidity.</note> of water within it. Furthermore, if the blood were not moist, the flesh could not endure but would be like the earth.

### On the Difference in Diet during Winter.

A person who, during the great cold of winter when he is cold within himself, takes in very warm foods, easily contracts black bile <note>original: "Schwarzgalle"; one of the four humors, associated with coldness and dryness, and linked to melancholy or physical ailments.</note> and stirs it up within his body. However, if someone enjoys very cold foods while suffering from their own internal coldness, they will develop a fever from it. But someone who, during the winter cold, consumes nourishment of the correct temperature—that is, neither too warm nor too cold—achieves only a moderate recovery through them, because they do not provide him with good blood. Yet, if someone during a time of frost takes in food of the correct temperature (neither very warm nor very cold) while in a very warm room, such food does not harm him, but the warmth he then possesses himself brings him physical weakness.

### On Proper Behavior during Winter Cold.

If a person wishes to eat during the cold of winter, he should seek a room that is neither too warm nor too cold, but of a middle temperature. He should also not enjoy very hot or very cold foods, but rather lukewarm ones; in this way, he will preserve what is healthy in the nourishment. On the other hand, even if he provides his own warmth through clothing, he should not sit in a cold room while eating. This is because if he draws cold air into himself while eating, it makes him sick. However, the warmth rising up a person's back from glowing coals during a meal is healthier for him than if the heat of the fire comes directly into his face.

### On Excessive Summer Heat and the Variety of Foods.

A person who, in summer when already possessing abundant internal heat, takes in very warm foods, easily excites gout <term>Gout: historically understood as a painful inflammation caused by the "dropping" of waste humors into the joints.</term> within himself. If he eats very cold foods in summer during great internal heat, he creates phlegm <note>original: "Phlegma"; the humor associated with cold and dampness, which in excess was thought to cause sluggishness or respiratory issues.</note> within himself. Therefore, in summer, a person should take in nourishment that is moderate in both warmth and cold; this brings him good blood and healthy flesh. If someone eats a great deal in summer when he is very warm internally, his blood becomes excessively heated by the heavy eating, the humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which must remain balanced for health.</note> are changed for the worse, and the flesh of his body becomes bloated and unnaturally swollen because the air—

<meta>This page continues the dietary and lifestyle advice regarding the balance of the four humors, moving from the impact of room temperature to the physical state of food, the influence of emotions on digestion, and seasonal drinking habits.</meta>

warmth is excessive. If it is then moderate, it brings him no harm but rather maintains his health. In winter, however, when a person is very cold internally, it brings him health and makes him fat <note>In a medieval context, being "fat" or "well-fleshed" was often a sign of vitality and successful nutrient absorption rather than a negative health marker.</note> if he eats a lot. At all times, however, a person should beware of consuming boiling hot and steaming foods; rather, he should wait until the heat and the steam from their moisture have dissipated after cooking. This is because if he consumes them while hot and still steaming, they bloat his stomach, drive it upward, and easily cause leprosy <note>original: "Aussatz"; in medieval medicine, this often referred to various severe skin diseases or "corruption" of the flesh thought to be caused by internal vapors.</note> in him.

If a person suffers from great sorrow, he should consume a sufficient amount of food that agrees with him, so that he may be revived through nourishment, because sadness weighs heavily upon him. In times of great joy, however, he should be moderate in eating, because the blood is thinned <note>original: "aufgelöst"; literally dissolved or loosened.</note> through the expansion of its path ²⁶), and if he then eats too much, the humors <term>Säfte: the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that determine health and temperament</term> contained within the blood are transformed into the storms of fevers. 

A person should also not drink much in winter, because the air then moistens his humors. If he drinks a lot then, he spoils his internal humors and brings diseases upon himself. In winter, he should drink wine or beer and avoid water if he can, because the waters in this season are not healthy due to the moisture of the earth. In summer, on the other hand, he should drink more than in winter, in accordance with the amount and type of food he then consumes, because the humors within him are dried out then. The water drunk at that time harms him less than in winter because of the dryness of the earth. 

In summer, when a person is very warm inside and physically healthy, he should drink moderately lukewarm water and walk back and forth a little immediately afterward so that it becomes warm within him. This is more advantageous for the health of the body than if he drinks wine. However, whoever is physically weak should drink wine or beer mixed with water in the summer, because this refreshes him more than if he drinks water. At all times, whether it be in summer or in winter, a person must guard himself against immoderate drinking. Just as excessive rain harms the soil through too much water, so too does one who drinks excessively bring the disadvantage of harmful humors to his body. 

A person should not deprive himself too much of drink either; for if he has made himself dry through his abstinence from drinking, he will suffer distress in mind and body. Furthermore, the food he has taken in can then bring him neither good digestion nor physical health, just as the soil becomes difficult to work, hard, and dry, and brings no good fruit if the moisture from rain is withdrawn from it. But when a person's belly is full of food and drink, it is necessary that he has the cleansing of digestion <note>Referring here to the regular evacuation of the bowels to maintain humoral balance.</note>.

<meta>This page transitions from seasonal dietary habits to the specific medical practice of bloodletting. It explains the theory of the humors, the physical process of phlebotomy, and the appropriate timing based on physical health and lunar cycles.</meta>

**On Bloodletting.** If a person’s vessels are filled with blood, they must be cleansed of harmful phlegm and the humors <term>Säfte: the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that medieval medicine believed governed health</term> produced by digestion through an incision. When a vessel is cut in a person, the blood undergoes a shock, as if from a sudden fright, and what first emerges is blood; then, putrid and decomposed blood flows out simultaneously. This is why the discharge is varied in color, as it consists of both rot and blood. As soon as the rot has flowed out with the blood, pure blood emerges, and at that point, one must stop the bloodletting. If bloodletting is performed on an otherwise healthy and strong person, the amount of blood taken should be as much as a strong, thirsty man can drink in a single draught <note>Medieval medical texts often used common physical experiences, like a swallow of water or the volume of an egg, as measurements instead of precise units.</note> of water. If a person is physically weak, the bloodletting should be as much as can fit into an ordinary-sized egg. For bloodletting that is performed beyond measure weakens the body just as a downpour of rain that falls immoderately upon the earth damages it. A correctly measured bloodletting, however, removes the bad humors and cares for the body like a rain that falls slowly and in a moderate amount upon the earth, watering it and enabling it to bring forth fruit.

**On the Different Kinds of Bloodletting.** As stated above, the first thing that emerges from the wound and the incision in the vein consists of blood, and with it, corrupt and all disease-bringing humors flow out at the same time. When the discharge then takes on its proper redness or another color, the blood and humors stand in equal proportion to one another.

If even more blood flows out, then the good and the bad humors follow along with the remaining blood. Then one must stop the flow because, if the bloodletting is continued even further, the phlegm in the remaining blood increases excessively and stirs up the black bile <term>Schwarzgalle: one of the four humors, associated with melancholy and, when imbalanced, serious illness</term> and other pestilence <note>original: "Peſtilenz"; here referring to general toxic conditions or infectious "corruption" within the body</note> in the person, because the impoverished blood can offer no resistance to them. But just as hunger causes bodily strength to fade, bloodletting carried too far also makes the body weak, whereas a moderately performed one brings health to the body, just as moderate eating and drinking build the body up.

**When One Should Let Blood.** Whoever is strong and healthy and has fat on their body should let blood in the third month, because the blood in a person has reached its normal state again after two cycles of the moon's increase and decrease, and the vessels are full. If he lets blood earlier,

...so he weakens himself, because the blood has not yet gathered its full strength again. However, if he delays it for too long, the overabundant blood becomes spoiled; that is to say, it is effectively turned into filth <note>original: "Kot" (feces or waste). The author uses this strong term to describe how stagnant blood loses its vital purity and becomes a waste product within the body.</note>. He should, however, let blood during the waning moon—that is, on the first day when the moon begins to wane, or on the second, third, fourth, fifth, or sixth day, and no longer after that, because a bloodletting performed earlier or later will not bring as much benefit. One should not let blood during a waxing moon, because such a bloodletting is harmful, as the putrid fluid mixed with the blood cannot easily be separated from it at that time.

For when the moon is waxing, the blood and the decomposed fluids flow through the person simultaneously and in equal measure, and they cannot easily be distinguished from one another. It is like a stream that, flowing with moderate speed in its bed, maintains its character. But when the moon wanes, the blood begins to become more agitated and to leave its bed; it also allows too much of the putrid fluid to flow out, just as is the case during a great flood, which makes the rot within it visible and casts out foam.

Bloodletting is more suitable for old people than for the young, because the blood in the vessels of the elderly is more permeated by putrescence than the blood in the veins of the young. If it should be necessary, however, the vein can be opened in males as early as the twelfth year, because the blood has already gained sufficient strength by then. Nevertheless, no more blood should be withdrawn then than both halves of a nutshell <note>Like the "draught of water" mentioned previously, the "nutshell" serves as a practical, everyday measurement for the practitioner.</note> can hold together. Until the fifteenth year of life, bloodletting should only be performed once a year.

In the fifteenth year, however, when a person's blood has reached its full strength and the veins their fullness, then—provided the person is physically healthy—as much blood should be taken as corresponds to the amount of water a thirsty man can drink in one draught, as has already been said. One can proceed in this manner until the fiftieth year of life. After the fiftieth year, however, when blood and phlegm <term>Phlegma: one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids; it was associated with coldness and moisture</term> begin to decrease in a man and the body begins to dry out, blood should only be let once a year, and only in half the amount of blood withdrawn up to that point. This continues until the eightieth year. After that, however, bloodletting is no longer good for a man, but is rather harmful, because the inherent strength of the blood has already vanished from him—unless a great "flooding" and outbreak of humors occurs within him. In such a great emergency, a moderate amount of blood may be taken from the vein.

Because a man's blood vessels already become weak after the eightieth year and bloodletting is no longer beneficial to him, he must be helped with the aid of medicinal herbs, for example through the use of black carline thistle <gloss>27) <note>Latin: Atractylis gummifera. A plant often used in medieval medicine for its perceived cleansing and purgative properties.</note></gloss> or...

**On Bloodletting in Women.** Therefore, from her twelfth year onward, a woman should observe the same physical conditions and rules for bloodletting that apply to men, except that she may continue bloodletting until her hundredth year. This is because she possesses a greater internal necessity for it due to the harmful humors and the corrupt blood-water <note>original: "Blutwasser"; likely referring to serous fluid or thinned, unhealthy blood</note> within her. This is also demonstrated by the time of her monthly cleansing <note>referring to menstruation</note>. If a woman were not freed from these harmful humors and corrupt fluids at that time, her entire body would swell and become bloated, and she would not be able to survive. After the hundredth year, however, she should no longer undergo bloodletting, for by then she has already been cleansed of blood and flowing humors. Should she nevertheless notice any bad humors within <unclear>herself</unclear>, she should allow pustules to swell up on those same parts of the body where burning cones <note>referring to cauterization, a practice of applying heat or caustic substances to the skin to "draw out" sickness</note> are typically placed on a person.

**On the Blood Vessels.** One must know that there are more humors in the cephalic vein <note>original: "Kopfader"; literally "head-vein," referring to the cephalic vein in the arm. Footnote 28.</note> than in the median vein or the hepatic vein <note>original: "Leberader"; literally "liver-vein," referring to the basilic vein.</note>, because more vessels carrying humors are connected to the cephalic vein than to the others. Therefore, it is healthier to draw blood more frequently from the cephalic vein than from other vessels. Anyone who has much <term>phlegm</term> <note>One of the four humors of ancient and medieval medicine; an excess was thought to cause sluggishness and respiratory issues.</note> in the head and chest, or whose head hums so loudly that their hearing is sometimes blocked, should let blood from the cephalic vein. However, one must take care that not too much blood is removed there. Otherwise, the eyes may eventually begin to grow weak, because several small vessels leading to the eyes are connected to it. If too much blood is taken, these small connecting vessels are also emptied of blood, and thus the person’s vision begins to fail.

**On Cupping.** If someone’s eyes begin to grow cloudy from bad humors, or if they are ulcerated, or if the flesh around the eyes protrudes, they should have blood carefully drawn from behind the ears and at the nape of the neck using cupping horns or suction cups <note>footnote 29</note>. This should be done three or four times a year. If one feels compelled to be cupped more often, they should allow less blood to flow out each time, so that they...

...so that he does not come to harm if he has removed blood beyond the proper measure. He should draw blood from the vein in that part of the body where he feels pain. If someone has pain in the tongue, whether it is swollen or ulcerated, he should cut it slightly with a small lancet <note>original: "Aderlaßmesser"</note> or with a thorn, so that the phlegm <note>original: "Schleim"</note> may break out there, and he will find himself better. Anyone suffering from toothaches should carefully cut the flesh around the tooth with a small lancet or a thorn in one spot, so that the pus may emerge, and he will become better.

However, those who have a sorrowful heart and an oppressed spirit, and those who feel pain in their side or in their lungs, should be bled from the median vein <note>original: "Mittelader"; the central vein of the inner elbow</note>, but only moderately, so that they retain the strength of the heart and do not diminish it by removing too much blood. If someone suffers from heart pains, they should have blood taken from the median vein of the right arm before extracting blood elsewhere; they will then feel better in their heart. If someone suffers from the liver or the spleen, or feels a shortness of breath in the throat and neck, or suffers from a darkening of the eyes, the bloodletting must be performed on the hepatic vein <note>original: "Leberader"; historically the basilic vein of the arm, thought to connect to the liver</note>, and they will find relief.

If a sufficient amount of blood has been drawn from the hepatic vein or the cephalic vein <note>original: "Kopfader"; the vein running along the outer side of the upper arm and forearm</note>, the person will suffer less than if much blood is taken from the median vein. Depending on the nature of their ailment, a person may be bled from either arm from the aforementioned veins as they wish, in both winter and summer.

Bloodletting should primarily take place at these three vessels—the cephalic vein, the median vein, and the hepatic vein—because these vessels are, in a sense, the head and the foundation for all other vessels; all other small vessels strive toward them and are connected to them. Therefore, the other small vessels should only rarely be cut, because their incision is unnecessary except in an urgent emergency. They might occasionally dry out if cut directly; however, when one of the three main veins is opened and emptied of blood, the small vessels connected to them are also freed from a portion of the bad humors <note>original: "Säfte"; refers to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that medieval medicine sought to balance</note> contained within them.

If, on the other hand, one of the small vessels is cut, the large main vessels and the other small ones connected to them do not notice much of the reduction in blood content. For if many small streams flow out of a large river, and then, by human will, a water-vein <note>an irrigation channel or source</note> is diverted from this river, all the streams originating from it will notice the decrease in water. But if a water-vein is diverted from a single stream connected to the river, the river and...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hydraulic nature of the circulatory system, comparing blood vessels to streams, and establishes the age-based protocols for bloodletting.</meta>

the streams emerging from it do not lose much of their water; rather, only the stream from which the water vein <term>Wasserader: a medieval term for a primary blood vessel or vein, conceptualized as a conduit for life-giving fluids</term> was diverted decreases and dries up. The cutting of the aforementioned main veins should be performed in the bend of the arm <note>the inner elbow or antecubital fossa</note>, because when the vein is cut there, the humors <term>Säfte: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance was thought to determine health</term> gather in greater quantity than in a place where no joint-bend exists.

If, because of some illness, a small blood vessel is cut above a joint, on the back of the foot, on the thumb, or elsewhere on a person's body—that is, not at the bends of the arms—this may indeed be of some benefit to him, but no bloodletting <note>original: "Aderlaß"</note> is worth as much as that which is performed in the bend of the arm on one of the three aforementioned main veins. No person, whether man or woman, should have blood let for the purpose of reducing their blood as long as they are naturally increasing in height and physical size during their youth, even if it might once seem necessary. For if someone cuts those veins and lets out blood while the vessels and blood in the person are still naturally growing, he weakens such a person physically and creates within his character and feelings something like an empty space, so to speak. 

Should it be necessary, one should instead use burning cones <term>Brennkegel: small cones of flammable material burned on the skin, similar to moxibustion, to draw out impurities</term> and extract the blood through cupping <term>Schröpfen: the practice of using heated glass cups to create a vacuum on the skin to draw out "tainted" blood</term>. For in the blood of young people, there is more blood-water <note>serum</note> than in the blood of the old. However, once a person has passed the time of that age beyond which he no longer develops physically, as is the case after the completion of the twentieth year, he may—if he requires it because of some illness—have his vessels cut, but should only have a little blood removed. But if he is physically healthy, he should not have the vessels cut, but rather have himself cupped or burned, because his blood vessels and blood have not yet reached their full strength. But once he has reached the mature age, that is thirty years, then he can—whether he be sick or healthy—have blood drawn at his pleasure, because his blood vessels and blood have now fully developed, and he will thereby preserve his physical health.

### **On the Diversity of the Blood Clot.**
<note>original: "Blutkuchen"; the coagulated mass of blood that forms after it has been drawn and allowed to sit</note>

A person whose blood, emptied from the vein, shows a cloudy coloration, much like a person’s breath, and has black spots within this discoloration, and furthermore looks almost like wax at its edge—that is, all around—will soon die, unless God brings him back to life. The cloudy color in the blood indicates that the humors, in their coldness, are moving toward death. The black streaks in the blood, however, reveal that the black bile <note>original: "Schwarzgalle"</note> is dying off, and the wax-yellow border of the blood announces that the bile, too, is on the way to the...

is approaching death. If the color of a person’s blood is cloudy and waxy, but without black spots, then he may escape death. However, he will become very ill because, although the humors <term>Säfte: the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance was thought to determine health</term> are already nearing death in their coldness, the dark streaks of black bile <term>Schwarzgalle: also known as melancholy; a humor associated with cold and dry properties</term> have not yet appeared. For this reason, he will escape death. But if the blood is black and cloudy without the wax-like coloration, it is a truly desperate case for the person, such that he cannot be released from his suffering unless God releases him. Nevertheless, he will escape death, for although the black bile and the humors are nearing death, the bile <note>Yellow bile, associated with heat and dryness</note> remains undisturbed in its place, and therefore the person will not die. If, however, all colors appear simultaneously when the vessels are cut, it is a dangerous matter, and the person cannot escape death unless God keeps him alive. For the humors as well as the black bile and the yellow bile are all equally nearing death. If, on the other hand, the colors are separated from one another, so that one color of the two is missing, then the person can escape death, though with much pain. This is the foreknowledge gained from the color of the blood of people, who until then either know or do not know that they are sick. The vapor emanating from the black bile is cloudy, but the bile itself also possesses the powers of yellow bile because it is of a wax-like nature. Such is the nature of black bile. After bloodletting <note>original: "Aderlaß"</note>, however, a person must beware of the bright rays of the sun as well as the glow of burning fire for three days, because during these three days the blood in the person is shaken and trembles due to this brightness, and it frequently brings harm to the heart.

### On Conduct After Bloodletting.

Daylight is moderate and does not harm the person who has been bled, provided it is without excessive solar radiation. At all times, however, and especially during bloodletting, the blood in the area of the eyes seethes due to the heat of the sun as well as the heat of fire; the delicate skin—that is, the membrane <note>original: "Membran"</note> that holds the eyes together—dries out and thus leads to weakness of sight. After a bloodletting, one should not eat unfamiliar foods, roasted meat, or those containing a particular juice, nor raw fruit or raw vegetables, because these would then increase the phlegm <term>Schleim: or mucus; the cold, moist humor</term> in the vessels more than the blood. Also, one must not drink strong wine, because this would excite the blood and easily make the person lose consciousness. One may take appropriate food and one or two dishes so that one is properly full, as well as drink a light, pure wine. This should be done for two days, because the thinned blood remains in a state of excitement for that long. On the third day

...but when the blood has regained its full strength and flows back to its proper places. One should avoid cheese after a bloodletting, because cheese provides the blood with phlegm and does not produce proper, pure blood, but rather infuses it with a sickly fat content. However, if a person has too much blood and completely full vessels, and does not purify his blood through phlebotomy <note>original: "Aderlaß"</note> or cupping, his blood will become somewhat waxy and weak, and thus the person falls into illness.

### When should one draw blood? 

If a person wishes to open a vein to reduce the blood, he should do this while fasting <note>original: "nüchtern"; meaning on an empty stomach, usually in the morning</note>. For as long as a person is fasting, the humors <term>Säfte: the four vital bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance was considered the foundation of health</term> present within him are still somewhat separated from the blood. The blood then flows through the person in the right way and not too quickly, like a stream flowing in its bed, free from any agitation by wind or weather, moving correctly and orderly. But if a person has taken food, then the blood begins to flow more strongly within him, the humors mix more with it, and the two can no longer be easily separated. Therefore, the bloodletting should be performed when the person is fasting, so that the humors separated from the blood can flow out more easily. An exception occurs only if a person is very frail and weak; he may take a little food before the vein is cut so that he does not faint.

### On Cupping

Whoever wishes to be cupped <note>original: "schröpfen"; a therapeutic practice where suction is applied to the skin to draw out "corrupt" fluids</note> must do it while fasting, because then the blood-water <note>the serum or lymphatic fluid</note> flows out separately from the blood. For if the person has eaten breakfast, the blood mixes with the blood-water, and if he then wishes to be cupped, the blood flows out together with the blood-water. However, so that the person does not become weakened in the heart, he should enjoy a little bread and wine before the cupping. 

Cupping is good and useful at any time, so that the harmful humors and phlegm <term>Schleime: thick, cold fluids believed to cause blockages and disease</term> found within the person may be reduced. These phlegms mostly sit between the skin and the flesh, and they are particularly disadvantageous to the person. However, cupping is more suitable for youths than for the elderly, because the young are richer in humors than the old. Cupping is also more suitable in summer than in winter, because in summer people enjoy more fresh food with young, strong juice than in winter, from which they also contract fresh phlegm. Those who have soft and fatty flesh can have blood drawn by cupping twice in a month. Lean people, on the other hand, should only do this once in any given month, if it appears necessary. Anyone who has pain in the eyes, ears, or the entire head should use the cupping horn or the dry

However, anyone suffering in the chest must place the cupping horn <note>original: "Schröpfhorn"</note> on the shoulder blades; whoever suffers from pain in the side should place it on each arm where the hand ends <note>the wrist area</note>; or, if he feels pain in the thighs, he should apply it to the sides of the lower abdomen; or if he is tormented in the genital area, he should apply it between the buttocks and the hollow of the knee, that is: on the thighs ³⁰). In the place where either the dry cupping glass or the cupping horn is set, these must not be applied more than three or four times in the same hour in which the blood is being drawn. 

On the calves, however, as well as on the shins, one should not cup, or do so only in exceptional cases, because more blood is present there than humors <term>Säfte: the four vital bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—whose balance was considered the foundation of health</term>, unless there is a great necessity caused by the humors. One must not draw the humors there, because the entire body is supported by the legs. 

For those who remove humors and blood through cupping in this way, it is not necessary for them to be as careful regarding the brightness of the sun or fire, or in the intake of food, as they would have to be if they had undergone bloodletting <note>referring to venesection, the direct cutting of a vein</note>. Rather, they should strengthen themselves again with food as they were previously accustomed, because through cupping the little tubes—that is: the vessels <note>original: "Gefäße"</note> that carry the life of man within them and bring about the cohesion of the limbs—are not cut into. For if such a tube is cut, all others feel this incision and suffer along with it. If, however, a vessel is to be cut during bloodletting for a horse, ox, or donkey, and the animal is strong and in good flesh, then as much blood should be taken from it as a cup of water can hold.

## On Bloodletting in Animals.

But if the horse, the ox, or the donkey is weak and thin, only half a cup full of blood should be drawn from it, according to its bodily condition and the weakness of its capacity. After the bloodletting, however, one should give it soft feed and dry, tender hay to eat. Then the animal should rest for one to two weeks, or for one to four days after the bloodletting, until it has regained its strength, since it is, after all, a working animal. After three months have passed, the blood in the vein of the same animal should be reduced once more in the fourth month and not earlier, unless its weakness makes this urgently necessary, because there are not as many bad humors in these animals as there are in humans. 

In sheep, however, one should perform bloodletting more often, but always only in small quantities, because otherwise it easily becomes ill with the frequent changes in the air. In sheep, the bloodletting is to be done in damp, well-tempered weather, because

### Regarding Cautery Cones 31).

But burning—that is, the application of a cautery cone—is good and useful at any time because, if carried out with caution, it reduces the humors and phlegms <note>original: "Schleime"</note> located under the skin and brings health to the body. It is suitable for young and old people alike. For the young, it is beneficial because, while their flesh and blood are still growing in their youth, the harmful humors also increase in them. For the old, it is useful because, while their flesh and blood decrease with age, the phlegm remains trapped between their skin and flesh. 

However, it is somewhat healthier for old people than for the young because, due to the decrease in flesh and blood and the shrunken skin, the serum <note>original: "Blutwasser"</note> flows back and forth between the skin and flesh in even greater quantities. For young people, however, it is not as beneficial as for the old because, while their flesh is still growing and their blood is hot, and their skin is also thin and tight, in many cases the blood that provides health and strength flows out along with the harmful humors from the burn wound produced by the <term>moxa: a small cone of dried organic matter, such as mugwort, burned on the skin to stimulate healing through heat</term>. 

On the other hand, burning is healthier for young people in winter than in summer. For since they themselves and the summer are both "hot" in temperament, they must be careful that they do not lose blood along with the phlegm through burning during the summer. They should instead have a cautery cone applied in winter, because winter is moist and cold, while they themselves are warm; for that reason, they retain their blood more easily and allow only the humors to flow out. 

For old people, however, burning is especially suitable in the summer because, while they themselves are already growing cold with age, the summer is warm. Thus, the humors stirred up by the summer heat flow out during burning, because they receive from the external summer heat the warmth they lack internally for the expulsion of phlegm. 

Those who apply a cautery cone should only pierce the outer skin, so that the flesh is not pierced more deeply, lest a person's health flow out with the blood rather than just the serum and harmful humors. Adult, obese persons should only be burned once in the course of a year and then refrain from it for half a year. Then they may, if they wish, apply a cautery cone to another part of the body. Lean people, however, can be burned once every six months and, if they have done so, repeat it after another half year at their discretion, but at a different location on their body; for if a <term>moxa</term> is placed too often on the same part of the body and the wound is kept open for too long, the flesh there [will produce] much pus...

...accumulates and thus becomes weak and hardened. If the wound site is kept open too long and without careful consideration in a person, then health and blood are driven out of the person due to a lack of bad humors <note>original: "Säfte"</note> and phlegms. In young people, however, it is sufficient if the burn wound is kept open for ten weeks. 

If a cauterization is to be performed, this should be done with tinder fungus <note>original: "Feuerschwamm"</note> or the pith of the spindle tree ³²) or also with a knotted piece of linen. It should not be done with iron, because this attracts many phlegms indiscriminately, nor with sulfur, because it causes the flesh to rot and become foul-smelling, nor with frankincense, because this flares up and causes the skin to dry out. For tinder fungus, spindle tree pith, and linen have a milder fire than other fuels; they only penetrate through the skin and do not pierce the flesh. Where only the skin is injured, only humors emerge and not the person's health. But where the flesh is pierced together with the skin by the fire, the person's health flows away along with the serum <note>original: "Blutwasser"</note>. 

If someone wishes to keep the wound open for a longer time and wrap it with a cloth, they should place pith from a hazelnut bush and some wrapped flax tow <note>original: "Flachshede"</note> upon it. If, on the other hand, he wants to keep the wound open only for a short time without a bandage, then he should place flax tow or hare's fur upon it. If necessary, a person may have a <term>cautery cone: a small cone of dried organic matter burned on the skin as a medical treatment</term> applied from the twelfth year of life until the sixtieth year; later, however, it should no longer be done, because it then causes more harm than health, provided that some urgent emergency caused by illness does not force its use. 

Whoever has pain in the eyes, ears, or the entire head should be burned only lightly behind the ears, without applying a bandage there; and whoever’s back hurts should be burned lightly between the shoulder blades or receive a <term>moxa: a localized heat treatment</term> on the arms where a bandage can be applied. Whoever has pain in the intestines should be burned at the boundary between the <term>ilium: the upper part of the pelvic bone</term> and the back; and whoever has many humors throughout the whole body should receive a moxa between the shin and calf, on the front of the lower leg, and receive a bandage there. 

Just as one who draws blood from a vein allows a pause to occur in between, so too should the one who performs a cauterization. If sufficient time has passed during the day, one can be burned again. But whoever applies a cautery cone should take a hemp cloth, dip it three or four times in wax, and place birch bark ³³) upon it, because this, together with the wax, adheres firmly to the burn wound. It should be applied to the wound so that the cloth extends a bit beyond the bark everywhere. Thus the cloth holds back the stench of the burn wound, so that it does not...

For the more the stench <note>In medieval medicine, the odor of a wound was a key indicator of the "bad humors" being expelled from the body.</note> is held back in the wound so that it cannot spread further, the more mucus <note>original: "Schleim"; likely referring to phlegm or pus being drawn out as part of the healing process</note> is expelled and no blood. If, on the other hand, the stench spreads further from the wound and is not held fast by the bandage, then all the more blood flows from the burn wound and all the more mucus remains behind in the body. The application of **cypress bark** to the wound is of no use, because the cypress sap is harmful to human flesh. 

When, however, the small cloth placed upon the burn wound is full of mucus, such that the cloth has become warm from the mucus, then the person should remove the cloth from the wound and apply another. This is because if the bandage has lain upon the wound for too long, the humors that have gathered there will dry up. If, conversely, one removes the cloth before it has been warmed by the mucus, it harms the patient, because the mucus that had accumulated there returns again into the flesh.

### On Clearing the Throat

Just as the earth is always moist and sweats out much that is foul and useless, so too does a person who is "moist" in their flesh cast out what is foul-smelling and decayed within them by **clearing the throat** <note>original: "Räuspern"</note>, since they are created from the clay of the earth. Were the earth not moist, it would be able to bring forth no fruit but would be parched; likewise, if a person had no moisture within them, they would be immobile and fruitless in every work.

### On the Fire of the Soul

The human soul is of a fiery nature; it draws the four elements to itself and thereby enables the human being to walk about with their sight, hearing, and similar functions. The soul is a kind of power within the human being, just as fire is in water; for a person could not live without a soul, just as water could not flow without fire if it did not feel this within itself.

### On Saliva

The soul extracts a power, the **saliva**, within the person from the water, because the water moistens and prepares the reason for speaking—just as strings are helped along with wax or pitch so that they may have a beautiful tone. Saliva would be pure and clear as water if the soul were not of a fiery nature. But as it is, the saliva is like a foam because of the soul’s fire, just as water throws up foam through fire and the heat of the sun—that is, through their warmth. Because the soul is fiery, it is also its nature to be of a watery kind, because the eyes, which have fire and water within them, are the windows of the soul. All moisture present in a person is of a watery nature and strives toward the path...

...towards reason, so that this very reason can resound in human beings. The soul draws the water from the brain and the internal organs to the saliva so that the person can speak; a human could neither utter a sound nor form a word if they possessed no internal moisture, but were instead dry. Thus, saliva is comparable to a good ointment <note>original: "Salbe"; in medieval medicine, ointments were primary means of healing external and internal ailments</note>, for just as ointment summons health, so too does saliva maintain and bring forth sight, hearing, smell, the voice, speech, and everything conducive to health in a human being.

## **On the Coldness of the Stomach.**

However, one who has a cold stomach <note>According to the theory of the four humors, a "cold" stomach was thought to be sluggish in digestion, often caused by an excess of phlegm</note> throws out a great deal of saliva because they are not warm, and for this reason is often sickly. One who has an overly warm stomach throws out only a moderate amount of saliva because they are somewhat dry internally, and for this reason falls easily into severe fevers.

## **On Flesh.**

All flesh is full of life-freshness <note>original: "lebensfrisch"; likely a translation of the Latin "viriditas," a central concept in Hildegard’s work meaning "greenness" or "vitality"</note> and from this life-freshness it derives its mucus. This is also seen in the meat of livestock when it is slaughtered; when it is hung up, drops of mucus flow out. A person who has lean and thin flesh, however, excretes mucus more easily through sweating than one burdened with fatty flesh. For one who has lean and thin flesh on their body is like a cheese that has not been properly pressed, riddled with numerous holes. The air and the other elements penetrate them easily. Therefore, they easily contain fluid and excrete it abundantly because they have such thin flesh. Then the heat and the flavor of food and drink rise from the stomach, smoking and ascending to the liver, the heart, and the lungs like a poison. But the heat of the liver, heart, and lungs cannot endure this impurity; instead, it drives it out through the chest and the throat, like a food that causes foam to rise from itself when it is cooked over a fire.

## **On Blowing the Nose.**

People with a cold, weak stomach and weak intestines send a cold, moist smoke into their brain because of this weakness. This then becomes like a cooked poison and is cast out through the nose and the mouth, just as the stars in the air purify themselves and as the earth also ejects all sorts of filth and foul-smelling matter from itself.

## **On the Cleansing of the Brain through Saliva and Blowing the Nose.**

The brain possesses window-like openings that are always accessible to the wind, and through which it is constantly kept soft and moist. These are the eyes,
