...the ears, the nose, and the mouth of a human. The cold and moist refuse from the humors <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säften"; in medieval medicine, these were the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that determined health and temperament</note> is gathered in the pathways of the nose and throat because the brain cannot tolerate it; instead, the brain expels it for the sake of cleansing the person, and it is driven out by a blast of air. If this cleansing were prevented in any way in a human, they would lose their senses and wither away, because their stomach would be destroyed and their brain would rot, as neither could endure this stinking waste—just as the sea tolerates no filth or impurity, but casts it out from itself. 

People with coarse flesh have a hardness in them like cheese that has been pressed so firmly that no liquid can flow out of it anymore. In such people, the power of the phlegm <note>original: "Schleimes"; here referring to the cold, moist humor</note> remains behind in their flesh and hardens there because the body cannot excrete the waste. The moisture from food and drink, as well as the other humors, are weaker in such people because they lack the external aid of the phlegm present in the flesh, which cannot flow out due to the narrow and hard nature of their flesh. These people are weak within their bodies and suffer externally from ulcers <note>original: "Geschwüren"; sores or lesions</note> in their flesh, because the impurities of the phlegm remain within them and they cannot excrete them. Therefore, they suffer much pain in the chest, the veins, and the rest of the body. 

However, those who have fatty flesh on their bodies possess an abundance of all sorts of fluids and cough easily, yet they expel only a moderate amount. This is because the air, as well as the other elements—which penetrate them only with difficulty—cannot easily escape due to the fattiness and density of their flesh; thus, they cannot set the humors in motion to free the body of them. For those who possess an abundance of phlegm <note>original: "Phlegma"</note> and do not excrete it, burden themselves with diseased and weak flesh; they are therefore not healthy and cannot be healthy. However, those who have an excess of phlegm but do excrete it are quite lean and physically healthy, because they do not retain the impurities within themselves. But those who do not excrete the phlegm and become sick from it, as mentioned above, should use purgatives <note>original: "Abführtränke"; medicinal drinks or tonics designed to induce the expulsion of harmful humors</note> and cleanse themselves through them.

When the human blood in the vessels is not lively and nimble, but lies there as if it were sleeping, and the humors too do not move quickly but are sluggish, the soul notices this according to its nature. It shakes the entire body through sneezing and allows the blood and humors of the human to awaken again and return to their proper state. If water were not moved by storms and floods, it would become foul. Likewise...

**On Nosebleeds.** When people carry much anger and stubbornness within themselves, but for some reason—whether due to a lack of courage, fear, or shame, because of their melancholic nature, or because it is otherwise impossible for them—do not show it or allow it to be known, the vessels of the brain, neck, and chest sometimes burst and break as a result of this unnatural behavior <note>The author suggests that suppressing intense emotions creates internal pressure that physically ruptures blood vessels.</note>. The blood then pours out through the passage and path by which scents are guided to the nose. 

There are also people who are occupied with vain, constantly shifting thoughts that they nevertheless cannot manage, or who wander from place to place with a confused and wavering mind. Others are tossed to and fro in their spirits due to an unusual disposition of character or an unrestrained mood, falling into a state of senselessness so that they no longer know how to hold their eyes or their face in the proper manner or right gesture. In these cases, the vessels of the brain, neck, and chest burst under the influence of these foolish thoughts, so that blood flows from the nose as mentioned above. Their senseless thoughts and foolish ideas cause the aforementioned vessels to swell and the blood to escape. 

Furthermore, if a person has too much blood in both their flesh and their veins, this blood finds an easier exit through the nose than through any other part of the body because of the breath that is drawn in and expelled through the nose. Consequently, the vessels of the brain and other neighboring vessels burst and flow out there due to the excessive blood volume. There are also some individuals in whom blood is present in such excess that it occasionally becomes thick and black. However, because such people are internally healthy and strong, they expel this superfluous and overflowing blood through the nose. Through this discharge, their brain is cleansed, their eyesight sharpens, and their strength is restored to health. 

Yet there are others whose blood is occasionally stirred by violent fevers and great internal heat—much like wine fermenting in a wineskin <note>The "fermenting wine" metaphor was a common way to describe the "boiling" of humors during a fever.</note>—and they empty their blood through the nose. Through this loss of blood, the brain becomes quite depleted, the eyes grow dim, and the person’s strength is diminished.

This is similar to how ulcers break open when they have ripened and empty the phlegm contained within them, or how no food can be cooked without casting out its impurities through a cleansing foam. The soul does the same within the human body when all the bodily humors in the eyes, ears, nose, mouth, and digestive tracts are cooked—each according to its kind—by the fire of the soul <note>Medieval medicine often compared digestion and metabolism to a "cooking" process powered by the soul's vital heat</note>, just as food is finished over a fire by casting up foam. If a person enjoys a new and unknown food, or drinks a new, foreign wine or any other unusual beverage, these foreign humors set the other humors in motion, liquefying them so that they flow out of the nose as a cleansing. This is like young wine which, when poured into a cask, casts out dregs and yeast during its fermentation. If a person were to somehow hold back or prevent such a cleansing from flowing out, they would harm themselves as much as if they held back their stool or urine so that they could not exit at the proper time. However, if the foreign humors are added so abundantly to the existing ones that violent physical pain results, then one must use a medicine so that they may flow out more easily.

### On Purgative Draughts.
However, those drinks which cleanse the stomach are not suitable for people who are very weakened or so diminished that they are constantly plagued by paralysis. Nor are they fit for those who have such volatile humors within them that they flow incessantly back and forth without stability, like rivers during a flood. Such people suffer more harm after taking purgative drinks than they receive healing from them. This is because humors of that nature, once expelled from the stomach, circulate at an accelerated pace between the skin and the flesh and through the vessels, and do not remain in the stomach long enough for the medicine to work. Therefore, if a purgative drink is introduced into the stomach of such a person, it does not find the humors there to drive them out.

These scents, through their virtues, counteract the harmful smoke that arises from the aforementioned humors and agitates the evil fluids...

### On Diet <note>In the medieval sense, "diet" refers to a total lifestyle regimen and the balanced consumption of food to maintain the body's internal harmony</note>.

Whoever wishes to be healthy should enjoy naturally cold foods after naturally warm ones, and naturally warm ones after naturally cold ones. Similarly, after naturally dry foods, one should have naturally moist ones, and after naturally moist ones, naturally dry ones—whether cooked or uncooked—which are by their nature either warm or cold, so that they are properly balanced against one another.

### On the Creation of Adam and the Shaping of Eve.

After God had created Adam, Adam experienced a powerful feeling of love in his sleep when God sent sleep upon him. And God created a form for the man’s love, and thus woman is the love of the man. As soon as woman was formed, God granted man the drive for procreation, so that through his love—the woman—he might produce sons. Indeed, when Adam beheld Eve, he was filled entirely with wisdom, because he saw before him the mother through whom he was to beget sons. But when Eve looked at Adam, she saw him as if she were looking into heaven, and just as the soul strives upward, longing for heavenly things, her hope was directed toward the man. In this way, there shall and may only be a mutual love between man and woman, and no other. In the heat of passion, the man's love toward the woman is like the fire of burning mountains <note>This likely refers to volcanoes, used as a metaphor for an intense, overwhelming, and difficult-to-extinguish passion</note>, which can only be stifled with difficulty, compared to a wood fire that is easily extinguished. The woman's love, however, stands toward the man's like the mild warmth emanating from the sun, which brings forth fruit, compared to the high-blazing fire of wood; because with her moderate warmth in her...

### On Sexual Desire.

When sexual desire stirs within a person, it is ignited by the fire of the marrow <term>Mark: in medieval medicine, the marrow within the bones was thought to be the source of vital heat and the substance from which reproductive fluids were refined</term>. The fire contained in the marrow rises within a person for various reasons—for instance, through a foolish craving for pleasure, immoderate eating and drinking, or vain and luxurious thoughts—and thus carries the person away. The fire in the human marrow ignites sexual desire with its trace of sin; then, desire and its trace stir the passion within the blood like a storm wind, so that the blood foams up. Desire then guides this milk-like foam, accompanied by a pleasant sensation, to the cavities of the sexual organs, for it is then "cooked through" and ripe.

For just as any food is more pleasant in taste after it has been cooked and has reached full maturity than it was before cooking and before it was fully ripe, so too with this substance. At the point where the full power of the vessels lies, the person feels this romantic stimulus because, as previously stated, it emerges from the veins—just as the bouquet, the flavor, and the entire character of wine emerge at one specific point in the cask. If a person is occasionally plagued by lustful thoughts, they may sometimes, even without mutual touch, discharge as much foam into the sexual organs as water tossed by the wind throws up from itself. However, if a man is aroused to lust by mere touch, he discharges a thin, cloudy, and only half-cooked foam, comparable to thin milk, because it has not been "cooked" by the fire of the other side. 

For just as food is not cooked by its own natural fire unless another fire is added to it, the seed of a man is not discharged fully "cooked" unless it receives help from a foreign fire <note>The "foreign fire" refers to the reciprocal heat and arousal generated by a partner during intercourse</note>. Therefore, it also happens that when a man is joined in pleasure with another man or any other living, sentient creature, he then discharges a seed which, cooked through by the mutual fire, resembles rich, high-quality marrow. When a man is united with a woman during the pouring out of his seed, he pours his seed into the right place, like one who takes the fully cooked food from the pot and places it on a dish to be consumed. ## **On Nocturnal Emission.**
During the emission of seed that occurs in a sleeping person without the influence of a dream-image—occurring solely from the person's nature—the heat in the marrow <term>Mark: in medieval physiology, the bone marrow was considered the source of vital heat and the substance from which reproductive "foam" was refined</term> is not fanned. Therefore, it is excreted like water that is only lukewarm from moderate heat. However, in an emission resulting from a dream of intercourse, the person's marrow burns fiercely, so the release occurs like boiling water, even if it is not "fully cooked" <note>the text suggests that because the person is asleep, the process of "cooking" or maturing the seed is incomplete</note> because the person is not awake. If a man is sexually aroused and feels this in his body, but the "foam" of his sex does not flow out, he is not physically weakened by it. If, however, the urge is so intense that the foam reaches the point of exit but is nonetheless held back in the body for some reason, the person often falls into an illness, such as a burning fever, a three-day fever <note>original: "dreitägige Fieber" (tertian fever); a recurring fever often associated in historical medicine with an imbalance of humors</note>, or other ailments.

## **On the Sexual Maturity of the Man.**
From the fifteenth year of life, a youth notices within himself the need for sexual intercourse and easily "sweats out" the foam of human seed through idle imaginings. However, the sexual feeling and the seed itself have not yet reached full maturity. Because the seed is still unripe at this time, it is necessary for the young man to be strictly guarded so that he does not engage in intercourse with a woman or fulfill any other passion; for through this, he easily becomes unwise and his insight becomes empty. His intellect diminishes, and he easily becomes of an irrational and unrestrained disposition, because he has not yet attained the maturity to be able to excrete ripe seed. If the young man is physically strong, he reaches the maturity to fulfill his desire by his sixteenth year; if he is physically weak, he attains the maturity for procreation only in the seventeenth year of his life. From that point on, in his maturity, he possesses a fully developed intellect and a better, more stable character than he had in the time before sexual maturity. After the fiftieth year, however, a man leaves behind his childish and inconstant habits and adopts a steady...

From the eightieth year onward, his sexual drive is extinguished. 

A girl notices the stirrings of desire from the twelfth year of her life, and she then sweats easily as a result of lascivious thoughts—the foam of lust <note>original: "Schaum der Lüsternheit"; in medieval physiology, arousal was often described as a "foam" or "froth" produced by the heating of bodily fluids</note> exudes from her, although this sexual desire is not yet mature enough for the reception of seed. Because she is not yet capable of procreation at this immature age and is not guarded as just mentioned, she easily loses her sense of honor and shame as well as her right judgment through premature, unrestrained sexual indulgence; rather, with this evil quality, she later imitates the manners of beasts more than those of humans. 

In the fifteenth year of her life, if she is vigorous and of a "moist" nature <note>medieval medicine categorized constitutions by "humors"; a "moist" or "humid" nature was often associated with fertility, youth, and vitality</note>, the sexual feeling in her is mature and capable of procreation. If, however, she is of a weak and sickly nature, she only reaches reproductive maturity in her sixteenth year and then possesses a right and full understanding and attains a more stable character than before. From about the fiftieth year of life, the woman sets aside girlish habits and the instability of outward behavior and from then on maintains a well-ordered, steady disposition in her being. If she is of a moist, vigorous, and strong nature, her carnal desire vanishes around the seventieth year of life. If, however, she had a delicate, sickly nature, this turns away from her around the sixtieth year and leaves her around the eightieth year, as was already mentioned regarding the man. 

Those, however, who spill their seed in their passion like donkeys <note>the donkey was a common medieval symbol for lechery and lack of self-control</note> afterward get red eyes and thick skin around the eyes and consequently become somewhat weak-sighted. But those who practice this act with moderation and discipline do not have to suffer from clouding of their eyes later on.

Furthermore, it burns with a tremendous heat, such that its warmth exceeds the heat of fire. For fire can be extinguished, but the fire of the marrow is not extinguished as long as the person lives. **Of the Three Powers of the Marrow.** The heat of the fire in the marrow behaves like the fire within a stone and possesses three powers of its own. With the first power, it ignites the blood so that it may flow; the second allows blood to exit at certain times in various ways in both man and woman <note>This likely refers to both menstruation and the emission of other bodily fluids which medieval medicine believed were refined from blood</note>; the third, however, produces that burning and sweet pleasure and the laboring, burning storm of the sweetest procreative instinct <term>Zeugungstrieb: the natural reproductive drive or sexual urge</term>.

**Of Intemperance.** <note>original: "Unenthaltsamkeit"; refers here to a lack of self-control or moral restraint</note> Sometimes this burning storm rises within a person when they are idle and occupied by no labor; it blows into their chest, makes the person somewhat lighthearted, and thus ascends from the chest to the brain, filling it entirely—as well as the vessels—with a burning heat. It further seizes the lungs and the heart and so arrives at the organs of sex: in the man, the loins; in the woman, the navel. Then the person's conscious awareness falls asleep, for they do not know what they are doing.

**Of Temptation.** Then, under the compulsion of this inner storm, the devil’s seduction joins in, and the human being, forgetting shame, burns with passion. However, just as when the sun rises, the air and the dew hasten to their service—in such a way that the air brings some cold and the dew brings some moisture, by which the sun is tempered in the heat of its fire—it then descends, warm, cold, and moist, for the general benefit of the earth’s produce.

**Of the Proper Heat of the Marrow.** Just as the sun provides its heat to the earth, so the marrow of a human being supplies the entire body with warmth. However, the strong wind—which issues like a draft from the stomach—cools the fire of the marrow slightly, and the moisture—which emerges like a dew from the bladder—dews and moistens the fire so that the fire itself provides the human body with the correct warmth, because it is now tempered by cold and moisture. But just as the terrors of storms and hailstorms disturb the air, so that in its service it does not provide the sun with the proper cold, and conversely the sun does not provide the air with the necessary heat, so too do various foods sometimes agitate the stomach such that, under their influence, the heat of the

**Of Intemperance.** For when a person consumes all kinds of meat and excessively hot and particularly savory foods without choice or order, their juice <note>original: "Saft"; in medieval medicine, this refers to the "humors" or fluids produced by digestion that were thought to influence temperament and health</note> throws the juice of their marrow into a harmful agitation, so that it rises up in lustful desire. Therefore, a person who wishes to eat meat dishes should enjoy them moderately and cooked with only a simple addition of spices, but not too hot nor prepared and served too savorsomely through all sorts of additions and spices. For their juice possesses a certain kinship with the juice of the human flesh and easily instills the desire for pleasure into their marrow. Just as an impure, dry wind diminishes the powers of the dew so much that it can no longer provide the sun's heat with the appropriate moisture, so too does strong and noble wine dry out the power of a person's bladder, so that it cannot supply the marrow with the right vitality <term>Lebensfrische: the vital freshness or vigor of life</term>.

**Of Wine.** For wine is the blood of the earth and exists within the earth just as blood exists within a person; it has a kind of kinship with human blood, and therefore conveys its heat—like a wheel spinning at the greatest speed—from the bladder toward the marrow and throws it into a powerful heat, so that this same marrow then imparts the glow of lustful desire to the blood. Therefore, a person who wishes to drink very good, strong wine should mix it with water, so that its power and heat may be somewhat diminished and tempered. Also, to the wine called Hunsrücker <note>original: "Hunsrücker"; a wine from the Hunsrück mountain range in Germany, a region familiar to the author</note>, one should add water until the water makes its inherent tartness and acidity bearable and mild; because just as blood is dry and does not flow without the moisture of water, so too does wine, when drunk without the addition of water, dry out and harm a person, impairing their physical health and bringing carnal lust into them. All food and all drinks should be enjoyed in a fitting manner, not too warm and with moderation, so that the person is not weakened by the various juices contained within them and so that their nature does not exceed its measure in harmful lust. Just as the earth is impaired in its fertility when the sun scorches it immoderately without the tempering influence of air and dew, so too does the human being suffer damage to their physical health and become stirred to carnal lust if they have consumed the heat of food and drink immoderately. Even if he has a healthy body, he should nevertheless [be moderate] in eating...

**Of Thinking.** The previously mentioned hot wind <note>In Hildegard’s physiology, "winds" or "breaths" within the body were thought to carry physical and spiritual impulses between the organs, acting as a bridge between the soul and the flesh.</note>, which rises from the human marrow as the lust of the flesh, sometimes awakens and promotes vain thoughts. This happens when a person ponders what they actually are, where they come from, and the nature of the desire that Adam brought upon himself through the bite of the apple <note>Referring to the "Original Sin" in the Garden of Eden, which Hildegard believed physically altered human biology.</note> when he transgressed the divine commandment; or it occurs when one sees or hears something that triggers a feeling of pleasure. Then, through diabolical seduction, those thoughts draw the aforementioned burning wind of pleasure out of the marrow as if by command, so that it rises, passes through the chest, touches the brain, pierces the liver and heart, and falls down into the genitals, as mentioned above. This is the experience of those who seek to fulfill their desire through action.

**Of the Nightmare.** <term>Alpdruck: literally "elf-pressure," a medieval term for a nightmare or sleep paralysis, often attributed to a demonic weight pressing on the sleeper's chest.</term> In many cases, a person's blood heats up intensely during sleep from the fire of the marrow, and the water within the blood is dried out by this heat. Then the diabolical art—which is already present in the first seduction during the initial coagulation <note>Hildegard refers here to the moment of conception, suggesting that the "seeds" of diabolical influence are present from the very beginning of human life.</note> when a person is conceived—arises and, with God's permission, sometimes creates a storm around the person, frightening them in their dreams. Under the pressure of a false imagination, it then appears as if the Devil were actually present. However, he is not there, because if he were, a person could not endure it. It is the same with thunderstorms: they let out terrifying peals of thunder to strike fear into people, making them tremble. Yet, the thunderstorms do not possess their full power in those moments. But on the Day of Judgment, they will show their strength in all its fullness when they strike down everything earthly.

**Of Dreams.** So too does the Devil act when he reveals himself as if in a swirling mist and torments a person until their soul, through the prophecy of dreams, has found its way back to itself and is deeply troubled, because it does not know what kind of terror it has endured. Such terrors easily befall all people in their sleep, except for those who are of a very secure and cheerful nature. ## **Of a Particular Disposition.**
But there are some other people (who are called sanguine or "full-blooded") <note>original: "blutreiche." In medieval humorism, the sanguine temperament was associated with an abundance of blood and was generally considered the most balanced, yet here Hildegard notes a specific vulnerability to black bile.</note>, in whose blood black bile <term>Schwarzgalle: Also known as "melancholy," this was one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids. An excess was thought to cause sadness, depression, and spiritual doubt.</term> frequently rises. This makes the blood black and dries up the water within it, so that these people are often severely afflicted by it both while awake and in sleep.

## **Of Adam’s Fall and Black Bile.**
For when Adam knew what was good and then made it evil by eating the apple, black bile rose within him as a retribution for this transformation. Without the prompting of the devil, this bile would not exist in a human, neither in waking nor in sleep; this is because sorrow and despair—such as Adam felt during his transgression—rise from the black bile. At the very moment Adam transgressed the divine commandment, the black bile accumulated in his blood, just as brightness fades when a light is extinguished and the smoldering wick remains with its foul odor. So it happened with Adam: after the heavenly light within him was extinguished, the black bile curdled in his blood, and from it, sorrow and despair rose within him. 

For at Adam’s fall, the devil blew the black bile into him, which sometimes turns a person into a doubter and an unbeliever. But because the human form is bound in such a way that it cannot rise above its measure <note>Hildegard suggests that human nature has inherent limits that prevent a person from becoming entirely like the devil; we are physically "tethered" to a state that requires us to look toward a higher power.</note>, the person fears God and is sorrowful; in this sadness, they often fall into despair and do not believe that God protects them. And because man is created in the image of God, he cannot cease to fear God. Therefore, it is difficult for the devil to consort with a person who resists him, because a person feels more fear toward God than the devil does; thus, the person places their hope in God, while the devil has no hope in Him. 

Often, however, the devil's suggestion works its way into the black bile and makes the person so sad and despairing that many such people, in their despair, suffocate themselves <note>This likely refers to a spiritual "suffocation" of the soul's joy, though it may also allude to the physical danger of suicidal ideation caused by "melancholy."</note> and gradually ruin themselves. But many others resist themselves so powerfully in this suffering that they stand like martyrs in this struggle.

## **Of the Oppression of the Mind.**
In this way, the devil plagues a person in waking and in sleeping, and sometimes besets them in sleep in such a way that they believe something is pressing down on them. <note>This refers back to the "Alpdruck" or nightmare mentioned on the previous page, where a spiritual weight is felt physically on the chest.</note>

**On Yawning.** When a person has eagerly begun new changes or new works, the soul rests from its aversion to this change. **On Stretching the Limbs.** If evil fevers dwell within a person, and bad humors <term>Säfte: Refers to the "four humors" (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile). The soul naturally notices some of these changes and, as if seized by disgust, recoils from the body; it raises the body itself with its vessels somewhat upward and stretches it out, just as it does when it must depart the body.

**On Lethargy.** Sometimes, bad humors also produce a smoke in certain people which rises to their brain and poisons it so that they thereafter become foolish, forgetful, and senseless.

**On Hiccups.** Also the pain which is called the hiccups <note>original: "Schlucken."</note> arises from the coldness of the stomach. This coldness is conducted around the liver and stretched around the lungs, so that the powers of the heart are also set in motion, just as when a person shudders from cold and their teeth chatter during this shivering. Thus, the person has the hiccups with the sound of their voice.

### On the Bile and Adam’s Punishment.

Before Adam had transgressed the divine commandment, that which is today called bile in man shone as brightly as crystal within him and contained the "flavor" of good works. That which is today called black bile <term>Schwarzgalle: Also known as melancholy, this humor was thought to cause depression and gloom; here, it is described as a corrupted form of an original, radiant internal light.</term> in man then radiated within him like the dawn and held within itself the awareness and the perfection of good works. But when Adam transgressed the commandment, the brilliance of innocence was darkened within him; his eyes, which previously saw heavenly things, were extinguished; the bile was turned into bitterness, the black bile into the darkness of godlessness, and he himself was completely transformed into another kind of being. Then sadness fell upon his soul, which soon sought an excuse for itself in anger. For from sadness, anger is born; this is why humanity has inherited sadness, anger, and whatever else brings them harm from their forefather.

### On Sadness and Anger.

When the soul of a person feels that something is repugnant to it and its body, it contracts the heart, the liver, and the vessels. In doing so, a kind of mist rises around the heart, enveloping it in darkness, and thus the person becomes sad. After the sadness, however, anger arises. If, in the meantime, the person has seen, heard, or thought of whatever caused their sadness, then the mist of sadness that has gripped the heart sometimes creates a warm smoke within all the humors <term>Säfte: Literally "juices." These are the four vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that medieval doctors believed governed health and personality.</term> and around the bile; this excites the bile, and so, from the bitterness of the bile, anger silently rises. If the person does not allow the anger to break out but deals with it silently, then the bile calms down again. But if the anger does not cease, then that smoke extends even to the black bile, unsettling it, and the black bile then emits a deep black mist. This mist moves over to the bile and squeezes an extremely bitter vapor out of it. Moving with this vapor toward the person's brain, both first make the person feel sick in the head; then they descend to the belly, shaking its vessels and internal organs, and make the person as if senseless. Thus the person, as if unaware of themselves, carries out their anger. For through...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physiological effects of the humors, specifically detailing how the balance of yellow and black bile dictates the nature of a person's anger and the soul's reaction through sighs and tears.</meta>

Through anger, man rages more violently than through any other mental disturbance. Man also frequently falls into severe illnesses because of anger; when the opposing humors of bile <note>original: "Galle." Here referring to yellow bile, associated with the choleric temperament.</note> and black bile are repeatedly stirred into an uproar, they occasionally make him sick. Indeed, if man did not possess the bitterness of bile and the darkness of black bile, he would always be healthy.

Why do the bile and black bile increase? A person in whom the bile is endowed with greater strength than the black bile easily tames the anger within himself. But one in whom the black bile possesses greater strength than the bile is prone to anger and is easily provoked. Just as strong and sour vinegar is made from good wine, so the bile increases from good and tasty foods and decreases from bad ones. Black bile <term>Schwarzgalle: Also known as melancholy, this humor was thought to cause gloom and physical wasting when out of balance.</term>, however, decreases from good and tasty foods, but increases from bad, bitter-tasting, unclean, and poorly prepared foods, as well as through the various fluids of different diseases. 

But he who turns red in the face during anger has blood that boils from the bile and is thus directed toward the face. Such a person is suddenly and violently moved to anger, but his anger soon subsides, like a sudden flare-up that quickly collapses; he suffers no great disadvantage from this fit of anger, nor does his body dry out. Often he departs without demanding satisfaction or seeking revenge. But he who turns pale in the face when moved by anger feels an anger that arises because his black bile is disturbed; this does not further excite the blood, but rather gradually throws his humors <note>original: "Säfte." The "juices" or fluids of the body that medieval doctors believed governed health.</note> into confusion. Because of this, the person is chilled, his strengths break and become limp, and so he turns pale in the face while hiding his anger. Meanwhile, however, an evil intention arises within him to take thorough revenge, and this lasts a long time because its owner cannot restrain himself from satisfying his anger.

### On Sighs.

Through their anger, such people often become physically ill and withered, yet their soul retains its knowledge and understanding. Therefore, when the soul considers where it comes from and what it is, it sighs deeply within, without the person knowing where the sighs come from. When the soul has noticed that some offense or grievance or some other adversity is imminent for its body, and it realizes it cannot prevent this, it sighs deeply from within.

### On Tears.

From the humors that are within man, a bitter smoke also arises through sorrow, which spreads around the heart. This overwhelms the blood-water <note>original: "Blutwasser." Likely referring to the serum or the thinner, watery components of the blood.</note>—that is, the water of the heart-blood and the blood in the remaining...

...vessels while emitting sighs, and guides it upward through the vessels—as if it were steaming—to the tiny veins of the brain and through these toward the eyes, because the eyes possess a certain kinship with water. Then this water flows out from the eyes, and these are tears. Indeed, tear-water is drawn out from a person’s blood through deep sighing in the same way that a person's seed <note>original: "Samen." Referring to the medieval understanding of reproductive fluids being derived from the blood and marrow.</note> is propelled outward from their marrow and blood. 

The tears that arise from sorrow, however, rise upward to the eyes in a kind of bitter smoke, as has already been said; they dry out the blood, cause the flesh to waste away, harm the person just as unwholesome food does, and bring a clouding to the eyes. But those tears that arise from joy are of a milder kind than those which rise from sorrow. For when the soul—prompted by tribulation and reflection—remembers that it is of a heavenly nature and is only a pilgrim here in the world, and when its body agrees with it in goodness so that they are one in holy works, then the soul sends tears gently to the eyes through the aforementioned vessels, without mist or swirling smoke, accompanied by sighs of joy and delight, letting them flow out like a lovely spring. Such tears do not attack the human heart, nor do they let the blood dry up or the flesh waste away, nor do they bring any clouding to the eyes.

**On Repentance.** When someone weeps for their sins in repentance <term>Buße: This refers to penance or the act of atonement, involving both internal contrition and outward signs of sorrow.</term>, these tears are a mixture of sorrow and joy and are shed without smoke, purely through the burdened spirit. However, because they have their basis in a spiritual oppression, they still dry the blood and thin the flesh to a certain extent, and bring some clouding to the eyes until joy is born within them through their improvement. 

But those who are born of a "fat" nature, having large vessels and a soft heart, weep easily and find joy easily. Those, however, who are born of a "dry" nature and have a heart as hard as calluses and tougher than the rest of their flesh, weep rarely and little, and are stern in their thoughts. The tears that are drawn to the eyes with sighs, however, return to the humors <note>original: "Säften." The four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were believed to determine health and temperament.</note> within the human body if they are not shed from the eyes; they make these humors sour and similar to vinegar, and they dry out the chest. Nevertheless, such tears—which reach the eyes but do not flow out, being instead retained within the person—are not particularly harmful to the eyes themselves, even though they do not drain from them.

## On Loud Laughter and Smiling. 

For just as in Adam’s transgression of the law, the holiness and chastity of the act of procreation were turned into a different kind of carnal lust, so also the voice of heavenly joys, which Adam had possessed, changed into the opposite kind of laughter and coarse laughter. Unfitting expressions of joy and laughter have, in fact, some communion with carnal lust; and the same wind that excites laughter also shakes the loins and intestines, coming from the marrow of the person. At times, laughter, through excessive shaking, drives the tear-water out from the blood of the vessels to the eyes, just as the foam of human seed <note>original: "Samen." Referring to the medieval physiological belief that reproductive fluids were a refined "foam" produced from the blood and marrow through heat.</note> is driven out from the blood of the vessels by the heat of lust.

## On Joy and Laughter. 

When the consciousness of the soul feels nothing of sorrow, adversity, or wickedness, then the heart of that person opens to joy, as flowers open to the warmth of the sun. Soon the liver also receives this joy and retains it within itself, just as the stomach retains food. Now, if a person is joyfully excited by good or bad things that please them, then the previously mentioned wind, after leaving the marrow, sometimes first strikes the loins, then seizes the spleen, fills the vessels of that same spleen, then spreads to the heart, fills the liver, thus bringing the person to laughter and making their voice similar to the voice of cattle in neighing laughter. But the person whose thoughts are driven lightly back and forth like the wind possesses a rather thick spleen, and therefore rejoices easily and likes to laugh. But just as sadness and anger weaken a person and make them withered, so immoderate laughter wounds the spleen, tires the stomach, and causes the humors <term>Säfte: The "juices" or fluids of the body (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) which medieval medicine believed must stay in balance for health.</term> to flow wrongly in their movement.

## On Obesity. 

When a person consumes excessively fatty meat or other dishes, or also excessively blood-rich foods, they become ill more easily than healthy, because such over-fatty foods—owing to the excessive and slippery moisture within them—cannot remain in the stomach long enough for a proper and healthy digestion. ### On Human Leanness.
If a person is very thin in their body and limbs, they should eat moderately fatty meat and food containing a moderate amount of blood, so that the leanness and dryness <note>The author uses the medieval concept of the four humors; "dryness" refers to a constitutional state that requires "moistening" or balancing through specific foods.</note> within them may be somewhat moistened and softened. For the consumption of animal meat makes human flesh fat, and wine increases a person's blood more than other foods or other drinks.

### On Wine.
When wine grows on soil that is fertile for grain, it is more wholesome for sick people to drink than wine grown on fruit-bearing soil—that is, soil that produces only moderate amounts of grain—even if the latter wine is more highly prized than the former. For wine heals and gladdens a person with its healthy warmth <note>original: "gesunden Wärme." A reference to the "innate heat" believed to be necessary for life and digestion in medieval medicine.</note> and its great strength.

### On Beer.
Beer, however, makes a person’s flesh fat and gives their face a beautiful color through the power and the good "sap" of the grain. Water, on the other hand, weakens a person and sometimes, if they are ill, generates mucus around the lungs because water is weak and possesses no great power. But if a person is healthy and drinks water now and then, it will not harm them.

### On Drunkenness.
If a person drinks too much wine or any other beverage by which they can become intoxicated without moderation, their entire blood becomes liquefied and flows chaotically through their veins. This causes the entire sense and reason of such a person to be thrown into disorder, just as rivers burst their banks and cause sudden flooding following excessive rain. Because a person's human insight is thoroughly confused in such a state, the "good insight" within them still drives toward the good, so that the person then frequently, without knowing it, speaks almost holy words in a disorganized and indiscriminate way. However, because the evil in their thoughts also tends toward the bad, they also—equally unknowingly—shamelessly utter shameful and bad words. This occurs because they <sup>37)</sup> possess a diseased and unrestrained mind rather than proper feeling, for their deliberation is then smothered and drowned within them.

...if cold foods dominate over warm ones, or if a person consumes an excessive amount of liquid so that liquid nourishment far exceeds the dry in quantity, then the person often suffers from vomiting in their stomach due to these conflicting forces, because in this state they cannot have proper digestion. Therefore, a person should consume their food with cold and warmth correctly balanced, so that they do not harm themselves through vomiting. However, those who suffer from vomiting due to some illness or because of an irregular diet are cold inside the stomach, so they do not have the warmth required for the food to be cooked <note>In medieval medicine, digestion was viewed as a process of "concoction" or internal cooking driven by the body's innate heat.</note> until it is fully digested. Then the only half-cooked foods rise back up because they cannot descend to their exit. Thus, the person must endure much distress through vomiting. But if a person induces vomiting themselves or uses some spice <note>In this context, "spice" (Gewürz) refers to medicinal herbs used as emetics to provoke purging.</note> to provoke it, this is neither healthy nor beneficial for them, because vomiting provoked in this way would force out their vessels and their blood and put them into a disordered state; furthermore, this vomiting would not follow the correct path outward, and so the person would often come to harm. Thus, a person should not induce vomiting in themselves, as it is of no use to their health. For vomiting that occurs unintentionally from within a person is healthier than that which one induces in oneself through some spice.

### On Poor Digestion.

When the humors <term>Säfte: literally "juices" or "saps," referring to the four bodily humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that were thought to determine health.</term>, whether due to physical weakness or unripe and unhealthy foods, are stirred up so that warmth joins with cold and cold with warmth, moisture with dryness and dryness with moisture, then the humors sometimes drive and push out the undigested food and drink with force. When such unhealthy foods are driven out, it is good for the person's health. But if the foods are good, it is detrimental to the person's physical health if they are expelled, because then the vessels are emptied of the good "sap" of such foods. And when the bad humors get the upper hand in a person, they sometimes cause a mist-like smoke <note>original: "nebelartigen Rauch." This refers to "vapors," which were thought to rise from the stomach and spread throughout the body, causing various ailments.</note> within them, which is neither warm nor cold. This spreads into the intestines, around the stomach, and through the whole body, setting in motion all the other diseases <note>original: "Seuchen." While often translated as plagues, here it refers to latent morbidities or bodily afflictions.</note> hidden within the person. It does not permit the nourishment they take in to enter the stomach through the correct and natural entrance, nor to exit by the right and natural path, but makes it surge and storm like puddles and gutters, thinning the vital and natural air within them, and therefore the food in them cannot—

### Concerning Dysentery
Very delicate, blood-containing vessels surround the thin skin—the membrane <note>original: "Membran"; likely referring to the meninges</note>—in which the human brain is hidden, and these are attached to other, stronger vessels that descend to the heart, the liver, the lungs, the stomach, and to all the other internal organs. They supply those larger vessels with blood just as small brooks carry their water to the larger rivers. The larger vessels, however, are to be compared to great rivers that guide their current through the lands, and to the pipes that bring water into buildings and let it flow out there. If, therefore, the bad humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which had to be balanced for health.</note> have gained the upper hand in a person and set the other existing fevers in motion, they then create in him a kind of improper flooding, which causes a thick, foul smoke and vapor to rise to his brain and causes all those tiny vessels surrounding the brain to move in a disordered flow. Then the blood in them overflows excessively and sets all the large vessels to which they are attached, as mentioned, into motion, so that these too pour out in a disordered current, streaming their blood throughout the whole body and sending it to the intestines and to the stool. Thus, this blood, mixed with the stool in the intestines and bowels, makes the stool bloody, so that sometimes even pure blood is discharged with the bowel movement.

### Concerning the Spitting of Blood
If bad, curdled, and poisonous humors have taken the upper hand in a person, they take away the right path from the blood that runs back and forth through the vessels of the body and force that same blood to enter the vital organs and intestines in a wrong way, whereby that person casts out blood in a dangerous manner, both through the stool and through vomiting, and dries out internally. Sometimes, however, foul humors that are watery and thin also exceed their measure in a person, opposing the blood in the vessels on its proper path and causing blood to exit through the anus without the excretion of food; this brings the person into danger and leads to the drying out of their life force. But if they have exited through the anus along with the excretion of food, they in many cases cleanse the person and restore health. Some people, however, have such bitterness in their heart and in their thoughts that this bitterness contracts and dries out the spleen. Then the healthy juice that should have nourished the spleen reaches the lungs by false paths, forms a kind of clot there with the blood, and so [the person] coughs...

<summary>This page describes the physiological causes of dysentery and internal bleeding, attributing them to disordered "humors" and vapors that disrupt the blood's natural flow. It also notes a psychosomatic connection where emotional bitterness can physically damage the spleen and lungs.</summary>
<keywords>dysentery, humors, bloodletting, anatomy of the brain, spleen health, respiratory issues, medieval physiology, digestion</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of internal medical conditions, specifically focusing on the rupture of blood vessels due to emotional distress (psychosomatic health) and the physical consequences of extreme fasting, which Hildegard describes using meteorological metaphors like storms, hail, and floods.</meta>

...and such a person often vomits blood in a dangerous manner. 

There are also other people who often have a sad and troubled heart, and this sadness contracts the small internal vessels that carry the blood through the body, so that one of them is easily injured. Therefore, it gradually spills drops of blood into the intestines, and so such a person sometimes vomits blood. But if he has regained the equanimity and cheerfulness of his spirit, the vessel returns to health, and the person stops vomiting blood.

### On Ill-Considered Fasting

If people abstain from food in an exaggerated way, so that they do not provide their body with the proper and appropriate replacement of nourishment—where some are inconsistent and frivolous in their nature, while others are oppressed by many great burdens—then it sometimes happens that "storms" <note>Hildegard frequently uses weather metaphors—storms, clouds, and lightning—to describe internal bodily disturbances.</note> occur within their body because the elements within them are brought into a disordered state. For when fire and water work against each other in such a person, it often happens that they collide in conflict in some joint or other part of the body, causing a boil with swelling of the flesh to arise. There are three types of such boils.

### On Suppurating Boils
<note>original: "Eiterbeulen"; likely referring to various types of abscesses, pustules, or carbuncles.</note>

One type is of an almost black color. It swells due to an excessive predominance of fire, brings danger to the person, and threatens death, comparable to a cloudburst that destroys and annihilates whatever it falls upon. — Another type is more gray in color and arises in a person through the "storm" in the aforementioned elements, which, as it were, flash like lightning and send out excessive rain. It indeed harms the body but usually does not kill it, like hail that damages the fruit but does not tear out its roots. — One type, however, is almost white in color and rises in a person due to an excessive swelling of the elements, as when rivers rise excessively. This type also weakens the body but does not generally have a destructive effect on it, just as a sudden rising and pouring out of rivers floods certain regions and some fields of crops but does not wash them away completely. The boil that is black is dangerous and almost incurable, while the gray and white ones are somewhat milder than the black and can be healed.

### On Swellings
<note>original: "Geschwulst"; in a medieval context, this could refer to tumors, edema (fluid retention), or general inflammation.</note>

The flesh and vessels of a person also swell through various good and bad humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the vital fluids or "juices" that Hildegard believed governed health.</note>, just as flour is leavened and swells up through yeast. The humors, however, which come from the heart, the liver, the lungs, the stomach...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of bodily fluids (humors) and their effects on physical health, moving into specific conditions like ulcers, rashes, and jaundice, before exploring the psychological and spiritual connection between the body's actions and the soul's periods of rest and recovery.</meta>

...and [those that] originate from the other internal organs, if they are once changed into a perverse, mutual relationship and to excess, they sometimes become viscous, slippery, and only lukewarm. If they then remain in the person, they make him ill. But if they break out of him, they make him healthier.

**On the Ulcer.** If such juices have fallen on one or more places on the person, so that they have produced one or more ulcers there, then the person must let these come to ripeness <note>Hildegard refers to the "maturation" or "ripening" of an abscess, where pus accumulates before it can safely be drained.</note>, so that they flow out and he does not suffer even greater pain if they remain inside. When the juices have emptied themselves in their ripeness, then the person must apply a treatment with ointments.

**On the Rash.** But if bad juices have broken out in a rash over the whole body of the person, one should likewise wait for a while until their ripeness and their outflow, until the skin between the ulcers begins to turn red and dry out, and then apply suitable ointments, so that the skin does not become even more painful through the ulcers and fall into decay by waiting too long.

**On Jaundice.** The plague <note>original: "Seuche"; used here as a general term for a disease rather than a literal epidemic.</note> which is called jaundice arises from an excess of bile, when the bile flows out due to diseased juices, fever, and great and frequent outbursts of anger. The liver and the other internal organs take up this discharge of bile, and the same discharge also penetrates the whole flesh of the person, just as a sharp vinegar penetrates a new barrel, and harms the person. Thus it is recognized in the person by its unusual color.

**On Physical Exercise and the Disquiet of the Soul.** Just as the human body is never without some activity and does not fail to always do something, the soul in the person also has the task, according to its nature, of being filled with disquiet <note>original: "Unbehagen"; suggesting a state of tension or restlessness caused by the body's demands.</note> through the various bodily actions, and occasionally secretly removing itself from its path, as if it were sleeping—like a watermill that, having been broken in some of its parts by too violent a rush of water, stops working for a time. So also the soul remains quiet and silent in between, until the body is overcome and oppressed by some kind of awe or by all sorts of terrors. Then the soul regains its powers, resumes its way, and awakens again. Then it is for the person as if he were newly created and as if he had a new character.

**On Anger.** Some people are by nature inclined to anger. When in them the soul, filled with disquiet, remains for once in silent rest, such people experience through their anger...

There are also people in whom, while their souls remain silent within them, weighed down by toil and weariness, their bodies are held in <term>fettered chains</term> <note>likely referring to a feeling of paralysis or physical heaviness</note> by a kind of impatience or doubt. Then the souls, which were sleeping in them at the same time, rise again, excited to their former strength. There are also people who, according to their whole disposition, more frequently flare up in anger.

**Of Frenzy and the Falling Sickness.** <note>Hildegard uses "falling sickness" (German: *fallende Sucht*) to refer to epilepsy.</note> The anger that resides in them often stirs their whole blood into a bloody and great flood, and from this flood, a kind of smoke and moisture attacks their brain and makes them <term>frenzied</term> <note>original: "rasend"; referring here to a state of acute mania or delirium</note>, because it also diminishes their consciousness. If such people are occasionally provoked to anger and burdened by any daily disagreements, then the devil—who notices this—frightens them through the breath of his suggestion. Then the soul that is in them sinks down, exhausted, and withdraws into itself. The body, however, collapses in a faint and lies there unconscious until the soul has gathered its strength again and stands up. Those afflicted by such an evil have an angry look and facial expression and angry movements. When they fall to the ground, they often cry out with an unnatural voice. However, the disease described here does not appear very frequently and can be suppressed only with difficulty.

**Of the Falling Sickness.** There are also other types of the same disease. In fickle, frivolous, and impatient minds, the soul within them becomes excessively faint through these qualities, frequently withdraws into itself, and succumbs. The body, thus deprived of its spiritual powers, falls to the ground and lies there as if dead until the soul has regained its strength. People of such a disposition have a winning facial expression and an engaging nature, and when they, overcome by their illness, fall to the ground, they may also cry out, but with a sad and natural tone. They discharge much foam from the mouth; nevertheless, they can be easily helped.

The heavy and manifold thoughts, which are also within them, excessively diminish the <term>phlegm</term> <note>One of the four "humors" or vital fluids in medieval medicine, believed to be cold and moist.</note> present in them, and so the water in them increases too much and gains the upper hand. For if the blood and phlegm in a person have dried up, these then expel the dregs of their digestion toward the bladder in the urine; and then the bladder, if it does not have the warmth supplied by the blood and phlegm, cannot sufficiently <term>concoct</term> <note>In medieval physiology, "concoction" was the process of "cooking" or refining food and fluids via bodily heat to turn them into useful humors.</note> the urine and then expels it, raw and "uncooked," not at the correct place of its path, but pours it, contrary to nature, between the skin and the flesh.

**On Swelling.** <note>Likely referring to edema or dropsy, a condition where fluid collects in the body's tissues.</note> For when the blood is chased away and the phlegm is diminished in such people, the water makes its incursions between the skin and flesh and causes the whole body to swell. When the water under the skin becomes recognizable in such a person, one must come to his aid with a medicine, because if one waits too long, the water turns into an insipid fluid and assumes a bloody coloration mixed with <term>serum</term> <note>original: "Blutwasser"; refers to the watery part of the blood.</note>. **On Cramps.** Such people, who are either too fat or too thin, often possess an abundance of bad humors because they do not have the correct constitution and the middle proportion of the humors within them. Thus, bad humors sometimes rise up from the heart, the liver, the lungs, the stomach, and the intestines, reaching the <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "Schwarzgalle"; the humor associated with melancholy and cold, dry conditions.</note>, causing it to steam up and creating a very wicked mucus within the person. It is somewhat like when, in a standing, non-flowing body of water, foul mud sometimes overgrows and overflows the bank. This mucus now reaches either the stomach or between the intestines, or even some other place between the skin and flesh, sticks there, and torments the person with much distress, as if it were biting and gnawing at him. It does not, however, possess the <term>vital spirit</term> <note>original: "Lebensgeist"; the animating force that allows for intentional movement or life.</note> that is necessary to actually bite the person, but only a kind of harsh bitterness. ## On Worms
If this foam ever penetrates the stomach, it causes a type of worm to bubble forth within it, and likewise, it causes the growth of a type of very malignant, thin-bodied lice in the flesh. In those places where <term>phlegm</term> <note>One of the four "humors" or vital fluids in medieval medicine, associated with cold and moisture.</note> lies within the human body, extremely thin little worms sometimes grow from the aforementioned foam; these are called <term>bacon maggots</term> <note>original: "Speckmaden." Likely referring to larvae or skin-burrowing parasites like *Dermestes* or perhaps a type of mite.</note>, much like how small worms sometimes arise in water that stands in one place and does not flow. If such worms then remain within the person and do not leave him again, they harm him greatly.

## On Roundworms
Worms easily arise in humans—specifically in very young and older children, because their <term>humors</term> <note>The "Säfte" or vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, black bile) that medieval doctors believed governed health and personality.</note> are still mixed with milk—from harmful, bad, and poisonous fluids that collect like phlegm and are transformed into a harmful, thin broth, similar to stale, spoiled wine. Worms are not born from a person’s habitual [healthy] humors, nor from humors that are acidic like vinegar. Should worms somehow begin to grow in people who carry their habitual humors within them, they nonetheless disappear again within a short time. However, if such worms grow to full size in a person, they drain him of his strength.

## On Lice
There are people of slender bone and limb structure, with thin vessels, but with fatty, healthy, and well-developed flesh that is neither too closed off nor too permeable. They possess a full <term>marrow</term> <note>In this context, marrow refers not just to the substance inside bones, but to a vital, heat-producing core that influences intelligence and vitality.</note> of the correct warmth and therefore also a wholesome and capable disposition—though here and there somewhat inclined toward boasting—and they are easily impressed and sensitive. Because they possess such full marrow, they also have a dense but tender fat that is white, healthy, and free of vermin. However, if this fat should ever excrete sweat, this sweat allows occasional lice to arise on the outside of the skin and nourishes them. 

Other people, however, have coarse bones, clumsy limbs, and thick vessels, but possess only moderately developed and not very warm marrow. Because of the weakness of their marrow, they have only a moderate intellect; they are gluttonous, eat often, and can work powerfully for a short time, but cannot endure for long at their work. For their flesh is quite permeable and somewhat lukewarm, and their vessels are rich...

<summary>This page explains the medieval belief that parasites like worms and lice are spontaneously generated from imbalanced or "spoiled" bodily humors. It also describes how physical traits—such as bone structure and marrow quality—determine a person's temperament, intelligence, and susceptibility to lice.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the classification of human temperaments based on their internal physiology—specifically the relationship between bone marrow, flesh density, and the production of sweat and parasites. ...quite narrow. Because they have a lean marrow, their fat is also thinner and weaker. When such people sweat, the perspiration quickly penetrates through the flesh because it is quite permeable; this produces a multitude of lice within the flesh, which pour out from the person immoderately. However, such people are not very weak and can live for quite a long time. 

Still other people have sturdy bones and limbs and thick vessels, along with firm, fatty marrow. Because their bones are filled with hot marrow, and due to the density, richness, and abundance of that marrow, such people are clever and upright. Their flesh is strong, rather firm, and not very permeable, because it is bound by taut vessels. For just as the individual threads of a net are connected to one another, so too do the vessels throughout the entire human body hang together. Because the flesh of these people is quite strong and hard, they produce only a little thin sweat; the thick and strong vessels surround their flesh so closely that they can excrete only a small amount of perspiration. 

Due to the great quantity and warmth of the marrow and the excess of <term>humors</term> <note>The "Säfte" or vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval medicine taught must be kept in balance for health.</note> within them, and because these fluids cannot escape, the fat within them is changed slightly into the redness of blood and is weak and unhealthy. Then lice grow abundantly within the fat; they cannot pass through the dense flesh to the outside, but remain in the fat, piercing and consuming it everywhere. As a result, these people suffer many pains in their bodies and do not know why they are suffering. They are sluggish, find joy in nothing, eat little, and often suffer from attacks of heart and bodily weakness. They also have a pale complexion, which appears more green than wax-colored. Such people cannot live long but die early because their fat, as just explained, is damaged internally by lice.

### On the Stone
<note>This refers to the formation of "stones" (calculi) in the bladder or kidneys, a common and painful ailment in the Middle Ages.</note>

A person who is in the prime of life, has soft, moisture-rich flesh, and constantly enjoys all kinds of tasty foods and heavy, noble wine, easily develops a stone. From the aforementioned food and drink—especially when the flesh is soft and moist—a kind of sediment gathers in the place where the urine should flow out and hardens into a stone. Due to the warmth and strength of the urine, the stone is quite soft as long as it remains inside the person; the sufferer voids urine slowly and only in small amounts, and with great difficulty. If the stone within the person were hard, they would perish in a short time. This occurs in both women and men, though a man's urine is stronger than a woman's; nevertheless, a man suffers greater pain from the stone than a woman. When boys or small children are plagued by the stone, however, this stems from the poor and unsuitable quality of the wet-nurses'—

### On Gluttony
In those people who are healthy and of robust build with strong tendons, but who are inclined toward drink and are eager for the enjoyment of meat and other savory foods and drinks, the blood takes on a wax-like color and continues to thicken. Because the blood, due to its thick consistency, cannot follow its proper path—nor is it thinned by bouts of fever or physical weakness in such people, precisely because they are healthy—it penetrates their flesh and skin, saturating them with a harmful <term>humor</term> <note>In medieval medicine, "humors" were bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) that governed health and personality.</note>, polluting them so to speak, and filling them with ulcers.

### On the Ulcers of Leprosy
However, even poor people who are physically healthy and possess the same inclination toward gluttony, but cannot constantly have choice foods and drinks, can contract the disease just described in three, two, or even just one week by letting whatever foods and drinks are available to them pass through their throats in an unregulated manner. One can even observe this in young people and children. For this disease often enough comes from eating meat, from various types of milk, and from strong wine, but not from bread, nor from vegetables, nor from beer.

### On Leprosy
There are also other people with a coarse consistency of flesh in their bodies who are prone to sudden fits of anger; their rage excites the blood within them so much that it sinks down around the liver, and the hardness as well as the blood of this liver <note>The author views the liver as a central organ for processing blood and emotions.</note> mixes with this blood. Thus it distributes itself through the person's entire body and throws their flesh and skin into disorder, so that the skin becomes cracked and the nostrils become thick and swell while cracking. 

Other people, however, are afflicted with unbridled sensuality, so that they neither possess nor wish to possess temperance. Because of this, their blood is repeatedly set into a disordered state of agitation <note>original: "Wallung"; a surging or "boiling" of the blood believed to be caused by intense emotion or arousal.</note> like the contents of a pot placed on the fire, which neither boils completely nor is entirely cold, and therefore retains its impurities because it does not have enough strength to cast them out. Now, when such people burn so fiercely in their desire that their blood is repeatedly set into a state of disordered excitement, such that it neither [attains] proper...

Blood is neither true water nor even true foam; instead, it is transformed into foul slime and discharge <note>original: "Jauche"; referring to putrid liquid or pus from a wound.</note>, which destroys the flesh and skin of a person and turns them into ulcers.

**On the Signs of Leprosy.** The aforementioned diseases are distinguished from one another in this way: Leprosy <term>Aussatz</term> <note>While "Aussatz" is translated as leprosy, in the Middle Ages it often referred to a broad category of skin diseases, not just Hansen's disease.</note> that stems from gluttony and drunkenness causes red-colored swellings and red skin rashes to appear, similar to dragon-growths <note>original: "Drachengeschwülste"; likely a reference to specific scaly or inflamed skin formations described in medieval medical texts.</note>. If leprosy originates from the liver, however, it creates cracks in the skin and flesh of the person that reach down to the bone, along with black-colored spots. But leprosy as a result of lust <note>original: "Wollust"; referring to sexual excess.</note> produces wide surfaces of ulcers, like tree bark, with red flesh beneath them. The first two types mentioned are brought to healing only with great difficulty; the third, by contrast, heals easily.

**On Gout.** People who have soft flesh permeated with many pores and who are very devoted to the excessive enjoyment of heavy wine are frequently visited by the plague called "the drop" <term>Gicht</term> <note>original: "der Tropfen." In medieval medicine, gout (from the Latin *gutta*, meaning drop) was believed to be caused by a "drop" of morbid humor falling into a joint.</note>. In people with soft flesh, due to immoderate drinking, bad humors <note>original: "Säfte"</note> that are within them suddenly fall into one of their limbs and destroy it like fire-arrows or great, unforeseen floods that sometimes destroy mills and other buildings in their immediate vicinity. Similarly, these humors would destroy the limbs onto which they drip if divine grace and the life-spirit within the person did not prevent it. Nevertheless, they still destroy many a limb and make some entirely useless, as if they were dead.

**On the Contraction of the Tendons.** It frequently happens that the tempests and storms of the bad humors fall upon some limb of a person and there, in their senselessness, block the path of the blood in its vessels, so that it cannot flow through them. As a result, these vessels dry up because they lack the passage for the blood. Then the person begins to limp. And because man is created from the elements, he is also sustained by the elements and is transformed in them and with them.

**On Fevers.** For this reason, he also contains within himself various fevers arising from the changing properties of the air and the other elements—that is, from heat, cold, and moisture. These do not bring him down or cause him harm; rather, they create health for him, as they [cleanse] his chest, stomach, and all internal organs through sweat and through

<meta>This page continues the discussion of bodily health and illness, moving from the specific types of leprosy and gout to how environmental conditions like air temperature and moisture influence the development of different types of fevers and the movement of bodily humors.</meta>

cleanse the urine, provided that they do not exceed their limits and the air also maintains its proper measure in moderate warmth. But if an excessive and unusual heat has arisen in the air, then sometimes in certain individuals those fevers, becoming excessively inflamed, are transformed into a burning fever. In cases of excessive cold in the air, however, they are so constricted by the cold that they cause a three-day fever <note>original: "dreitägiges Fieber"; historically known as a tertian fever, often associated with malaria where the paroxysm occurs every third day.</note>. But in a damp condition of the air, which is watery and smells foul, they are often transformed into such a slimy clot that from this, they bring the four-day fever <note>original: "viertägige Fieber"; known as a quartan fever, occurring every fourth day.</note> to many people. Physically healthy people, however, who do not carry within them overly mobile <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säfte"; refers to the four bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) which medieval medicine believed must be balanced for health.</note> that can be driven here and there, sometimes fall ill when the condition of the air is not moderately warm, while they are healthy when the warmth is correct and the air behaves calmly. Once they have contracted a burning fever and it is God's will that they recover, they discharge sweat with pain on the fifth or seventh day and soon become healthy, because they were also healthy in their body beforehand.

### On Paralysis.

But those who have within them easily moved <term>humors</term>, that is, those who preserve no stability of their proper behavior in neither heat nor cold nor moisture, but are tossed to and fro in the bodies of men like the inconstant air—like some rivers and water waves that devastate much land, and like ice that is not properly frozen but still brittle—these are plagued by <term>paralysis</term>, that is, the <term>gout</term> <note>original: "Gicht"; in this context, it refers to various joint and limb pains or "the drop," believed to be caused by humors dripping into the joints.</note> through such humors. If they have once had a burning <term>fever</term> and their life should be prolonged by God's grace, then before they sweat with pain, they will often lie in their ailments until the twentieth or thirtieth or an even later day, because they were often already frail and weak beforehand. For if a person suffers from all kinds of ailments and anxieties, as well as from the consequences of unsuitable food and drink, so that through the unsuitable meals and drinks, perverse humors and mucus <note>original: "Schleime"; referring to phlegm or metabolic waste.</note> accumulate in him, then the soul—shaken and exhausted by these adversities—succumbs, so that it ceases its life-movements to a certain degree.

### On Fever and the Critical Days.

Thus the harmful <term>humors</term> that are in the human being are set in motion, and the <term>fevers</term> increase because the soul has restricted its life-activity. Then the blood in the person also decreases, and the intestines and other internal parts dry out. The heat, which ought to be present in the <term>liver</term> and the other internal parts for life, rises to the outer skin, and the internal cold remains

It usually does this for up to seven days, because it could not detach itself from those <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säfte"; referring to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were believed to govern health and temperament.</note> and <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "Schleime"; often used interchangeably with the humor 'phlegm' or indicating morbid discharge.</note> until that point. But once it has noticed that the onslaught of these fluids begins to subside through God's grace, it then comes to the realization that it can free itself from them. So it gathers its strength once more and drives those humors and phlegm out of the body through sweat. And in this way, the person regains their health. 

Very often, however, it happens that the soul—due to the excessive heat and cold of these humors—cannot completely expel them through sweat. Gripped by fear due to the person's happy or sad mood, or even through their impatience or states of anxiety, the soul withdraws into itself and remains in silence again. It can then sometimes take until the third, fifth, seventh, or thirtieth morning—or even more or fewer days, as previously mentioned—until it once again reaches the awareness that it can resume its ability to revitalize the body through God's grace. If such a person is to return to bodily health, they will suffer less in the following period than before, because the humors that were previously within them have already lost some of their volume through the first outbreak of sweat. But if the soul is so entangled in harmful humors and phlegm that it is unable to expel them from this person's body, and if it has felt that divine grace is not standing by its side in this effort, then it is defeated; it submits and leaves the body according to divine decree.

### On burning fever. 
If a person suffers from a burning fever <note>original: "hitzigen Fieber"; likely referring to a high, continuous fever such as that caused by acute infection.</note>, their humors are transformed into a violently burning glow within them, and the heat of these fluids does not permit such a person to take in food. Instead, it drives them to drink because of the excessive dryness in their interior, which is why they should drink water so that they suffer less. When a burning fever has seized a person, however, it is not healthy for them to take medicine to drive it away, because it will not completely leave them until the outbreak of sweat; rather, depending on the medicine taken, it remains hidden within them all the longer and causes them to be sick for a greater duration, because the harmful humors have not been expelled from them openly and in the manner they rightfully should be. Sometimes, however, fevers arise from excess in eating and drinking, as well as from sleeping too long, or even from boredom and laziness when a person does not keep themselves occupied.

The daily fever <note>original: "tägliche Fieber"; also known as a quotidian fever, where paroxysms occur every day.</note> arises from an abundance of water, and certainly also from <term>phlegm</term>...
