The three-day fever <term>tertian fever</term> <note>A fever recurring every third day, historically associated with malaria or specific humoral imbalances.</note> develops from too much dryness, similar to the nature of fire; the four-day fever <term>quartan fever</term>, on the other hand, arises from an abundance of black bile <term>melancholia</term> <note>One of the four humors, associated with the earth and a cold, dry temperament.</note>. Also, a person who has soft flesh on their body—of such a kind that the phlegm, which is viscous, lukewarm, and unstable, predominates—easily develops worms within their flesh. Because their flesh is soft and the phlegm within it is weak and viscous, ulcers easily break out, in which the worm is easily born and causes harm to the person.

### On Diet.

Just as curdled milk is continually added to cheese being pressed in its container until it is fully prepared, so too must food and drink be constantly given to the very young child as well as the older one, until they have reached the proper age. Otherwise, a small or older child would not be able to grow, but would perish. But food and drink are also appropriate for the elderly and those who are physically diminished, since their blood and flesh are decreasing and both must be sustained by nourishment. For man is like the earth. If the earth has had too much moisture, it is damaged by it; if, on the other hand, it has been touched by only a little or perhaps no moisture at all, then this will not be beneficial for its fertility. If it has received the proper amount of moisture, it is good for it. So it is also with man. If a person suffers from too much or too abundant and liquid moisture in their interior—that is, in the eyes, ears, nose, and mouth—they will become more sick than healthy. If, however, they have only a little or no flowing fluid in these parts, this is dangerous for them. But if they possess exactly enough moisture, it serves their health.

### III.

The remedies specified below for the diseases previously discussed are shown by God and will either restore a person to health, or else they must die, or it is God's will that they not be freed from their illness. <note>This reflects the medieval belief that while medicine was a gift from God, the ultimate outcome of life and death remained in divine hands.</note>

**On Hair Loss.** If a young person’s hair begins to fall out prematurely, they should take bear fat and some ash prepared from wheat or spelt straw and rub these together. Then, they should anoint their entire head with this mixture, especially in the places where the hair is beginning to fall out. Afterward, they must refrain from cleaning this ointment off their head by washing for a long time. The hair that has not yet fallen out is so moistened and strengthened by this application that it will not fall out for a long time. They must proceed in this manner often and avoid washing their head. For the inherent warmth of bear fat is of such a nature that it tends to produce abundant hair, and the ash of wheat or spelt straw strengthens the hair so that it does not fall out early. If both are mixed together according to the above instructions, they protect the person's hair from falling out all the longer.

**On Headaches caused by Black Bile.** When black bile <term>melancholia</term> <note>One of the four humors of ancient and medieval medicine, thought to be cold and dry. An excess was believed to cause various physical and mental ailments.</note>, stirred by various fevers, causes pain in a person’s brain, they should take mallow and twice as much sage, crush them in a mortar into a pulp, and sprinkle this with a little olive oil—or, if they have no oil, with a little vinegar. This should be applied from the forehead over the crown of the head to the back of the neck, and simultaneously bound with a cloth. This should be done for three days. During these three days, the olive oil or vinegar should be renewed at night, and they should continue in this manner until they feel better. For the juice of the mallow dissolves the black bile, while the juice of the sage dries it out; the olive oil soothes the afflicted head, and the vinegar draws out the sharpness of the black bile. Therefore, these remedies mixed together alleviate this headache.

**On Senselessness.** If someone's brain has cooled and they have thereby become senseless <note>original: "besinnungslos"; likely referring to delirium, a state of confusion, or a loss of consciousness attributed to a lack of "vital heat" in the brain.</note>, take laurels...

Prepare a dough by adding thistle water <note>original: "Distelwasser"</note>. After the patient's hair has been shaved off, apply this dough to the entire head and secure it with a cap made of felt so that the head becomes warm internally and the patient can fall asleep. This brings warmth to the brain. If the dough dries out, prepare another in the same manner, place it on the head, and repeat this often. Then the patient will regain their senses.

## On One-Sided Headaches <note>Migraines</note>

Whoever suffers from a one-sided headache should take aloe and twice the amount of myrrh and pulverize both very finely. Then, take wheat flour and add poppy seed oil to all of this. Prepare a mass, much like sourdough, and cover the entire head with this dough down to the ears and the neck. After putting a cap over it, leave it on the head for three days and three nights. For the warmth of the aloe and the dryness of the myrrh, tempered by the softness of the wheat flour and the coldness of the poppy seed oil, will remove this headache; a dough prepared in this way restores the "fatness" <note>In Hildegard's medical system, the "fatness" of the brain refers to its healthy moisture and vitality, which prevents it from becoming brittle or "dry."</note> to the brain.

## On Headaches Caused by Vapors of the Stomach

If a food containing a corrupt humor <note>original: "verdorbenen Saft"; literally "spoiled juice," referring to the digestive fluids or "humors" that were believed to rise as harmful vapors to the head.</note> causes pain in a person's head, they should take equal parts by weight of sage, marjoram, and fennel, and an amount of horehound <note>original: "Andorn"</note> that is more than the total weight of the others. After grinding these herbs into a pulp, add enough butter—or if butter is unavailable, add fat <note>lard</note>—to make an ointment. Rub the head with this, and the person will feel better. For sage, marjoram, and horehound are dry by nature and therefore dry out the aforementioned humors. The juice of the fennel, however, is moist, and this softens the effect of the dried-out humors. Therefore, when an ointment is prepared from them with butter or fat, which are healing, they relieve the aforementioned headache. — Take olive oil and slightly less rose water and bring both to a boil in a pan. Meanwhile, take nightshade <note>original: "Nachtschatten"</note>, a good half as much as the olive oil, grind it in a mortar, strain it through a cloth, and pour the juice into the pan with the aforementioned oil and rose water. Let everything boil together once more. Once you have done this, strain it again through a cloth and pour the strained liquid into a new earthen vessel. Then, rub the patient on the crown of the head, the skull, the forehead, and the temples with this ointment whenever they are in pain, and wrap the forehead and temples with a wax-saturated linen cloth so that the ointment is not wiped away.

### On Headaches Caused by Phlegm.
Whoever suffers from a headache in the forehead due to an excess of phlegm <term>phlegm</term> <note>In medieval medicine, phlegm was one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids. It was considered cold and moist; an excess was thought to cause sluggishness and dull, heavy pain.</note> should chew a white pea⁸) with their teeth and mix the chewed pea with the purest honey. This should be applied to the temples and held in place with a bandage. Continue this process until they feel better. For the pea is of a somewhat phlegmatic nature, but the white variety grows in pure, good soil. When chewed into a paste and placed on the temples, it removes the sharpness of this pain, because the vessels in the temples contain the strength of the forehead. Furthermore, the warmth of the honey, which is gathered from various plants, softens the coldness of the phlegm, as has already been stated.

### Concerning Afflictions of the Lungs.
Take galangal⁹) and fennel in equal parts, twice as much nutmeg, and as much pellitory¹⁰) <note>original: "Bertram"; Spanish chamomile (Anacyclus pyrethrum), a pungent root used to stimulate digestion and clear the head.</note> as the first two combined—ensuring that the nutmeg and pellitory are also in equal balance. Grind these into a powder and mix them together. Every day, while fasting, eat as much of this powder as weighs two pennies <note>original: "zwei Pfennige schwer"; a common medieval measurement based on the weight of a standard silver coin.</note> with a small morsel of bread, and immediately afterward drink some warmed wine. Also, eat other noble herbs that have a fine scent repeatedly, both while fasting and after breakfast, so that their good fragrance may travel to the lungs and suppress foul-smelling breath. 

However, anyone who suffers in any way with their lungs should abstain from eating fatty meat, as well as foods heavy in blood and aged cheese, which create pus in the vicinity of the lungs. They should also not eat peas or lentils, raw fruit, or uncooked vegetables; likewise, they should avoid nuts and oil. If they wish to enjoy meat, they should eat lean meat, and if they eat cheese, it should be neither aged nor unripened, but rather lean cheese. If they wish to use oil, let them use it in moderation. They must not drink water, however, because this produces mucus in the lungs. They should not drink young, raw must <note>unfermented grape juice</note> that has not yet cast off its impurities through fermentation; beer, on the other hand, does not harm them much because it has been boiled. They should not drink wine and must beware of damp, foggy air.

### On Mindlessness.
When, through excessive and repetitive pondering, a person's consciousness and feeling are brought to naught so that they fall into mindlessness <note>original: "Sinnlosigkeit"; refers to a state of mental exhaustion, confusion, or a loss of sensory awareness.</note>, they should take balsam herb¹¹) <note>original: "Balsamkraut"; likely referring to costmary or a type of mint known for its aromatic, clarifying properties.</note> and three times as much fennel. Boil both together in water and, after the herbs have been removed, drink the cooled water repeatedly. For the juice of the balsam herb tames the bad humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the bodily fluids or "juices" believed to govern health and temperament.</note> and holds them back so that they do not recklessly exceed their measure; it also brings the person back to their senses. The juice of the fennel, however, draws these senses back toward a proper and cheerful mood. These herbs, taken in the correct proportion and boiled with soft water, ensure that the—

But they should also not drink plain water, because this would lead their senses to even greater emptiness. Instead, they should drink the beverage mentioned above as well as beer, both of which maintain the suppressed humors and the senses in their proper state and turn the rage of senselessness away from them. They should cover their head with a cap made of either felt or pure linen until the brain, which has been cooled by the suppressed humors, becomes gently and gradually warm again—but not suddenly or excessively, so that they do not fare even worse due to too rapid and too strong a warming. — Take nutmeg and twice as much galangal, pulverize both, and add iris root <note>original: "Schwertel"; referring to the root of the Iris plant, used in ancient medicine for its aromatic and drying properties.</note> and plantain root <note>original: "Wegerichwurzel"; a common herb used to cool inflammation.</note> in equal parts, though the weight of both should be less than that of the nutmeg. Pound everything together after adding a little salt. From all this, make a thin morning drink with wheat flour and water, and give it to the sick person to drink.

### On the Weakening of the Eyes.

When the blood and water in a person's eyes have decreased too much due to old age or some illness, they should go to a green meadow and look at it until their eyes become wet as if from crying. This is because the greenness of the grass removes the dimness found in the eyes and makes them pure and clear. One can also go to a river or pour fresh water into a vessel and, leaning over it, catch the moisture of this water with the eyes; this moisture stimulates the water in the eyes—which was already beginning to dry out—and makes them clear again. One can also take a linen cloth, dip it in pure, cold water, and tie it over the temples and eyes. In doing so, one must be careful not to touch the inner part of the eyes, so that they do not become ulcerated by the water. Therefore, you should use a soft linen rag and cold water to [cleanse] the...

## On Gray Eyes
If someone has <term>gray eyes</term> <note>original: "graue Augen"; in medieval medicine, this often referred to cataracts or glaucoma rather than just eye color.</note>, and sees poorly with them or feels pain in them, they should take fennel or its seeds when the pain is fresh. They should crush it, take the juice, and add the dew found on standing grass, along with some fine wheat flour, and knead this together like a small cake. They should place this over their eyes at night, securing it with a bandage, and they will feel better. The mild warmth of the fennel, softened by the dew and the strength of the wheat flour, removes these pains. For gray eyes are of an airy nature, and therefore dew is added to these soothing remedies.

## On Fiery Eyes
If a person who has <term>fiery eyes</term> <note>likely referring to inflammation, redness, or a "burning" sensation.</note> cannot see properly with them and feels pain in them, they should take violet juice, twice as much rose juice, and a third as much fennel juice as the rose juice. They should add a little wine and rub this eye-water around the outside of their eyes before going to sleep, taking care that the eyes are not accidentally harmed by its strong effect. However, if it happens to touch the inside of the eyes slightly, they will not suffer much from it. Because these eyes originate from fire, the mild coldness of the violet and the rose—when tempered by the gentle effect of the fennel and the warmth of the wine—dispels the <term>dimness</term> <note>original: "Trübsichtigkeit"; clouded or blurry vision.</note> and the pain in such eyes, because all these remedies turn green in summer and grow in moderate heat. The violet emerges first during warm winds, and therefore, with the mentioned soothing agents, it makes these eyes healthy again.

## On Eyes of Indeterminate Color
Anyone who has eyes similar to a cloud in which a rainbow shines <note>This likely describes an iridescent or clouded appearance, perhaps related to severe cataracts or corneal issues.</note>, and suffers from weak vision or any pain in them, should take <term>calamine</term> <note>original: "Galmei"; a zinc ore historically used in ointments and eye-salves.</note> and place it in pure white wine. At night, when they wish to go to sleep, they should remove the calamine and brush the wine onto the eyelashes from the outside, with caution not to touch the inside of the eyes so that they are not harmed by the sharpness of the calamine and made even weaker in sight. Calamine possesses an equal amount of heat and cold and, when tempered with the warmth of the wine, removes the bad <term>humors</term> <note>The bodily fluids believed to cause illness when imbalanced.</note> which make the eyes sick. ### On Cloudy Eyes
If someone has eyes like a cloudy cloud, which is neither entirely fiery nor entirely dim, but rather discolored toward a greenish hue, and suffers from weak vision and pain, they should, in the summertime, crush fennel herb, or in winter, its seeds. They should stir the crushed matter into carefully clarified egg white and, when preparing for sleep, place it on the eyes. The gentle warmth of the fennel, softened by the coldness of the egg white, reduces the weakness and pain in such eyes. For fennel mixed in the proper proportion with egg white, which has a slightly bluish tint, provides an effective remedy for eyes that have become ill from bluish-colored <term>earthy moisture</term> <note>In Hildegard’s system, health was a balance of various "moistures" or humors; an excess of "earthy" moisture was thought to cause dullness or opacity in the organs.</note>.

### On Black Eyes
Whoever has black or darkened eyes—resembling the way a cloud sometimes looks—and can see poorly with them and suffers pain, should take <term>rue juice</term> <note>original: "Rautensaft"; juice from the common rue plant, which was highly regarded in the Middle Ages for sharpening the eyesight.</note> and twice as much pure, liquid honey, and mix in some pure, good wine. They should place a small piece of wheat bread into this mixture and, securing it with a bandage, keep it on the eyes overnight. Because such eyes are of an "earthy" nature, they benefit from the warmth of the rue and the warmth of the honey, combined with that of the wine, which has drawn its juice from the earth, especially when a piece of wheat bread is added, which likewise draws its strength from the earth.

### On the White Spot of the Eyes
If someone suffers from a <term>white spot</term> <note>original: "weißen Fleck"; likely referring to a corneal scar, leukoma, or perhaps a cataract.</note> on the eyes, they should, if the spot is still fresh, take fresh <term>ox gall</term> <note>original: "Ochsengalle"; bile from an ox. In pre-modern medicine, the bitter and corrosive properties of bile were used to "eat away" films or growths on the eye.</note> and place it, while fresh, on the eyes at night. They should secure it with a bandage so that it does not slip off, and do this for three days, because the bitterness of this gall breaks up and removes the pain. However, one should do this cautiously, so as not to cause harm by exceeding the proper measure. After the three days have passed, they should take <term>fenugreek</term> <note>original: "Bockshornklee"; a plant used for its soothing, mucilaginous properties to calm inflammation.</note>, dip it in rose oil, and place it on the eyes, as this acts more mildly. They should secure it with a bandage, because the moderate cold of the fenugreek, combined with the mild nature of the rose oil, more easily removes the white spot that was broken up by the aforementioned gall.

Whoever has weeping eyes, as if they were constantly tearing, should pick a fig leaf that, during the night...

[...take a leaf that] has been thoroughly wetted by dew, once the sun has already warmed it upon its branch, and place it while it is still warm upon the eyes to restrict their moisture; keep it there until the leaf becomes warm from the body's heat. If one has no fig leaf, let them take an <term>alder leaf</term> <note>original: "Erlenblatt"</note>, which has likewise been wetted by the nightly dew and thereafter warmed by the rays of the sun, and place it upon the eyes as directed. One should not do this every day, but only every third day, and only once during that day.

After this has been moderately warmed upon a hot brick or at a heated and covered hearth, it should be applied around the eyes while it remains warm. This should be done once a day every fourth day, so that one does not damage the weeping eyes more than helping them by over-treatment. For the warmth of the fig leaves and the coldness of the alder leaves are of such a nature that they draw moisture to themselves. They must, however, be thoroughly wetted by dew so that their vital force is tempered by its mild effect, and also be touched by the sun's warmth, which lends a delicacy to their sap so that it does not harm the eyes. The resin of the peach or plum tree, however, possesses the inherent power of these trees and, according to its nature, draws the moisture of the eyes into itself using the heat it has received from the brick or the hearth. This heat must only be moderate and not too strong. In this way, as has been said, the tearing of the eyes will cease.

**On the Decline of Hearing.** If a person's power of hearing is damaged by any <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "Phlegma"; in medieval medicine, an excess of this cold, moist humor was thought to cause blockages in the head and sense organs.</note> or any other kind of illness, they should take white frankincense, produce smoke with it over glowing coals, and let this smoke rise into the blocked ear. However, they should not do this too often, so that they do not find themselves in a worse state by exceeding the proper measure. For the warm smoke of white frankincense, which is purer than that of other varieties, being stirred by the external fire, drives out the harmful vapor that brings disorder to a person's brain and hearing.

**On Toothache.** Whoever suffers in their teeth from <term>putrid blood</term> <note>medieval practitioners believed that "corrupt" blood or stagnant fluids (the "excretion of the brain") caused localized pain and decay.</note> or through the excretion of the brain, should take equal weights of wormwood<gloss>wormwood</gloss> and vervain<gloss>vervain</gloss>, boil them in a new vessel with pure, good wine, strain the boiled wine through a cloth, and drink it after adding a little sugar. The herbs, still warm and boiled as just described, should be placed upon the jaw where the teeth ache at bedtime, and secured with an applied cloth. This should be done until the person is healed. For the wine, tempered with the aforementioned herbs and then drunk, cleanses the small vessels which lead from...

For when the warmth of the aloe and the warmth of the myrrh are stimulated by the steady heat and cold of the burning coals, the power of their smoke destroys the toothworms.

## Concerning Lung Pain.

When bad, foul-smelling humors send their vapors toward the human brain, which in turn directs those same vapors to the lungs and causes them pain, such a person should take **lungwort** and boil it with water, but not with wine, because this herb boiled with wine would act too violently; pour the decoction into a pot and, strained through a cloth, drink it over the course of a week. Once the drink is consumed, he must prepare a fresh one in the same manner. He should drink it every day on an empty stomach and after eating until he is healed. For the lungs are often weakened by heart pain and the heat of the stomach, but the coldness of this healing herb, tempered by the soft nature of the water, removes this weakness. — Take **juniper berries**, twice as much **mullein** <note>original: "Wollkraut"</note> as juniper berries, and twice as much **pellitory** as there is mullein, and boil these in pure, good wine. When you have done that, pour it into a pot, add raw **elecampane** <note>original: "Alant"</note> cut into small pieces, and drink a little of what is strained through a cloth for two to three weeks on an empty stomach. Likewise, you can also drink it after your meal until you are healthy again. — Take also **dill** and three times as much **lovage** and just as much **nettle** as dill, and fill a pot with these, boiled with pure, good wine. As long as it still retains its scent, you can drink from it, after it is strained through a cloth, on an empty stomach and after breakfast, but only a little and with caution. There are also other people who were <term>conceived in foggy and damp weather</term> <note>In medieval medical theory, the environmental conditions at the moment of conception were believed to influence a person's lifelong constitution and temperament.</note> and for this reason constantly have a putrid and foul-smelling breath and an ill-smelling sweat. Their putrid breath and the bad humors that dwell within them penetrate to the brain and make it sick and weary, so that they frequently forget themselves. When they expel the **phlegm** from their head, they suffer less because their brain is being purified. If—

### Concerning the Hardening of the Liver.

When someone has consumed many kinds of **foods** without **moderation** and without **selection**, and his **liver** becomes damaged and hard due to the various **humors** of these **foods**, he should take small **coltsfoot** <note>item 26 in original</note>, twice as much **plantain roots**, and as much **slime** found in **pear-tree mistletoe** <note>item 27 in original</note> as there is **coltsfoot**. He should cut the aforementioned **herbs** into small, thin **pieces**, pierce them with a **bodkin** <note>a small pointed tool for piercing holes</note> or some other sharp **tool**, place the aforementioned **mistletoe slime** into the **holes**, and then lay them in pure **wine**. One can also take the weight of a **heller** <note>a small silver coin used here as a unit of measurement</note> of the **swelling** <note>likely a gall or growth</note> that has developed on the **leaf** and thin **branch** of a **walnut tree**, about the size of a **bean** or **pea**, place it in the same **wine**, and then—after breakfast as well as while fasting—drink that which has not been **boiled**, but only **steeped** in the **wine**. For the **warmth** and the **coldness** of the **coltsfoot** drive out the **swelling** of the **liver**, while the **warmth** of the **plantain root** protects the **liver** from curdling and hardening. The **coldness** of the **mistletoe slime** reduces the **phlegm** within it, and the **knot** that sits on the **leaf** or the thin **branch** of the **walnut** removes, through its **bitterness**, the bad **humors** that make the **liver** ill. All of this must not be boiled, but only prepared with unboiled **wine**, so that it may reach the **liver** more easily. The **sufferer** may also frequently drink **mulberry wine** <note>item 28 in original</note>, because **liver pain** in many **cases** stems from an excess of **blood**, which is calmed by the **warmth** and the **juice** of the **mulberry**, which is in a sense related to the **blood**. He should also eat the **food** he enjoys prepared with **vinegar**, because the **warmth** and the **sharpness** of the **vinegar** contract the **liver**. He may also eat **wheat bread** that some **people**, for the sake of **flavor**, place between a split, dried **pig's shoulder** <note>likely a cured ham or cut of pork</note> and douse with **wine**. For the dry **juice** from this **shoulder**, when it is driven out by the **warmth** of the **wine** and poured onto the **bread**, as well as the **bread** prepared with it, contracts the **liver** so that it does not swell. However, he must not drink the **wine** with which this **meat** was doused, because the **wine** has been permeated by and has drawn into itself the **harmful elements** that were in the **meat**.

**Foods** sometimes rise up to the **spleen** and cause it to become painful because they have not been tempered by any **spice**. Therefore, this person should take **chervil** <note>original: "Kerbel"</note> and slightly less **dill**, and with **wheat bread** and **vinegar**, prepare cakes like a **gingerbread** <note>original: "Gewürzkuchen"; a spiced medicinal cake</note> and eat of them repeatedly. For the mild **coldness** of the **chervil** expels and heals the **spleen pain**, which arises from both **warm** and **cold humors** <note>original: "Säfte"; the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that medieval doctors believed governed health</note>, whereas the **coldness** of the **dill** strengthens the **spleen**, the **wheat bread** causes the **spleen** to grow <note>in this context, likely meaning to restore it to health or proper size</note>, and the **vinegar** cleanses it through its **sharpness**. Some time later, he should take **linseed**, boil it in a **pot**, then remove it from the **pot** and, after the water has been pressed out, fill it into a small **sachet** and place the **sachet** upon the area of the **spleen** as hot as it can be endured. For **linseed** is **warm** and **mucilaginous**; when it is stimulated to release its power through the gentle influence of water, its **warmth** and **moisture** touch the **spleen** and make it healthy.

### Concerning Stomach Pain.

When **undigested foods** occasionally clump together and harden in a person's **stomach**, they cause **pain** in the **stomach**. Therefore, the person should take **peonies** <note>original: "Pfingstrosen"</note>, a fourth part as much **southernwood** <note>original: "Eberraute"; a woody herb related to wormwood</note>, and slightly less **cinquefoil** <note>original: "Fünffingerkraut"; literally "five-finger herb"</note> than the **southernwood**. He should grind these into a **pulp** in a **mortar** and then boil them in good, pure **wine**, but in such a way that the **wine** exceeds this **herb juice** by double. Then, he should strain it through a **cloth** and pour it into a **glass vessel** or a new **pot**. Then he shall pour the **wine** treated with the specified **herbs** into a **crucible**, thrust a **glowing steel** <note>a red-hot piece of metal used to transfer "fortitude" and intense heat to the liquid</note> into it two or three times, and into this same **wine**—while it begins to boil from the heat of the **steel**—he should add **galangal powder** and some **pepper** or, if he has no **galangal**, crushed **pellitory** <note>original: "Bertram"; a root used for its pungent, warming properties</note>. He should drink the **wine** warmed by the **glowing steel** while **fasting**. For five days, he must drink this **wine** in moderate amounts while **fasting**, having warmed it each time beforehand with the **glowing steel**. Once he has done this for five days, he should take **wheat bread** or fine **wheat flour** and, using the aforementioned **wine** warmed by the **glowing steel**, prepare a small **appetizer** <note>original: "Vorspeise"; here referring to a medicinal mash or porridge</note> to which an **egg yolk** has been added for better flavor, but no **fat** or **oil**, and eat of this for another five days while **fasting**. When these days have passed, he must again drink the aforementioned **wine**, previously warmed with the **glowing steel**, until he feels better. For the **peony** strengthens the **stomach** through its **warmth**, the **southernwood** keeps **gout** <note>medieval "gout" often referred to various types of sharp, wandering pains</note> away from the **stomach** with its **warmth**, the **cinquefoil** invigorates it through its own **innate warmth**, the **warmth** of the **wine** eliminates its **mucus**, and the power of the **steel** powerfully promotes all these effects. Furthermore, the **warmth** of the **galangal** and the **warmth** of the **pepper**, or even that of the **pellitory**, gives to everything...

### Concerning Indigestion.

If a person is unable to digest the food they have taken, let them take the weight of two **hellers** <note>a "heller" was a small medieval coin used here as a unit of weight, roughly equivalent to half a gram</note> of juice from **long birthwort** <gloss>34)</gloss> <note>original: "Osterluzei"; an herb often used in ancient medicine despite its modern known toxicity</note>, the weight of one **heller** of **burnet-saxifrage** juice <gloss>35)</gloss> <note>original: "Bibernell"</note>, the weight of one **obolus** <note>a small unit of weight, half of a denarius</note> of **spurge** juice <gloss>36)</gloss> <note>original: "Wolfsmilch"; a plant with a milky, irritating sap used as a drastic purgative</note>, an equal amount of **ginger**, and then some **wheat flour**. From these juices, make small **cakes** the size of a **heller** coin but quite thick, and bake them in the **sun** or in a **baking oven** that has almost cooled. 

If the person suffering from the aforementioned ailment is **internally warm**, so that the food is "burnt" within them, they should eat one of these little cakes in the morning on an empty stomach. But if they are **internally cold**, so that the consumed food congeals and is compressed within them from the cold, they should take two or three cakes in the morning on an empty stomach. The first nourishment they take afterward should be a **broth** or a light **appetizer**, and after that, they may consume other good and light dishes. They should continue this until they feel relief in the **stomach**. 

For the **warmth** of the **long birthwort**, which is quite sharp and strong, is tempered by the **coldness** of the **burnet**, setting the **bad humors** <note>original: "Säfte"; the bodily fluids believed to control health</note> in the person into motion; the **warmth** of the **ginger** dissolves them, and the **coldness** of the **spurge** carries them away most swiftly. The **wheat flour** strengthens the **stomach** so that it does not suffer from these treatments. When all this is baked together in the **sun**, whose heat is strong, or in a pre-warmed **oven**, whose heat is healthy, and administered to the patient as directed, it cleanses the stomach as has been said. 

— Alternatively, take **ginger**, grind it into a powder, mix it with some juice of the plant called **marigold** <gloss>37)</gloss> <note>original: "Ringelblume"</note>, and with some **bean flour**, make small cakes from this powder. Bake these in a cooling oven and eat these little cakes both after breakfast and on an empty stomach.

### Concerning Kidney Pain.
If a person occasionally suffers from **kidney** or **loin pain**, this very often arises from a weakness of the **stomach**. Such a person should take **rue** and **wormwood** in equal parts by weight and, in addition, **bear fat**. He should pound all these together and then, by an open fire, rub himself with it in the kidney and loin region where the pain is located. For pain in the kidneys and loins arises in many cases from **perverse humors** <note>original: "verkehrten Säften"; in medieval medicine, health depended on the balance of four bodily fluids or "humors"</note>. But when the warmth of the **rue**, the warmth of the **wormwood**, and that of the **bear fat** are mixed together, they drive out these incorrectly cold humors.

### Concerning Abdominal Pain.
If an **evil vapor** <note>original: "übler Rauch"; literally "foul smoke," referring to gas or miasma produced by poor digestion</note> spreads from the stomach into a person's intestines and causes pain there, then he should take **sage**, five times as much **bryony root** <gloss>41)</gloss> <note>original: "Zaunrübe"; a potent climbing plant</note> as sage, and ten times as much **rue** as sage. He should cook these herbs with water in a new pot until they come to a single boil. After the water has been pressed out, he should place the cooked, still-warm herbs on the place where it hurts in the manner described, and fasten them there with a cloth. For when the warmth of the **sage** and that of the **bryony** are mixed with the warmth of the **rue** and drawn out by the warm water, they drive out the **bad humors** from which the pain in the bowels originates through their strength.

### **Concerning Pain in the Side.**
Take **linseed** and a slightly smaller amount of **resin** from a **peach tree**, so that there is a quarter more linseed than resin, and boil these together in a pot until they form a glue-like consistency. Next, take **mistletoe** from a **pear tree** <note>In medieval medicine, mistletoe was believed to take on different properties depending on the host tree it grew upon; pear tree mistletoe was specifically valued for its cooling or drawing effects.</note> and crush it in a mortar into a pulp, so that its quantity is greater than that of the resin. Take this pulp along with **deer marrow**, using more by weight than the resin and the pear tree mistletoe combined. Put this into a pot with the aforementioned linseed and tree resin, and let the whole mixture boil up once more, as before. If you do not have deer marrow, add the same amount of **tallow** <term>tallow: rendered animal fat used as a base for ointments</term> from a **young bull** as would have been required of the deer marrow. Once this is done, strain everything through a cloth that has been pierced all over with an awl. Store it in a new, waxed earthenware vessel and thoroughly rub it into the person suffering from **pain in the side** wherever they feel the pain, doing so by an open fire.

### **Concerning Swelling of the Scrotum.**
If a man, due to **bad humors** <note>original: "schlechte Säfte"; referring to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were believed to cause disease if they became imbalanced or "corrupt."</note>, develops a swelling of the genitals through a very malignant growth that causes him pain there, he should take **fennel** and three times as much **fenugreek**, along with a little **cow butter**. He should crush these all together and apply them externally. These remedies draw out the bad humors of the pain from the male genitals. For the mild warmth of the fennel, tempered by the coldness of the fenugreek and the gentle warmth of the butter, reduces the aforementioned pain. The warmth of the fennel and the coldness of the fenugreek diminish those bad humors, and the gentle warmth of the butter heals the pain itself. 

Furthermore, the man should take the **malt cakes** <note>original: "Kuchen, aus denen das Bier wird"; these are the spent grains or malted loaves used in the early stages of brewing.</note> from which beer is made, moisten them slightly with lukewarm water, warm them up, and place them upon the aforementioned swelling. For these cakes, tempered by the mild warmth of the water, draw out the bad humors with their sharp juice and heal the person suffering there. (Thus, if someone suffers in this way in the genitals, let him take fennel, three times as much fenugreek, and some **beef fat**, crush these together and apply it, and it will draw out the bad humors. Furthermore, let him take a malt cake, moisten it with a little lukewarm water, and apply it.)

### **Concerning Involuntary Dribbling of Urine <gloss>42)</gloss>.**
If someone cannot hold their urine because of the **coldness of their stomach**, they should frequently drink **wine** warmed over a fire, mix all their food with **vinegar**, and drink vinegar as often as they are able. In this way, they will become warm in the stomach and the bladder. For the warmth of the wine, softened by the fire and taken as a drink, warms the stomach and bladder and holds the urine until the prop— <note>The text cuts off mid-word ("richtigen" - proper/right); likely "until the proper time."</note>

### **Concerning the Infertility of Man.**

A man whose semen is of a thin, watery consistency, such that he does not conceive, should take **hazel catkins**, a third part **wall pepper** <note>43) Wall pepper, or stonecrop (*Sedum acre*), is a pungent herb often used in folk medicine to "warm" the system.</note>, and an amount equal to a fourth part of the wall pepper of **bindweed** <note>44) Likely *Convolvulus arvensis*.</note>, and some of the other, common **pepper**. He should boil this together with the **liver of a young, sexually mature he-goat**, after adding some freshly slaughtered, **fatty pork**. Then, after those herbs have been removed, he should eat the meat, dip bread into the water in which the meat was boiled, and chew it. He should frequently eat the meat prepared in this way until his semen gains the power of procreation from its juice—if the righteous decree of God allows this to happen.

### **Concerning the Infertility of Woman.**

A woman whose womb is internally cold and too weak to receive offspring will be helped in this way, if it is God's will that she becomes fertile: Take the **womb of a lamb** or the **womb of a cow** that is of a mature age to give birth, yet untouched—meaning they have not been burdened by offspring nor ever were burdened. Boil these together with **bacon** and other **fatty meat** and **fat**, and give it to the woman to eat when she is sexually joined with her husband or will be joined within a short time. She should frequently enjoy this kind of meat, and the juice of the womb of the aforementioned animals will unite with the juice of the woman's womb, so that it becomes somewhat richer in fat and stronger. Thus, if God wills, she may conceive more easily, because by divine decree it often happens that the power to procreate is taken from humans.

### **Concerning Gout.**

This should be done in such a way that they begin at the ankle without wounding the skin, so that they draw the humors to themselves; then remove them from that spot and place them higher up, so that they again draw to themselves the humors sitting lower down. He should continue in this manner, without cutting the skin or wounding it, until he has reached the buttocks. Once he has reached the buttocks, he should wrap himself...

### Concerning the Fistula.

If someone suffers anywhere on their body from a **fistula** <term>fistula</term><note>An abnormal, tube-like passage that forms between organs or from an internal organ to the skin surface, often draining fluid.</note> arising from bad and superfluous humors, he should frequently use **purgative drinks** <note>original: "Abführtränke"; medicines used to cleanse the bowels and balance the humors.</note> so that he reduces the superfluous humors within himself. If afterward the skin at the diseased spot contracts as if it wants to heal there, and it is then burst open again by the humors, he should take purgative drinks anew so that the bad humors do not gain the upper hand in him. Repeat this frequently, and he will feel less pain. Both the fistula and the gout are difficult to cure.

### Concerning the Ulcer.

If any **ulcer** or **boil** causes a person severe pain before it breaks open, he should take a linen cloth, soak it with new wax, spread olive oil over it, and place it on the ulcer. Through this, the ulcer will be softened more easily and will break open with less pain; the humors will be expelled, and healing will occur with less discomfort. However, if it concerns the swelling which is called **"the cold one,"** <note>A "cold" swelling was typically chronic and pale, attributed to an excess of phlegm or black bile, whereas "hot" swellings were red and acute.</note> do not place the compress on it, because it is dangerous. But if drops and bad humors flow together from the broken skin anywhere on the human body without the presence of a malignant ulcer, then this person should take **mugwort** <note>45) original: "Beifuß".</note>, squeeze out its juice by rubbing it in a mortar, and add as much honey to this juice that the mugwort juice is a third more than the honey, and rub it where he has pain. Also, immediately afterward, he should spread the clear part of egg white over it, wrap it with a cloth placed on top, and do this until he is healed.

He can also take green **sage**, sprinkle it with a little wine, and place it over his heart and around his neck, and he will be refreshed by sleep. If it is winter time, he should [take] fennel...

### Concerning Spices.

Now, the medicines written down for the ailments discussed so far and shown by God will either help the person, or he will die, or God does not wish him to be freed from his illness. For various noble herbs, as well as powders and **spices** <note>original: "Gewürze"; in this era, spices were viewed as potent drugs that could alter the body's internal balance.</note> prepared from noble plants, will not benefit healthy people if they are consumed without order. Instead, they will bring them harm by drying out their blood and causing their flesh to become lean, because the spices do not find those **humors** <note>original: "Säfte"; the vital fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that medieval doctors sought to balance.</note> within them upon which they can exercise their powers. Indeed, they do not increase a person's strength, nor do they make their flesh grow; rather, they only diminish the "bad humors" to which they are naturally opposed. 

If someone is to take these, it should be done cautiously and only in cases of rational need. They should be taken with bread or in wine, or with some other food ingredient, and only in rare cases by a person who is still fasting. Otherwise, they constrict the chest of the one who consumes them, damage his lungs, and make his stomach weak when they fall into it, because they were consumed without an accompaniment. Just as the dust of the earth, which a person inhales, harms him, so too do these remedies, if not used properly, bring more harm than health. Spices should therefore primarily be taken with food or immediately after eating, because they then thin the humors of the food and enable the person to digest the nourishment they have taken—unless a person has such ailments that require him to take noble, powerful herbs or a precious powder while fasting.

### IV.

**On the Suppression of the Monthly Flow.**

A woman who suffers from suppressed **menstruation** <term>monthly flow</term><note>The German *Monatsfluss* refers to the menstrual cycle. In medieval medicine, its suppression was seen as a dangerous imbalance of the bodily humors.</note> and suffers pain as a result, should take equal weights of **anise** <term>anise</term><note>Pimpinella anisum; a seed valued for its "warming" properties and ability to move stagnant fluids.</note> and **feverfew** <term>feverfew</term>¹) <note>original: "Mutterkraut"; Tanacetum parthenium, literally "mother's herb," traditionally used for uterine health and "female" ailments.</note>, and add slightly more **mullein** <term>mullein</term><note>Verbascum; the German *Wollkraut* or "wool-herb" refers to its soft, fuzzy leaves.</note> than the weight of one of the first two. She should boil these in water taken from an open, flowing watercourse that has been tempered by the sun. Furthermore, she should take bricks, place them in a fire, and then prepare a **sweat bath** <term>sweat bath</term><note>A form of steam therapy where the patient is exposed to moist heat to open the pores and vessels.</note> using the aforementioned water and herbs. 

As soon as she enters the bathing room, she should place the warm herbs on a stool, sit upon them, and then apply the warm herbs directly to the genitals, up to the navel, and all around the navel. If the herbs have grown cold in the meantime, she should reheat them in the same water and reapply them to those same parts of the body. She should continue this for as long as she sits in the bath, so that the juices of those herbs may soften her skin and flesh from the outside and the womb from the inside, so that the vessels of the womb, which are closed, may be opened. For the warmth of the anise sets the juices in motion, the warmth of the feverfew has a healing effect, and the warmth of the mullein produces the blood flow. 

When those herbs are correctly mixed together and their inherent heat is drawn out in the warmed river water—which is healthier and milder than the water of a living spring because, lying open, it is also touched by the other elements—and when they (the inherent heats) are additionally set in motion by the hot bricks—which are unlike other stones because they were prepared with fire, from which they gain healing power—they will, as stated, bring forth the monthly flow again through the mild action of the bath.

She should grind all this together, add enough new, fresh, and clean honey, and let all of this simmer with the best wine. ## Concerning Difficult Birth.

If a pregnant woman suffers greatly during labor, one should carefully and with great caution boil tender herbs—namely fennel <term>Fenchel</term> and ground ivy <term>Gundelrebe</term>—in water. After the water is pressed out, apply the herbs while they are still warm to her thighs and her back, securing them lightly with a cloth covering. This is done so the pain may be eased and her closed birth passages may be opened more gently and easily. For the "bad and cold humors" <note>The text refers to the medieval medical theory of the four humors, where "cold" fluids were thought to cause tissues to contract or blockages to form.</note> within the woman sometimes cause her to contract and close up during pregnancy. But when the mild warmth of the fennel and the gentle heat of the ground ivy are activated by the delicate properties of the water over the fire and applied to her thighs and back—where she suffers most from the constriction—they encourage those parts of the body to open.

## Concerning the Cleansing of Saliva and Phlegm.

To cleanse a person of saliva and excreted mucus from the nose and throat, one should take agrimony <term>Odermennig</term> and twice as much fenugreek <term>Bockshornklee</term>, crush them in a mortar, and squeeze out the juice. Furthermore, crush crane's-bill <term>Storchschnabelkraut</term> and add as much of its juice to the agrimony and fenugreek as an *obolus* <note>An ancient unit of weight, roughly equivalent to half a gram.</note> weighs. Then take galangal <term>Galgant</term>, as much as those first three combined, six *hellers* <note>A small German coin, here used as a measurement of weight.</note> weight of storax <term>Storax</term> <note>A fragrant resin used in medicine and incense.</note>, and two *hellers* weight of polypody <term>Engelsüß</term> <note>Also known as "sweet fern" or "wood fern."</note>. Grind these into a powder, knead the powder thoroughly with the aforementioned juices, and form them into pills the size of a bean. 

Furthermore, squeeze the juice from greater celandine <term>Schöllkraut</term>, take as much as a quarter of a *heller* weighs, dip a pill into it while turning it, and place it in the sun to dry. Do the same with the remaining pills, dipping each one in the same amount of juice and placing it in the warmth of the sun, but not by a wood fire or a hot oven. If no sun is available, place the pills in a light breeze or moderately warm air so they dry slowly. ### Concerning the Flow of Blood.

Whoever suffers from frequent nosebleeds should take dill <term>Dill</term> and twice the amount of yarrow <term>Schafgarbe</term>, and place these herbs while fresh around the forehead, the temples, and the chest. For the dryness and coldness of the dill extinguishes the heat of the blood, and the warmth of the yarrow draws the blood to itself and restrains its excessive flow. These herbs must be fresh because their power is most pronounced in a fresh state. They should be applied to the forehead, temples, and chest because the strength of the vessels that carry the blood is located there. If it is wintertime, the person must grind the herbs into a powder; this powder, sprinkled with a little wine, should be placed in a small pouch and applied to the forehead, temples, and chest as previously described, because the warmth of the wine gives the powder the ability to staunch the blood.

## On the Common Cold.

If an excessive pain has occurred due to a heavy flow <note>original: "starken Fluß"; referring to a runny nose or catarrh, which medieval medicine viewed as an excess of "phlegm" moving from the head.</note> in a person's nose, they should take fennel and four times as much dill and place them on a stone roof tile or a thin brick that has been heated in a fire. They should turn the fennel and the dill back and forth until they begin to smoke, and then draw the smoke and the scent into themselves through the nose and mouth. Furthermore, they should also eat that same fennel and dill, warmed on the aforementioned stone, together with bread. This should be done for three, four, or five consecutive days so that the flow in the head and nose may loosen more easily and the outflowing humors <note>The "humors" or "saps" were the internal fluids believed to regulate health.</note> may be more easily removed. For the warmth and moisture of the fennel gathers the unnaturally poured-out and wrongly secreted humors and draws them together, and the dry coldness of the dill dries them out, provided both herbs are heated together on the hot stone—owing to the stone’s own healing properties—according to the instructions.

## How One Should Use Purgative Draughts.

A person who wishes to prepare and use purgative draughts should take ginger, half as much licorice, and one-third as much zedoary <term>Zitwer</term> <note>Zedoary is a root similar to ginger, often used in medieval medicine to treat digestive issues.</note> as the ginger. They should pulverize and sift these, and finally weigh the entire powder. Then, they should take as much sugar as the powder weighs. Once this is done, they should bring the whole mixture to the weight of thirty hellers <note>The "Heller" was a small copper coin; here it is used as a standard unit of weight.</note>. Now, they should take as much of the finest wheat flour as would fit into half a nutshell, and as much wolf's milk sap <term>Wolfsmilchsaft</term> <note>The milky sap from the spurge plant (Euphorbia), used as a potent, sometimes toxic, laxative.</note> as the slit of a scribe’s quill can hold when the scribe dips his pen into the inkwell. From the aforementioned powder, the wheat flour, and the wolf's milk sap, they should prepare a very thin dough, like that for a small cake, divide this dough into four parts, and dry them in the sun during March or April. This is because in these months, the sun’s rays are positioned such that they are neither too warm nor too cold, making them especially beneficial to health.

If one cannot obtain the milk from the aforementioned wolf’s milk plant in those months and must postpone it until May, one should let the aforementioned dough, prepared in May, dry in the May sun and keep it until the proper time. For the warmth of the ginger and the coldness of the zedoary drive the humors together; the warmth and moisture of the sugar hold them together and moisten them; and the warmth and inherent power of the wheat flour prevents them from flowing in a disorderly fashion. Finally, the wolf's milk sap, through its coldness, leads the humors out of the body in a pleasant and appropriate manner, provided everything is balanced among itself as stated above. The ginger, however, and the zedoary, the sugar, and the fine wheat flour retain the good humors within the person, and the wolf's milk

...expels the bad humors <note>In medieval medicine, "humors" or "saps" were the internal fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—that were believed to regulate health and temperament.</note>. If a person were to take spurge <term>Wolfsmilch</term> <note>Spurge (Euphorbia) contains a milky sap that acts as a powerful, often toxic, purgative; it was used to "cleanse" the system.</note> alone without the other listed ingredients, it would drain away both the good and the bad humors, because there would be no inhibiting effect from the beneficial herbs to hold back the good humors. This purgative draught should be prepared in the specified months because the sun and air are in a favorable relationship to one another then. Anyone wishing to use the purgative draught at that time should drink a quarter of the total amount on an empty stomach. If their stomach is so strong and thick-walled that they do not feel the touch of the drink, they should take half of the third part of the aforementioned cake, brush this whole half with spurge sap, dry it likewise in the sun, and take it on an empty stomach. Before anyone takes this drink, however, if it is cold, they should first warm themselves by the fire and only then take it; once it has been taken, they should rest in bed for a while while awake, and after getting up, walk slowly back and forth, but in such a way that the cold does not harm them.

## Concerning Diet.

After a successful evacuation <note>Referring to the bowel movement caused by the purgative.</note>, one should eat wheat bread—not dry, but dipped into morning soup—and enjoy young chickens, pork, and other light meat dishes. However, one should avoid coarse bread, beef, fish, and other heavy as well as fried foods, with the exception of stewed pears. One should also abstain from cheese, green herbs, and raw fruit, but drink wine—though in moderation—and leave water aside. One should flee the bright glare of the sun and the fire, and follow all of this for three days.

Take the liver of that fish which is called the <term>catfish</term> <note>12) original: "Wels".</note>, place it on linden wood, light a fire, and place the linden wood with the liver on the glowing coals so that the liver is dried, but in such a way that neither smoke nor flame from the fire touches the liver. Give the liver time to dry until you can rub the dried piece into powder. If you notice that the linden wood is consumed by the fire in the meantime before the liver is dried, then place it on other wood of the same kind. Once the liver is fully dried, crush it into powder. Then take nutmeg and fenugreek, dry both on a sandstone moderately heated in the fire, and pulverize them. Then pack this powder and the one you prepared from the liver of the aforementioned fish together into a small cloth with the addition of some <term>musk</term> <note>13) original: "Bisam"; an aromatic substance harvested from animals, highly prized in medieval medicine for its "potency."</note>, so that the aforementioned powder prepared from the catfish liver is three times as much as the nutmeg, and the powder from the fenugreek is just as much as the aforementioned liver powder. Carry it with you always, and this powder will maintain the health of your body.

### Concerning Lack of Restraint.

If a man is so sexually aroused that the foam <note>original: *Schaum*; in this medical context, it refers to the initial agitation of seminal fluids during arousal.</note> reaches the organ of excretion <term>Ausscheidungsglied</term> <note>The penis.</note>, but is held back in the body by some obstacle and has begun to grow weak because of it, let him take rue <term>Raute</term> and a little less wormwood <term>Wermut</term>, press out their juice, and add to this juice sugar and more honey than there is sugar, along with as much wine as there is herbal juice. He should warm the whole mixture in a new pot or bowl five times using a hot steel <note>A common medieval practice where a red-hot metal rod was plunged into a liquid to heat it and imbue it with the "strength" of the metal.</note> and drink it warm, after having eaten a little beforehand. If it is winter and he cannot obtain the aforementioned herbs, let him crush bay leaves <term>Lorbeeren</term> and twice as much dittany <term>Diptam</term> <note>White dittany (*Dictamnus albus*), an herb highly valued in medieval medicine for its "warming" properties.</note> into powder, and drink this, after taking some food, in wine warmed by a hot steel. Then the harmful phlegm <note>original: *Schleim*; refers to congested or corrupted bodily fluids.</note> that remained within him will be expelled along with the urine and stool. For in summer those juices are available. Indeed, the heat and the cold of the rue, combined with the heat of the wormwood, drives the congealed humors out of the man, and the heat of the sugar and the heat of the honey heal such a sufferer. When the heat of the wine is tempered by the repeated dipping of the hot steel and the addition of the specified plant juices, and the whole is warmed five times to increase its effectiveness, it should be consumed after eating a little first, so that the man does not suffer a fainting spell, and the man will then find himself in better health. In winter, however, when the juice of those herbs is not available, the heat of the bay leaves and the dittany are tempered with the repeatedly warmed wine and strengthened by the repeated action of the steel's heat; these drive the aforementioned suffering from the person, as stated above.

### Concerning the Clouding of the Eyes.

A person who suffers from a clouding of the eyes due to excessive sexual desire, whether man or woman, should take the skin of the gallbladder of the fish called the catfish <term>Wels</term> and dry this skin in the sun after the bile has been emptied, so that if any harmful liquid remains on it, it may be removed by the power of the sun's heat; otherwise, its inherent strength would be too strong for the eyes and would damage them. Then place this skin in the purest and best wine, so that it becomes tempered and supple through the mildness and power of the wine. At night, when lying down to rest in bed, one should place it over the eyes and secure it with a cloth, yet in such a way that the moisture of this skin does not touch the eyes internally, lest they be harmed by its strong effect. ### On Forgetfulness.

A person who is forgetful against their will should take the burning nettle and crush it into a pulp, then add some olive oil <note>original: *Baumöl*; literally "tree oil," the standard historical term for olive oil.</note> to it. When they go to sleep, they should rub their chest and temples vigorously with it; do this repeatedly, and the forgetfulness will decrease. For the sharp heat of the burning nettle and the warmth of the olive oil stimulate the constricted vessels of the chest and temples, which sleep somewhat even while the consciousness is awake.

### On Hiccups.

Whoever suffers from hiccups <note>original: *Schlucken*; while this can mean "swallowing," the context of "cold" and "dryness" in humoral medicine usually identifies this as the singultus or hiccup.</note> should take a good portion of sugar, dissolve it in warm water, and drink this water warm. This is because the warmth of the sugar, together with the mildness of the heated water, moistens the dryness that the cold of the hiccups causes in a person. Indeed, warm water is better suited for this ailment than wine, because water is mild, whereas wine is potent. One can also eat dry sugar and frequently chew cloves <note>original: *Gewürznelken*.</note> while fasting; <term>zedoary</term> <note>a root similar to ginger used in medieval medicine to treat "cold" ailments of the stomach.</note>, however, should only be consumed frequently after breakfast, and this should be done for an entire month. For the sugar reduces the dryness of such a person, and the warmth of the cloves penetrates the person while they are still fasting and makes them warm. The warmth of the zedoary, on the other hand, acts powerfully and removes the cold of the aforementioned illness. It does this for a person who has eaten breakfast because, if they were fasting, the strong heat of the zedoary would harm the empty stomach. A person should use these remedies for a month so that they may be all the more strengthened by them.

A powder against poison and against magic spells: Whoever carries this powder with them, it brings them health, strength, and well-being. Pull these plants out with their roots in the middle of the month of April at midday; lay them on damp earth and sprinkle them with a little water so that they remain green for a time. As the day then turns toward evening, place the herbs in the light of the sun until the sun sets. At sunset, however, take the herbs away from their place and lay them again on moist earth during the following night, sprinkling them with a little water so that they do not dry out too quickly. At the first glimmer of the following day, when the dawn rises, place the herbs in the glow of the morning light until the third hour <note>In the medieval system of "temporal hours," the third hour (Terce) was approximately 9:00 AM.</note> of the day back upon the moist ground, without sprinkling them with water, until midday when the sun turns toward the south. Then remove the herbs at noon and place them in the south in the full sunlight until the ninth hour <note>Nones, approximately 3:00 PM.</note> of the same day. Thereupon take them and lay them on a cloth stretched over a wooden frame, so that the herbs are not bent or damaged, and leave them lying there until shortly before midnight. 

When the Wheel of the North <note>This likely refers to the circumpolar stars, such as the Big Dipper, which appear to rotate around the North Star like a wheel.</note> turns like a millwheel and turns again toward the darkness because it can receive no light, and because then all dark and nightly evils flee, for the night must now turn again toward the day—thus: just before midnight, place the herbs in a high window or on a door beam or in some garden, so that they may have the moderately warm air and be touched by the breeze. There they must remain until just after midnight. Once midnight has passed, take the herbs away from the place where they lie, rub them carefully with your fingers, put them into a new, fine linen cloth <gloss>18)</gloss> and add a little musk <note>original: *Bifam*; a pungent substance secreted by the musk deer, highly prized in medieval pharmacy for its aromatic and preservative qualities.</note> to them, but only so much that the scent of the herbs is not displaced by that of the musk. 

The musk preserves the herbs for a time so that they do not rot. Then, however, everyone should hold the herbs prepared in this way before their eyes, ears, nose, and mouth daily to ward off illness and maintain health, so that they may take in their scent. If the sexual drive is strong in a man, he should apply the herbs, bound in a cloth, from the loins down to the member. A woman, on the other hand, should place them on the navel and she will notice the cooling effect. 

If someone has eaten something that causes them pain, they should place the herbs in the neck of a narrow vessel filled with wine, but in such a way that they are not touched by the wine, but only so far that the wine can absorb their fragrance. From this wine, prepare a small morning drink—that is: "to sip" <note>original: *suffen*; an archaic term for drinking or sipping, often used for medicinal drafts.</note>—and drink it thus. But if someone has taken poison or is afflicted by a magic spell, they should drink the scent-infused wine and they will feel better. Whoever carries the aforementioned herbs, treated according to the instructions, with them will maintain health and strength, because these herbs have been prepared at all hours and at every temperature, of both day and night.

### On Cramps.
If a person suffers from a cramp anywhere in their body, they should take olive oil <note>original: "Baumöl"; literally "tree oil," a common historical term for olive oil.</note> and rub the painful area vigorously with it. If they cannot obtain olive oil, they should rub the area thoroughly with any other good ointment. However, if they have neither olive oil nor any other ointment, they should move their hands back and forth with a vigorous motion over the place where they suffer the cramp, and the pain will pass. For the warmth and the natural power of the olive oil—or the power of other ointments—dispel the "smoke" of the black bile <term>Black bile (melancholia) was one of the four humors; "smoke" refers to harmful vapors thought to rise from these fluids and cause pain.</term> in that spot; even if the painful area is simply rubbed lightly with the hand, the pain is driven away.

### On Colic.
A person suffering from colic should take feverfew <note>Original footnote 19 likely refers to *Chrysanthemum parthenium*.</note>, pound it vigorously into a juicy pulp, and then add a little cow's butter. With this, they should rub themselves thoroughly where they have pain, and they will be cured. This works because the warmth and natural power of the feverfew, combined with the warmth and mildness of the butter, drives away and soothes the pain. [Take less sage than zedoary <note>A root similar to ginger.</note>, and more fennel than sage and zedoary. Then take duckweed <note>Original footnote 20.</note> and tormentil root <note>Original footnote 21; *Potentilla erecta*.</note>, twice as much as the duckweed; mustard <note>Original footnote 22.</note> that grows in the field, the same amount as the tormentil roots; and of the herb on which burrs <note>Original footnote 23; likely burdock.</note> grow, take less than the duckweed.]

### On Anger and Sadness.
If any person is moved to anger or sadness, they should quickly warm wine over a fire, mix it with a little cold water, and drink it. In this way, the "smoke" of the black bile, which has risen within them to trigger the outburst of anger, is kept within bounds.

### On Anger.
But someone who is moved by anger to such an extent that they suffer painfully from it should take bay leaves and dry them on a hot brick, along with sage and marjoram. They should pulverize these after they have been dried in the sun and put this powder—together with the other specified powder prepared from the bay leaves—into a fine linen cloth. Ensure there is more bay powder than sage powder, and more sage than marjoram powder. They should hold this to their nose for its pleasant scent. Furthermore, they should put a portion of the same powder into some cold wine, mix it thoroughly, and rub their forehead, temples, and chest well with it. For the bay leaves possess a warm and suitable dryness and moisten the juices that anger has caused to dry up in the person; the warmth of the marjoram calms that which is stirred up by anger...

## On the Clouding of the Eyes from Crying.

Whoever has become dim-sighted through much weeping should take yarrow or even its root, carefully pound it into a pulp, and place this crushed herb over the eyes during the night. However, this must be done so that the juice does not get inside the eyes; secure it with a cloth placed over it until about midnight and then remove it. As soon as this is done, he should carefully smear the eyelashes with the best and purest wine. For the warmth of the yarrow has a healing effect, its juice is somewhat sharp and cleanses the ulcers <note>original: "Geschwüre"; in this context, it likely refers to inflammation or sores caused by the salt in tears.</note>, and since the wine is also warm and somewhat acidic, it removes the sharpness of the yarrow juice, and thus the eyes are healed.

## On Excessive Laughter.

Whoever feels pain from being agitated and shaken by much and excessive laughter should pound a nutmeg into powder, add half as much sugar to it <note>original: "zweimal weniger Zucker"; literally "twice less," suggesting a ratio of two parts nutmeg to one part sugar.</note>, pour it into warmed wine, and drink it both while fasting and after breakfast. For excessive laughter dries out the lungs and shakes the liver, but the warmth of the nutmeg acts healingly upon the liver, and the warmth of the dissolved sugar restores the lungs. When these two are mixed with the moderate warmth of the wine and drunk in this way, they bring the good humors—which were thrown into disorder by the excessive laughter—back into order.

## On Drunkenness.

So that a drunk person may come back to their senses, one should take nightshade, place it in cold water, and moisten their forehead, temples, and neck with this water. He will feel better upon recovering his senses because when the coldness of the nightshade combines with the coldness of the water, it calms the storm and the heat that is in the vessels of the person's forehead and temples. If it is autumn, one should take a vine of bryony <note>original: "Zaunrebe"; likely referring to *Bryonia alba*, often used in folk medicine to "cool" the blood.</note> with the fresh leaves and place it around the drunk person's forehead, temples, and neck. He will be cooled because in this season in the bryony...

### Against **Vomiting**.
Whoever suffers from nausea should take **cumin**, a third part as much **pepper**, and about a fourth part (relative to the cumin) of **burnet** <note>original: "Bibernell"; likely Burnet-saxifrage (Pimpinella), used in medieval medicine for digestive issues.</note>. Pulverize these, take pure **wheat flour**, mix the powder with this **flour**, and prepare small **cakes** from it using **egg yolk** and a little **water**, baking them either in a hot hearth or under hot ashes. He should eat these cakes and also consume the aforementioned powder sprinkled on bread. For when the coldness of the cumin, the coldness of the burnet, and that of the egg yolk are tempered by the warmth of the pepper and the warmth of the wheat flour, and are combined with the mild effect of the water—as well as being thoroughly cooked by the moderate heat of the hearth as prescribed—they tame the "unrightfully" warm and cold humors <term>Humors (Säfte): The vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow and black bile) believed to regulate health.</term> that bring nausea to a person.

### On **Dysentery**.
Whoever is sick with dysentery <note>original: "Ruhr"; specifically the "bloody flux" or severe diarrhea.</note> should take **egg yolks**, after the whites have been removed, and beat them in a bowl. When he has done this, he should put **cumin** and some crushed **pepper** into the eggshells, roast them over the fire, and give them to the patient to eat after the patient has taken a little food. For the egg yolk is dry and permeated with phlegm; it draws everything to itself and forces what has been "loosened" back together, just as a chick is formed from it; therefore, it draws the dissolved humors back together. The cumin also acts through its coldness to unify what has been loosened, while the pepper tempers both of these through its warmth so that, as stated, they hold the liquefied humors together all the more strongly. Furthermore, take other egg yolks, press the oil out in a pan over the fire, and prepare small cakes from this **egg yolk oil** and pure wheat flour. Have the patient eat these after a moderate meal, because the egg yolk oil, tempered by the warmth and strength of the wheat flour, cancels out the liquefaction of the humors. Everything else such a sick person consumes must be enjoyed warm, because his stomach, intestines, and the humors within him have grown cold. He should eat what is soft and has a pleasant taste, including young chickens and other tender meats and fish; however, he must avoid herring and salmon. Also beef and cheese,

Coarse, uncooked vegetables and leeks should be avoided; furthermore, one must not eat rye or barley bread, nor anything roasted—with the exception of stewed pears. All of these things, due to their hardness and difficulty of digestion, make the waste in the intestines even worse, and water produces mucus within them. Therefore, all of these must be avoided for as long as the ailment lasts. Wine, however, contracts the intestines through its warmth.

### On **Blood Flow**.

If someone suffers from a flow of blood <note>original: "Blutfluß"; can refer to various types of hemorrhaging, including heavy menstruation or nosebleeds.</note>, take two egg yolks and stir them. Add an amount of motherwort juice <note>original: "Mutterkrautsaft"; likely referring to feverfew (*Tanacetum parthenium*) or motherwort (*Leonurus cardiaca*), both common in traditional women's medicine.</note> equal to half a yolk, and as much vinegar as two eggshells would hold. Then add some cinnamon powder and a smaller amount of zedoary powder <note>original: "Zitwerpulver"; zedoary is a root similar to ginger, used in medieval medicine to treat digestive and circulatory issues.</note> than there is cinnamon. Once you have mixed all of this together, use a little water to prepare a fairly thick morning drink. Give this, slightly warmed, to the one who suffers from blood flow to drink, both on an empty stomach and after breakfast. Do this often, and the patient will find themselves better.

### On **Blood Flow from the Anus**.

Whoever suffers from a flow of blood [from the anus] should take blackberry leaves <gloss>24)</gloss> and twice as much bloodroot <gloss>25)</gloss> <note>original: "Blutkraut"; likely tormentil (*Potentilla erecta*), prized for its red root and ability to stop bleeding.</note>. Crush them moderately until they release their juice, place them in wine, and drink this during and after the meal, but not on an empty stomach. Blackberry leaves and bloodroot have a juice that somewhat resembles the fluid of blood, and both—when tempered <term>Tempered (temperiert): The medieval practice of mixing substances with opposing qualities (hot/cold, wet/dry) to create a balanced, medicinal "middle" state.</term> with wine—still the flowing blood through their combined warmth and coldness. They are taken by a person who is no longer fasting so that they may hold back the blood, which then flows toward the person’s food [rather than being lost]. One can also take wheat flour and prepare and eat small cakes made from it using only honey and a little salt. For wheat flour, through its warmth and natural power, causes the flesh of a person to grow and closes off the "wrong path" of the blood; the honey, through its warmth and juice, stills the unnaturally flowing blood; and the warmth of the salt dries it up. When these ingredients temper one another, they hold together the blood that is flowing out "unrightfully."

As long as one suffers from this disease, however, they should eat wheat bread in the meantime, while avoiding rye or barley bread. One should also avoid beef and pork, as well as all fish that have no scales, cheese, uncooked vegetables, raw fruit, and everything roasted. Other soft meats as well as other types of fish may be eaten; one may also drink pea soup, but the peas, lentils, and beans themselves should be avoided. Furthermore, one must not eat hot foods, but only take lukewarm ones that are neither too warm nor too cold. One may also drink light wine, but no water in between, because all of this harms a person who has this disease, as was already mentioned regarding dysentery.
