## On Spitting Blood.

If bad, clotted, and poisonous humors <note>original: "Säfte"; referring to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which medieval medicine believed caused illness when imbalanced or "corrupt."</note> have overwhelmed a person and cause them to occasionally spit or vomit blood, that person should not use any medicine in the meantime. This is so the blood is not "startled" by the medicine, which might cause internal ulcers or lead to even greater bleeding. However, once the blood has stopped enough that the person is no longer severely weakened, they should boil sage in a light, sweet wine mixed with a little water. After adding olive oil <note>original: "Baumöl"; literally "tree oil," typically referring to olive oil in medieval pharmaceutical contexts.</note> or butter, strain the mixture through a cloth. They should drink this in moderation—not until full, and not on an empty stomach, but rather after breakfast. For sage resists the internal decay of the humors; the wine, combined with the mild effect of the water, revives and strengthens the patient; and the olive oil or butter heals them internally.

## On Hemorrhoids.

If bad, watery, or thin humors have overwhelmed a person and caused blood to flow from the anus without the simultaneous discharge of digested food, one should not attempt to hold back such a flow of blood. Otherwise, that same blood will be thrown into a greater state of "alarm" and will flow out in an even stronger gush.

## Also on Blood.

If the blood in a person, stirred up by bad and watered-down humors, begins to flow from the anus at the same time as a bowel movement, they should not restrain it, for it cleanses them and serves as a purification. But if it passes through them too strongly during the bowel movement, they should add germander <gloss>26)</gloss> <note>A medicinal herb of the Teucrium genus, traditionally used for digestive and inflammatory issues.</note> to vegetables and other good herbs, prepare a dish from this, and eat it in moderation. Germander diminishes and restrains the aforementioned flow of blood, while the vegetables and other herbs restore the patient's strength.

## Also on Spitting Blood.

If the spleen and lungs are damaged by bitterness of the heart and thoughts, such that the person, oppressed internally by this, is forced to cough up and vomit blood, they must not stop this flow of blood without careful consideration. This is to prevent them from developing internal ulcers and vomiting even more blood. Rather, when the vomiting of blood has ceased for a time, they should let blood <note>original: "zur Ader lassen"; the practice of phlebotomy (bloodletting), which was used to balance the humors or redirect the flow of blood away from a diseased organ.</note> from the thumb of the right hand, so that the blood flows toward that side, since the ailment is caused by the blood on the person's left side. In this way, they will be healed.

If a person's internal vessels are so contracted by sadness,

## On the **Rose**.

When the skin eruption known as the **Rose** <note>original: "Rose"; historically referring to Erysipelas, a painful bacterial skin infection characterized by a bright red, swollen rash and fever.</note> has developed on a **person** along with a swelling, they should catch **flies**. After their heads have been removed, they should be crushed; with the ground remains, draw a circle around the swelling. This is because the "poison" of the **flies** provides resistance to the poison of the rash, preventing it from spreading further.

Following this, take the **red snail** <note>original: "rote Schnecke"; likely the red slug (*Arion rufus*), commonly used in folk medicine.</note> which is without a shell, tear it apart, and with it draw a circle around the circle already made with the **flies**. Its slime tames the decayed matter of the aforementioned swelling so that it must retreat, for that which is bad resists that which is bad. 

Next, one should take **lily juice** and thoroughly rub the skin around the ring drawn with the snail slime, as this juice drives away the pain and brings healing. Then, take a leaf from the **lady's thistle** <gloss>27)</gloss> <note>original: "Frauendistel"; also known as Milk Thistle (*Silybum marianum*), traditionally associated with the Virgin Mary.</note> and place it upon the blister. Prepare a small cake from pure **wheat flour**, place it on the leaf and over the entire swelling, and fasten it there with a cloth laid over it until the swelling becomes soft and breaks open of its own accord. This is because the coldness and the juice of the **lady's thistle** are not dangerous, but rather work with the warmth of the **wheat flour** to soften the hard swelling.

If it does not break open by itself, one should open the swelling with a dry, **wooden thorn** or some other dry **little skewer**, but not with hot or cold iron or a **needle**. This is because the blister and the swelling would be "startled" by a hot or cold object, which could easily lead to a dangerous turn <note>Medieval medicine often cautioned against using metal tools on certain inflammations, fearing that the "shock" of the metal's temperature or nature would drive the illness deeper into the body.</note>. 

As long as a **person** suffers from the swelling of this rash, they should protect themselves from fire, cold, wind, and damp air. They should also abstain from all hot, roasted, and heavy foods, as well as the **consumption of wine**, and avoid raw vegetables and uncooked fruit. These things collectively would increase and inflame the harmful humors <note>original: "Säfte"; the bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were thought to cause disease when "inflamed" or imbalanced.</note> within them. They may eat only good bread, especially **wheat bread** soaked in water, and drink water, as this is not dangerous for a **person** at such a time due to its mild nature. 

If such a **person** is a picky eater, they may prepare a light **wheat flour soup** with pure **egg yolk**, without the addition of fat or cheese, and consume it after it has cooled. This regimen must be observed until they notice that the pain has lessened and ceased. In the meantime, however, they must not drink **wine**, because it would fill the vessels more heavily and so the existing swelling...

**Against Cancer, All Kinds of Ulcers, and Headaches.**
Take violets, press out their juice, and strain it through a cloth. Weigh out a third of the juice's weight in olive oil, and an amount of buck tallow <note>The rendered fat of a male goat.</note> equal to the weight of the violet juice. Let all this boil in a new pot until it becomes an ointment. Then, rub the area of the body all around as well as on top where the cancer and other worms <note>original: "Würmer"; in medieval pathology, "eating" or "gnawing" pains and certain growths were often attributed to literal or metaphorical worms within the flesh.</note> consume the person. They will die once they have tasted this ointment. Furthermore, for other ulcers, one should vigorously rub the same ointment wherever the person feels pain; if someone is plagued by a headache, rub it across their forehead.

**Concerning the Itch.**
A person suffering from all kinds of ulcers and the itch <note>original: "Krätze"; historically referring to scabies, a contagious skin condition caused by mites, or similar itchy eruptions.</note> should take chervil, three times as much polypody <note>original: "Engelsüß"; *Polypodium vulgare*, a common fern whose root has a sweet taste.</note> as chervil, and five times as much elecampane <note>original: "Alant"; *Inula helenium*, a plant long used for its antiseptic properties.</note> as chervil, and boil these in water. Then, pour the pressed-out water, strained through a cloth, into a pot; add some fresh incense <note>Frankincense.</note> and sulfur, as well as more fresh lard than all the aforementioned ingredients combined, so that it may be thickened over a fire into something like an ointment. With this ointment, the patient should rub around and over the ulcers. Furthermore, they should apply the specified herbs—boiled and still warm after the water has been sufficiently pressed from them—onto the skin and the ulcers. This must be done for five days so that the skin and flesh are thoroughly permeated by it. Afterward, the patient should cleanse themselves in a bath so that the scabs and the stench are removed from them.

For chervil possesses as much heat as cold; when these are tempered by the heat of the polypody and the heat of the elecampane, as well as by the mildness of the water, the heat of the sulfur, the heat of the incense, the heat of the lard, and the different kind of heat from the fire, the chervil drives out the bad humors in the person. Because chervil possesses both heat and cold, it removes the "unrighteous" <note>Improper, imbalanced, or diseased.</note> heat and "unrighteous" cold of the ulcers and the itch. The heat of the polypody dries up the harmful humors, the heat of the elecampane frightens them away, the heat of the incense has a healing effect, and the heat of the sulfur thins them. The heat of the lard, when fresh, heals the ulcers and the itch in a gentle manner.

### Concerning Jaundice.

A person who has jaundice should take vervain <note>original: "Eisenkraut"; *Verbena officinalis*, a plant historically used for liver and digestive issues.</note>, twice as much garlic ^28)^ as vervain, and moneywort ^29)^ <note>original: "Pfennigkraut"; *Lysimachia nummularia*, also known as herb twopence.</note>, three times as much as the vervain. Or, if he has no garlic, let him take saxifrage ^30)^ <note>original: "Steinbrech"; *Saxifraga*, literally "stone-breaker," often used for stones in the body.</note> in the same weight as the vervain. Place these plants with the best wine into a deep vessel that is tightly sealed at the top. He must drink this wine for nine days while fasting; after breakfast, however, he should drink only a little. With this same wine, an egg, and fat, he should prepare a soup and consume it. When he goes to sleep, he shall drink the same wine, having warmed it with hot steel <note>This refers to the practice of quenching a red-hot iron or steel rod in liquid to "fortify" it with the metal's perceived heat and strength.</note>, and cover himself with a warm garment so that he sweats. He should do this until he is healed.

The heat of the vervain, the heat of the garlic, and that of the moneywort—or the coldness of the saxifrage—act somewhat sharply in their juices. When these are tempered by the heat of the wine and taken as a drink, or combined according to the instructions with the heat of the wine or fat and the coldness of the egg for better flavor, they counteract the bitterness of the yellow bile and the black bile <term>Schwarzgalle: one of the four humors (melancholy); an excess was thought to cause various physical and mental illnesses.</term>, so that they subdue the aforementioned disease. The drink should especially be taken by a person who is fasting, so that it may penetrate the limbs more quickly than if they had already breakfasted, and it must likewise be strengthened with the hot steel so that it may more effectively tame that suffering.

### Concerning Epilepsy.

One who is plagued by the falling sickness <note>original: "fallenden Leid"; a common historical term for epilepsy.</note> should take mole’s blood and dry it. Likewise, he shall take the beak of a female duck and the claws of a female goose, also female, without skin or flesh. These he shall make into a powder by scraping and rubbing, so that the powder from the duck’s beak is twice as much as the powder from the goose, and the mole’s blood is twice as much as what was produced from the duck’s beak. All this, bound together in a cloth with the aforementioned blood, he shall place for three days in the spot where the mole has recently pushed up the earth.

After it is taken away from there, he should place it where ice is found, so that it freezes together. After it is taken away from there as well, let it be dried in the sun. Then he shall take a piece of liver from any animal or bird used for food, as much as he can get, and from this, with a little wheat flour, form small cakes. He should add less of the aforementioned powder than the amount of the liver, add some cumin, and consume it. Because the mole only shows itself sometimes and then hides again, and because it is accustomed to digging in the earth, its blood resists this disease, which also makes itself known at times and then disap- <meta>The final word "ver-" (from "verschwindet") is incomplete at the bottom of the page.</meta>

**On Dropsy.** <term>Wassersucht: an archaic term for edema, the painful swelling caused by fluid retention</term> Whoever has dropsy should take a peacock—that is, a male—and boil it with hyssop in well water, but not in water from a bubbling spring, and eat the meat in this manner. Then he shall take betony <note>original: "Betonienkraut"; a common medicinal herb in the Middle Ages used for a variety of protective and healing purposes</note>, three times as much lovage, and a little eel fat, boil them in wine and strain through a cloth; into this same wine, he should pour the aforementioned powder and drink it. He must do this for eighteen days. For peacock meat is dry and acts powerfully against dropsy; moreover, it must be from a male animal because of its particular strength. It should be cooked with hyssop because hyssop, through its dryness and its mild coldness, dries out the superfluous moisture belonging to this disease. It must be boiled with well water because this also possesses "dry" water-veins and consumes the dropsy more strongly than spring water, which is somewhat "waterier" than well water and would accordingly worsen the disease through its moisture. The powdered peacock heart stifles this disease, while the bone that rotates in the knees binds the disease—just as it joins the vessels of the leg—so that it cannot make progress. The claws, however, hold down the "breach" <note>original: "Verschlußstelle"; literally a closure or blockage point</note> of the illness so that it cannot rise up like bubbling water. The water of this disease flees the warmth of the betony, whereas the coldness of the lovage gladdens the spirit of the sick person (because he is sad), and the eel fat keeps the breach of the disease closed, which otherwise allows it to rise. Also, the warmth of the wine calms the disease more than water, and all of this, taken together in the correct proportions according to the instructions, makes the illness milder.

**On Roundworms.** When worms have emerged in any person from the harmful and bad humors that act like poison within a human, that person should take nettle juice and mullein juice <note>original: "Wollblumensaft"; likely derived from Verbascum thapsus, known for its soft, woolly leaves</note> in equal parts by weight. After it has boiled, he should take the pot from the fire and, for fifteen days, drink a little of it while fasting, so that he is not harmed by the strong effect of the remedy. After eating, however, he may drink a sufficient amount of it, because the food counteracts the power of that remedy. **On Lice.** If lice harm a person internally within the body so that they do not emerge, he should take eel gall and add to it one-third as much strong vinegar <note>original: "dreimal weniger"; literally "three times less," indicating a ratio of one part vinegar to three parts gall</note>, and as much honey as the two combined, and boil it in a pot. Then he shall take ginger, twice as much long pepper <note>original: "langen Pfeffer"; a hotter, more aromatic relative of black pepper common in medieval pharmacy</note> and about as much basil; these should be pounded into a powder. Furthermore, he should take ivory, a third as much as the basil, as well as half the weight of the ivory in vulture's beak, and add the shavings <note>original: "Geschabsel"</note> of these to the aforementioned powder. Immediately after, he shall put this powder into the aforementioned vinegar and let it boil once more. Once he has done this, he should pour the whole mixture into a small bag so that it filters through like a clarified elixir <note>original: "Lautertrank"; a strained, medicinal spiced wine or potion</note>, catching it in a new earthenware vessel. 

Then the person whom the lice are harming internally, such that they do not leave him, should drink this potion daily while fasting, and also at night when he goes to bed. The lice in his body will become sick and die, and the fat <note>likely referring to healthy flesh or bodily vigor</note> will grow back within him. For the heat and bitterness of the eel gall weaken the lice; the heat and acidity of the vinegar wear them down; the heat and dryness of the ivory cause them to wither; and the vulture’s beak kills them because it is cold and "poisoned" by all sorts of carrion, and is further saturated by the "brain-sweat" of that same vulture. These effects are tempered by the warmth of the honey, the intense heat of the long pepper—which is greater than that of other peppers—and the coldness of the basil. The mixture is heated by the distinct warmth of the fire and then poured into a clay vessel so that it does not soften or rot. It should be taken both while fasting and after breakfast; it kills the lice because when a person takes this potion while fasting, it destroys the lice more quickly, and when taken after a meal, it thins the digestive juices <note>original: "Speisesäfte"</note> from which the lice are formed.

**On the Stone.** Whoever has a stone <note>referring to kidney or bladder stones</note> within them should take the fresh gall and twice as much blood of a young bull and dry it. Furthermore, he should take saxifrage <note>original: "Steinbrech"; literally "stone-breaker," a plant traditionally used to dissolve urinary stones</note>, pulverize it, and take as much of this powder as there is gall; bind this together in a fine cloth and place it in strong, good, and pure wine. He should drink from this repeatedly while fasting, and also about the same amount after a meal, but not during...

<meta>This page continues the treatment for internal stones, concluding the dietary advice and the humoral logic behind the remedy before transitioning to the treatment of leprosy, specifically distinguishing between cases caused by lifestyle and those caused by temperament.</meta>

...of eating, because then the wine would be consumed by the foods. The bitterness of the gall <note>original: "Galle"; bile was considered a potent medicinal substance due to its sharp properties</note> tempered with the warmth of the aforementioned blood, the coldness of the stonecrop <term>Steinbrech: literally "stone-breaker," likely Saxifrage or similar plants used since antiquity to break up kidney or bladder stones</term>, and the warmth of the wine, dissolves the congealed stone within the person.

**On Leprosy.** <note>original: "Vom Aussatz"; in the Middle Ages, this term was a broad category for various chronic skin conditions, including what we now call Hansen's disease</note> Whoever becomes leprous from gluttony and excessive drinking should take swallow droppings and four times as much of the herb called burdock <note>original: "Klette"</note> which has reddish flowers, and make a powder from them. He should also take stork fat and slightly more vulture fat, melt them in a crucible, and then mix the aforementioned powder and some sulfur <note>original: "Schwefel"</note> with this fat to prepare an ointment. He should further arrange to be thoroughly rubbed with this ointment while in a sweat bath <note>original: "Schwitzbade"; a steam or heat treatment intended to open the pores</note> and then lie down in bed. He should proceed in this manner until the fifth day or even longer. For when the warmth of the swallow droppings and the coldness of the burdock are tempered with the warmth of the stork fat, the coldness of the vulture fat, and the warmth of the sulfur, they eliminate the foul matter <note>original: "faulige Materie"</note> of this leprosy; the swallow droppings dissolve it, the burdock herb eats it away, and the fat from the stork and vulture, combined with the harsh effect of the sulfur, draws it out of the body. The sick person will become healthy, unless it is God's will that he does not recover.

**Also on Leprosy.** A person who becomes leprous through their inclination toward anger should go to the place where the blood of horses flows onto the earth when they are bled <term>Aderlaß: bloodletting, the practice of withdrawing blood to balance the body's humors</term>, or where clean animals are slaughtered, and he should take this blood along with the blood-soaked earth. He should let the blood, mixed with the earth upon which it fell, boil with water in a warm bath and thus prepare a bath for himself. However, too much water must not be used, as this could draw the strength out of the blood; only enough should be used so that it reaches the sick person's neck. Then he should sit in the bath. From that same blood and earth, he should put some into a small bag <note>original: "Säckchen"</note> and place it on his face if he has pain there. After he has left the bath, he should lie on his bed and place the bag with the aforementioned blood and earth over his heart, so that he does not become weak and to keep the harmful blood away from his heart. This he should do four or five times, or even more often. For the warmth of this blood, united with the admixture of the earth soaked in it, resists the leprosy just as an enemy overcomes his foe. This, however, seems difficult to the person because human nature stands in opposition to that of animals and because man has a revulsion toward animal blood. And the person will be healed, or else God does not want him to be healthy.

### On Leprosy Caused by Intemperance.
If a person becomes leprous as a result of carnal desire and intemperance <note>original: "Unenthaltsamkeit"; in medieval medicine, excessive sexual activity was believed to corrupt the bodily humors, manifesting as skin diseases</note>, they should take agrimony <term>Odermennig: *Agrimonia eupatoria*, a herb used since antiquity for its healing and astringent properties</term>, along with a third as much hyssop <note>original: "Ysop"</note> and twice as much ground ivy <term>Gundelrebe: *Glechoma hederacea*, also known as Alehoof, traditionally used to treat "hot" inflammations</term> as both the others combined. They should boil these herbs in a warm bath and prepare a bath from them. To this, they should add as much menstrual blood as they can obtain and then sit in the bath. Alternatively, they may take any amount of goose fat, twice as much chicken fat, and a little chicken droppings to prepare an ointment. Once they have emerged from the bath described above, they should rub themselves thoroughly with this ointment and then lie down in bed. This must be done frequently until they are healed. For the warmth of the agrimony combined with the coldness of the hyssop, and the warmth of the ground ivy joined with the warm menstrual blood in the proper proportion, remove the putrid matter <note>original: "faulige Materie"</note> of such a leprosy. Indeed, the agrimony, hyssop, and ground ivy cause the toxins to be sweated out, while the menstrual blood overcomes and destroys them—just as an enemy defeats an opponent—because it originates from the various juices <note>original: "Säfte"; refers to the four humors of the body</note> of the woman. The goose fat and the chicken fat gently anoint and heal the skin. The chicken droppings, however, draw out any impurities that still remain, and thus the sick person will be healed, unless God does not permit it.

### On Gout.
A person who has soft flesh and is plagued by gout <note>original: "Gicht"; a term used broadly in the Middle Ages for various types of joint pain and arthritis</note> in one of their limbs as a result of immoderate drinking, should take parsley and four times as much rue <term>Raute: *Ruta graveolens*, a bitter herb used in medieval medicine to "balance" moist humors</term> and roast them in a dish with olive oil or, if they cannot obtain that oil, fry them with goat tallow <note>original: "Bockstalg"</note>. They should place the herbs, as hot as they are, onto the place where the pain is felt and secure them with a cloth tied over them. For the coldness of the parsley juice tames the swelling of the gouty humors, the warmth of the sharp rue juice holds those same humors together so they do not increase excessively, and the oil or goat tallow penetrates and dissolves them; this they achieve when they are mixed together in the specified manner.

### On Swollen Glands.
Whoever has swollen glands <note>original: "geschwollene Drüsen"; likely referring to scrofula or lymphadenopathy</note> on the body should, before they break open, take coltsfoot leaves <term>Huflattichblätter: *Tussilago farfara*, recognizable by its large leaves that are felt-like and white on the underside</term>—specifically the large ones that are white on the outside and green on the inside. They should pluck them by the stem according to the size of the swollen gland, discard the excess, and coat what remains with honey. They should place these on the glands for three days and three nights, and if they dry out, apply a new leaf prepared in the same way; then the glands will begin to shrink. On the fourth day

If the dough dries out, he must refresh it with new honey. He is to proceed this way until the swelling disappears.

### On Paralysis.
If someone begins to become paralyzed anywhere in their limbs, they should take dittany <term>Diptamkraut: *Dictamnus albus*, also known as burning bush, valued in herbalism for its ability to "drive out" poisons and blockages</term>, throw away the part that sits in the middle like a heart, and boil what remains thoroughly in water. While this is boiling, add twice as much houseleek <gloss>36)</gloss> <note>original: "Hauslauch"; *Sempervivum tectorum*, a succulent used to soothe and cool inflammations</note> as there was dittany, and twice as much nettle as there was houseleek, and mix all of these together in the boiling water. After the herbs are cooked and the water has been squeezed out under moderate pressure, he should apply them while they are still warm to the joint and the vessels of the limb where the person is beginning to become paralyzed. When they begin to grow cold, he should warm them anew and apply them again, doing this frequently. However, the part that is inside the dittany and is dry must be thrown away; the warmth of the remaining dittany, tempered by the sluggish action of the houseleek, drives out the bad humors <note>original: "schlechten Säfte"; refers to the four bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which were believed to cause disease when imbalanced or "corrupted"</note> that bring weakness to the person’s limb. The heat of the nettle destroys these same humors so that they diminish, provided it is tempered by the mildness of the warm water, as specified. In this way, the sick person will be healed, unless it be that God does not will it.

### On Hot Fever.
A person who eats little and drinks much must, if they suffer from a hot fever, drink water so that they do not—if they were to drink wine—become more and more heated and suffer an even more violent fever. However, they should drink the water cold or boiled and then cooled again, and will then suffer less, because it refreshes them and extinguishes the burning heat within them. If the water were warm, it would increase the fire of the hot fever.

### Likewise on Hot Fever.
If a hot fever has seized a person, they should prepare the following medicine so that they may then suffer somewhat less until the onset of sweat. Thus: He should take some dry maple wood and twice as much dry—not yet green—willow wood, and shave a small amount of both into cold water, much like a woodcarver. Furthermore, he should take as much agrimony <term>Odermennig: *Agrimonia eupatoria*, a common healing herb used here to balance the "heat" of the fever</term> as there was willow wood, put it in the same water, and drink from it repeatedly after it has been prepared in this way, and he will have less discomfort. The maple—

Thus the coldness of both woods resists the unnatural heat of the hot fever, and the mild warmth of the agrimony <note>original: "Odermennig"; *Agrimonia eupatoria*, a herb traditionally used in medieval medicine for its ability to balance internal heat and treat the liver</note> restrains the heat of the hot fever so that it is not increased. ### On Daily Fever.
Whoever suffers from daily fever <note>original: "täglichen Fieber"; known historically as quotidian fever, where paroxysms occur every twenty-four hours</note>, let him take wild marjoram <gloss>37)</gloss> <note>original: "wilden Majoran"; likely *Origanum vulgare*, used here for its warming properties</note>, camphor, and of the tormentil root <note>original: "Tormentillwurzel"; *Potentilla erecta*, a powerful astringent used to "cool" the blood</note> more than of the other two, crush these into a powder, put this powder into warm wine at the beginning of the feverish attack, drink it thus, lie down in bed and sleep. The warmth of the wild marjoram and that of the camphor, tempered by the coldness of the tormentil root, drive out the aforementioned fever at the start of the feverish attack when drunk in warm wine, so that it subsides all the more quickly.

### For Tertian Fever.
A person whom the fever plagues every second day <note>original: "dreitägige Fieber"; Tertian fever, where the attack recurs on the third day by inclusive counting—meaning every 48 hours</note> shall take yarrow <note>original: "Schafgarbe"; *Achillea millefolium*, named for Achilles and used to treat wounds and blood disorders</note>, twice as much common polypody <note>original: "Engelsüß"; *Polypodium vulgare*, a fern with a sweet root used as a purgative and to treat lung ailments</note>, boil both in light, good wine, strain through a cloth, and drink this wine at the onset of the feverish attack. He shall leave these herbs in the wine for three days so that it is all the more tempered by them, and thus drink it for three days and, if necessary, replace them with fresh, corresponding herbs. For the warmth of the yarrow and the warmth of the common polypody, softened by the reduced warmth of the wine and taken in the drink according to the instruction, cause the fever to become milder.

### **For the four-day fever.**
If someone suffers from the **four-day fever** <note>original: "viertägige Fieber"; known as quartan fever, often associated with malaria, where paroxysms occur every fourth day</note>, let him take **madder** <gloss>38)</gloss>, an equal weight of **blackberry leaves**, and **masterwort** <gloss>39)</gloss> <note>original: "Brachwurz"; likely *Peucedanum ostruthium* or sanicle, used to treat wounds and internal heat</note> weighing three times as much as the first two combined, and boil these in wine. Furthermore, he should pour pure, high-quality wine into a bowl and dip a **red-hot steel**, as hot as it can be, into it. He should dip that same glowing steel a second time into the same wine in the same vessel, and repeat this process ten times with the same wine and the same steel. Afterward, he should take the wine that he boiled with the specified herbs and pour it into the wine in which he held the red-hot steel. Let the whole mixture boil up just once more, and drink it at the onset of the aforementioned fever until he returns to health. For when the coldness of the madder and the warmth of the blackberry leaves, as well as the warmth of the masterwort, are softened by the altered warmth of the wine and then strengthened again by the power and the altered heat of the steel, as described, they cause the fever to diminish.

### **Against the worm.**
A person whom **the worm** <note>original: "der Wurm"; a medieval term for a variety of conditions, including skin parasites, ulcers, or even dental decay, thought to be caused by "worms" eating the flesh</note> consumes at any part of the body should take **chalk**, twice as much **gypsum** <gloss>40)</gloss>, and make a kind of thin mortar from these using sour wine. He should brush this onto the spot where he suffers from the worm using a feather, and do this every day until the fourth day. After that, he should take **aloe** and a third part as much **myrrh resin**, rub these together, and make a plaster of this with fresh **wax**. He should spread this on a coarse cloth and bind it upon the painful spot for twelve days. The chalk is inherently warm and the gypsum cold; thus, the warmth of the chalk—brought to the proper measure by the coldness of the gypsum and the warmth and acidity of the wine—kills the worms. However, the warmth of the aloe, increased by the warmth of the myrrh, draws the pus out of the ulcers and brings the area to healing.

**On Sheep.** If sheep begin to fall ill, take **fennel** and **dill**, so that there is more fennel than dill, and place them in water so the water takes on their flavor; give it to the sheep to drink, and they will become better.

**On the Horse.** If a horse has a catarrhal discharge running from its nose and coughs as a result, boil **nettle** and more **lovage** than nettle in water. Let the rising steam, while it is still warm, be drawn into the nose and mouth of the animal after a bridle has been placed on it, and it will become healthy. If the horse has a stomachache, likewise take nettle and more lovage than nettle, and mix these repeatedly into the fodder so it can consume them with its food, and it will become healthy.

**On the Donkey.** If a donkey has a headache so that it coughs, take **beech ash**—that is, such beech ash from which lye has already been prepared—and while the ash is still warm, let the steam be drawn into the mouth and nose of the donkey, and it will become healthy. If a donkey has a stomachache, mix scraped **coltsfoot** <gloss>42)</gloss> into bran moderately warmed in water; do this often and it will become healthy again.

**On the Goat.** If a goat becomes sick in any way, give it plenty of oak leaves to eat frequently; do this often and it will return to health.

[**Likewise on the Horse and on Cattle.** If you wish to open a vein for bloodletting <note>Bloodletting, or phlebotomy, was a standard medical practice based on the theory that illness was caused by an imbalance of bodily fluids called humors.</note> on a horse, a cow, or a donkey, and the animal is strong and well-nourished, then take as much blood from it as a cup can hold. If, however, the horse, the cow, or the donkey is weak and thin, then draw only half a cup of blood, according to its nutritional state and its low vitality, and after the bloodletting give it soft fodder and dry, tender hay to eat. After the bloodletting, it should rest for two weeks, or for one to four days, so that it may regain its strength, since it is always at work. Once three months have passed, take blood from the vein of the same animal in the fourth month, but not earlier, unless it is absolutely necessary because of its poor condition, because the bad humors <note>original: "schlechten Säfte"; referring to the Galenic humors (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) which were thought to cause disease when they became "corrupt."</note> in these animals are not as excessive as they are in humans.

**On the Sheep.** Bleed the sheep frequently, but always take only a small amount of blood.

**On Pain of the Tongue.** If someone suffers in the tongue, such that it is either swollen or ulcerated, scratch it slightly in one spot with a small bloodletting knife or with a thorn so that the pus comes out, and they will feel better.

**On the Chest.** If someone has a cough in the chest such that they first feel pain within it, take **sage** and **lovage** in equal parts, and twice as much **fennel** as both of them together, and place these together in good wine until the wine has taken on their flavor. Then, after removing the herbs, heat the wine and drink it warm after eating until you are healthy again.

**Where Forgetfulness Comes From.** In some people, bad humors sometimes stir up a "smoke" <note>In medieval physiology, "vapors" or "smokes" were thought to rise from the stomach or the blood to the brain, clouding the mind and causing various mental or sensory conditions.</note> that rises into their brain and affects it so much that they become forgetful as a result. Therefore, take **nettle**, crush it into a pulp, then add some tree oil <term>Baumöl<gloss>A common historical term for olive oil or sometimes a general vegetable oil.</gloss></term> to it, and when you go to sleep, rub your chest and temples thoroughly with it. Do this many times, and your forgetfulness will decrease.]

<meta>This page continues the transition from general health signs into a specific semiotic of life and death, following the medical observations of the previous page. While page 202 focused on bloodletting and physical ailments, page 203 shifts toward "physiognomy"—the practice of judging a person's health and soul through their external appearance, specifically the eyes and complexion.</meta>

### V.

**On the Signs of Life.** When a person is physically healthy, if they have clear, bright eyes—no matter what color—they possess the sign of life. This is also true if their eyes are as transparent as a bright cloud through which, at times, another cloud is visible as if made of glass <note>The author uses the metaphor of "glassy clouds" to describe a specific depth and clarity in the eye, suggesting that the light of the soul is shining through without obstruction.</note>, and such a person will live and not die soon.

**On the Eyes.** For the expression of the soul is powerful in the eyes of such a person; when the eyes are clear and transparent, it is because the soul dwells vigorously within the body so that it may accomplish many works through it. For the eyes of man are the **windows of the soul**.

**On Cloudy Eyes and the Signs of Death.** On the other hand, whoever has cloudy eyes despite being otherwise healthy—such that their eyes, regardless of color, are not transparent—carries the sign of death. Likewise, if the eyes are as murky as a cloud that is so dense on its surface that the "glass-like" cloud behind it cannot be observed, such a person will soon become ill, and death will follow. In the gaze of such a person, the soul is not strong, because it will accomplish little more there; it sits, as it were, veiled by clouds, like a man who is contemplating and in doubt about when he should leave his place and step out of his house.

**On the Signs of Life.** Likewise, a healthy person whose color on the cheeks under the skin is red or bright red—so that this coloring is visible beneath the skin like a polished and clean apple—carries the sign of life. This is true when the red color of the cheeks is seen under the skin in the same way a glassy cloud is sometimes visible through a white cloud. This person will live and not die soon. For the red color visible under the skin of the cheeks—as was said above—is the **fiery breath** <note>original: "feurige Hauch"; this refers to the medieval concept of the *spiritus vitalis*, a vital heat or breath that was believed to animate the body.</note> of life, which is to say: the soul, because the soul is fire. Thus, it is made visible on the cheeks that the soul sits securely within the body and will not soon depart.

<summary>This page explains how to diagnose a person's vitality and proximity to death by observing the clarity of their eyes and the redness of their cheeks. It presents the soul as a "fiery breath" that reveals its strength through the transparency and color of the physical body.</summary>

<keywords>signs of life, signs of death, windows of the soul, fiery breath, physiognomy, vitality, medieval medicine, soul as fire</keywords>

### On the Signs of Death.
If a person is otherwise healthy, but the red or bright red color of their cheeks sits atop the skin—in such a way that no skin can be perceived beneath the redness of their cheeks—then they bear the sign of death. This is because the redness of the cheeks rests on the skin with such density that the skin cannot be seen beneath it, much like a **red apple**, beneath whose red color no skin is visible, but only a certain redness on the surface. Such a person will soon fall ill, and death will follow. For the redness lying upon the skin is the <term>fiery breath of life</term> <note>original: "feurige Lebenshauch"; this refers to the vital heat of the soul</note> of the soul, because here the soul makes its power known outside the body and indicates that it feels weak and insecure within the body, like a man who runs repeatedly to the door of his house when he intends to go out through it.

Likewise, on the signs of death: The voice, which in a person always had a pure sound, also indicates death if—having been healthy and strong until then—the voice becomes somewhat hoarse. This occurs in such a way that the person then habitually remains hoarse without any other ailment, just as a clear-sounding **trumpet**, which always gave out a pure sound, becomes hoarse if it is somehow damaged. For when a person's voice remained continuously pure, this was caused by the surging power of <term>consciousness</term> <note>original: "Bewußtsein"; referring to the soul's active awareness and command over the physical form</note>, because it had to remain for a long time in its place, from which man also derives his **reason**. If he now becomes continuously hoarse without a cause that induces illness, the soul is preparing itself for the path upon which it must leave the body.

Also, the voice which was habitually rough, as if darkened, in a healthy person without a simultaneous illness, and then becomes clear—so that the person now possesses a bright voice by habit without any illness—indicates death. It is like an unusable, habitually hoarse-sounding trumpet that is somehow forced to sound pure; when blown for a short time, it indeed delivers a pure tone, yet this soon falls away again and falls silent. For where the voice of a person is rough without an underlying illness, the soul is working more inwardly in secret rather than publicly outside the body. Therefore, such a person is also *cunning*. But if that same voice changes to a continuously bright tonal color without a cause of illness, then the soul proclaims that it will shortly leave the body, because it now makes itself openly known outside that same body, whereas until then it worked silently within it. 

When a person lies sick in bed, regardless of which ailment they are held down by, and the flesh of the face is **puffed up** like the flesh in the face of a sleeping person,

...one who wakes from a dream; if his eyes are clear like the water in a cistern and somewhat watery, then he will undoubtedly not recover from his illness, but will die. For if the facial flesh of such a person is swollen, this results from the various <term>morbid humors</term> <note>original: "krankhaften Säften"; medieval medicine attributed illness to an imbalance of four primary bodily fluids or humors</note> that make themselves visible in his face. As for the striking clarity of his eyes mentioned before, it arises because the soul reveals its fiery nature through those eyes. However, the fact that they appear somewhat watery is because the fire of the soul is extinguishing its flames, as the soul is on the verge of leaving its body in a short time. 

But a person whose face is moderately puffy, like that of a sleeping person, and whose eyes are very bright but not watery, will barely escape death through much anxiety and great danger. The bad humors indeed make this patient's face puffy, but his bright eyes indicate that the fire of the soul is still present within him; since they are not watery, the soul is not leading its flames toward their end, but keeps them low to preserve life.

**On the Signs of Life.** If, however, his eyes are very bright, yet somewhat cloudy and quite watery, he will soon become healthy and live. The fact that this patient's eyes are not completely clear is because the soul has not sent its fire into them as a final departure. Their cloudiness stems from an excess of blood, which manifests in them as a sign of life. That they are very watery results from that same excess of blood purifying itself through "foaming" and secreting the corrupted matter so that the person may live.

**On the Signs of Death.** Furthermore, if a person who was always habitually prudent and rational while physically healthy loses his mind during an illness—becoming like a man whose <term>intellect</term> <note>original: "Verstand"; referring to the capacity for rational thought</note> is severely shaken—and remains in this state of irrationality, he will die and not remain among the living. If he was clever and deliberate in his usual course of life, this was brought about, so to speak, by the wings of his soulful insight. If he is then continuously confused during his illness, the soul folds the wings of its insight and prepares to depart.

**On the Signs of Life.** But if someone was always very wise and deliberate through the wings of his intellect while he was healthy, and now, sick in bed, loses his mind, his soul withdraws to rational...

### **Likewise on the Signs of Death.**

The person who, as long as he possessed health in his body, was constantly unwise and foolish, is one in whom the soul has not allowed the wings of reason <note>original: "Fittige der Vernunft"; in medieval thought, the "wings" of the soul represent the intellectual faculties that allow a human to rise toward understanding.</note> to manifest in their perfection. If such a person becomes insightful while lying sick in bed and remains in that state of insight, he will die and not remain alive. This is because his soul, upon its departure, is already making known the understanding and the paths it will travel in another life. 

However, if someone was constantly unwise and foolish while in full health because his soul did not fully unfold the wings of the intellect, and then becomes sensible as a sick person, his soul is preparing itself for knowledge and for the paths in another life. But if he then suddenly returns to his former folly during the illness itself and remains within it, he will narrowly escape death. This is because his soul turns back again to the accustomed place and the accustomed state it previously held in that same body, for it is not yet ready to depart from it.

### **On the Pulse and the Signs of Life.**

If a person has become bedridden through some illness and the vessel <term>artery</term> <note>original: "Ader"; while this word can mean "vein," in the context of taking a pulse (Pulsschlag), it refers to the arterial pressure points.</note> on his right arm shows a pulse that is steady and even—like a person who draws and releases his breath in an orderly and even manner—then he will live and not die. For no matter how great the weakness caused by the burning fevers of the <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säfte"; medieval medicine was based on the balance of four "humors" or bodily fluids; fever was seen as these fluids "burning" or boiling.</note> may be in a person, his soul nevertheless maintains the nature of its breath <note>original: "Hauches"; this refers to the vital spirit or "pneuma" associated with the soul's presence.</note> in an orderly way within itself, as it does not intend to leave the body. Therefore, the beat of the pulse is orderly and moderate, because the soul is not moving the artery toward death.

### **Likewise on the Signs of Death.**

If, however, the artery on the right arm of a sick person races, like the breath of a person before suffocation, and no pause occurs after any pulse-beat, then he will die, because his soul is being forced to depart. It then moves the artery only weakly and detaches itself from it, which is why the pulse of the sick person races toward death.

...[is beating rapidly], but if that same vessel <term>artery</term> <note>original: "Ader"; Hildegard uses this term to refer generally to the vessels where a pulse can be felt.</note> during this racing has provided one or two beats properly and then races again as it did before, the soul is indicating through that racing that it finds difficulties in leaving the body; therefore, it moves that vessel hurriedly. It shows one or two pulse beats properly, however, because in this person it remembers life and strives to return to the life that is still present, and so that person will not die but shall live. 

The pulse of the vessel is to be observed especially on the right arm, because the signs that announce life or death in advance can be perceived particularly well on the right arm, since the greatest strength lies in the right side <note>In medieval physiology, the right side was associated with heat, strength, and the "active" principle of the body.</note> and this side is itself constantly active. On the left, by contrast, there is a kind of sluggishness and nothing is done upon it. The pulse of the vessel can also be judged according to the truth in the bend of the right arm and in the bend of the right leg—that is, behind the knee—because the strength of the soul is located there. For the soul holds the joints of the limbs firmly in its power, and when it wishes to leave the body, it releases these joints. Therefore, the pulse of its vessels indicates the storms before death. But if the soul does not wish to depart, even though the person's body has many pains to endure, then the pulse in the vessels of those same joints remains calm and in order, because the soul is not preparing for its exit.

### On the Signs from the Urine.

Whatever a person may drink, be it wine or beer or mead or water: every urine indicates the condition of their health or illness, from whatever drink it may come. One urine is not "better" than another, because all urine originates from the yeast of the body <note>original: "Hefe des Körpers"; this refers to the sediment or metabolic byproducts produced during the "cooking" of food and drink in the stomach.</note>, even if one drink is more highly valued than another.

### On Gouty Paralysis.

If a person is cold and afflicted by gouty paralysis <term>gichtische Lähmung</term> <note>This term likely refers to a combination of joint pain (gout) and loss of motor function or sensation.</note> while lying sick in bed, their urine is white, like the foam of new must <note>original: "Most"; freshly pressed fruit juice that has not yet fermented into wine.</note>, and thick like wine that has been shaken while being transported. Because the cold and the gout are in constant motion within them, their urine is white like the foam of the must and thick as long as they are sick. For it is white because of the cold—whereas warmth gives the urine either a red or another color—and it is thick because the juices <term>humors</term> in such a person are corrupted and do not have their proper path, much like when a body of water overflows during a storm and becomes thick with silt. And if he is to die, then the thickness of his urine sinks to the bottom, because the juices in this person [withdraw] from—

One with such a burning fever feels no appetite nor any taste for food, because the humors are not fulfilling their duty.

### On the Daily Fever 
If a person suffers from a <term>daily fever</term> <note>original: "täglichen Fieber." Historically known as a quotidian fever, it was often associated with specific digestive or humoral imbalances.</note> and their urine is thick and has a color similar to wine, they will soon recover. These daily fevers arise from an improper movement of the humors, which is also why the person's urine is thick—because the humors are in a state of disordered motion. Such fevers also arise from improper digestion, specifically when the stool is either unnaturally hard or unnaturally thin. Accordingly, the urine of such a person has the color of spoiled wine. 

However, if the urine is clear and bright like water, the person must die, because their blood has been transformed into coldness. Consequently, the other humors also congeal, just as when milk curdles, because they lack warmth and blood. In this case, the urine is clear and bright because it is not mixed with the humors, as they are no longer performing their function. If the urine is somewhat pale in color, thick, and rather watery, the person has much to suffer and will only just escape death. Because such a person has coldness within them, their urine is pale and somewhat watery; and because the humors within them overflow, like a storm before death, the urine is thick. Therefore, the patient suffers for a long time but will not die.

### On the Three-Day Fever 
But for one who has the <term>three-day fever</term> <note>original: "dreitägigen Fieber." Known as a tertian fever, typically characterized by paroxysms occurring every 48 hours.</note>, if the freshly collected urine is colored like blood...

...is, but the bloody discoloration does not change and instead remains red, and the urine becomes thick, then the patient will not die but will remain suffering for a long time. In such a person, the <term>humors</term> <note>original: "Säfte." In the medical tradition of Hildegard of Bingen, these are the vital "saps" or fluids that determine health through their balance.</note> have turned into a burning heat, and therefore the urine is bloody and remains red because of the intensity of this heat. For the urine, which is colored red due to the great heat and warmth of such a person, no longer possesses the full power of that strong heat when it turns pale, because the person has become cold internally. Then, those various differently-colored streaks appear in the urine because the humors are being separated from each other in such a person, which, according to their nature, possess different colors. One of the four classical humors, often associated with cold and dry diseases.</note> and similar fluids are separating from one another. 

If the same urine possesses these manifold streaks originating from the various humors, but no red streaks are visible within it, then the blood and the heat are still joined and not yet separated; thus the urine indicates that life is still present. Such a person suffers much, however, and will only narrowly escape death.

### Also regarding quartan fever.
<note>original: "viertägiges Fieber." A fever that recurs every fourth day, historically associated with malaria.</note>
If someone has quartan fever, and if their urine is thick and red-colored and various structures like veins are visible within it, the person will suffer because they are being parched internally. They will, however, live. For if the urine of this person is thick, then the <term>digestion</term> <note>original: "Verdauung." In this context, it refers to the "coction" or the body's processing of food and fluids.</note> is mixing with the urine, and this makes it thick. If it looks red, the warmth still remains in the blood and does not separate from it. If it shows various streaked structures, the different humors are still connected to one another. Thus, although the person withers due to these morbid conditions, they will be able to live because a dissolution of the humors has not yet taken place within them.

The patient will die. The urine, white like pus and comparable to curdled milk, indicates that the **natural warmth** <note>In medieval medicine, "natural warmth" or "innate heat" was the vital energy fueled by the heart that maintained life; its loss meant certain death.</note> has departed from the person, which is why their urine is white. Therefore, the pus-like substances that were in the **humors** <term>humors: the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) whose balance determined health.</term> congeal together because they do not have the proper warmth. 

It is also like a cloud in its center, appearing purple-red, white, and cloudy, because the **black bile** <note>original: "Schwarzgalle." Also known as melancholy, this humor was considered cold, dry, and often associated with the most serious or terminal illnesses.</note> is in the center of the humors, as if held within their strong protection. Thus: it is purple-red because it has changed its color to that of a wound, as it is about to dissolve; it looks white because the pus is flowing apart and because it shows that it is losing its strength; and it is cloudy because it now releases the foul and natural **vapors** <note>original: "Rauch" (smoke). Medieval doctors believed the body produced "sooty" vapors as a byproduct of digestion and metabolism which needed to be expelled.</note> that it previously held within it. 

Therefore, as said, such urine is similar to a cloud that is purple-red, white, and cloudy; and if it is then somewhat clear at the edge because it is not thick throughout, then the person has much pain and will hardly escape death. Nonetheless, he will recover faster than the one whose urine is red. For dark red urine indicates that the black bile is damaged, white shows that it is already decaying, and cloudy proves that it is releasing its vapors at the end. The clear coloring at the edge, however—when the urine is thick only in the middle and not throughout—indicates that the humors of that person are not yet entirely separated from one another. If clarity is present at the edge of this urine and the thick consistency is only in the middle, it is because the humors are still in connection with one another. Thus the person will escape death and regain their health faster than the person whose urine is red, because that person has such great internal heat that they cannot be released from it so soon.

### On the Diversity of Rivers

It should be noted that the water of rivers behaves differently according to the condition of the air. When a strong wind prevails, storms arise on the water, causing terror and agitation. When the sun burns in its heat, the waters boil and have foam. But in calm and steady air, the waters are mild, peaceful, and beautiful. 

It is the same with the urine of a person. For when a dangerous illness rises in a person like a violent wind, so that their soul wishes to separate from the body, then great storms also accumulate in their urine. But if an excessive heat of the humors prevails in a person, comparable to the glowing sun, then the urine in the person also appears corresponding to the warmth and glow within them. For when a person is in a proper and calm state of their body, the urine also shows itself according to its proper and healthy condition.

<summary>This page explains how white, cloudy, or "curdled" urine signifies the loss of vital heat and impending death, while using the metaphor of a river's response to weather to illustrate how urine reflects the body's internal "storms" or "calm."</summary>

<meta>This page continues the medieval medical analysis of diagnostic signs, moving from the visual inspection of urine (uroscopy) to the diagnostic value of bowel movements. The previous page established that certain colors and textures in urine—specifically those resembling pus or clouds—signal the departure of "natural warmth" and the presence of "black bile," often indicating a terminal state.</meta>

### On the Inspection of Urine

The urine of a sick person, for the purpose of considering their health or illness, should be saved when they wake up after sleeping, because then the urine is correctly mixed and colored according to the patient's condition. For when a person sleeps, they are at rest, without any movement, and therefore the **humors** <term>humors: the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) whose balance was believed to determine a person's physical and mental health.</term> and the sweat within them flow according to their nature and according to the person's internal state at that time. If, however, the sick person cannot sleep, then for the assessment of their condition, the urine must be collected that they pass during the night or that they excrete at the break of dawn <note>original: "im Zwielicht des Tages" (in the twilight of the day).</note>, because the temperature of the night as well as that prevailing at daybreak unites the humors within them and the condition that prevails in them to some extent.

### On the Immobility of the Earth and the Excretion of Stool

The earth and the clay are immobile, so that they cannot be moved by the air. They do, however, emit a certain scent when rain is already falling or is about to fall in the very near future. It is the same with the bowel movements of a human. For in the excreted stool, no signs can be perceived that announce life or death in advance, because human stool is immobile like the earth. 

In contrast, life or death can be distinguished to a small degree from its smell, though nonetheless with difficulty and rarely. If the bowel movement smells strongly according to the person's habit, as it is always accustomed to smell, this is no sign of death, because the **warm putrefaction** <note>original: "warme Fäulnis." Medieval medicine viewed digestion as a process of "cooking" or fermentation driven by the body's internal heat; a "warm" smell indicated that this vital heat was still active.</note> makes itself known in this smell. But if the same bowel movement does not smell strongly and the smell it has then is different from the accustomed smell, this is a sign of death, because the heat is missing from this putrefaction and because it has been turned into another kind, even if the humors in that same person have been changed. 

Also, if the stool is black and dry, it is a precursor of death, because the **black bile** <note>original: "Schwarzgalle." Also known as melancholy, this humor was associated with coldness, dryness, and serious illness.</note> has turned the digestion into blackness and dryness at the point where that person’s humors have prepared themselves for death. If, however, the stool is black and dry and nonetheless smells as usual, the sick person may escape death with great difficulty <note>original: "mit genauer Not" (only just, or with barely a hope).</note>, because although the black bile indicates its poor condition through the blackness and dryness of the person's stool, the person who is internally transformed into blackness and dryness by the black bile nonetheless shows the correct warmth in the smell of the putrefaction. But if this smell differs from the usual, it is a fatal sign, because the person's correct warmth has been lost alongside the unfavorable state of the putrefaction.

<summary>This page explains the proper timing for collecting diagnostic urine and compares the diagnostic value of stool to the earth, focusing on how the presence or absence of "natural warmth" in the smell of excrement indicates a patient's proximity to death.</summary>
<keywords>uroscopy, digestion, humors, black bile, innate heat, putrefaction, diagnostic signs, medieval pathology</keywords>

### On the Diversity of Waters and Baths.

It is not, however, beneficial for a person to bathe in water frequently, unless they are thin and dry, easily chilled or easily warmed, because such a person has weak flesh. Such a person may bathe in water so that they may grant their body some warmth and moisten their frame. However, for those who possess robust flesh <note>The original German "fettes Fleisch" (fat flesh) refers to a person with a sturdy or heavy constitution according to the theory of humors.</note>, water baths are not suitable, because they are already internally warm and moist. Therefore, bathing harms their body by adding even greater heat and moisture, except in cases where they use water baths only extremely rarely to wash away dirt and exit the bath quickly.

The waters that are well-suited for drinking are also good for baths. They must, however, be moderately warmed; in this way, a person may sit in them for a long time if they wish, because such baths do not bring sickness to a person, but rather grant them a good and beautiful complexion. However, waters that are bad for drinking are also bad for baths. If it is necessary for someone to bathe in them, they must be boiled thoroughly so that the impurities <note>original: "Unrat" (refuse or filth).</note> within them are diminished by the boiling. Furthermore, a person should only sit in them for a short time, as they are not healthy.

Rainwater, however, is somewhat sharp and harsh in its effect, because the clouds and the air draw the rain up from various watercourses—both good and bad—as well as from the moisture of the earth, and for this reason, it is unhealthy. Rainwater flows down through the air like **lye** <term>lye: a harsh alkaline solution traditionally made by leaching wood ashes, used for washing or making soap.</term> through ash, which is why it becomes sharp and violent in its action. If it is heated because a person wishes to take a bath in it, its sharpness penetrates the skin and causes some degree of harm.

Snow water is also quite impure, and if someone takes a bath in it, they draw into themselves bad **humors** <term>humors: the vital bodily fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile). Medieval medicine taught that an imbalance of these fluids caused disease.</term> and a high degree of skin rashes, because snow water originates from the external waste of the elements and the cold and the dirt of the earth.

Cistern water is somewhat softer and more useful for baths than rain or snow water, because it has been somewhat purified. People who take baths in flowing rivers during the summer suffer no harm from it, because the rivers are so tempered by the heat of the sun and the air that they are neither too warm nor too cold, but moderate. Nevertheless, they do not greatly influence harmful or bad humors, nor do the bad humors themselves increase because of them.

### On the Punishments in Purgatory.

Certain unquenchable fires are in the air, which are ignited by the various works of men; because that which should have served them for glory becomes a punishing fire for them due to their bad actions, and thus they descend upon certain regions of the lands.

<summary>This page provides medieval health advice on the suitability of bathing for different body types and evaluates the safety of various water sources, ending with a theological note on the origin of purgatorial fires.</summary>

They gather there, where certain rivers also originate and flow out, which draw warmth and glowing heat from these fires, just as—by divine decree—individual souls are purified in these fires and waters. From these waters, however, small streams sometimes flow out into various lands among people; these are always warm because they emerge from the inextinguishable fires. There are also some parts of the earth upon which fire sometimes falls as an avenging, divine punishment, as it is written: "He rains fire down upon the coals, and the spirit of the storms is a portion of their fate!" <note>original: "Es regnet Feuer auf die Kohlen herab..." This is a reference to Psalm 11:6, often interpreted in the Middle Ages as a description of the destruction of Sodom or the nature of Hell.</note> The land, the mountains, and the stones struck by that fire will burn in fire forever until Judgment Day, and in those places that burn so, small streams sometimes spring forth, which are constantly warm and flow warm because of that same fire. By their skill, however, people have directed some watercourses to these places so they might be warmed by them; then, flowing through these regions and receiving heat there, they continue to stream out warm. These waters do not harm those who use them for baths, but rather bring them health, because their warmth restrains the improper heat within them and consumes the bad humors <note>original: "Säfte" (juices/fluids). In medieval medicine, health was determined by the balance of four bodily fluids or "humors."</note> in them.

### On the Sweat Bath.

For a person who is thin and dry, the sweat bath—specifically the one prepared with glowing stones—is not suitable, because he makes himself even drier through it. But for one who has heavy flesh <note>The original German "fettes Fleisch" refers to a person with a sturdy or "phlegmatic" constitution who carries excess fluid.</note>, the sweat bath is good and useful, because he restrains and reduces the humors that are superfluous within him through it. Also for one who suffers from gout <term>Gout (Gicht): In this period, gout referred generally to painful joints or "wandering" pains caused by a buildup of harmful fluids.</term>, baths prepared with hot stones are advantageous, because the humors that repeatedly rise up within him are somewhat suppressed by the sweat bath. After using a water bath, on the other hand, they begin to rise and stir in an improper way, because the flesh, blood, and vessels of such people who suffer from gout flow apart in instability. 

The stones, however, contain within themselves fire and various moistures. When they are placed in the fire, the moisture in them cannot be entirely driven out, and it is therefore not healing to prepare a sweat bath with them; rather, it is much healthier with bricks, because these are fired and dry, since the moistures that were in them have been consumed and removed by the burning of the fire. Therefore, anyone who wishes to bathe in a sweat bath should set it up with bricks. If he cannot have bricks, he can take sandstones instead, because these have a milder fire and gentler moistures within them than other stones. However, he must not take pebbles, because these have a powerful fire within them...

It also provides practical medical advice on sweat baths, recommending fired bricks over natural stones to avoid harmful moisture.</summary>

**On the Diversity of Eyes.** Whoever has gray eyes is sometimes frivolous, sometimes thoughtless, or also very mischievous, or lazy, or unregulated in their conduct. Nevertheless, they bring everything they do to a prosperous end. <note>Medieval physiognomy often linked physical traits like eye color to a person's "complexion" or temperament, believing that external features revealed the internal soul.</note> Whoever has fiery eyes, which are similar to a black cloud next to the sun, is clever, of sharp understanding, and quick-tempered.

**Also on the Eyes.** Whoever has eyes similar to the cloud in which the rainbow shines is of a rather fickle nature—thus at one time sad, at another time cheerful—but is nevertheless honorable in their conduct.

**Likewise.** Whoever has eyes like a dark cloud, which is neither distinctly fiery nor entirely dull, but rather colored more blue-gray, is of an inconsistent nature, easy-going in their conduct, and cunning; yet they possess a good understanding of manual crafts, so that they easily grasp and remember what they do not already know of handiwork.

**Likewise.** Whoever has black or dark eyes, as a cloud sometimes appears, is clever and receptive to good advice, but nevertheless feels constrained in all their works.

### On Cherries and Their Abundant Enjoyment.

If someone has eaten cherries, they should immediately drink plenty of wine, so that their juice is suppressed by the wine and they do not come to harm through this juice. <note>In medieval medicine, fruit was often considered "cold" and "moist," potentially causing an imbalance in the bodily humors; wine, being "warm," was used to "cook" or balance the fruit in the stomach.</note>

**A Note on Conception.** When people are conceived while the moon carries many water-bursts within itself during rainy weather, the water is prone to attracting them toward drowning. Those who are conceived when the moon shines during the greatest summer heat are prone to being attracted by fire to be burned. Those, however, who are conceived during the Dog Days <term>Dog Days: The hottest weeks of summer (July–August), named for the "Dog Star" Sirius. In folklore, these days were often considered dangerous or "biting" times of extreme heat and madness.</term> will, because these are "biting" days, be easily devoured by wild animals. Those conceived in May will easily fall from trees or other high places.

**The First Moon.** The person who is conceived during the first moon, when it receives its light from the sun, will—if it is a boy—become proud and hard-hearted; he loves no human except for the one who fears and honors him. He likes to speak poorly of his fellow men, both of their...

**The second Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the second moon possesses, if it is a boy, a comprehensive understanding and extensive knowledge, a steady character, and is treated with reverence by people; but in a fit of fear, he is easily terrified to his core. He is often, though only lightly, ill and can live longer than one conceived in the first moon. **The third Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the third moon will be, if it is a boy, righteous; yet this righteousness is of no use to him because he is an eccentric <note>original: "Sonderling"; a person who is odd, isolated, or lives outside social norms.</note>, concerning himself more with foreign affairs than his own, and sighing more for strangers than for those he knows. **The fourth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the fourth moon will be, if it is a boy, weak of mind and easily deceived by other people. Yet, at the same time, he is good-natured and also has good fortune, so that he becomes rich and honored in his pride; he is physically healthy and will live quite long, though not very long. If it is a woman, however, she will be of a praiseworthy nature, valued by her fellow human beings, and gets along well with people; she becomes easily ill, often feels weakness in her body, and will not live long.

**The sixth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the sixth moon will, if it is a boy, be good-natured and of a charming character, but he will lack masculine habits and instead be soft like a woman; he is also easily sickened and will not live long. If it is a woman, however, she will be righteous, modest, and beloved by her fellow human beings; she is physically healthy and will not live long.

**The seventh Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the seventh moon will, if it is a boy, be foolish and devoid of understanding, yet he believes himself to be wise when he is not; he will not be lovable to others, possesses strong vessels <note>original: "Gefäße"; in medieval anatomy, this refers to the strength of the veins and arteries, suggesting a robust circulatory system.</note> and is physically not often ill. If an illness does strike him, however, he views himself as a long-suffering martyr and will become melancholic. He will live for a moderate time. If it is a woman, however, she will be bold, but foolish and without understanding, prone to sudden fits of rage <note>original: "Jähzorn"; associated with the choleric temperament of being "hot-blooded."</note>, offensive to others, healthy in body, and will live a long life.

**The eighth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the eighth moon will, if it is a boy, be wise, morally strict, and moderate in all his affairs; he will be a helper to his fellow man and physically healthy. Should he become ill, he recovers easily and will reach a fair, though not an excessive, age. If it is a woman, however, she will be delightful and lovable, fond of adorning herself, and honorable; she does not love men, is physically healthy, and will reach a fair, though not an excessive, age.

**The ninth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the ninth moon is, if it is a boy, easily frightened, will be unchaste <note>original: "unkeusch"; referring here to a lack of sexual or moral restraint.</note>, physically weak, and will not live long. If it is a woman, however, she will be modest, loving men only within the bounds of decency <note>original: "in Züchten"; implying proper upbringing and chaste behavior.</note>, weak of body, and will not live long.

**The tenth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the tenth moon will be righteous, upright <note>original: "bieder"; a term for honest, reliable, and salt-of-the-earth integrity.</note>, and capable, as well as happy, physically healthy, and will live a long life. If it is a woman, however, she will be righteous and loved by her fellow human beings, as desirable as a lily, orderly, and happy. She falls ill easily but will soon recover and live a long life.
