**The eleventh Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the eleventh moon will, if it is a boy, be prone to sudden rage <note>original: "jähzornig"; in medieval medicine, this was often linked to an excess of yellow bile.</note>, have no luck, will not love women, will not be physically healthy, and will also not live very long. If it is a woman, however, she will be easily provoked, busy, and talkative in a way that leads to malicious gossip, but otherwise she will be upright. She will easily be struck by serious illness but will recover quickly, and she will not live long.

**The twelfth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the twelfth moon will, if it is a boy, become fickle, having his mind directed now here, now there; he will strive for foreign places and strange things and love them. His entire outward behavior is uncomfortable for his fellow men; he will be melancholic <note>A state of sadness or introspection believed to be caused by an excess of "black bile."</note> and will not live long. If it is a woman, however, she will have an inconsistent character, be ignorant, and have no desire to be improved by good teachings. She will be a burden to her fellow men and will not have much to do with illness, but nonetheless, she will not live long.

**The thirteenth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the thirteenth moon will, if it is a boy, be of a grumpy temperament, fearful and unreliable; he enjoys slandering people, becomes angry easily, and lives a long time. If it is a woman, however, she will not be amiable, but she will be clever and offer her advice out of deceit. She will be easily plagued by <term>gout</term> <note>original: "Gicht"; a painful form of arthritis often associated with diet and lifestyle in historical texts.</note> and will live a long time.

**The fourteenth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the fourteenth moon will, if it is a boy, be courageous and proud, lead a hardworking life, uphold his honor through work until death, and will not father many children. He will fall ill easily but soon become healthy again and will not live long. If it is a woman, she will be modest, striving for no honor because she is already held in honor herself, and she will keep her honor intact. She will be hardworking but unable to be loved; she will be healthy in body but will not live long.

**The fifteenth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the fifteenth moon will, if it is a boy, live in honor and happiness, being capable in all the works he has begun, and will not falter in them—whether they be good or evil—because he was conceived during a full moon <note>The full moon was believed to provide a peak of energy or "fullness" that made a person's nature more intense and persistent.</note>. He is healthy in body and will not live long. If it is a woman, she will be of a praiseworthy nature, fond of novelties; she will be honored and, in matters that are due to God, she may easily be lost if she has not given God the honor. She will easily become physically ill but soon recover and will not live long.

**The sixteenth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the sixteenth moon will, if it is a boy, have an ordinary character that will please no one, but will instead be dishonest <note>original: "unlauter"; implies a lack of integrity or moral purity.</note>. In his endeavors he will have no success, yet he will possess so much self-conceit that he will be able to get by in this life without want. If it is a woman, however, she will become a fool and possess a character with little perseverance, yet she will nonetheless carry out her lifestyle with pride. Physically she is healthy and will live a long life.

**The seventeenth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the seventeenth moon will, if it is a boy, become a silly person and have a lack of understanding. However, he is useful in other respects, and people will amuse themselves with him as they would with a child. He will be loved and will easily become sick in his marrow <term>marrow</term> <note>original: "Mark"; in medieval thought, the marrow was considered a vital substance linked to strength and the nervous system.</note>; he will also not live very long, though he will reach a tolerable age. If it is a woman, however, she will be foolish, quarrelsome, and inclined to sudden rage, though good-natured in between, so that people will like her for it. She will be frequently plagued by mind-robbing cramps <note>original: "sinnberaubenden Krämpfen"; likely referring to epileptic seizures or fits that were thought to overwhelm the senses.</note> and will also not live long, though she will become old enough.

**The eighteenth Moon.** Whoever is conceived in the eighteenth moon will, if it is a boy, become a thief; he has a passion for stealing and will thus be found to be a thief. He has no landed property because he desires to possess almost no property of the earth—that is to say: no fields, vineyards, or the like—but always desires only to take from others what is not his. Physically he is healthy and, being as he is, will live a long life. If it is a woman, however, she will be deceitful and have a fox-like character. She says almost nothing as she truly means it in her heart, but rather betrays people with her speech because of the worthlessness of her character and, if she manages it, brings honest people to their death. She is physically healthy but suffers at times from mental disturbance <note>original: "Geistesstörung".</note> and can live a long time as she is. Such characters, however, in both the man and the woman, are not pleasing to God.

Physically he is healthy; nonetheless, he will not live long. If it is a woman, however, she will be foolish, yet lovable to her fellow human beings, and will in her self-conceit

...easily come to harm if she is not given help by others. She will fall ill easily, but soon become healthy again and will not live long.

**The Twentieth.** Whoever is conceived in the twentieth moon will, if it is a boy, be strong and wicked, a robber and a murderer, and take delight in it. He will not easily fall ill, but if a sickness has seized him, he will become gravely ill and not live long. If it is a woman, however, she will become a traitress and a destroyer and a poisoner, and she takes pleasure in poisoning her fellow human beings. She will easily be moonstruck <note>original: "mondsüchtig"; in this context, it refers to lunacy or somnambulism, conditions historically believed to be triggered by the phases of the moon.</note> and will live a long life.

**The Twenty-first.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-first moon will, if it is a boy, be empty of understanding and feeling, full of every ill humor; he knows not how to help himself in any matter, but behaves like a fool <term>fool</term> <note>original: "Tor"; a common medieval archetype for someone lacking spiritual or practical wisdom.</note> who wanders astray on his path. Such a person withers away internally, is sorrowful, and cannot be comforted. He will not have proper diseases, but through the sorrowful depression of his spirit, he will sometimes become ill and, in this manner, live a long life. If it is a woman, however, she will be loved by other people but will be fearful and anxious; she also does not know how to set herself upon the right path, so that she might nearly perish from fear even if a child were to threaten her. She also does not have much to suffer physically but is weighed down by the sadness in her soul <note>original: "Gemüt"; refers to one’s emotional nature or disposition.</note> and thereby occasionally becomes unwell, and thus lives long.

**The Twenty-second.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-second moon will, if it is a boy, become of a double-minded nature and behave in war like one who does not fight bravely. But in regard to that which seems advantageous to him, and for that which he recognizes as profitable, he presents himself so before men; and just as the air changes, so too is his spirit. Nonetheless, he is somewhat capable, but not very lovable to his fellow men; he is physically healthy and will live long. If it is a woman, she is hollow and empty in her character and attracts men to herself with this character, though not through dalliance <note>original: "Buhlerei"; refers to flirtation, illicit amorousness, or harlotry.</note>. She enjoys lying and easily falls into heavy illnesses, such as the illnesses of the brain-frenzied <note>original: "Hirnwütigen"; likely referring to phrenitis or those suffering from acute mania and brain fever.</note> and of those whose flesh the worms devour, and she will live long.

**The Twenty-third.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-third moon will, if it is a boy, be benevolent and have a mild disposition; he gladly turns toward a...

...takes good advice, but on the other hand, does not understand how to protect himself from the disadvantages caused by the cunning of others. He does not lack for luck, although he holds onto his fortune only with great difficulty. He will fall ill easily in his body, but will soon recover and live a long time. If it is a woman, however, she will be chaste and, because of the modesty that is natural to her, lovable to all. In her self-esteem, however, she is neither deliberate nor clever, but nevertheless fairly happy, rarely physically ill, and will live a long time.

**The Twenty-fourth.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-fourth moon will, if it is a boy, be prone to bad reputation and will be cautious, always striving to be wealthy for himself alone. He is miserly and of use to hardly any person. Physical illness does not trouble him much, and he will live a fairly long time. If it is a woman, however, she will be clever and cunning and appear righteous to people, but she does not benefit them. She is not much ill in the body, but will sometimes have to suffer from the <term>dragon-swelling</term> <note>original: "Drachengeschwulst"; historically used to describe hard, malignant tumors or severe glandular swellings, sometimes associated in folklore with "dracontiasis" or simply "evil" growths.</note> and will live a fairly long time.

**The Twenty-fifth.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-fifth moon will, if it is a boy, be proud and wicked-minded <note>original: "frevelmütig"; refers to a spirit of defiance against divine or social laws.</note> and would be wise if the pride and wickedness within him did not blow his wisdom away, like the wind that scatters the dust. He is like one who desires more than he is capable of, thus like a ship that can barely float in the water. His pride will often turn to his disadvantage, and he is unpleasant to his fellow human beings. He will easily fall into loathsome diseases and will not live long. If it is a woman, however, she will have a beautiful face, making herself appear great, so to speak, in her righteousness, yet she is not righteous; for if one looks for righteousness in her, it will not be found. Because of this, she makes herself hated and will fall due to her arrogance. She is not much ill in the body, but will not live long.

**The Twenty-sixth.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-sixth moon will, if it is a boy, be wise and deliberate upon all his actions with understanding. He will be easily plagued by fevers and can live a long time. If it is a woman, however, she will be clever and industrious, steadfast and chaste, but easily plagued by <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "Phlegma"; in the medieval theory of the four humors, an excess of phlegm was thought to cause sluggishness, respiratory issues, or "cold" illnesses.</note> and will live a long time.

**The Twenty-seventh.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-seventh moon will, if it is a boy, be fearful, easily consumed by melancholy, and he is filled with dread...

...easily. Nonetheless, he is righteous and useful, and is also loved by his fellow men. However, severe illnesses, such as <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "Schwarzgalle"; refers to melancholy, one of the four "humors" in medieval medicine believed to cause sadness or depression.</note>, easily torment him, and he will live quite long. If it is a woman, she will be virtuous, so that she is well-liked by her fellow men; she will be physically weak, but will nonetheless live quite long.

**The Twenty-eighth.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-eighth moon will, if it is a boy, be difficult to see through in his mindset, his habits, and his outward behavior, and he acts as if he is not in his right mind. He has, however, sufficient insight and knowledge for his own person, but is insufferable and easily falls into the illness of <term>senselessness</term> <note>original: "Sinnlosigkeit"; likely referring to dementia, delirium, or a loss of rational thought.</note> and will live quite long. If it is a woman, she will be foolish and stupid, possess a character that is difficult to bear, and cannot be loved by people; she will be easily plagued by fever and will become relatively old.

**The Twenty-ninth.** Whoever is conceived in the twenty-ninth moon will, if it is a boy, be curious and difficult to understand in his customs and outward behavior; he loves new fashions in clothing and the customs of foreign lands, and warms to upstart, unreliable people. He easily develops <term>poisonous juices</term> <note>original: "giftige Säfte"; referring to toxic humors or fluids in the body believed to cause chronic illness.</note> in his body and will therefore often be sick and not live long. If it is a woman, she will be indolent and vain, drawing men after her with her manners and behavior; she will easily fall ill in the stomach and will not live long.

**The thirtieth moon.** Whoever is conceived in the thirtieth moon will, if it is a boy, be poor, and, if he is of noble birth, he will continue to decline and have no luck. He easily loses weight, strength, and flesh, but will live long enough. If it is a woman, she will be poor and talkative when scolding, and she prefers to be with strangers rather than with her acquaintances. She will not be physically ill much and will live quite long.

[Which countries' plants are suitable for various cures. Those who were previously healthy and suddenly fall ill must be helped with plants growing in the **East** <note>original: "Orient."</note>. For those suffering from <term>black bile</term> and pain in the side, one should assist with herbs growing in the **West**. For those suffering from paralysis and severe, daily fevers—including the three-day <note>tertian</note> and four-day <note>quartan</note> fevers—as well as those with stomach ailments, help should come from plants that grow in the **South**. Those who are <term>raving</term> <note>original: "Tollwütige"; could refer to rabies or general mania/insanity.</note> and those suffering from liver ailments must be treated with plants growing in the **North**.]

<meta>This page concludes the medical and prognostic treatise, continuing from the discussion of conception and constitutional health based on humoral theory. It marks the end of the section attributed to Hildegard of Bingen and the conclusion of the volume.</meta>

### On the Diversity of Conception

Whoever is conceived from black blood will walk on his feet as a child and speak early, but is dull and possesses a difficult nature. If he falls ill, he remains weak for a long time, because black blood is full of phlegm and pestilence <note>original: "Pestilenz"; here referring to corrupt or infectious matter rather than the specific plague disease.</note>. A child conceived from red blood will walk earlier and speak earlier than one born of black blood; he is nimble in his movements, surprising in his habits—though he lacks endurance in them—is not ill for long but soon recovers, and will become clever.

Red blood is healthy. A child conceived from thick, rightly colored blood will be stout of body and would soon walk if he were able to do so despite the weight of his body. He is healthy, crawls agilely on his hands, and will not be ill for long; he is clever and capable, and such blood is healthy. A child conceived from watery, thin blood is empty of understanding, can endure nothing, falls ill easily, and is not easily angered; such blood is full of phlegm, and he remains a child for a long time because he does not walk on his feet or speak.

### Why People Sleep After a Purge and After Bloodletting

That people sleep after taking a <term>purge</term> <note>original: "Abführtrank"; a medicinal drink, often a laxative or emetic, used to expel "excess" humors from the body.</note> or after undergoing <term>bloodletting</term> <note>original: "Aderlaß"; the surgical practice of withdrawing blood to balance the four humors.</note> comes from the emptying of the vessels; for when the vessels sense the emptying of humors and blood within themselves, they desire to rest, and therefore the person suddenly sinks into sleep.

### Against the Common Cold

If the <term>cold flow</term> <note>original: "kalte Fluß"; referring to catarrh or nasal discharge, categorized as a "cold" humor in medieval medicine.</note> flows excessively from the nose, the smoke of fir wood should be drawn in through the nose, and the flow will be more easily loosened and will cease. One can also prepare the ash of fir wood and make a <term>lye</term> <note>original: "Lauge"; an alkaline solution used for washing.</note> from it to wash the person's head. It causes the bad humors in the head to diminish and makes the eyes bright.

### On the Causes of Fevers

Fevers, however, arise from sleeping too long, or from immoderate eating and drinking, or from weariness and idleness, when a person does not work.

->Let every creature say: Amen!<-

<meta>This page serves as an appendix to the preceding medical texts, providing scholarly commentary and definitions for specific terms used in the works of Hildegard of Bingen, particularly focusing on her unique vocabulary and the sources of her natural history.</meta>

# Appendix

### I.

1) Confusion of <term>Agate</term> <note>original: "Achates"</note> with <term>Magnetite</term> <note>original: "Magnes"</note>, or lodestone.
2) Specifically regarding the fluid present in <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "Wassersucht"; an archaic term for edema or the swelling caused by excess bodily fluids.</note>.
3) In the Latin text, on page 30, line 4, the word <term>Livor</term> appears here for the first time. *Livor* actually signifies a blue-red discoloration of the skin, such as occurs through a blow, bruising, or internal bleeding. Similarly, Dieffenbach, in his *New Glossary of Medieval and Late Latin* <note>original: "Novum glossarium latino-germanicum mediae et infimae aetatis"</note>, translates the word *livor* as a bump, a bruise, or a mark from a blow. — In a figurative sense, *Livor* also means malice or envy, just as the expression "pale envy" remains a popular idiom today. Hildegard herself, however, says in the second book—where she speaks of the stomach and its digestive disorders—that undigested food left in the stomach causes "<note>original: "multum livorem, id est slim"</note> much discoloration, that is, slime" to appear. See page 97. The expression <term>livorosus</term> <note>meaning "slimy" or "full of impurities"</note> is also found in Hildegard's *Physica*. In the *Causae et Curae* <note>Hildegard's work on the "Causes and Cures" of diseases</note>, Hildegard employs the word "Livor" on all possible occasions, in which one is sometimes unable to make sense of it using either its primary meaning or its figurative use as "slime." One is tempted to assume that Hildegard used the word "Livor" wherever the appropriate Latin expression for what she actually wanted to say was not available to her. It is possible that the word was also taken from monastery jargon. It is unlikely that it belonged to the collection of secret expressions favored by Hildegard, as the *Causae et Curae* was, after all, written for a wider circle of readers. In the translation into German, I have adhered as closely as possible to the meaning given by Hildegard: *Livor* equals slime. Only where it was truly inadmissible have I chosen the translation that seemed most appropriate for the specific passage at hand.

### II.

1) Konrad von Megenberg: *Book of Nature* <note>original: "Buch der Natur"</note>, page 106: "<term>Pliny</term> <note>Gaius Plinius Secundus, a Roman author and natural philosopher</note> states that when the stag perceives that he is suffering from an illness or from old age, he draws snakes out of their holes with his nostrils and consumes them. When he has eaten them, he becomes thirsty from their poison and immediately runs to a spring to drink. Through this, he rejuvenates himself and regains his strength."
2) The word <term>Phlegm</term> <note>original: "Phlegma"</note> is not so easy to interpret; in any case, it is not sufficient—as has often happened—to simply translate it as "slime." *Phlegm* actually means "the burnt," that which remains as residue after burning: the slag. Alchemy generally used the term *Phlegm* to describe the residue left over during a distillation. In the Hippocratic book *On Winds* <note>original: "de Flatibus (peri physon)"</note>, it says in the 16th chapter: "Where the phlegm itself is mixed with sharp juices and has broken through somewhere, especially in unaccustomed places, it..."

<summary>This appendix page provides philological notes on Hildegard of Bingen's unique Latin vocabulary, particularly her use of the word "Livor" to mean slime or impurity, and explains medieval natural history references regarding stags and the etymology of "Phlegm."</summary>
<keywords>Hildegard of Bingen, Livor, Phlegm, Medieval Latin, Konrad von Megenberg, medical terminology, etymology, humoral theory, natural history</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the scholarly commentary on Hildegard of Bingen’s medical terminology, specifically addressing the definitions of bodily fluids (humors) and corrections to the Latin manuscript.</meta>

inflames these." **Galen** <note>a prominent Greek physician in the Roman Empire</note> says in his explanation of the terms used by **Hippocrates**: "The **phlegm** <note>original: "Phlegma"</note> proves not only the presence of a white juice, but also a **phlogosis** <note>original: "Phlogosis"; Greek for inflammation or burning</note>, an inflammation, a burning." (Cited from **L. A. Kraus**: *Critical-Etymological Lexicon of Medicine*, 1844.) According to our modern view, those seen as afflicted with excess **phlegm** would be those in whose organs substances have accumulated that have not been overcome by normal metabolism, such as uric acid, bile components, and also sugar. To avoid the dilemma of providing a correct translation every time, I have consistently used the original word **phlegm**. As will be shown further in the text, **Hildegard** herself assumed several types of **phlegm**.

3) "They emit, as it were, ignited drops." <note>original: "Emittunt velut ignitas guttas."</note> This concept, unique to ancient medicine, provides the explanation for the use of the word *gutta* <note>Latin for "drop"</note> for **gout** <note>a disease once believed to be caused by "drops" of morbid humors falling into the joints</note>.

4) The **black bile** (**Melancholia** <note>from the Greek *melas*, meaning black, and *chole*, meaning bile</note>) belonged to the four so-called cardinal humors: blood, yellow bile, **phlegm**, and black bile. **Paulus Aegineta** <note>a 7th-century Byzantine physician</note> says of it: "Melancholy is a mental disorder without fever, stemming primarily from a black-biliary fluid which has cast itself upon the spiritual organs, whereby either the brain suffers originally and primarily, or the entire body suffers." (**Kraus**, as cited above.)

5) See page 21: Regarding the dawn.

6) In the text on page 43, line 2, it says *folii*, of a leaf. I suggest reading it instead as *follis*, of a bellows, as otherwise no proper sense can be made of the passage.

7) Genesis Chapter 6, Verse 2.

8) In the text on page 49, line 26, it says *adversum poenas*, against the punishments. This could refer to the punishments of hell. However, it is also possible that through a scribal error, the word *poenas* entered the text in place of *pruinas*, in which case it would mean: against the winter frost.

9) Text page 50, line 29: "the remaining two follow moderately with slime." <note>original: "reliqua duo cum livore moderate subsequuntur."</note> The interpretation of *cum livore* is difficult. I have translated it as "hesitantly," but literally it would have to be: with slime, or slimy.

10) Text page 52, line 2: "attracted." <note>original: "attracta"</note>. As clearly follows from the section "On the Mute" further below, it must be *attractus* instead of *attracta*, as this word refers to the following term *livor* <note>slime or impurity</note>.

11) In the text, the last two sentences begin the following section.

12) This sentence belongs at the beginning of the following section.

13) **Python** is the name of the demon invoked during the conjuring of the devil.

14) Revelation <note>original: "Apokalypse"</note> Chapter 10, Verse 4.

15) **P. Kaiser** points out in his preface that he has used brackets [ ] to indicate passages or sentences that he considers to not have originally belonged in the text. I have followed **Kaiser's** procedure in this translation.

16) In the beginning was the deed! <note>A famous line from **Goethe's** Faust I.</note>

17) In the text on page 66, line 8, it says: "it is nourished by the black blood of the woman's liver." <note>original: "nutritur de nigro sanguine jecoris mulieris"</note> **Kraus** (as cited above) states that in place of the word **Placenta**, the expression *Hepar (Jecur) matricis*—the liver of the womb—is also found in ancient medicine. Accordingly, the present passage can also be translated as: through the black blood of the afterbirth (*Secundina* = **Placenta** with the fetal membranes) of the woman, which would correspond more closely to the facts.

18) In the text on page 81, line 36: "while life rejoices," <note>original: "laetante vita"</note>. In connection with what immediately precedes it, it seems better to me to read *latente* instead of *laetante*, meaning: while life remains hidden.

The notes also suggest corrections for scribal errors in the Latin manuscript regarding anatomical and theological terms.</summary>

<keywords>phlegm, black bile, melancholy, gout, Galen, Hippocrates, Paulus Aegineta, L. A. Kraus, Hildegard of Bingen, medical etymology, placenta, philology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philological and medical commentary on the text of Hildegard of Bingen, focusing on specific terminology for diseases, anatomical corrections, and the history of medical practices like cupping and bloodletting.</meta>

19) Text Page 89, Line 5: "they incur the black dragon-swelling" <note>original: "nigrum tumorem dragunculi (dracunculi) incurrunt"</note>. I have not succeeded in determining what is actually meant here. It is unlikely to refer to the swelling caused by the Medina worm <note>original: *Filaria medinensis*; a parasitic nematode</note>, which was called *dracunculus*. So-called blackheads <note>original: *comedones*</note> also went by the name *dracunculi*. Might one consider varicose veins <note>the so-called "vein knots"</note> due to their blue-black color, or perhaps even pathological pigment accumulations?

20) Text Page 93, Line 11: Instead of "is" <note>original: *est*</note>, it should logically read "are" <note>original: *sunt*</note>.

21) The sentence beginning on Text Page 95, Line 8: "But also the stomach..." <note>original: "Sed et stomachus..."</note> belongs to the following section regarding the swelling of the spleen.

22) This sentence on Text Page 97, Line 35 forms a section of its own, titled: *On the irritation of the nerves* <note>original: "De nervorum indignatione"</note>. Contextually, it belongs at the end of the preceding section.

23) Text Page 98, Line 35: I have translated "to be put to flight" <note>original: "fugari"</note> as "to shock" <note>original: "schrecken"</note>. "Shocking" is a popular culinary expression in some regions. When water in which tough-to-boil foods—such as dried peas, beans, and the like—are cooking has mostly evaporated, it is replaced by pouring in cold water. The contents of the cooking vessel are thereby "shocked" <note>a technique used to soften the skins of legumes or stop the cooking process</note>.

24) Text Page 101, Line 12: Difficulty in urinating <note>original: *Dissuria (Dysuria)*</note>. This word usually describes the condition involving painful or difficult urination <note>original: *Stranguria*; see section III. 42</note>.

25) Text Page 103, Line 25: I have translated "the wheel" <note>original: "rotam"</note> as "joint." Perhaps it is used as an abbreviation for "rotation" <note>original: *rotationem*; referring to a pivot joint or *Diarthrosis*</note>, although that does not make the matter much clearer. — In the same section, Line 27, "veins" <note>original: "venis"</note> appears instead of "tendons" <note>original: "nervis"</note>.

26) Text Page 118, Line 14: "since its blood is then dissolved in the opening of wandering." <note>original: "quoniam sanguis ejus in apertione vagationis tunc dissolutus est"</note> According to Hildegard’s view, joyful excitement causes the blood vessels to expand, and the blood consequently loses its internal cohesion.

27) Text Page 121, Line 16: "black thistles" <note>original: "cardi (cardui) nigri"</note>. **Matthiolius** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a 16th-century doctor and botanist</note>, in his *Herbal* (German edition by **J. Camerarius**, 1590), reports on Page 221 A regarding this species of Carline thistle not native to Germany: "Large or black Carline thistle <note>original: *Carlina caulescens*, formerly *Chamaeleon niger*</note>. It is also used in medicines with which one is accustomed to cauterize."

28) Text Page 121, Line 35. **Konrad von Megenberg** <note>a 14th-century scholar</note> states in the aforementioned work: "Vessels also run through the large tubes located in the middle of the chest up into the head, and from there three blood-bearing vessels go to the arms. One originates from the head and is called: head-vein, or in Latin: *Cephalica*. The second comes from the liver; it bears the Latin name: *Hepatica*. Our book calls it: *Basilica*, which means: 'base-vein,' because the liver is the ground and origin of the blood. The third comes from the heart and runs along the arm between the two mentioned above. Therefore, it bears the Latin name: *Mediana*, in German: the middle [vein]."

29) Text Page 122, Line 14: "horns and cupping glasses" <note>original: "cornibus et ventosis"</note>. The cupping glass <note>original: *Ventosa* or *Cucurbita sicca*</note> was used for so-called "dry" or bloodless cupping, where blood is merely collected under the skin in specific areas. The "horns" <note>original: *Cornua*</note>, cupping vessels made of horn, were used for "wet" or bloody cupping. In **Matthiolius** (cited above), an interesting note is found on Page 251 C: "Water boiled with marjoram is quite good when placed in cupping glasses or horns, or used to moisten them; it protects the person so that nothing evil strikes after cupping, as often happens through unclean irons or cups."

30) Text Page 127, Line 5: "into the hips" <note>original: "in coxas"</note>. However, the term for hip <note>original: *Coxa*</note> can also mean the region between the knee and the hip, thus—

the thigh. **Dieffenbach**, in the work cited: "thus above the knees" <note>original German: „also bouen den kneen“</note>, above the knees.

31) Text Page 128, Line 5: "On the boilings" <note>original Latin: „De cocturis“</note>, actually: on the boilings. This refers to the application of so-called burning cones or moxas, as well as cauterization with a glowing iron <note>Moxibustion is a traditional technique where dried plant matter is burned on or near the skin to stimulate healing</note>.

32) Text Page 129, Line 11: "pith of the spindle tree" <note>original Latin: „medulla fusarii“</note>, the marrow of the spindle tree, *Euonymus europaeus*, also known as the European spindle or "priest's cap." At least, that is what the relevant specifications state.

33) Text Page 130, Line 5: "bark of the gorse" <note>original Latin: „corticem de ulice“</note>, bark from the common gorse (*Ulex europaeus*). There is clearly a clerical error here. Instead of *de ulice* (from gorse), it should be read as *de vibice* (from the birch or willow-twig). **K. v. Megenberg**, in the work cited, page 283: "Myrica is a name for a birch, in Latin also called *vibex*." — Birch bark is rich in resin.

34) **P. Kaiser** rightly identifies the bracketed passage as not belonging here.

35) Text Page 145, Line 18: "On melancholy and the *psalmo*" <note>original Latin: „De melancolia et psalmo“</note>. The meaning of the word *psalmo* was not discoverable to me. **P. Kaiser** says on page 249 of his edition: "*psalmus* is a certain disease of the nerves." I have not been able to find this interpretation elsewhere. Is "*psalmo*" a misspelling of "*psammo*" <note>Greek for "sand," referring to urinary grit or gravel</note>?

36) Text Page 148, Line 30. A monochord is a stringed instrument with only one string, the so-called "Brummscheit" <note>drone-log</note> or *tromba marina* (marine trumpet), which replaced the missing bass voice during the nuns' choral chants.

37) On drunkenness.

### III.

1) Wild mallow (*Malva silvestris*), Dwarf mallow (*M. rotundifolia*).
2) Common sage (*Salvia officinalis*).
3) Text Page 166, Line 23: "water of *cnith*" <note>original Latin: „aqua cnith“</note>. **P. Kaiser** equates *cnith* with *depse* <note>Teasel, *Dipsacus*</note>. It is more likely that "water of *cnith*" or thistle water refers to the water prepared from the Blessed Thistle (*Cnicus benedictus*), also known as the St. Benedict's thistle. The Blessed Thistle is an ancient folk remedy and medicinal plant.
4) Marjoram (*Origanum Majorana*). **Matthiolius**, in the work cited, page 251 A: "Marjoram is a noble medicine for all cold or phlegmatic ailments of the brain."
5) Common fennel (*Foeniculum officinale*).
6) Common horehound (*Marrubium vulgare*).
7) Black nightshade (*Solanum nigrum*).
8) Garden pea as opposed to the field pea?*)
9) Rhizome of the Lesser Galangal (*Alpinia officinarum*).
10) Several herbs, mostly belonging to folk medicine, go by the name "Bertram": Sneezewort (*Achillea Ptarmica*), German pellitory (*Anacyclus officinarum*), Feverfew (*Chrysanthemum Parthenium*), or Roman chamomile (*Anthemis nobilis*).
11) Costmary (*Tanacetum Balsamita*). It could also be a curled form of Water Mint (*Mentha aquatica*). Compare **Matthiolius**, in the work cited, page 244 C.
12) German iris (*Iris germanica*).
13) Species of Plantain (*Plantago*).
14) Zinc ore, also the flue dust from a zinc smelter <note>Historically used to make "tutty," a powder for medicinal eye salves</note>.
15) Common rue (*Ruta graveolens*).

---

*) In the *Salerno Health Regimen* <note>original Latin: *Regimen sanitatis salernitanum*, a famous medieval poem on domestic medicine</note> it says in verse 94 and following: "We have decided to praise and condemn the pea: once the skins are removed, the pea is good enough. With the skins, it is bloating and harmful." <note>original Latin: „Sisam laudare decrevimus ac reprobare: Pellibus ablatis est bona pisa satis. Est inflativa cum pellibus atque nociva“</note>. Here, a distinction is made between peeled and unpeeled peas.

26) A species of Germander <note>*Teucrium*</note>.
27) Milk Thistle <note>*Silybum marianum*</note>.
28) Text page 205, line 27: "cephania" <note>original: *cephania*</note>. This likely refers to Garlic <note>*Allium sativum*</note>, of which **Matthiolus** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli, a famous 16th-century physician and botanist</note> says in the work cited above on page 172 A: "Against the yellow color of the body after jaundice <note>original: *Geelsucht*</note>: Eat raw or cooked garlic."
29) Text page 205, line 27: "nimmoli" <note>original: *nimmoli*</note>, the yellow-flowering Moneywort <note>*Lysimachia nummularis*</note>, also known as Creeping Jenny?

39) Field Eryngo <note>*Eryngium campestre*</note>? **Matthiolus** in the work cited above, page 229 A: "it also helps against daily and quartan <note>recurring every four days</note> fevers."
40) Text page 217, line 6: "critum" <note>original: *critum*</note>, Gypsum?, of which **K. v. Megenberg** <note>Konrad von Megenberg, author of the 14th-century *Book of Nature*</note> says in the work cited above: "It also helps people who suffer from the wasting disease that is called *Phthisis* <note>tuberculosis</note> in Latin."
41) **K. v. Megenberg**, page 372: "One crushes it (the diamond) with still-fresh goat's blood." <note>A common medieval belief that the blood of a goat was the only substance capable of breaking a diamond.</note>
42) Butterbur <note>*Petasites vulgaris*</note>.

->Published by the Medical Review, Otto Gmelin, Munich 2 NW<-

## Like cures like
<note>original Latin: "Similia similibus curantur," the foundational principle of homeopathy</note>
->By the late Privy Councilor Prof. Dr. Hugo Schulz, Greifswald<-
<term>Privy Councilor: (German: "Geheimrat") a prestigious honorary title bestowed upon high-ranking officials or distinguished professors in Imperial and Weimar Germany.</term>
->3rd edition. 1925. 44 pages. Large octavo. 1.40 Marks.<-

> The position of the Greifswald pharmacologist regarding the question of appropriate therapy is well known. In the present study, Schulz proves that the famous thesis "Like cures like" was not first introduced to the world by Hahnemann <note>Samuel Hahnemann (1755–1843), the founder of homeopathy</note>, but was already explicitly established by Hippocrates and his school and defended again by Paracelsus over a millennium later. This well-written booklet, which in its second part provides an excellent explanation of the considerations and facts underlying this thesis and its value for clinical practice, can be recommended for reading to all who are interested in the subject. It must be emphasized that, despite all intended objectivity, the circumstance that the author himself has decided in favor of the principle of "Like cures like" leaves its mark on the study.
->“Pharmaceutical Journal”<-

---

## My Position on Homeopathy
->By the late Privy Councilor Prof. Dr. Hugo Schulz, Greifswald<-
->1925. 23 pages. Octavo. 1 Mark.<-

---

# Outstanding Tropical Doctors
### in Word and Image
->By University Prof. G. Olpp, MD,<-
->Director of the Tübingen Tropical Institute.<-
->VIII, 440 pages. Large octavo with 280 portraits on 72 plates. 1932.<-
->Paperback 30 Marks, bound 33 Marks.<-

The author, the well-known director of the German Institute for Medical Missions in Tübingen, presents in this excellently produced collection 300 biographies of outstanding men and women who ventured out to the tropics as pioneers of medical science to combat the diseases native to those regions with dedication and self-sacrifice. He also includes those physicians who, in the laboratories and hospitals of their home countries, sought to get to the bottom of the causes of tropical diseases, investigating the life habits of pathogenic <note>disease-causing</note> microorganisms and insects and participating in the fight against these pests with the experience they gathered; and finally, those physicians in their tropical homelands who dedicated themselves particularly to the diseases associated with the location and the living habits of the local populations. Thus, his presentation extends to researchers from all countries, including Japanese, Chinese, Russian, American, and European figures.

<vocab>Like cures like, Hugo Schulz, Homeopathy, G. Olpp, Tropical doctors, Pharmacology, Hippocrates, Paracelsus, Hahnemann</vocab>

<summary>This page contains advertisements for medical publications from 1925 and 1932, highlighting works on the historical origins of homeopathy by Hugo Schulz and a comprehensive biographical encyclopedia of tropical medicine pioneers by G. Olpp.</summary>
<keywords>homeopathy, medical history, tropical medicine, Hugo Schulz, G. Olpp, medical biography, pharmacology, German medical publishing</keywords>
